# Courses Listing 

## List of Certifications – Study guides

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/credentials/certifications/resources/study-guides/az-104">https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/credentials/certifications/resources/study-guides/az-104</a></p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image2.webp" style="width:7.56907in;height:4.08333in" /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Skills measured as of April 18, 2025</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Audience profile</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>As a candidate for this exam, you should have subject matter expertise in implementing, managing, and monitoring an organization’s Microsoft Azure environment, including virtual networks, storage, compute, identity, security, and governance.</p>
<p>As an Azure administrator, you often serve as part of a larger team dedicated to implementing an organization's cloud infrastructure. You also coordinate with other roles to deliver Azure networking, security, database, application development, and DevOps solutions.</p>
<p>You should be familiar with:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Operating systems</p></li>
<li><p>Networking</p></li>
<li><p>Servers</p></li>
<li><p>Virtualization</p></li>
</ul>
<p>In addition, you should have experience with:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>PowerShell</p></li>
<li><p>Azure CLI</p></li>
<li><p>The Azure portal</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Resource Manager templates</p></li>
<li><p>Microsoft Entra ID</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Skills at a glance</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Manage Azure identities and governance (20–25%)</p></li>
<li><p>Implement and manage storage (15–20%)</p></li>
<li><p>Deploy and manage Azure compute resources (20–25%)</p></li>
<li><p>Implement and manage virtual networking (15–20%)</p></li>
<li><p>Monitor and maintain Azure resources (10–15%)</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Manage Azure identities and governance (20–25%)</strong></p>
<p><strong>Manage Microsoft Entra users and groups</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create users and groups</p></li>
<li><p>Manage user and group properties</p></li>
<li><p>Manage licenses in Microsoft Entra ID</p></li>
<li><p>Manage external users</p></li>
<li><p>Configure self-service password reset (SSPR)</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Manage access to Azure resources</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Manage built-in Azure roles</p></li>
<li><p>Assign roles at different scopes</p></li>
<li><p>Interpret access assignments</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Manage Azure subscriptions and governance</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Implement and manage Azure Policy</p></li>
<li><p>Configure resource locks</p></li>
<li><p>Apply and manage tags on resources</p></li>
<li><p>Manage resource groups</p></li>
<li><p>Manage subscriptions</p></li>
<li><p>Manage costs by using alerts, budgets, and Azure Advisor recommendations</p></li>
<li><p>Configure management groups</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Implement and manage storage (15–20%)</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Configure access to storage</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p>Configure Azure Storage firewalls and virtual networks</p></li>
<li><p>Create and use shared access signature (SAS) tokens</p></li>
<li><p>Configure stored access policies</p></li>
<li><p>Manage access keys</p></li>
<li><p>Configure identity-based access for Azure Files</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Configure and manage storage accounts</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create and configure storage accounts</p></li>
<li><p>Configure Azure Storage redundancy</p></li>
<li><p>Configure object replication</p></li>
<li><p>Configure storage account encryption</p></li>
<li><p>Manage data by using Azure Storage Explorer and AzCopy</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Configure Azure Files and Azure Blob Storage</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create and configure a file share in Azure Storage</p></li>
<li><p>Create and configure a container in Blob Storage</p></li>
<li><p>Configure storage tiers</p></li>
<li><p>Configure soft delete for blobs and containers</p></li>
<li><p>Configure snapshots and soft delete for Azure Files</p></li>
<li><p>Configure blob lifecycle management</p></li>
<li><p>Configure blob versioning</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Deploy and manage Azure compute resources (20–25%)</strong></p>
<p><strong>Automate deployment of resources by using Azure Resource Manager (ARM) templates or Bicep files</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Interpret an Azure Resource Manager template or a Bicep file</p></li>
<li><p>Modify an existing Azure Resource Manager template</p></li>
<li><p>Modify an existing Bicep file</p></li>
<li><p>Deploy resources by using an Azure Resource Manager template or a Bicep file</p></li>
<li><p>Export a deployment as an Azure Resource Manager template or convert an Azure Resource Manager template to a Bicep file</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Create and configure virtual machines</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create a virtual machine</p></li>
<li><p>Configure Azure Disk Encryption</p></li>
<li><p>Move a virtual machine to another resource group, subscription, or region</p></li>
<li><p>Manage virtual machine sizes</p></li>
<li><p>Manage virtual machine disks</p></li>
<li><p>Deploy virtual machines to availability zones and availability sets</p></li>
<li><p>Deploy and configure an Azure Virtual Machine Scale Sets</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Provision and manage containers in the Azure portal</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create and manage an Azure container registry</p></li>
<li><p>Provision a container by using Azure Container Instances</p></li>
<li><p>Provision a container by using Azure Container Apps</p></li>
<li><p>Manage sizing and scaling for containers, including Azure Container Instances and Azure Container Apps</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Create and configure Azure App Service</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Provision an App Service plan</p></li>
<li><p>Configure scaling for an App Service plan</p></li>
<li><p>Create an App Service</p></li>
<li><p>Configure certificates and Transport Layer Security (TLS) for an App Service</p></li>
<li><p>Map an existing custom DNS name to an App Service</p></li>
<li><p>Configure backup for an App Service</p></li>
<li><p>Configure networking settings for an App Service</p></li>
<li><p>Configure deployment slots for an App Service</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Implement and manage virtual networking (15–20%)</strong></p>
<p><strong>Configure and manage virtual networks in Azure</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create and configure virtual networks and subnets</p></li>
<li><p>Create and configure virtual network peering</p></li>
<li><p>Configure public IP addresses</p></li>
<li><p>Configure user-defined network routes</p></li>
<li><p>Troubleshoot network connectivity</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Configure secure access to virtual networks</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create and configure network security groups (NSGs) and application security groups</p></li>
<li><p>Evaluate effective security rules in NSGs</p></li>
<li><p>Implement Azure Bastion</p></li>
<li><p>Configure service endpoints for Azure platform as a service (PaaS)</p></li>
<li><p>Configure private endpoints for Azure PaaS</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Configure name resolution and load balancing</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Configure Azure DNS</p></li>
<li><p>Configure an internal or public load balancer</p></li>
<li><p>Troubleshoot load balancing</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Monitor and maintain Azure resources (10–15%)</strong></p>
<p><strong>Monitor resources in Azure</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Interpret metrics in Azure Monitor</p></li>
<li><p>Configure log settings in Azure Monitor</p></li>
<li><p>Query and analyze logs in Azure Monitor</p></li>
<li><p>Set up alert rules, action groups, and alert processing rules in Azure Monitor</p></li>
<li><p>Configure and interpret monitoring of virtual machines, storage accounts, and networks by using Azure Monitor Insights</p></li>
<li><p>Use Azure Network Watcher and Connection Monitor</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Implement backup and recovery</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create a Recovery Services vault</p></li>
<li><p>Create an Azure Backup vault</p></li>
<li><p>Create and configure a backup policy</p></li>
<li><p>Perform backup and restore operations by using Azure Backup</p></li>
<li><p>Configure Azure Site Recovery for Azure resources</p></li>
<li><p>Perform a failover to a secondary region by using Site Recovery</p></li>
<li><p>Configure and interpret reports and alerts for backups</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### AZ-104: Monitor and back up Azure resources

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>Modules: <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/paths/az-104-monitor-backup-resources/">https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/paths/az-104-monitor-backup-resources/</a></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p>Introduction to Azure Backup</p>
<p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/intro-to-azure-backup/">https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/intro-to-azure-backup/</a></p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p>Protect your virtual machines by using Azure Backup</p>

## Introduction to Azure Cloud Shell

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>Azure Cloud Shell is a browser-accessible command-line experience for managing Azure resources. It provides the flexibility of choosing the shell experience that best suits the way you work, either Bash or PowerShell. Traditionally, to interact with Azure resources via command-line, you need to install the necessary components into your local computer (PC, Mac, Linux). With Cloud Shell, you have an authenticated, interactive shell that isn't part of a local machine.</p>
<p>You're an IT admin for Contoso Corporation, responsible for the cloud infrastructure on which the company hosts its applications. These applications use different cloud resources, such as Azure VMs, Azure blob storage, Azure networking, and others. On many occasions, you're asked to perform operations on these cloud resources at moments when you’re not using your work laptop. In some cases, you have a friend’s laptop, a smartphone, or another personal PC.</p>
<p>This module explains what Azure Cloud Shell does, how it works, and when you should choose to use Cloud Shell as a solution to meet your organization's needs.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### What is Azure Cloud Shell

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>Azure Cloud Shell is a command-line environment you can access through your web browser. You can use this environment to manage Azure resources, including VMs, storage, and networking. Just like you do when using the Azure CLI or Azure PowerShell.</p>
<p>Because Microsoft manages Cloud Shell, you always have access to the most recent versions of the Azure CLI and PowerShell modules right from any browser. You don't have to worry about keeping modules up to date. With Cloud Shell, you just open your browser and sign in. Just like that, you have access to a command-line environment fully connected with your account's permissions and the resources to which you have access. All that works in an infrastructure that's compliant with double encryption at rest by default. You don't need to take any further action!</p>
<p>Azure Cloud Shell also provides cloud storage to persist files such as SSH keys, scripts, and more. This functionality lets you access important files in between sessions and with different machines. Finally, you can use the Cloud Shell editor to make changes to files, such as scripts, that are saved into this cloud storage directly from the Cloud Shell interface</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### How does it work 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>As an IT admin for Contoso Corporation, you're frequently on-call to perform administrative tasks and resolve workload disruptions to resources in your organization's Azure subscriptions. When visiting a family member during a weekend that you're on call, the development team notifies you of a problem with an Azure virtual machine (VM). The VM became nonresponsive during scheduled maintenance for the upgrade of an application that runs on it. Because the developers weren't granted access to the underlying Azure virtual machine hosting infrastructure, they're only able to remotely access the VM when it's operating normally. So, you're being called to diagnose and remediate the problem.</p>
<p>Since you're visiting family, you don’t have access to your administrative workstation and diagnostic scripts. You do have access to a laptop with an internet browser. Using the laptop, you browse to the Azure portal, authenticate against your organization’s Azure subscription, open Azure Cloud Shell, mount an Azure File Share, access your diagnostic scripts, and diagnose and remediate the problems with the VM, returning it to operation.</p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p><strong>Access Cloud Shell</strong></p></li>
</ol>
<p>You have a few different options for accessing Azure Cloud Shell:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>From a direct link: <a href="https://shell.azure.com/">https://shell.azure.com</a></p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image18.webp" style="width:6.91138in;height:4.16119in" alt="A screenshot of Cloud Shell accessed directly from a link." /></p>
<ul>
<li><p>From the Azure portal</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image19.webp" style="width:4.3125in;height:0.52083in" alt="A screenshot of Cloud Shell accessed from Azure portal." /></p>
<ul>
<li><p>From code snippets when accessing Microsoft Learn:</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image20.webp" style="width:6.99347in;height:1.98433in" alt="A screenshot of Cloud Shell accessed from code snippets." /></p>
<p>When you open a Cloud Shell session, a temporary host is allocated to your session. This VM is preconfigured with the latest versions of PowerShell and Bash. You can then select the command-line experience you want to use:</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image21.webp" style="width:4.38542in;height:1.4375in" alt="A screenshot of how to choose a command-line experience in a Cloud Shell session." /></p>
<p>After you select the shell experience you want to use, you can start managing your Azure resources:</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image22.webp" style="width:7.13526in;height:3.84092in" alt="A screenshot of how to use Cloud Shell to manage Azure resources." /></p>
<p>Cloud Shell sessions terminate after 20 minutes of inactivity. When a session terminates, files on your CloudDrive are persisted, but you need to start a new session to access the Cloud Shell environment.</p>
<p><strong>Access your own scripts and files</strong></p>
<p>When using Cloud Shell, you might also need to run scripts or use files for different actions. You can persist files on Cloud Shell by using the Azure CloudDrive:</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image23.webp" style="width:6.54167in;height:2.86458in" alt="A screenshot of how to access CloudDrive in a Cloud Shell session." /></p>
<p>After uploading files, you can interact with them as you would in a regular PowerShell or Bash session:</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image24.webp" style="width:7.09795in;height:1.98063in" alt="A screenshot of how to manage files in CloudDrive." /></p>
<p>Now that your file resides on CloudDrive, you can close the session and open another session on a different device and still access the same file. Cloud Shell also lets you map an Azure Storage File Share, which is tied to a specific region. Access to an Azure File Share lets you work with the contents of that share through Cloud Shell.</p>
<p>If you need to edit scripts hosted on the CloudDrive or File Share, you can use the Cloud Shell editor. Select the curly brackets {} icon on the browser and open the file you want to edit, or use the command code and specify the filename; for example:</p>
<p>BashCopy</p>
<p>code temp.txt</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image25.webp" style="width:6.69496in;height:3.61359in" alt="A screenshot of how to access the Cloud Shell editor mode." /></p>
<p><strong> Note</strong></p>
<p>The code command only works in the Classic mode in Cloud Shell. To enable Classic mode, select the <strong>More</strong> icon (<strong>...</strong>), then select <strong>Settings</strong> &gt; <strong>Go to Classic version</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>Cloud Shell tools</strong></p>
<p>If you need to manage resources (such as Docker containers or Kubernetes Clusters) or want to use non-Microsoft tools (such as Ansible and Terraform) in Cloud Shell, the Cloud Shell session comes with these add-ons already preconfigured.</p>
<p>Here’s a list of all add-ons available to you within a Cloud Shell session:</p>
<table style="width:62%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 9%" />
<col style="width: 53%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Category</strong></th>
<th><strong>Name</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Linux tools</strong></td>
<td>bash<br />
zsh<br />
sh<br />
tmux<br />
dig</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Azure tools</strong></td>
<td><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cli/azure/">Azure CLI</a><br />
AzCopy<br />
Azure Functions CLI<br />
Service Fabric CLI<br />
Batch Shipyard<br />
blobxfer</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Text editors</strong></td>
<td>code (Cloud Shell editor)<br />
vim<br />
nano<br />
emacs</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Source control</strong></td>
<td>git</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Build tools</strong></td>
<td>make<br />
maven<br />
npm<br />
pip</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Containers</strong></td>
<td>Docker Machine<br />
Kubectl<br />
Helm<br />
DC/OS CLI</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Databases</strong></td>
<td>MySQL client<br />
PostgreSql client<br />
sqlcmd Utility<br />
mssql-scripter</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Other</strong></td>
<td>iPython Client<br />
Cloud Foundry CLI<br />
Terraform<br />
Ansible<br />
Chef InSpec<br />
Puppet Bolt<br />
HashiCorp Packer<br />
Office 365 CLI</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### When to use it

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>As an IT Admin for Contoso Corporation, you need alternatives to interact with Azure resources from the command line even when not using your default administrative device.</p>
<p>You can use Azure Cloud Shell to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Open a secure command-line session from any browser-based device.</p></li>
<li><p>Interact with Azure resources without the need to install plug-ins or add-ons to your device.</p></li>
<li><p>Persist files between sessions for later use.</p></li>
<li><p>Use either Bash or PowerShell, whichever you prefer, to manage Azure resources.</p></li>
<li><p>Edit files (such as scripts) via the Cloud Shell editor.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>You shouldn't use Azure Cloud Shell if:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>You intend to leave a session open for more than 20 minutes for long running scripts or activities. In these cases, your session is disconnected without warning, and the current state is lost.</p></li>
<li><p>You need admin permissions, such as sudo access, from within the Azure CLI or PowerShell environment.</p></li>
<li><p>You need to install tools that aren't supported in the limited Cloud Shell environment, but instead require an environment such as a custom virtual machine or container.</p></li>
<li><p>You need storage from different regions. You might need to back up and synchronize this content since only one region can have the storage allocated to Azure Cloud Shell.</p></li>
<li><p>You need to open multiple sessions at the same time. Azure Cloud Shell allows only one instance at time and isn't suitable for concurrent work across multiple subscriptions or tenants.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Check your knowledge

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Check your knowledge</strong></p>
<p>Top of Form</p>
<p><strong>1. Which of these methods can you use to access Azure Cloud Shell from a computer running Windows 11?</strong></p>
<p>Install Windows Subsystem for Linux and make an SSH connection to Azure.</p>
<p>Make a Remote Desktop Protocol connection to Azure.</p>
<p><mark>Use the Microsoft Edge Browser to log into an Azure Subscription.</mark></p>
<p><strong>2. You want to store a script that you constantly use for operations on Azure resources. This script needs to be rapidly available when you open a new Azure Cloud Shell session. Which of these procedures should you use?</strong></p>
<p><mark>Upload the script to your CloudDrive on an Azure Cloud Shell session.</mark></p>
<p>Create a new script and store it on an Azure Storage Blob.</p>
<p>Use Cloud Shell editor to create and edit the script you want to use.</p>
<p><strong>3. You have a script stored on the Cloud Shell storage. You constantly use this script for resource management, but you need to perform small changes to it. Which of these solutions is the best way to handle the situation?</strong></p>
<p>Download the script from the Azure Cloud Shell session, edit the script, and upload it back before using it.</p>
<p>Push the script to GitHub and run it from the Azure Cloud Shell session.</p>
<p><mark>Use the Cloud Shell editor to make the necessary changes and save it directly on the CloudDrive.</mark></p>
<p>Bottom of Form</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

## Bash

|     |
|-----|

### Intro

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>Imagine you just started a new job as a system administrator (sysadmin) at Northwind, a high-frequency trading (HFT) firm that runs Windows on its client computers and Linux on its server computers. Computers are the lifeblood of the company, and you need to learn more about how to manage Linux boxes. It's time to skill up!</p>
<p>Bash is the standard shell scripting language for Linux. Let's learn the basics of Bash, starting with the syntax and commonly used commands, like ls and cat.</p>
<p><strong>Learning objectives</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>In this module, you will:</p></li>
<li><p>Learn what shells are and what Bash is</p></li>
<li><p>Learn about the syntax of Bash commands</p></li>
<li><p>Learn about important Bash commands, such as ls, cat, and ps</p></li>
<li><p>Learn how to use I/O operators to redirect input and output</p></li>
<li><p>Learn how to update a server's operating system</p></li>
<li><p>Learn how to find and terminate rogue processes</p></li>
<li><p>Learn how to use Bash to filter Azure CLI output</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### What is Bash

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>Bash is a vital tool for managing Linux machines. The name is short for "<strong>B</strong>ourne <strong>A</strong>gain <strong>Sh</strong>ell."</p>
<p>A shell is a program that commands the operating system to perform actions. You can enter commands in a console on your computer and run the commands directly, or you can use scripts to run batches of commands. Shells like PowerShell and Bash give system administrators the power and precision they need for fine-tuned control of the computers they're responsible for.</p>
<p>There are other Linux shells, including csh and zsh, but Bash became the de facto Linux standard. That's because Bash is compatible with Unix's first serious shell, the Bourne shell, also known as sh. Bash incorporates the best features of its predecessors. But Bash also has some fine features of its own, including built-in commands and the ability to invoke external programs.</p>
<p>One reason for Bash's success is its simplicity. Bash, like the rest of Linux, is based on the Unix design philosophy. As Peter Salus summarized in his book <em>A Quarter Century of Unix</em>, three of the "big ideas" embodied in Unix are:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Programs do one thing and do it well</p></li>
<li><p>Programs work together</p></li>
<li><p>Programs use text streams as the universal interface</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The last part is key to understanding how Bash works. In Unix and Linux, everything is a file. That means you can use the same commands without worrying about whether the I/O stream—the input and output—comes from a keyboard, a disk file, a socket, a pipe, or another I/O abstraction.</p>
<p>Let's learn the basics of Bash, starting with the syntax and commonly used commands, like ls and cat.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Fundamental 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Bash fundamentals</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>8 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>An understanding of Bash starts with an understanding of Bash syntax. After you know the syntax, you can apply it to every Bash command you run.</p>
<p>The full syntax for a Bash command is:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>command [options] [arguments]</p>
<p>Bash treats the first string it encounters as a command. The following command uses Bash's ls (for "list") command to display the contents of the current working directory:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls</p>
<p>Arguments often accompany Bash commands. For example, you can include a path name in an ls command to list the contents of another directory:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls /etc</p>
<p>Most Bash commands have options for modifying how they work. Options, also called <em>flags</em>, give a command more specific instructions. As an example, files and directories whose names begin with a period are hidden from the user and aren't displayed by ls. However, you can include the -a (for "all") flag in an ls command and see everything in the target directory:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls -a /etc</p>
<p>You can even combine flags for brevity. For example, rather than enter ls -a -l /etc to show all files and directories in Linux's <strong>/etc</strong> directory in long form, you can enter this instead:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls -al /etc</p>
<p>Bash is concise. It's sometimes remarkable (and a point of pride among Bash aficionados) how much you can accomplish with a single command.</p>
<p><strong>Get help</strong></p>
<p>Which options and arguments can be used, or must be used, varies from command to command. Fortunately, Bash documentation is built into the operating system. Help is never more than a command away. To learn about the options for a command, use the man (for "manual") command. For instance, to see all the options for the mkdir ("make directory") command, do this:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>man mkdir</p>
<p>man is your best friend as you learn Bash. man is how you find the information you need to understand how any command works.</p>
<p>Most Bash and Linux commands support the --help option. This shows a description of the command's syntax and options. To demonstrate, enter mkdir --help. The output looks something like this:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p>Usage: mkdir [OPTION]... DIRECTORY...</p>
<p>Create the DIRECTORY(ies), if they do not already exist.</p>
<p>Mandatory arguments to long options are mandatory for short options too.</p>
<p>-m, --mode=MODE set file mode (as in chmod), not a=rwx - umask</p>
<p>-p, --parents no error if existing, make parent directories as needed</p>
<p>-v, --verbose print a message for each created directory</p>
<p>-Z set SELinux security context of each created directory</p>
<p>to the default type</p>
<p>--context[=CTX] like -Z, or if CTX is specified then set the SELinux</p>
<p>or SMACK security context to CTX</p>
<p>--help display this help and exit</p>
<p>--version output version information and exit</p>
<p>GNU coreutils online help: &lt;http://www.gnu.org/software/coreutils/&gt;</p>
<p>Report mkdir translation bugs to &lt;http://translationproject.org/team/&gt;</p>
<p>Full documentation at: &lt;http://www.gnu.org/software/coreutils/mkdir&gt;</p>
<p>or available locally via: info '(coreutils) mkdir invocation'</p>
<p>Help obtained this way is typically more concise than help obtained with man.</p>
<p><strong>Use wildcards</strong></p>
<p>Wildcards are symbols that represent one or more characters in Bash commands. The most frequently used wildcard is the asterisk. It represents zero characters or a sequence of characters. Suppose your current directory contains hundreds of image files, but you only want to see the PNG files; the ones whose file names end with <strong>.png</strong>. Here's the command to list only those files:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls *.png</p>
<p>Note</p>
<p>Linux has no formal concept of a file-name extension as other operating systems do. This doesn't mean that PNG files won't have a <strong>.png</strong> extension. It simply means Linux attaches no special significance to the fact that the file names end with <strong>.png</strong>.</p>
<p>Now let's say the current directory also contains JPEG files. Some end in <strong>.jpg</strong>, while others end in <strong>.jpeg.</strong> Here's one way to list all the JPEG files:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls *.jpg *.jpeg</p>
<p>And here's another:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls *.jp*g</p>
<p>The * wildcard matches on zero or more characters, but the ? wildcard represents a single character. If the current directory contains files named <strong>0001.jpg</strong>, <strong>0002.jpg</strong>, and so on through <strong>0009.jpg</strong>, the following command lists them all:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls 000?.jpg</p>
<p>Yet another way to use wildcards to filter output is to use square brackets, which denote groups of characters. The following command lists all the files in the current directory whose names contain a period immediately followed a lowercase J or P. It lists all the <strong>.jpg</strong>, <strong>.jpeg</strong>, and <strong>.png</strong> files, but not <strong>.gif</strong> files:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls *.[jp]*</p>
<p>In Linux, file names and the commands that operate upon them are case-sensitive. So to list all the files in the current directory whose names contain periods followed by an uppercase <em>or</em> lowercase J or P, you could enter this:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls *.[jpJP]*</p>
<p>Expressions in square brackets can represent ranges of characters. For example, the following command lists all the files in the current directory whose names begin with a lowercase letter:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls [a-z]*</p>
<p>This command, by contrast, lists all the files in the current directory whose names begin with an uppercase letter:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls [A-Z]*</p>
<p>And this one lists all the files in the current directory whose names begin with a lowercase <em>or</em> uppercase letter:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls [a-zA-Z]*</p>
<p>Based on all this, can you guess what the following commands do?</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls [0-9]*</p>
<p>ls *[0-9]*</p>
<p>ls *[0-9]</p>
<p>If you need to use one of the wildcard characters as an ordinary character, you make it literal or "escape it" by prefacing it with a backslash. So, if for some reason you had an asterisk as part of a file name—something you should never do intentionally—you could search for it by using a command such as:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>$ ls *\**</p>
<hr /></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Commands and operators

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Bash commands and operators</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>10 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Every shell language has its most-used commands. Let's start building your Bash repertoire by examining the most commonly used commands.</p>
<p><strong>Bash commands</strong></p>
<p>Let's look at common Bash commands and how to use them.</p>
<p><strong>ls command</strong></p>
<p>ls lists the contents of your current directory or the directory specified in an argument to the command. By itself, it lists the files and directories in the current directory:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls</p>
<p>Files and directories whose names begin with a period are hidden by default. To include these items in a directory listing, use an -a flag:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls -a</p>
<p>To get even more information about the files and directories in the current directory, use an -l flag:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls -l</p>
<p>Here's some sample output from a directory that contains a handful of JPEGs and PNGs and a subdirectory named <strong>gifs</strong>:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p>-rw-rw-r-- 1 azureuser azureuser 473774 Jun 13 15:38 0001.png</p>
<p>-rw-rw-r-- 1 azureuser azureuser 1557965 Jun 13 14:43 0002.jpg</p>
<p>-rw-rw-r-- 1 azureuser azureuser 473774 Mar 26 09:21 0003.png</p>
<p>-rw-rw-r-- 1 azureuser azureuser 4193680 Jun 13 09:40 0004.jpg</p>
<p>-rw-rw-r-- 1 azureuser azureuser 423325 Jun 10 12:53 0005.jpg</p>
<p>-rw-rw-r-- 1 azureuser azureuser 2278001 Jun 12 04:21 0006.jpg</p>
<p>-rw-rw-r-- 1 azureuser azureuser 1220517 Jun 13 14:44 0007.jpg</p>
<p>drwxrwxr-x 2 azureuser azureuser 4096 Jun 13 20:16 gifs</p>
<p>Each line provides detailed information about the corresponding file or directory. That information includes the permissions assigned to it, its owner, its size in bytes, the last time it was modified, and the file or directory name.</p>
<p><strong>cat command</strong></p>
<p>Suppose you want to see what's inside a file. You can use the cat command for that. The output won't make much sense unless the file is a text file. The following command shows the contents of the <strong>os-release</strong> file stored in the <strong>/etc</strong> directory:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cat /etc/os-release</p>
<p>This is a useful command because it tells you which Linux distribution you're running:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p>NAME="Ubuntu"</p>
<p>VERSION="18.04.2 LTS (Bionic Beaver)"</p>
<p>ID=ubuntu</p>
<p>ID_LIKE=debian</p>
<p>PRETTY_NAME="Ubuntu 18.04.2 LTS"</p>
<p>VERSION_ID="18.04"</p>
<p>HOME_URL="https://www.ubuntu.com/"</p>
<p>SUPPORT_URL="https://help.ubuntu.com/"</p>
<p>BUG_REPORT_URL="https://bugs.launchpad.net/ubuntu/"</p>
<p>PRIVACY_POLICY_URL="https://www.ubuntu.com/legal/terms-and-policies/privacy-policy"</p>
<p>VERSION_CODENAME=bionic</p>
<p>UBUNTU_CODENAME=bionic</p>
<p>The <strong>/etc</strong> directory is a special one in Linux. It contains system-configuration files. You don't want to delete any files from this directory unless you know what you're doing.</p>
<p><strong>sudo command</strong></p>
<p>Some Bash commands can only be run by the root user; a system administrator or superuser. If you try one of these commands without sufficient privileges, it fails. For example, only users logged in as a superuser can use cat to display the contents of <strong>/etc/at.deny</strong>:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cat /etc/at.deny</p>
<p><strong>at.deny</strong> is a special file that determines who can use other Bash commands to submit jobs for later execution.</p>
<p>You don't want to run as root most of the time; it's too dangerous. To run commands that require admin privilege without logging in as a superuser, you'll preface the commands with sudo:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>sudo cat /etc/at.deny</p>
<p>sudo stands for "superuser do." When you use it, you're telling the shell that for this one command, you're acting with the root-user level of permission.</p>
<p><strong>cd, mkdir, and rmdir commands</strong></p>
<p>cd stands for "change directory," and it does exactly what the name suggests: it changes the current directory to another directory. It enables you to move from one directory to another just like its counterpart in Windows. The following command changes to a subdirectory of the current directory named <strong>orders</strong>:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cd orders</p>
<p>You can move up a directory by specifying .. as the directory name:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cd ..</p>
<p>This command changes to your home directory; the one you land in when you first sign in:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cd ~</p>
<p>You can create directories by using the mkdir command. The following command creates a subdirectory named <strong>orders</strong> in the current working directory:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>mkdir orders</p>
<p>If you want to create a subdirectory and another subdirectory under it with one command, use the --parents flag:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>mkdir --parents orders/2019</p>
<p>The rmdir command deletes (removes) a directory, but only if it's empty. If it's not empty, you get a warning instead. Fortunately, you can use the rm command to delete directories that aren't empty in combination with the -r (recursive) flag. The command would then look like so, rm -r.</p>
<p><strong>rm command</strong></p>
<p>The rm command is short for "remove." As you'd expect, rm deletes files. So this command puts an end to <strong>0001.jpg</strong>:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>rm 0001.jpg</p>
<p>And this command deletes all the files in the current directory:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>rm *</p>
<p>Be wary of rm. It's a dangerous command.</p>
<p>Running rm with a -i flag lets you think before you delete:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>rm -i *</p>
<p>Make it a habit to include -i in every rm command, and you might avoid falling victim to one of Linux's biggest blunders. The dreaded rm -rf / command deletes every file on an entire drive. It works by recursively deleting all the subdirectories of root and their subdirectories. The -f (for "force") flag compounds the problem by suppressing prompts. <em>Don't do this.</em></p>
<p>If you want to delete a subdirectory named <strong>orders</strong> that isn't empty, you can use the rm command this way:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>rm -r orders</p>
<p>This deletes the <strong>orders</strong> subdirectory and everything in it, including other subdirectories.</p>
<p><strong>cp command</strong></p>
<p>The cp command copies not just files, but entire directories (and subdirectories) if you want. To make a copy of <strong>0001.jpg</strong> named <strong>0002.jpg</strong>, use this command:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cp 0001.jpg 0002.jpg</p>
<p>If <strong>0002.jpg</strong> already exists, Bash silently replaces it. That's great if it's what you intended, but not so wonderful if you didn't realize you were about to overwrite the old version.</p>
<p>Fortunately, if you use the -i (for "interactive") flag, Bash warns you before deleting existing files. This is safer:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cp -i 0001.jpg 0002.jpg</p>
<p>You can use wildcards to copy several files at once. To copy all the files in the current directory to a subdirectory named <strong>photos</strong>, do this:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cp * photos</p>
<p>To copy all the files in a subdirectory named <strong>photos</strong> into a subdirectory named <strong>images</strong>, do this:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cp photos/* images</p>
<p>This assumes that the <strong>images</strong> directory already exists. If it doesn't, you can create it <em>and</em> copy the contents of the <strong>photos</strong> directory by using this command:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cp -r photos images</p>
<p>The -r stands for "recursive." An added benefit of the -r flag is that if <strong>photos</strong> contains subdirectories of its own, they too are copied to the <strong>images</strong> directory.</p>
<p><strong>ps command</strong></p>
<p>The ps command gives you a snapshot of all the currently running processes. By itself, with no arguments, it shows all your shell processes; in other words, not much. But it's a different story when you include a -e flag:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ps -e</p>
<p>-e lists <em>all</em> running processes, and there are typically many of them.</p>
<p>For a more comprehensive look at what processes are running in the system, use the -ef flag:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ps -ef</p>
<p>This flag shows the names of all the running processes, their process identification numbers (PIDs), the PIDs of their parents (PPIDs), and when they began (STIME). It also shows what terminal, if any, they're attached to (TTY), how much CPU time they've racked up (TIME), and their full path names. Here's an abbreviated example:</p>
<p>UID PID PPID C STIME TTY TIME CMD</p>
<p>root 1 0 0 13:35 ? 00:00:03 /sbin/init</p>
<p>root 2 0 0 13:35 ? 00:00:00 [kthreadd]</p>
<p>root 3 2 0 13:35 ? 00:00:00 [rcu_gp]</p>
<p>root 4 2 0 13:35 ? 00:00:00 [rcu_par_gp]</p>
<p>root 5 2 0 13:35 ? 00:00:00 [kworker/0:0-cgr]</p>
<p>root 6 2 0 13:35 ? 00:00:00 [kworker/0:0H-kb]</p>
<p>root 8 2 0 13:35 ? 00:00:00 [mm_percpu_wq]</p>
<p>root 9 2 0 13:35 ? 00:00:01 [ksoftirqd/0]</p>
<p>root 10 2 0 13:35 ? 00:00:02 [rcu_sched]</p>
<p>As an aside, you might find documentation that shows ps being used this way:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ps aux</p>
<p>ps aux and ps -ef are the same. This duality traces back to historical differences between POSIX Unix systems (of which Linux is one) and BSD Unix systems (the most common of which is macOS). In the beginning, POSIX used -ef while the BSD required aux. Today, both operating-system families accept either format.</p>
<p>This serves as an excellent reminder of why you should look closely at the manual for all Linux commands. Learning Bash is like learning English as a second language. There are many exceptions to the rules.</p>
<p><strong>w command</strong></p>
<p>Users come, users go, and sometimes you get users you don't want at all. When an employee leaves to pursue other opportunities, the sysadmin is called upon to ensure that the worker can no longer sign in to the company's computer systems. Sysadmins are also expected to know who's logged in, and who shouldn't be.</p>
<p>To find out who's on your servers, Linux provides the w (for "who") command. It displays information about the users currently on the computer system and those users' activities. w shows user names, their IP addresses, when they logged in, what processes they're currently running, and how much time those processes are consuming. It's a valuable tool for sysadmins.</p>
<p><strong>Bash I/O operators</strong></p>
<p>You can do a lot in Linux just by exercising Bash commands and their many options. But you can really get work done when you combine commands by using I/O operators:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>&lt; for redirecting input to a source other than the keyboard</p></li>
<li><p>&gt; for redirecting output to destination other than the screen</p></li>
<li><p>&gt;&gt; for doing the same, but appending rather than overwriting</p></li>
<li><p>| for piping output from one command to the input of another</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Suppose you want to list everything in the current directory but capture the output in a file named <strong>listing.txt</strong>. The following command does just that:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls &gt; listing.txt</p>
<p>If <strong>listing.txt</strong> already exists, it gets overwritten. If you use the &gt;&gt; operator instead, the output from ls is appended to what's already in <strong>listing.txt</strong>:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls &gt;&gt; listing.txt</p>
<p>The piping operator is powerful (and often used). It redirects the output of the first command to the input of the second command. Let's say you use cat to display the contents of a large file, but the content scrolls by too quickly for you to read. You can make the output more manageable by piping the results to another command such as more. The following command lists all the currently running processes. But once the screen is full, the output pauses until you select <strong>Enter</strong> to show the next line:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ps -ef | more</p>
<p>You can also pipe output to head to see just the first several lines:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ps -ef | head</p>
<p>Or suppose you want to filter the output to include only the lines that contain the word "daemon." One way to do that is by piping the output from ps to Linux's useful grep tool:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ps -ef | grep daemon</p>
<p>The output might look like this:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p>azureus+ 52463 50702 0 23:28 pts/0 00:00:00 grep --color=auto deamon</p>
<p>azureuser@bash-vm:~$ ps -ef | grep daemon</p>
<p>root 449 1 0 13:35 ? 00:00:17 /usr/lib/linux-tools/4.18.0-1018-azure/hv_kvp_daemon -n</p>
<p>root 988 1 0 13:35 ? 00:00:00 /usr/lib/accountsservice/accounts-daemon</p>
<p>message+ 1002 1 0 13:35 ? 00:00:00 /usr/bin/dbus-daemon --system --address=systemd: --nofork --nopidfile --systemd-activation --syslog-only</p>
<p>daemon 1035 1 0 13:35 ? 00:00:00 /usr/sbin/atd -f</p>
<p>root 1037 1 0 13:35 ? 00:00:00 /usr/bin/python3 -u /usr/sbin/waagent -daemon</p>
<p>root 1039 1 0 13:35 ? 00:00:00 /usr/lib/linux-tools/4.18.0-1018-azure/hv_vss_daemon -n</p>
<p>azureus+ 52477 50702 0 23:28 pts/0 00:00:00 grep --color=auto daemon</p>
<p>You can also use files as input. By default, standard input comes from the keyboard, but it too can be redirected. To get input from a file instead of the keyboard, use the &lt; operator. One common sysadmin task is to sort the contents of a file. As the name suggests, sort sorts text in alphabetical order:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>sort &lt; file.txt</p>
<p>To save the sorted results to a new file, you can redirect input <em>and</em> output:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>sort &lt; file.txt &gt; sorted_file.txt</p>
<p>You can use I/O operators to chain Linux commands as needed. Consider the following command:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cat file.txt | fmt | pr | lpr</p>
<p>The output from cat goes to fmt, the output from fmt goes to pr, and so on. fmt formats the results into a tidy paragraph. pr paginates the results. And lpr sends the paginated output to the printer. All in a single line!</p>
<p>On your own Linux computer, you can run Bash commands locally. If you have access to Linux servers, you can remote in to them and run Bash commands there. But nobody wants to experiment on a live production system, particularly on their first day at Northwind.</p>
<p>In this unit, you use Azure Cloud Shell on the right as your Linux terminal. Azure Cloud Shell is a shell you can access through the Azure portal or at <a href="https://shell.azure.com">https://shell.azure.com</a>. You don't have to install anything on your PC or laptop to use it.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Sandbox Exercise : Familiarize yourself with Cloud Shell

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Familiarize yourself with Cloud Shell</strong></p>
<p>First, let's explore what's in Cloud Shell by using the Bash commands we've learned.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Use the ls command to list all files and subdirectories in the current directory:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> ls</p>
<p> You should see output that looks similar to this:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> yourname@Azure:~$ ls</p>
<p>clouddrive</p>
<p><strong>clouddrive</strong> is a subdirectory of your current directory. It's a mounted file share that persists if you're using Cloud Shell on your own account. Right now, you're using it on the Microsoft Learn sandbox.</p>
<p> But wait, what <em>is</em> the current directory? Let's use the pwd command to find out. pwd stands for "print working directory." It prints the long-form path to what directory you're in now.</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> pwd</p>
<p> You should see an output like this:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> yourname@Azure:~$ pwd</p>
<p>/home/yourname</p>
<p>This output means that you're in a directory called <strong>yourname</strong> within a directory called <strong>home</strong>, at the root of the Linux file system.</p>
<p> There doesn't appear to be much in our current directory. Let's use a Bash <em>flag</em> to print all hidden files and directories to double check that's correct.</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> ls -a</p>
<p> That output showed us a lot more stuff in this directory than we initially thought.</p>
<p>Output</p>
<ol start="6" type="1">
<li><p>yourname@Azure:~$ ls -a</p></li>
<li><p>. .. .azure .bash_history .bash_logout .bashrc clouddrive .profile .tmux.conf .viminfo</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>What were all of those files and subdirectories? Some are behind-the-scenes files to make Cloud Shell work. Let's discuss a few of the others.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>. refers to your current directory, and .. refers to your parent directory. Wherever you are, if you print all hidden files and directories, you see . and .. printed.</p></li>
<li><p>.bash_history is a special Bash file where all commands that you enter into the shell are stored. Bash remembers your command history, which, as we see later, is useful.</p></li>
<li><p>.bash_logout is another special Bash file that is read and run every time a sign-in shell exists. Linux superusers can modify it to customize your environment.</p></li>
<li><p>.bashrc is an important Bash configuration file that runs whenever you start a new shell. If you decide to open this file to look at it, be careful about making changes, because they can have unintended consequences.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Recall your history and autocomplete commands</strong></p>
<p>When you're entering complicated commands like this one, it's easy to make a mistake:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ls -a .azure/commands/202?*.log</p>
<p>Fortunately, Bash offers a couple pieces of functionality to help you.</p>
<p><strong>Recalling previous commands</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Try entering this command that has a typo (203? instead of 202?):</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> ls -a .azure/commands/203?*.log</p>
<p> You should see this output letting you know that there weren't any files that matched that pattern:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> ls: cannot access '.azure/commands/203?*.log': No such file or directory</p>
<p> Rather than entering the whole thing again to correct your mistake, you can recall previously entered commands by using the <strong>Up arrow</strong> and <strong>Down arrow</strong> keys. Try using the <strong>Up arrow</strong> key to bring back your incorrect command. Then use the <strong>Left arrow key</strong> to fix it by replacing the final 3 with a 2. Select <strong>Enter</strong> again to submit the corrected command.</p>
<p>Using the <strong>Up arrow</strong> key multiple times in a row moves you back multiple commands. Use the <strong>Down arrow</strong> key to move to later commands.</p>
<p> Now you should see something like the following output. It lets you know that your command worked correctly to list files that matched the given pattern.</p>
<p>Output</p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>.azure/commands/2020-01-29.21-56-35.login.103.log</p></li>
<li><p>.azure/commands/2020-01-29.21-56-38.account_set.112.log</p></li>
<li></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Autocompletion</strong></p>
<p>Let's say you want to read the contents of one of the files that you just found. You can use the cat (short for "catenate") command to print the contents of a file to the screen.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>To use this command, you could use the full file name, such as:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> cat .azure/commands/2020-01-29.21-56-35.login.103.log</p>
<p> But that's a lot to type, and very error prone. Instead, you can use Bash's rudimentary autocompletion to do most of the work for you. Try typing:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> cat .a</p>
<p>Then select the <strong>Tab</strong> key. What happens?</p>
<p> You should see the rest of the word "azure/" appear in your command:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> cat .azure/</p>
<p>Keep typing the beginnings of words and using <strong>Tab</strong> to autocomplete. Keep in mind that if there's an ambiguity, Bash won't fill in anything. You can select <strong>Tab</strong> twice to have Bash print all the files and directories in a given path that match the letters you typed already.</p>
<p> Play around until you get to a real <strong>.log</strong> file in the command directory. Then select <strong>Enter</strong> to use the cat command to print its contents to screen. It might look something like this:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>CMD-LOG-LINE-BEGIN 103 | 2020-01-29 21:56:35,426 | INFO | az_command_data_logger | command args: login --identity</p></li>
<li><p>CMD-LOG-LINE-BEGIN 103 | 2020-01-29 21:56:37,604 | INFO | az_command_data_logger | exit code: 0</p></li>
<li><p>Keep in mind that if you've typed an incorrect letter already, Bash won't be able to correctly guess what letter you meant to type.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Use man</strong></p>
<p>We just used the cat command, but you don't know much about it yet. Practice man to bring up more information about the cat command.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Enter the following command to understand more about what cat is and how to use it:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> man cat</p>
<p>Yes, you entered "man cat" into your shell. Bash commands can be both cryptic and amusing!</p>
<p> You should see an output like this:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>CAT(1) User Commands CAT(1)</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>NAME</p></li>
<li><p>cat - concatenate files and print on the standard output</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>SYNOPSIS</p></li>
<li><p>cat [OPTION]... [FILE]...</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>DESCRIPTION</p></li>
<li><p>Concatenate FILE(s) to standard output.</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>With no FILE, or when FILE is -, read standard input.</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>-A, --show-all</p></li>
<li><p>equivalent to -vET</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>-b, --number-nonblank</p></li>
<li><p>number nonempty output lines, overrides -n</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>-e equivalent to -vE</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>...</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>Use up and down arrows to scroll through the manual page, and enter q to exit.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Change directories</strong></p>
<p>Let's practice one more basic Bash command: cd.</p>
<p>While using the shell, you're always sitting inside a directory—just like a folder on your PC or Mac. To change folders, you use the cd (change directory) command.</p>
<p>It's simple, but let's get some practice.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>First, enter this command to make sure you're in the right place:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> cd ~</p>
<p>This command moved you back to your special <strong>home</strong> directory in the shell, if you weren't already there.</p>
<p> Double check by using the pwd command one more time:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> pwd</p>
<p> You should see an output like this:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> /home/yourname</p>
<p>~ is another special character in Bash that refers to this home directory. You can use ~ to refer to the location <strong>/home/yourname</strong> no matter where you are in the shell.</p>
<p> Let's change to the directory that holds log files (where we were earlier):</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cd .azure/commands/</p>
<p>You can either enter the full command yourself, or use <strong>Tab</strong> to autocomplete.</p>
<p>Now you should see that the line where you enter commands looks different, showing you where you are in the shell:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> yourname@Azure:~/.azure/commands$</p>
<p> Try using the special .. syntax to move up one directory:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>cd ..</p>
<p>Now you should be one level up in the directory structure, and your command line should look like this:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>yourname@Azure:~/.azure$</p></li>
<li></li>
</ol>
<p>Great work! You've taken your first steps to being a Bash expert. Let's keep learning.</p>
<p><strong>Check your knowledge</strong></p>
<p>Top of Form</p>
<p>1.</p>
<p>What directory would you switch to if you entered cd . as a Bash command?</p>
<p>My special "home" directory</p>
<p>The parent directory</p>
<p>The first alphabetical subdirectory</p>
<p>I wouldn't switch directories.</p>
<p>Bottom of Form</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Terminate a Process

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Exercise - Terminate a misbehaving process</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>10 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This module requires a sandbox to complete. You have used 1 of 10 sandboxes for today. More sandboxes will be available tomorrow.</p>
<p>Computers are imperfect. Sooner or later, something <em>will</em> go wrong. That's why you have a job as a sysadmin; it's up to you to troubleshoot and fix system problems.</p>
<p>Imagine that a Python application is causing problems. Perhaps it's taking up too much CPU time, or maybe it stopped responding. In either case, you want to stop the application. To identify a process or application, you can use ps and grep. Then, to stop it, you can use the kill command. Let's practice this in your Linux virtual machine.</p>
<p><strong>Start a misbehaving process</strong></p>
<p>If you're going to kill a process, you need a process to kill. Let's create one.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Get back to your home base by typing the following command:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> cd ~</p>
<p> In Azure Cloud Shell, enter the following command to start Linux's <a href="https://wikipedia.org/wiki/Vi">vi</a> editor:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> vi bad.py</p>
<p>vi is a widely used text editor that Linux inherited from Unix. Love it or hate it, a Bash user needs to know the basics of vi.</p>
<p> Select the <strong>i</strong> key to put vi in insert mode. Then type in the following Python program:</p>
<p>Python</p>
<p> i = 0</p>
<p>while i == 0:</p>
<p>pass</p>
<p>This program, when executed, runs in an infinite loop—clearly not something you want running on your server.</p>
<p> Select the <strong>Esc</strong> key to exit insert mode. Then type the following command followed by the <strong>Enter</strong> key to save the program and exit vi:</p>
<p>vim</p>
<p> :wq</p>
<p>Be sure to include the colon at the beginning of the command. As for the remainder of the command, w stands for "write" and q stands for "quit."</p>
<p> Now use the following command to start the program and leave it running in the background:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>python3 bad.py &amp;</p></li>
<li><p>Be sure to include the ampersand (&amp;) at the end of the command. Otherwise, you won't return to the Bash prompt. In Bash, the ampersand runs a command and returns to the command line, even if the command hasn't finished running.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>It's not obvious, but <strong>bad.py</strong> is now running in the background and stealing CPU cycles from other processes. Let's take a close look at what's happening.</p>
<p><strong>Kill the process</strong></p>
<p>To kill a process, you need the process name or process ID. This is a job for ps.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>To refresh your memory, a ps -ef command lists all running processes and displays a great deal of information about each. Use the following command to list all running processes and filter the results to lines that contain "python":</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ps -ef | grep python</p>
<p>The results should look something like this:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> yourname+ 342 254 99 23:34 pts/1 00:00:31 python3 bad.py</p>
<p>yourname+ 344 254 0 23:35 pts/1 00:00:00 grep --color=auto python</p>
<p> From the listing, it appears that <strong>bad.py</strong> is consuming 99 percent of the server's CPU time. The program is living up to its name.</p>
<p>The kill command kills a running process based on its process ID. (A related command named killall kills a process based on the process name.) When you call kill, you have to decide what kind of "signal" to use to kill the process. Use the following command to display a list of signal types:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> kill -l</p>
<p> If you were killing a daemon process—one that runs in the background and provides vital services to the operating system—you might want to kill it and immediately restart it. To do that, you could use a SIGHUP signal.</p>
<p>In this example, you want to kill the process without restarting it. Therefore, you want to use the SIGKILL signal, which corresponds to the number 9. To that end, grab <strong>bad.py</strong>'s process ID from the ps -ef output (it's in the second column) and use the following command to terminate the process. Replace PROCESS_ID with the process ID.</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>kill -9 PROCESS_ID</p></li>
<li><p>The same command can also be entered as kill -s SIGKILL PROCESS_ID. Whether you use a signal's name or number is up to you.</p></li>
<li><p>Finish by running ps again to confirm that <strong>bad.py</strong> is no longer running.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Another common use for ps and kill is to identify and terminate "zombie processes," which are child processes left behind by poorly written programs.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Filter CLI Output

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Exercise - Use Bash and grep to filter CLI output</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This module requires a sandbox to complete. You have used 1 of 10 sandboxes for today. More sandboxes will be available tomorrow.</p>
<p>Until now, you've been running Bash commands on their own. Bash is powerful when combined with other tools, so let's get some practice by using Bash to filter output from the Azure CLI.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Let's say you want to see an up-to-date list of the virtual machine (VM) sizes available in the westus region of Azure. You can do that with this command:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> az vm list-sizes --location westus --output table</p>
<p> You should see a long list of VM types as an output. To narrow down this list to the VM sizes that interest you, you can use grep, Linux's universal pattern-matching program. To find the "DS" sizes, popular for use in data science, use the following command:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> az vm list-sizes --location westus --output table | grep DS</p>
<p>This pipes output from the az command to grep, which filters out lines that lack the "DS" string.</p>
<p> That's still a lot of VMs. You know that DS V2 VMs are a more recent series. Let's adjust the grep command to use a more intricate regular expression:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>az vm list-sizes --location westus --output table | grep DS.*_v2</p></li>
<li><p>This filters out lines that don't match the regular expression DS.*_v2. You might recognize some of the characters in that expression from our discussion of "wildcards" in an earlier unit. Regular expressions make great use of wildcards.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Regular expressions are a topic for another module, but come in handy for Bash scripting.</p>
<p>Using Bash with other CLI commands makes the latter easier to work with. And because a sysadmin's work never ends, any tool that reduces the workload is welcome.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Knowledge check 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Check your knowledge</strong></p>
<p><em>Top of Form</em></p>
<p><em>1. Which of the following commands writes a list of processes associated with a user named <strong>scottgu</strong> to a file?</em></p>
<p>cat | grep scottgu &gt; processes.txt</p>
<p>cat &gt; grep scottgu | processes.txt</p>
<p><mark>ps -ef | grep scottgu &gt; processes.txt</mark></p>
<p>Correct! The ps -ef command lists all running processes. The grep command searches for patterns in each file.</p>
<p><em>2. Which of the following commands, called with the -r option, would you use to delete a subdirectory that isn't empty?</em></p>
<p><mark>rm</mark></p>
<p>Correct! Use the rm -r command to delete a subdirectory and everything in it.</p>
<p>rmdir</p>
<p>destroy</p>
<p><em>3. Which of the following commands combines the contents of <strong>foo.txt</strong> and <strong>bar.txt</strong> into a new file named <strong>foobar.txt</strong>?</em></p>
<p>concat foo.txt bar.txt &gt; foobar.txt</p>
<p>cat foo.txt bar.txt | foobar.txt</p>
<p><mark>cat foo.txt bar.txt &gt; foobar.txt</mark></p>
<p>Correct! cat concatenates the two files given as arguments, and then &gt; writes that data out to a file.</p>
<p><em>4. The purpose of the sudo command is to:</em></p>
<p><mark>Run a command with elevated privilege</mark></p>
<p>Correct! Use sudo to run a command with elevated privilege.</p>
<p>Run a program and leave it running in the background</p>
<p>Prevent nonadministrative users from deleting system files</p>
<p><em>5. Which of the following statements is true about the command python3 app.py &amp;?</em></p>
<p>It runs <strong>app.py</strong> after creating a restore point in the system</p>
<p>Incorrect. Recall that &amp; lets a process run in the background.</p>
<p><mark>It runs <strong>app.py</strong> and returns immediately to the command prompt</mark></p>
<p>Correct! python3 app.py &amp; runs <strong>app.py</strong> and returns immediately to the command prompt.</p>
<p>It runs <strong>app.py</strong>, but only if it's located in the <strong>/etc</strong> directory</p>
<p>Bottom of Form</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
