</tbody>
</table>

## PowerShell

|     |
|-----|

### Basics

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>PowerShell consists of two parts: a command-line shell and a scripting language. It started out as a framework to automate administrative tasks in Windows. PowerShell has grown into a cross-platform tool that's used for many kinds of tasks.</p>
<p>A command-line shell lacks a graphical interface, where you use a mouse to interact with graphical elements. Instead, you type text commands into a computer console. Here are some of the benefits of using a console:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Interacting with a console is often faster than using a graphical interface.</p></li>
<li><p>In a console, you can run batches of commands, so it's ideal for task automation for continuous-integration pipelines.</p></li>
<li><p>You can use a console to interact with cloud resources and other resources.</p></li>
<li><p>You can store commands and scripts in a text file and use a source-control system. This capability is probably one of the biggest benefits, because your commands are repeatable and auditable. In many systems, especially government systems, everything must be traced and evaluated, or <em>audited</em>. Audits cover everything from database changes to changes done by a script.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Features</strong></p>
<p>PowerShell shares some features with traditional shells:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Built-in help system</strong>: Most shells have some kind of help system, in which you can learn more about a command. For example, you can learn what the command does and what parameters it supports. The help system in PowerShell provides information about commands and also integrates with online help articles.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Pipeline</strong>: Traditional shells use a pipeline to run many commands sequentially. The output of one command is the input for the next command. PowerShell implements this concept like traditional shells, but it differs because it operates on objects over text. You learn more about this feature later in this module.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Aliases</strong>: Aliases are alternate names that can be used to run commands. PowerShell supports the use of common aliases such as cls (clear the screen) and ls (list the files). Therefore, new users can use their knowledge of other frameworks and don't necessarily have to remember the PowerShell name for familiar commands.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>PowerShell differs from a traditional command-line shell in a few ways:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>It operates on objects over text.</strong> In a command-line shell, you have to run scripts whose output and input might differ, so you end up spending time formatting the output and extracting the data you need. By contrast, in PowerShell you use objects as input and output. That means you spend less time formatting and extracting.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>It has cmdlets.</strong> Commands in PowerShell are called cmdlets (pronounced <em>commandlets</em>). In PowerShell, cmdlets are built on a common runtime rather than separate executables as they are in many other shell environments. This characteristic provides a consistent experience in parameter parsing and pipeline behavior. Cmdlets typically take object input and return objects. The core cmdlets in PowerShell are built in .NET Core, and are open source. You can extend PowerShell by using more cmdlets, scripts, and functions from the community and other sources, or you can build your own cmdlets in .NET Core or PowerShell.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>It has many types of commands.</strong> Commands in PowerShell can be native executables, cmdlets, functions, scripts, or aliases. Every command you run belongs to one of these types. The words <em>command</em> and <em>cmdlet</em> are often used interchangeably, because a cmdlet is a type of command.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### What is Powershell 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>PowerShell consists of two parts: a command-line shell and a scripting language. It started out as a framework to automate administrative tasks in Windows. PowerShell has grown into a cross-platform tool that's used for many kinds of tasks.</p>
<p>A command-line shell lacks a graphical interface, where you use a mouse to interact with graphical elements. Instead, you type text commands into a computer console. Here are some of the benefits of using a console:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Interacting with a console is often faster than using a graphical interface.</p></li>
<li><p>In a console, you can run batches of commands, so it's ideal for task automation for continuous-integration pipelines.</p></li>
<li><p>You can use a console to interact with cloud resources and other resources.</p></li>
<li><p>You can store commands and scripts in a text file and use a source-control system. This capability is probably one of the biggest benefits, because your commands are repeatable and auditable. In many systems, especially government systems, everything must be traced and evaluated, or <em>audited</em>. Audits cover everything from database changes to changes done by a script.</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Features</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>PowerShell shares some features with traditional shells:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Built-in help system</strong>: Most shells have some kind of help system, in which you can learn more about a command. For example, you can learn what the command does and what parameters it supports. The help system in PowerShell provides information about commands and also integrates with online help articles.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Pipeline</strong>: Traditional shells use a pipeline to run many commands sequentially. The output of one command is the input for the next command. PowerShell implements this concept like traditional shells, but it differs because it operates on objects over text. You learn more about this feature later in this module.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Aliases</strong>: Aliases are alternate names that can be used to run commands. PowerShell supports the use of common aliases such as cls (clear the screen) and ls (list the files). Therefore, new users can use their knowledge of other frameworks and don't necessarily have to remember the PowerShell name for familiar commands.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>PowerShell differs from a traditional command-line shell in a few ways:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>It operates on objects over text.</strong> In a command-line shell, you have to run scripts whose output and input might differ, so you end up spending time formatting the output and extracting the data you need. By contrast, in PowerShell you use objects as input and output. That means you spend less time formatting and extracting.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>It has cmdlets.</strong> Commands in PowerShell are called cmdlets (pronounced <em>commandlets</em>). In PowerShell, cmdlets are built on a common runtime rather than separate executables as they are in many other shell environments. This characteristic provides a consistent experience in parameter parsing and pipeline behavior. Cmdlets typically take object input and return objects. The core cmdlets in PowerShell are built in .NET Core, and are open source. You can extend PowerShell by using more cmdlets, scripts, and functions from the community and other sources, or you can build your own cmdlets in .NET Core or PowerShell.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>It has many types of commands.</strong> Commands in PowerShell can be native executables, cmdlets, functions, scripts, or aliases. Every command you run belongs to one of these types. The words <em>command</em> and <em>cmdlet</em> are often used interchangeably, because a cmdlet is a type of command.</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Installation</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>In this module, you practice using PowerShell on your computer. PowerShell is available across platforms. However, if you use a computer that runs Linux, macOS, or an older version of Windows, you need to install it.</p>
<p>Instructions for installing PowerShell are different for each OS. Before you continue, take a few minutes to install PowerShell or to verify your PowerShell installation. The next unit in this module shows you how to verify your installation.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Windows</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>If you're running Windows 8 or later, a version of PowerShell called <em>Windows PowerShell</em> should already be installed. This version differs slightly from the most up-to-date PowerShell release, but it works fine for learning purposes.</p>
<p>You can open Windows PowerShell from the Start menu.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Other operating systems</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>If your computer runs something other than Windows 8 or later, you need to install PowerShell. To find the installation instructions for your OS, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/powershell/scripting/install/installing-powershell">Install various versions of PowerShell</a>.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>PowerShell extension for Visual Studio Code</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>We recommend that you use the <a href="https://marketplace.visualstudio.com/items?itemName=ms-vscode.PowerShell">PowerShell extension for Visual Studio Code</a> to author your PowerShell scripts and to run the commands in this module. This extension lets you run commands, and also helps you with snippets, code completion, and syntax highlighting.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Exercise 1

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>$PSVersionTable</p>
<p>Your output resembles the following table:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> Name Value</p>
<p>---- -----</p>
<p>PSVersion 7.3.6</p>
<p>PSEdition Core</p>
<p>GitCommitId 7.3.6</p>
<p>OS Linux 5.4.0-1058-azure #60~18.04.1-Ubuntu SMP Tue Aug 31 20:34:4…</p>
<p>Platform Unix</p>
<p>PSCompatibleVersions {1.0, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0…}</p>
<p>PSRemotingProtocolVersion 2.3</p>
<p>SerializationVersion 1.1.0.1</p>
<p>WSManStackVersion 3.0</p>
<p>The output provides information about your PowerShell version, and your platform and edition.</p>
<p>For information limited to your version of PowerShell, you can run a modified version of $PSVersionTable.</p>
<p> Run the following command in Cloud Shell, and then press Enter.</p>
<p>PowerShell</p>
<p>$PSVersionTable.PSVersion</p>
<p>Your output now resembles the following table:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>Major Minor Patch PreReleaseLabel BuildLabel</p></li>
<li><p>----- ----- ----- --------------- ----------</p></li>
<li><p>7 3 6</p></li>
<li><p>This output gives you more details about your version of PowerShell.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Running $PSVersionTable results in output that looks like a table, but is actually an object. For this reason, you can use a period (.) to access a specific property, such as PSVersion.</p>
<p><strong>Locate commands</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>A <em>cmdlet</em> (pronounced "command-let") is a compiled command. A cmdlet can be developed in .NET or .NET Core and invoked as a command within PowerShell. Thousands of cmdlets are available in your PowerShell installation. The challenge lies in discovering what the cmdlets are and what they can do for you.</p>
<p>Cmdlets are named according to a verb-noun naming standard. This pattern can help you to understand what they do and how to search for them. It also helps cmdlet developers create consistent names. You can see the list of approved verbs by using the Get-Verb cmdlet. Verbs are organized according to activity type and function.</p>
<p>Here's a part of the output from running Get-Verb:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p>Verb AliasPrefix Group Description</p>
<p>---- ----------- ----- -----------</p>
<p>Add a Common Adds a resource to a container, or atta…</p>
<p>Clear cl Common Removes all the resources from a contai…</p>
<p>This listing shows the verb and its description. Cmdlet developers should use an approved verb, and also ensure that the verb description fits their cmdlet's function.</p>
<p>Three core cmdlets allow you to delve into what cmdlets exist and what they do:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Get-Command</strong>: The Get-Command cmdlet lists all of the available cmdlets on your system. Filter the list to quickly find the command you need.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Get-Help</strong>: Run the Get-Help core cmdlet to invoke a built-in help system. You can also run an alias help command to invoke Get-Help but improve the reading experience by paginating the response.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Get-Member</strong>: When you call a command, the response is an object that contains many properties. Run the Get-Member core cmdlet to drill down into that response and learn more about it.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Locate commands by using Get-Command</strong></p>
<p>When you run the Get-Command cmdlet in Cloud Shell, you get a list of every command that's installed in PowerShell. Because thousands of commands are installed, you need a way to filter the response so you can quickly locate the command that you need.</p>
<p>To filter the list, keep in mind the verb-noun naming standard for cmdlets. For example, in the Get-Random command, Get is the verb and Random is the noun. Use flags to target either the verb or the noun in the command you want. The flag you specify expects a value that's a string. You can add pattern-matching characters to that string to ensure you express that, for example, a flag's value should start or end with a certain string.</p>
<p>These examples show how to use flags to filter a command list:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>-Noun</strong>: The -Noun flag targets the part of the command name that's related to the noun. Here's a typical search for a command name using <em>alias</em> as the noun for which we're searching:</p></li>
</ul>
<p>PowerShell</p>
<p> Get-Command -Noun alias*</p>
<p>This command searches for all cmdlets whose noun part starts with alias.</p>
<p> <strong>-Verb</strong>: The -Verb flag targets the part of the command name that's related to the verb. You can combine the -Noun flag and the -Verb flag to create an even more detailed search query and type. Here's an example:</p>
<p>PowerShell</p>
<p>Get-Command -Verb Get -Noun alias*</p>
<p>Now you've narrowed the search to specify that the verb part needs to match Get, and the noun part needs to match alias.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Locate a command

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>A <em>cmdlet</em> (pronounced "command-let") is a compiled command. A cmdlet can be developed in .NET or .NET Core and invoked as a command within PowerShell. Thousands of cmdlets are available in your PowerShell installation. The challenge lies in discovering what the cmdlets are and what they can do for you.</p>
<p>Cmdlets are named according to a verb-noun naming standard. This pattern can help you to understand what they do and how to search for them. It also helps cmdlet developers create consistent names. You can see the list of approved verbs by using the Get-Verb cmdlet. Verbs are organized according to activity type and function.</p>
<p>Here's a part of the output from running Get-Verb:</p>
<p>OutputCopy</p>
<p>Verb AliasPrefix Group Description</p>
<p>---- ----------- ----- -----------</p>
<p>Add a Common Adds a resource to a container, or atta…</p>
<p>Clear cl Common Removes all the resources from a contai…</p>
<p>This listing shows the verb and its description. Cmdlet developers should use an approved verb, and also ensure that the verb description fits their cmdlet's function.</p>
<p>Three core cmdlets allow you to delve into what cmdlets exist and what they do:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Get-Command</strong>: The Get-Command cmdlet lists all of the available cmdlets on your system. Filter the list to quickly find the command you need.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Get-Help</strong>: Run the Get-Help core cmdlet to invoke a built-in help system. You can also run an alias help command to invoke Get-Help but improve the reading experience by paginating the response.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Get-Member</strong>: When you call a command, the response is an object that contains many properties. Run the Get-Member core cmdlet to drill down into that response and learn more about it.</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Locate commands by using Get-Command</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>When you run the Get-Command cmdlet in Cloud Shell, you get a list of every command that's installed in PowerShell. Because thousands of commands are installed, you need a way to filter the response so you can quickly locate the command that you need.</p>
<p>To filter the list, keep in mind the verb-noun naming standard for cmdlets. For example, in the Get-Random command, Get is the verb and Random is the noun. Use flags to target either the verb or the noun in the command you want. The flag you specify expects a value that's a string. You can add pattern-matching characters to that string to ensure you express that, for example, a flag's value should start or end with a certain string.</p>
<p>These examples show how to use flags to filter a command list:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>-Noun</strong>: The -Noun flag targets the part of the command name that's related to the noun. Here's a typical search for a command name using <em>alias</em> as the noun for which we're searching:</p></li>
</ul>
<p>PowerShellCopy</p>
<p>Get-Command -Noun alias*</p>
<p>This command searches for all cmdlets whose noun part starts with alias.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>-Verb</strong>: The -Verb flag targets the part of the command name that's related to the verb. You can combine the -Noun flag and the -Verb flag to create an even more detailed search query and type. Here's an example:</p></li>
</ul>
<p>PowerShellCopy</p>
<p>Get-Command -Verb Get -Noun alias*</p>
<p>Now you've narrowed the search to specify that the verb part needs to match Get, and the noun part needs to match alias.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Exercise – Locate Command

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Locate a command</strong></p>
<p>Locate commands by running the Get-Command cmdlet. This cmdlet helps you search all of the cmdlets installed on your system. Use flags to narrow down your search results to just the cmdlets that fit your scenario.</p>
<p>In this scenario, you're looking for a cmdlet that can help you work with files.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Run the command Get-Command with the flag -Noun. Specify File* to find anything related to files.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>PowerShell</p>
<p>Get-Command -Noun File*</p>
<p>The response shows something similar to the following text:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> CommandType Name Version Source</p>
<p>----------- ---- ------- ------</p>
<p>Cmdlet Get-FileHash 7.0.0.0 Microsoft.PowerShell.Utility</p>
<p>Cmdlet Out-File 7.0.0.0 Microsoft.PowerShell.Utility</p>
<p>Cmdlet Unblock-File 7.0.0.0 Microsoft.PowerShell.Utility</p>
<p>The cmdlets Get-FileHash, Out-File, and Unblock-File all match your query. Now, you have a manageable response. To further filter the response, add the -Verb parameter to your query.</p>
<p> Run Get-Command. Specify the flags -Verb and -Noun.</p>
<p>PowerShell</p>
<p>Get-Command -Verb Get -Noun File*</p>
<p>The result is similar to the following output:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>CommandType Name Version Source</p></li>
<li><p>----------- ---- ------- ------</p></li>
<li><p>Cmdlet Get-FileHash 7.0.0.0 Microsoft.PowerShell.Utility</p></li>
<li><p>This time, only one record matches your search, because you specified both the -Noun parameter and the -Verb parameter.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Because the domain you work in is file management, you specified File as the noun. If you know what you want to do within that domain, you can specify -Verb parameters. By using one or possibly two parameters, you can quickly find the cmdlet you need.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Knowledge Check 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>

</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>Choose the best response for each question.</p>
<p><em>Top of Form</em></p>
<p><em>1. What's a correct way to locate a command in PowerShell?</em></p>
<p><mark>Call Get-Command 'name of command'</mark></p>
<p>Correct. Using the Get-Command cmdlet is the correct way to locate a command.</p>

## ARM Template

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Introduction</strong></p>
<p>JSON Azure Resource Manager templates (ARM templates) allow you to specify your project's infrastructure in a declarative and reusable way. You can version and save the templates in the same source control as your development project.</p>
<p>Suppose you're managing a software team that's developing an inventory system for your partner companies. You plan to deploy this product to Azure, and let each partner company have its own solution. You plan to implement different policies for each deployment through different Azure storage accounts. You decide to use the practice of <em>infrastructure as code</em> by using ARM templates. This approach lets you track the different versions and ensure that your infrastructure deployments for each environment are consistent and flexible.</p>
<p>In this module, we introduce you to ARM template structure and let you practice creating and deploying an ARM template to Azure.</p>
<p><strong> Note</strong></p>
<p>Bicep is a language for defining your Azure resources. It has a simpler authoring experience than JSON, along with other features that help improve the quality of your infrastructure as code. We recommend that anyone new to infrastructure as code on Azure use Bicep instead of JSON. To learn about Bicep, see the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/paths/fundamentals-bicep/"><strong>Fundamentals of Bicep</strong></a> learning path.</p>
<ol start="6" type="1">
<li><p><strong>Learning objectives</strong></p></li>
</ol>
<p>In this module, you will:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Implement a JSON ARM template by using Visual Studio Code.</p></li>
<li><p>Declare resources and add flexibility to your template by adding parameters and outputs.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Basics

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Explore Azure Resource Manager template structure</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>7 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>In this unit, you learn about using Azure Resource Manager templates (ARM templates) to implement infrastructure as code. You survey the sections of an ARM template, learn how to deploy your ARM template to Azure, and delve into detail on the <em>resources</em> section of the ARM template.</p>
<p><strong>What is infrastructure as code?</strong></p>
<p><em>Infrastructure as code</em> allows you to describe, through code, the infrastructure that you need for your application.</p>
<p>With infrastructure as code, you can maintain both your application code and everything you need to deploy your application in a central code repository. The advantages to infrastructure as code are:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Consistent configurations</p></li>
<li><p>Improved scalability</p></li>
<li><p>Faster deployments</p></li>
<li><p>Better traceability</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>What is an ARM template?</strong></p>
<p>ARM templates are JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) files that define the infrastructure and configuration for your deployment. The template uses a <em>declarative syntax</em>. The declarative syntax is a way of building the structure and elements that outline what resources look like without describing the control flow. Declarative syntax is different than <em>imperative syntax</em>, which uses commands for the computer to perform. Imperative scripting focuses on specifying each step in deploying the resources.</p>
<p>ARM templates allow you to declare what you intend to deploy without having to write the sequence of programming commands to create it. In an ARM template, you specify the resources and the properties for those resources. <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-resource-manager/management/overview">Azure Resource Manager</a> then uses that information to deploy the resources in an organized and consistent manner.</p>
<p><strong>Benefits of using ARM templates</strong></p>
<p>ARM templates allow you to automate deployments and use the practice of infrastructure as code (IaC). The template code becomes part of your infrastructure and development projects. Just like application code, you can store the IaC files in a source repository and version it.</p>
<p>ARM templates are <em>idempotent</em>, which means you can deploy the same template many times and get the same resource types in the same state.</p>
<p>Resource Manager orchestrates deploying the resources so they're created in the correct order. When possible, resources are created in parallel, so ARM template deployments finish faster than scripted deployments.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image26.webp" style="width:4.73958in;height:2.1875in" alt="Diagram showing a mapping of the template processing procedure. There&#39;s only one call to process a template as opposed to several calls to process scripts." /></p>
<p>Resource Manager also has built-in validation. It checks the template before starting the deployment to make sure the deployment succeeds.</p>
<p>If your deployments become more complex, you can break your ARM templates into smaller, reusable components. You can link these smaller templates together at deployment time. You can also nest templates inside other templates.</p>
<p>In the Azure portal, you can review your deployment history and get information about the state of the deployment. The portal displays values for all parameters and outputs.</p>
<p>You can also integrate your ARM templates into continuous integration and continuous deployment (CI/CD) tools like <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/services/devops/pipelines">Azure Pipelines</a>, which can automate your release pipelines for fast and reliable application and infrastructure updates. By using Azure DevOps and ARM template tasks, you can continuously build and deploy your projects.</p>
<p><strong>ARM template file structure</strong></p>
<p>When you're writing an ARM template, you need to understand all the parts that make up the template and what they do. ARM template files are made up of the following elements:</p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 13%" />
<col style="width: 84%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Element</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>schema</strong></td>
<td>A required section that defines the location of the JSON schema file that describes the structure of JSON data. The version number you use depends on the scope of the deployment and your JSON editor.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>contentVersion</strong></td>
<td>A required section that defines the version of your template (such as 1.0.0.0). You can use this value to document significant changes in your template to ensure you're deploying the right template.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>apiProfile</strong></td>
<td>An optional section that defines a collection of API versions for resource types. You can use this value to avoid having to specify API versions for each resource in the template.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>parameters</strong></td>
<td>An optional section where you define values that are provided during deployment. You can provide these values in a parameter file, by command-line parameters, or in the Azure portal.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>variables</strong></td>
<td>An optional section where you define values that are used to simplify template language expressions.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>functions</strong></td>
<td>An optional section where you can define <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-resource-manager/templates/template-user-defined-functions">user-defined functions</a> that are available within the template. User-defined functions can simplify your template when complicated expressions are used repeatedly in your template.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>resources</strong></td>
<td>A required section that defines the actual items you want to deploy or update in a resource group or a subscription.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>output</strong></td>
<td>An optional section where you specify the values that are returned at the end of the deployment.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Deploy a template

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Deploy an ARM template to Azure</strong></p>
<p>You can deploy an ARM template to Azure in one of the following ways:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Deploy a local template</p></li>
<li><p>Deploy a linked template</p></li>
<li><p>Deploy in a continuous deployment pipeline</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This module focuses on deploying a local ARM template. In future Learn modules, you learn how to deploy more complicated infrastructure and how to integrate with Azure Pipelines.</p>
<p>To deploy a local template, you need to have either <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/powershell/azure/install-az-ps">Azure PowerShell</a> or the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cli/azure/install-azure-cli">Azure CLI</a> installed locally.</p>
<p>First, sign in to Azure by using the Azure CLI or Azure PowerShell.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/create-azure-resource-manager-template-vs-code/2-explore-template-structure?tabs=azure-cli#tabpanel_1_azure-cli">Azure CLI</a></p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/create-azure-resource-manager-template-vs-code/2-explore-template-structure?tabs=azure-cli#tabpanel_1_azure-powershell">PowerShell</a></p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az login</p>
<p>Next, define your resource group. You can use an already-defined resource group or create a new one with the following command. You can obtain available location values from: az account list-locations (CLI) or Get-AzLocation (PowerShell). You can configure the default location using az configure --defaults location=&lt;location&gt;.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/create-azure-resource-manager-template-vs-code/2-explore-template-structure?tabs=azure-cli#tabpanel_2_azure-cli">Azure CLI</a></p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/create-azure-resource-manager-template-vs-code/2-explore-template-structure?tabs=azure-cli#tabpanel_2_azure-powershell">PowerShell</a></p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az group create \</p>
<p>--name {name of your resource group} \</p>
<p>--location "{location}"</p>
<p>To start a template deployment at the resource group, use either the Azure CLI command <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cli/azure/deployment/group#az-deployment-group-create">az deployment group create</a> or the Azure PowerShell command <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/powershell/module/az.resources/new-azresourcegroupdeployment">New-AzResourceGroupDeployment</a>.</p>
<p>Tip</p>
<p>The difference between az deployment group create and az group deployment create is that az group deployment create is an old command to be deprecated and will be replaced by az deployment group create. Therefore, we recommend using az deployment group create to deploy resources under the resource group scope.</p>
<p>Both commands require the resource group, the region, and the name for the deployment so you can easily identify it in the deployment history. For convenience, the exercises create a variable that stores the path to the template file. This variable makes it easier for you to run deployment commands, because you don't have to retype the path every time you deploy. Here's an example:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/create-azure-resource-manager-template-vs-code/2-explore-template-structure?tabs=azure-cli#tabpanel_3_azure-cli">Azure CLI</a></p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/create-azure-resource-manager-template-vs-code/2-explore-template-structure?tabs=azure-cli#tabpanel_3_azure-powershell">PowerShell</a></p></li>
</ul>
<p>To run this deployment command, you must have the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cli/azure/install-azure-cli">latest version</a> of Azure CLI.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>templateFile="{provide-the-path-to-the-template-file}"</p>
<p>az deployment group create \</p>
<p>--name blanktemplate \</p>
<p>--resource-group myResourceGroup \</p>
<p>--template-file $templateFile</p>
<p>Use linked templates to deploy complex solutions. You can break a template into many templates and deploy these templates through a main template. When you deploy the main template, it triggers the linked template's deployment. You can store and secure the linked template by using a SAS token.</p>
<p>A CI/CD pipeline automates the creation and deployment of development projects, which includes ARM template projects. The two most common pipelines used for template deployment are <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/paths/deploy-applications-with-azure-devops/">Azure Pipelines</a> or <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/paths/github-actions/">GitHub Actions</a>.</p>
<p>More information on these two types of deployment is covered in other modules.</p>
<p><strong>Add resources to the template</strong></p>
<p>To add a resource to your template, you need to know the resource provider and its types of resources. The syntax for this combination is in the form of <em>{resource-provider}/{resource-type}</em>. For example, to add a storage account resource to your template, you need the Microsoft.Storage resource provider. One of the types for this provider is storageAccount. So your resource type is displayed as Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts. You can use a list of <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-resource-manager/management/azure-services-resource-providers">resource providers for Azure services</a> to find the providers you need.</p>
<p>After you define the provider and resource type, you need to understand the properties for each resource type you want to use. For details, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/templates">Define resources in Azure Resource Manager templates</a>. To find the resource, view the list in the left column. Notice that the properties are sorted by API version.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image27.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.06042in" alt="Screenshot of a Microsoft documentation page showing the storage account documentation selected." /></p>
<p>Here's an example of some of the listed properties from the Storage Accounts page:</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image28.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:6.81597in" alt="Screenshot of a Microsoft documentation page showing some of the storage account properties." /></p>
<p>For our storage example, your template might look like this:</p>
<p>JSON</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"$schema": "https://schema.management.azure.com/schemas/2019-04-01/deploymentTemplate.json#",</p>
<p>"contentVersion": "1.0.0.1",</p>
<p>"apiProfile": "",</p>
<p>"parameters": {},</p>
<p>"variables": {},</p>
<p>"functions": [],</p>
<p>"resources": [</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"type": "Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts",</p>
<p>"apiVersion": "2023-05-01",</p>
<p>"name": "learntemplatestorage123",</p>
<p>"location": "westus",</p>
<p>"sku": {</p>
<p>"name": "Standard_LRS"</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"kind": "StorageV2",</p>
<p>"properties": {</p>
<p>"supportsHttpsTrafficOnly": true</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>],</p>
<p>"outputs": {}</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Exercise – Create template

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Create an ARM template</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Open Visual Studio Code and create a new file called <em>azuredeploy.json</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>The Visual Studio Code ARM template extension comes configured with snippets to help you develop templates. Let's start by adding a blank template. On line 1 of the file, enter <em>arm</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>Visual Studio Code automatically displays several potential choices that start with <strong>arm!</strong>. Select the <strong>Azure Resource Manager (ARM) template</strong>. Visual Studio Code automatically processes the schemas and languages for your template.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image29.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.37431in" alt="Visual Studio Code azuredeploy.json file showing the snippet choices for Azure Resource Manager templates." /></p>
<p>Your file now looks like this:</p>
<p>JSON</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>{</p></li>
<li><p>"$schema": "https://schema.management.azure.com/schemas/2019-04-01/deploymentTemplate.json#",</p></li>
<li><p>"contentVersion": "1.0.0.0",</p></li>
<li><p>"parameters": {},</p></li>
<li><p>"functions": [],</p></li>
<li><p>"variables": {},</p></li>
<li><p>"resources": [],</p></li>
<li><p>"outputs": {}</p></li>
<li><p>}</p></li>
<li><p>Notice that this file has all of the sections of an ARM template that we described in the previous unit.</p></li>
<li><p>Save the changes to the file by pressing Ctrl+S.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Deploy the ARM template to Azure</strong></p>
<p>To deploy this template to Azure, you need to sign in to your Azure account from the Visual Studio Code terminal. Be sure that Azure PowerShell Tools are installed from the Visual Studio Code Extensions, and sign in to the same account that activated the sandbox.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the command bar, select <strong>Terminal &gt; New Terminal</strong> to open a PowerShell window.</p></li>
<li><p>If the command bar of the terminal window shows <strong>PowerShell</strong>, you have the right shell from which to work, and you can skip to the next section.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image30.webp" style="width:4.41667in;height:0.65625in" alt="Screenshot of the Visual Studio Code terminal window with the &#39;pwsh&#39; terminal selected." /></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>If not, select the down arrow and in the dropdown list select PowerShell. If that option is missing, then select <strong>Select Default Profile</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>In the input field, scroll down and select <strong>PowerShell</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image31.webp" style="width:2.14583in;height:2.35417in" alt="Screenshot of the Visual Studio Code terminal window showing the select shell drop-down." /></p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Terminal &gt; New Terminal</strong> to open a PowerShell terminal window.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Sign in to Azure by using Azure PowerShell</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>From the terminal in Visual Studio Code, run the following command to sign in to Azure. A browser opens so you can sign in to your account.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure PowerShell</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Connect-AzAccount</p></li>
<li><p>Tip</p></li>
</ol>
<p>The <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/powershell/azure/new-azureps-module-az">Az PowerShell module</a> is the replacement of AzureRM and is the recommended version to use for interacting with Azure.</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>Sign in using the account you used to activate the sandbox. After you sign in, Visual Studio Code lists the subscriptions associated with your account in the terminal window. If you activated the sandbox, you see a code block that contains "name": "Concierge Subscription". You use this subscription for the rest of the exercise.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Set the default subscription for all PowerShell commands in this session</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Run the following command to obtain your subscription(s) and their ID(s). The subscription ID is the second column. Look for <em>Concierge Subscription</em>, and copy the value in the second column. It looks something like <em>aaaa0a0a-bb1b-cc2c-dd3d-eeeeee4e4e4e</em>:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure PowerShell</p>
<p> Get-AzSubscription</p>
<p> Run the following command, replacing <em>{Your subscription ID}</em> with the one you copied in the previous step. This command changes your active subscription to the Concierge Subscription.</p>
<p>Azure PowerShell</p>
<p> $context = Get-AzSubscription -SubscriptionId {Your subscription ID}</p>
<p>Set-AzContext $context</p>
<p> Run the following command to let the default resource group be the resource group created for you in the sandbox environment. This action lets you omit that parameter from the rest of the Azure PowerShell commands in this exercise.</p>
<p>Azure PowerShell</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>Set-AzDefault -ResourceGroupName learn-6092bf65-dff0-4da4-97cd-172665ed0464</p></li>
<li></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Deploy the template to Azure</strong></p>
<p>Deploy the template to Azure by running the following commands. The ARM template doesn't have any resources yet, so there aren't any resources created.</p>
<p>Azure PowerShell</p>
<p>$templateFile="azuredeploy.json"</p>
<p>$today=Get-Date -Format "MM-dd-yyyy"</p>
<p>$deploymentName="blanktemplate-"+"$today"</p>
<p>New-AzResourceGroupDeployment `</p>
<p>-Name $deploymentName `</p>
<p>-TemplateFile $templateFile</p>
<p>The top section of the preceding code sets Azure PowerShell variables, which includes the path to the deployment file and the name of the deployment. Then, the New-AzResourceGroupDeployment command deploys the template to Azure. Notice that the deployment name is blanktemplate with the date as a suffix.</p>
<p>When you deploy your ARM template to Azure, go to the <a href="https://portal.azure.com/">Azure portal</a> and make sure you're in the sandbox subscription. To do that, select your avatar in the upper-right corner of the page. Select <strong>Switch directory</strong>. In the list, choose the <strong>Microsoft Learn Sandbox</strong> directory.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the resource menu, select <strong>Resource groups</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the <em>learn-6092bf65-dff0-4da4-97cd-172665ed0464</em> resource group.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Overview</strong> pane, you see that one deployment succeeded.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image32.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:1.32014in" alt="Azure portal interface for the resource group overview with the deployments section showing that one succeeded." /></p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>1 Succeeded</strong> to see the details of the deployment.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image33.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:1.68125in" alt="Azure portal interface for the deployments with the one deployment listed and a succeeded status." /></p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>Select blanktemplate to see what resources were deployed. In this case, it's empty because you didn't specify any resources in the template yet.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image34.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.44028in" alt="Azure portal interface for the specific deployment with no resources listed." /></p>
<ol start="6" type="1">
<li><p>Leave the page open in your browser so that you can check on deployments again.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Add a resource to the ARM template</strong></p>
<p>In the previous task, you learned how to create a blank template and deploy it. Now, you're ready to deploy an actual resource. In this task, you add an Azure storage account resource to the ARM template, by using a snippet from the Azure Resource Manager Tools extension for Visual Studio Code.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the <em>azuredeploy.json</em> file in Visual Studio Code, place your cursor inside the brackets in the resources block "resources":[],.</p></li>
<li><p>Enter <em>storage</em> inside the brackets. A list of related snippets appears. Select <strong>arm-storage</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image35.webp" style="width:4.42708in;height:1.53125in" alt="Visual Studio Code azure Resource Manager-storage snippet shown under the typed word storage." /></p>
<p>Your file looks like this:</p>
<p>JSON</p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>{</p></li>
<li><p>"$schema": "https://schema.management.azure.com/schemas/2019-04-01/deploymentTemplate.json#",</p></li>
<li><p>"contentVersion": "1.0.0.0",</p></li>
<li><p>"parameters": {},</p></li>
<li><p>"functions": [],</p></li>
<li><p>"variables": {},</p></li>
<li><p>"resources": [</p></li>
<li><p>{</p></li>
<li><p>"type": "Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts",</p></li>
<li><p>"apiVersion": "2023-05-01",</p></li>
<li><p>"name": "storageaccount1",</p></li>
<li><p>"tags": {</p></li>
<li><p>"displayName": "storageaccount1"</p></li>
<li><p>},</p></li>
<li><p>"location": "[resourceGroup().location]",</p></li>
<li><p>"kind": "StorageV2",</p></li>
<li><p>"sku": {</p></li>
<li><p>"name": "Premium_LRS"</p></li>
<li><p>}</p></li>
<li><p>}</p></li>
<li><p>],</p></li>
<li><p>"outputs": {}</p></li>
<li><p>}</p></li>
<li><p>Values that you should edit are highlighted in the new section of your file, and you can navigate them by pressing the Tab key.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Notice the tags and location attributes are filled in. The location attribute uses a function to set the location of the resource to the location of the resource group. You'll learn about tags and functions in the next module.</p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>Change the values of the resource <em>name</em> and <em>displayName</em> to something unique (for example, <strong>learnexercise12321</strong>). This name must be unique across all of Azure, so choose something unique to you.</p></li>
<li><p>Change the value of the sku <em>name</em> from <strong>Premium_LRS</strong> to <strong>Standard_LRS</strong>. Change the value of <em>tier</em> to <strong>Standard</strong>. Notice that Visual Studio Code gives you the proper choices for your attribute values in IntelliSense. Delete the default value including the quotation marks, and enter quotation marks to see this work.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image36.webp" style="width:5.64583in;height:3.33333in" alt="Screenshot of Visual Studio Code showing the IntelliSense choices for the name attribute of the storage SKU." /></p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>The resource location is set to the same location as the resource group where the resource is deployed. Leave the default here.</p></li>
<li><p>Save the file.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Deploy the updated ARM template</strong></p>
<p>Here, you change the name of the deployment to better reflect what this deployment does.</p>
<p>Run the following Azure PowerShell commands in the terminal. This snippet is the same code you used previously, but the name of the deployment is changed.</p>
<p>Azure PowerShell</p>
<p>$templateFile="azuredeploy.json"</p>
<p>$today=Get-Date -Format "MM-dd-yyyy"</p>
<p>$deploymentName="addstorage-"+"$today"</p>
<p>New-AzResourceGroupDeployment `</p>
<p>-Name $deploymentName `</p>
<p>-TemplateFile $templateFile</p>
<p><strong>Check your deployment</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>When the deployment finishes, go back to the Azure portal in your browser. Go to your resource group, and you see that there are now <strong>2 Succeeded</strong> deployments. Select this link.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Notice that both deployments are in the list.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image37.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:1.54861in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure portal interface for the deployments with the two deployments listed and succeeded statuses." /></p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>addstorage</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image38.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.09028in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure portal interface for the specific deployment with one resource listed." /></p>
<p>Notice that the storage account is deployed.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Exercise - Parameters

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Add flexibility to your Azure Resource Manager template by using parameters and outputs</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>8 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>In the last unit, you created an Azure Resource Manager (ARM) template and added an Azure storage account to it. You might notice that there's a problem with your template. The storage account name is hardcoded. You can only use this template to deploy the same storage account every time. To deploy a storage account with a different name, you have to create a new template, which isn't a practical way to automate your deployments. The storage account SKU is also hardcoded, which means you can't vary the type of storage account for different environments. Recall that in our scenario, each deployment might have a different type of storage account. You can make your template more reusable by adding a parameter for the storage account SKU.</p>
<p>In this unit, you learn about the <em>parameters</em> and <em>outputs</em> sections of the template.</p>
<p><strong>ARM-template parameters</strong></p>
<p>ARM-template parameters let you customize the deployment by providing values that are tailored for a particular environment. For example, you pass in different values based on whether you're deploying to an environment for development, test, production, or others. For example, the previous template uses the <em>Standard_LRS</em> storage account SKU. You can reuse this template for other deployments that create a storage account by making the name of the storage account SKU a parameter. Then, you pass in the name of the SKU you want for this particular deployment when the template is deployed. You can do this step either at the command line or by using a parameter file.</p>
<p>In the parameters section of the template, you specify which values you can input when you deploy the resources. You're limited to 256 parameters in a template. Parameter definitions can use most template functions.</p>
<p>The available properties for a parameter are:</p>
<p>JSON</p>
<p>"parameters": {</p>
<p>"&lt;parameter-name&gt;": {</p>
<p>"type": "&lt;type-of-parameter-value&gt;",</p>
<p>"defaultValue": "&lt;default-value-of-parameter&gt;",</p>
<p>"allowedValues": [</p>
<p>"&lt;array-of-allowed-values&gt;"</p>
<p>],</p>
<p>"minValue": &lt;minimum-value-for-int&gt;,</p>
<p>"maxValue": &lt;maximum-value-for-int&gt;,</p>
<p>"minLength": &lt;minimum-length-for-string-or-array&gt;,</p>
<p>"maxLength": &lt;maximum-length-for-string-or-array-parameters&gt;,</p>
<p>"metadata": {</p>
<p>"description": "&lt;description-of-the-parameter&gt;"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>The allowed types of parameters are:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>string</p></li>
<li><p>secureString</p></li>
<li><p>integers</p></li>
<li><p>boolean</p></li>
<li><p>object</p></li>
<li><p>secureObject</p></li>
<li><p>array</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Recommendations for using parameters</strong></p>
<p>Use parameters for settings that vary according to the environment; for example, SKU, size, or capacity. Also use parameters for resource names that you want to specify yourself for easy identification or to comply with internal naming conventions. Provide a description for each parameter, and use default values whenever possible.</p>
<p>For security reasons, never hardcode or provide default values for usernames and/or passwords in templates. Always use parameters for usernames and passwords (or secrets). Use <em>secureString</em> for all passwords and secrets. If you pass sensitive data in a JSON object, use the <em>secureObject</em> type. Template parameters with <em>secureString</em> or <em>secureObject</em> types can't be read or harvested after the deployment of the resource.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

### Add Flexibility to ARM 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p><strong>Add flexibility to your Azure Resource Manager template by using parameters and outputs</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>Completed100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>8 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>In the last unit, you created an Azure Resource Manager (ARM) template and added an Azure storage account to it. You might notice that there's a problem with your template. The storage account name is hardcoded. You can only use this template to deploy the same storage account every time. To deploy a storage account with a different name, you have to create a new template, which isn't a practical way to automate your deployments. The storage account SKU is also hardcoded, which means you can't vary the type of storage account for different environments. Recall that in our scenario, each deployment might have a different type of storage account. You can make your template more reusable by adding a parameter for the storage account SKU.</p>
<p>In this unit, you learn about the <em>parameters</em> and <em>outputs</em> sections of the template.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>ARM-template parameters</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>ARM-template parameters let you customize the deployment by providing values that are tailored for a particular environment. For example, you pass in different values based on whether you're deploying to an environment for development, test, production, or others. For example, the previous template uses the <em>Standard_LRS</em> storage account SKU. You can reuse this template for other deployments that create a storage account by making the name of the storage account SKU a parameter. Then, you pass in the name of the SKU you want for this particular deployment when the template is deployed. You can do this step either at the command line or by using a parameter file.</p>
<p>In the parameters section of the template, you specify which values you can input when you deploy the resources. You're limited to 256 parameters in a template. Parameter definitions can use most template functions.</p>
<p>The available properties for a parameter are:</p>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"parameters": {</p>
<p>"&lt;parameter-name&gt;": {</p>
<p>"type": "&lt;type-of-parameter-value&gt;",</p>
<p>"defaultValue": "&lt;default-value-of-parameter&gt;",</p>
<p>"allowedValues": [</p>
<p>"&lt;array-of-allowed-values&gt;"</p>
<p>],</p>
<p>"minValue": &lt;minimum-value-for-int&gt;,</p>
<p>"maxValue": &lt;maximum-value-for-int&gt;,</p>
<p>"minLength": &lt;minimum-length-for-string-or-array&gt;,</p>
<p>"maxLength": &lt;maximum-length-for-string-or-array-parameters&gt;,</p>
<p>"metadata": {</p>
<p>"description": "&lt;description-of-the-parameter&gt;"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>The allowed types of parameters are:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>string</p></li>
<li><p>secureString</p></li>
<li><p>integers</p></li>
<li><p>boolean</p></li>
<li><p>object</p></li>
<li><p>secureObject</p></li>
<li><p>array</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Recommendations for using parameters</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>Use parameters for settings that vary according to the environment; for example, SKU, size, or capacity. Also use parameters for resource names that you want to specify yourself for easy identification or to comply with internal naming conventions. Provide a description for each parameter, and use default values whenever possible.</p>
<p>For security reasons, never hardcode or provide default values for usernames and/or passwords in templates. Always use parameters for usernames and passwords (or secrets). Use <em>secureString</em> for all passwords and secrets. If you pass sensitive data in a JSON object, use the <em>secureObject</em> type. Template parameters with <em>secureString</em> or <em>secureObject</em> types can't be read or harvested after the deployment of the resource.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Use parameters in an ARM template</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>In the parameters section of the ARM template, specify the parameters that you can input when you deploy the resources. You're limited to 256 parameters in a template.</p>
<p>Here's an example of a template file with a parameter for the storage-account SKU defined in the template's parameters section. You can provide a default for the parameter to be used if no value is specified at execution.</p>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"parameters": {</p>
<p>"storageAccountType": {</p>
<p>"type": "string",</p>
<p>"defaultValue": "Standard_LRS",</p>
<p>"allowedValues": [</p>
<p>"Standard_LRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_GRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_ZRS",</p>
<p>"Premium_LRS"</p>
<p>],</p>
<p>"metadata": {</p>
<p>"description": "Storage Account type"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>Then, use the parameter in the resource definition. The syntax is [parameters('name of the parameter')]. Then, when you deploy you use the parameters function. In the next module, you learn more about functions.</p>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"resources": [</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"type": "Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts",</p>
<p>"apiVersion": "2023-05-01",</p>
<p>"name": "learntemplatestorage123",</p>
<p>"location": "[resourceGroup().location]",</p>
<p>"sku": {</p>
<p>"name": "[parameters('storageAccountType')]"</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"kind": "StorageV2",</p>
<p>"properties": {</p>
<p>"supportsHttpsTrafficOnly": true</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>When you deploy the template, you can provide a value for the parameter. Notice the last line in the following command:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/create-azure-resource-manager-template-vs-code/4-add-flexibility-arm-template?tabs=azure-cli#tabpanel_1_azure-cli">Azure CLI</a></p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/create-azure-resource-manager-template-vs-code/4-add-flexibility-arm-template?tabs=azure-cli#tabpanel_1_azure-powershell">PowerShell</a></p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure CLICopy</p>
<p>templateFile="azuredeploy.json"</p>
<p>az deployment group create \</p>
<p>--name testdeployment1 \</p>
<p>--template-file $templateFile \</p>
<p>--parameters storageAccountType=Standard_LRS</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>ARM template outputs</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>In your ARM template's outputs section, you can specify the values that are returned after a successful deployment. Here are the elements that make up the outputs section.</p>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"outputs": {</p>
<p>"&lt;output-name&gt;": {</p>
<p>"condition": "&lt;boolean-value-whether-to-output-value&gt;",</p>
<p>"type": "&lt;type-of-output-value&gt;",</p>
<p>"value": "&lt;output-value-expression&gt;",</p>
<p>"copy": {</p>
<p>"count": &lt;number-of-iterations&gt;,</p>
<p>"input": &lt;values-for-the-variable&gt;</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>Expand table</p>
<table>
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 8%" />
<col style="width: 91%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Element</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>output-name</strong></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Must be a valid JavaScript identifier.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>condition</strong></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">(Optional) A Boolean value that indicates whether this output value is returned. When true, the value is included in the output for the deployment. When false, the output value is skipped for this deployment. When not specified, the default value is true.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>type</strong></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The type of the output value.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>value</strong></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">(Optional) A template language expression to be evaluated and returned as an output value.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>copy</strong></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">(Optional) Copy is used to return more than one value for an output.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Use outputs in an ARM template</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>Here's an example to output the storage account's endpoints:</p>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"outputs": {</p>
<p>"storageEndpoint": {</p>
<p>"type": "object",</p>
<p>"value": "[reference('learntemplatestorage123').primaryEndpoints]"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>Notice the reference part of the expression. This function gets the runtime state of the storage account.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Deploy an ARM template again</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>Recall that ARM templates are <em>idempotent</em>, which means you can deploy the template to the same environment again, and if nothing changes in the template, nothing changes in the environment. If a change is made to the template (for example, you change a parameter value), only that change is deployed. Your template can contain all of the resources you need for your Azure solution, and you can safely execute a template again. Resources are created only if they don't already exist, and updated only if there's a change.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Add Parms and outputs to ARM 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Create parameters for the ARM template</strong></p>
<p>Here, you make your ARM template more flexible by adding parameters that can be set at runtime. Create a parameter for the storageName value.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the <em>azuredeploy.json</em> file in Visual Studio Code, place your cursor inside the braces in the <em>parameters</em> attribute. It looks like this: "parameters":{},</p></li>
<li><p>Select Enter, and then enter <strong>par</strong>. You see a list of related snippets. Choose <strong>new-parameter</strong>, which adds a generic parameter to the template. It looks like this example:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"parameters": {</p>
<p>"parameter1": {</p>
<p>"type": "string",</p>
<p>"metadata": {</p>
<p>"description": "description"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>},</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>Change the parameter from <strong>parameter1</strong> to <strong>storageName</strong> and leave the type as a string. Add a <strong>minLength</strong> value of <strong>3</strong> and a <strong>maxLength</strong> value of <strong>24</strong>. Add a description value of <strong>The name of the Azure storage resource</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>The parameter block should now look like this example:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"parameters": {</p>
<p>"storageName": {</p>
<p>"type": "string",</p>
<p>"minLength": 3,</p>
<p>"maxLength": 24,</p>
<p>"metadata": {</p>
<p>"description": "The name of the Azure storage resource"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>},</p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>Use the new parameter in the resources block in both the name and displayName values. The entire file looks like this code example:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"$schema": "https://schema.management.azure.com/schemas/2019-04-01/deploymentTemplate.json#",</p>
<p>"contentVersion": "1.0.0.0",</p>
<p>"parameters": {</p>
<p>"storageName": {</p>
<p>"type": "string",</p>
<p>"minLength": 3,</p>
<p>"maxLength": 24,</p>
<p>"metadata": {</p>
<p>"description": "The name of the Azure storage resource"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"functions": [],</p>
<p>"variables": {},</p>
<p>"resources": [</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"type": "Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts",</p>
<p>"apiVersion": "2023-05-01",</p>
<p>"name": "[parameters('storageName')]",</p>
<p>"tags": {</p>
<p>"displayName": "[parameters('storageName')]"</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"location": "[resourceGroup().location]",</p>
<p>"kind": "StorageV2",</p>
<p>"sku": {</p>
<p>"name": "Standard_LRS"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>],</p>
<p>"outputs": {}</p>
<p>}</p>
<ol start="6" type="1">
<li><p>Save the file.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Deploy the parameterized ARM template</strong></p>
<p>Here, you change the name of the deployment to better reflect what this deployment does and fill in a value for the new parameter.</p>
<p>Run the following Azure PowerShell commands in the terminal. This snippet is the same code you used previously, but the name of the deployment is changed. Fill in a unique name for the storageName parameter. Remember, this name must be unique across all of Azure. You can use the unique name you created in the last unit. In that case, Azure updates the resource instead of creating a new one.</p>
<p>Azure PowerShellCopy</p>
<p>$templateFile="azuredeploy.json"</p>
<p>$today=Get-Date -Format "MM-dd-yyyy"</p>
<p>$deploymentName="addnameparameter-"+"$today"</p>
<p>New-AzResourceGroupDeployment `</p>
<p>-Name $deploymentName `</p>
<p>-TemplateFile $templateFile `</p>
<p>-storageName {your-unique-name}</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><strong>Check your deployment</strong></p></li>
<li><p>When the deployment finishes, go back to the Azure portal in your browser. Go to your resource group, and see that there are now <strong>3 Succeeded</strong> deployments. Select this link.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Notice that all three deployments are in the list.</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>Explore the <em>addnameparameter</em> deployment as you did previously.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Add another parameter that limits allowed values</strong></p></li>
</ol>
<p>Here, you use parameters to limit the values allowed for a parameter.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Place your cursor after the closing brace for the storageNameparameter. Add a comma, and select Enter.</p></li>
<li><p>Again, enter <strong>par</strong>, and select <strong>new-parameter</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Change the new generic parameter to the following code:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"storageSKU": {</p>
<p>"type": "string",</p>
<p>"defaultValue": "Standard_LRS",</p>
<p>"allowedValues": [</p>
<p>"Standard_LRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_GRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_RAGRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_ZRS",</p>
<p>"Premium_LRS",</p>
<p>"Premium_ZRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_GZRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_RAGZRS"</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>Here, you're listing the values that this parameter allows. If the template runs with a value that isn't allowed, the deployment fails.</p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>Add a comment to this parameter.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image39.webp" style="width:6.2598in;height:3.51821in" alt="Screenshot of the azuredeploy.json file showing the comment This is the allowed values for an Azure storage account in the line preceding the storageSKU parameter." /></p>
<p>ARM templates support // and /* */ comments.</p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>Update <strong>resources</strong> to use the storageSKU parameter. If you take advantage of IntelliSense in Visual Studio Code, it makes this step easier.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"sku": {</p>
<p>"name": "[parameters('storageSKU')]"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>The entire file looks like this code example:</p>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"$schema": "https://schema.management.azure.com/schemas/2019-04-01/deploymentTemplate.json#",</p>
<p>"contentVersion": "1.0.0.0",</p>
<p>"parameters": {</p>
<p>"storageName": {</p>
<p>"type": "string",</p>
<p>"minLength": 3,</p>
<p>"maxLength": 24,</p>
<p>"metadata": {</p>
<p>"description": "The name of the Azure storage resource"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"storageSKU": {</p>
<p>"type": "string",</p>
<p>"defaultValue": "Standard_LRS",</p>
<p>"allowedValues": [</p>
<p>"Standard_LRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_GRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_RAGRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_ZRS",</p>
<p>"Premium_LRS",</p>
<p>"Premium_ZRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_GZRS",</p>
<p>"Standard_RAGZRS"</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"functions": [],</p>
<p>"variables": {},</p>
<p>"resources": [</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"type": "Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts",</p>
<p>"apiVersion": "2023-05-01",</p>
<p>"name": "[parameters('storageName')]",</p>
<p>"tags": {</p>
<p>"displayName": "[parameters('storageName')]"</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"location": "[resourceGroup().location]",</p>
<p>"kind": "StorageV2",</p>
<p>"sku": {</p>
<p>"name": "[parameters('storageSKU')]"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>],</p>
<p>"outputs": {}</p>
<p>}</p>
<ol start="6" type="1">
<li><p>Save the file.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Deploy the ARM template</strong></p>
<p>Here, you deploy successfully by using a storageSKU parameter that's in the allowed list. Then, you try to deploy the template by using a storageSKU parameter that isn't in the allowed list. The second deployment fails as expected.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Deploy the template by running the following commands. Fill in a unique name for the storageName parameter. Remember, this name must be unique across all of Azure. You can use the unique name you created in the last section. In that case, Azure updates the resource instead of creating a new one.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure PowerShellCopy</p>
<p>$today=Get-Date -Format "MM-dd-yyyy"</p>
<p>$deploymentName="addSkuParameter-"+"$today"</p>
<p>New-AzResourceGroupDeployment `</p>
<p>-Name $deploymentName `</p>
<p>-TemplateFile $templateFile `</p>
<p>-storageName {your-unique-name} `</p>
<p>-storageSKU Standard_GRS</p>
<p>Allow this deployment to finish. This deployment succeeds as expected. Your list of allowed values, prevents your template's users from passing in parameter values that don't work for the resource. Let's see what happens when you provide an invalid SKU.</p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>Run the following commands to deploy the template with a parameter that isn't allowed. Here, you changed the storageSKU parameter to <strong>Basic</strong>. Fill in a unique name for the storageName parameter. Remember, this name must be unique across all of Azure. You can use the unique name you created in the last section. In that case, Azure updates the resource instead of creating a new one.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure PowerShellCopy</p>
<p>$today=Get-Date -Format "MM-dd-yyyy"</p>
<p>$deploymentName="addSkuParameter-"+"$today"</p>
<p>New-AzResourceGroupDeployment `</p>
<p>-Name $deploymentName `</p>
<p>-TemplateFile $templateFile `</p>
<p>-storageName {your-unique-name} `</p>
<p>-storageSKU Basic</p>
<p>This deployment fails. Notice the error.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image40.webp" style="width:6.7472in;height:1.63164in" alt="Screenshot of the Terminal window showing the deployment validation error." /></p>
<p><strong>Add output to the ARM template</strong></p>
<p>Here, you add to the outputs section of the ARM template to output the endpoints for the storage account resource.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the <em>azuredeploy.json</em> file in Visual Studio Code, place your cursor inside the braces in the outputs attribute "outputs":{},.</p></li>
<li><p>Press Enter, and then enter <em>out</em>. You get a list of related snippets. Select <strong>new-output</strong>. It adds a generic output to the template that looks like this example:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"outputs": {</p>
<p>"output1": {</p>
<p>"type": "string",</p>
<p>"value": "value"</p>
<p>}</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>Change <strong>"output1"</strong> to <strong>"storageEndpoint"</strong>, then change the value of type to <strong>"object"</strong>. Change the value of value to <strong>"[reference(parameters('storageName')).primaryEndpoints]"</strong>. This expression is the one we described in the previous unit that gets the endpoint data. Because we specified <em>object</em> as the type, it returns the object in JSON format.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"outputs": {</p>
<p>"storageEndpoint": {</p>
<p>"type": "object",</p>
<p>"value": "[reference(parameters('storageName')).primaryEndpoints]"</p>
<p>}</p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>Save the file.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Deploy the ARM template with an output</strong></p>
<p>Here, you deploy the template and see the endpoints output as JSON. You need to fill in a unique name for the storageName parameter. Remember, this name must be unique across all of Azure. You can use the unique name you created in the last section. In that case, Azure updates the resource instead of creating a new one.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Deploy the template by running the following commands. Be sure to replace <em>{your-unique-name}</em> with a string unique to you.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure PowerShellCopy</p>
<p>$today=Get-Date -Format "MM-dd-yyyy"</p>
<p>$deploymentName="addOutputs-"+"$today"</p>
<p>New-AzResourceGroupDeployment `</p>
<p>-Name $deploymentName `</p>
<p>-TemplateFile $templateFile `</p>
<p>-storageName {your-unique-name} `</p>
<p>-storageSKU Standard_LRS</p>
<p>Notice the output.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image41.webp" style="width:5.90392in;height:2.1381in" alt="Screenshot of the Terminal window showing the primary endpoints output as JSON." /></p>
<p><strong>Check your output deployment</strong></p>
<p>In the Azure portal, go to your <em>addOutputs</em> deployment. You can find your output there as well.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image42.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:2.40903in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure portal showing the output selection in the left menu." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Knowledge check 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><em>1. What is an Azure Resource Manager template?</em></p>
<p>A series of Azure CLI commands to deploy infrastructure to Azure.</p>
<p><mark>A JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) file that defines the infrastructure and configuration for your deployment.</mark></p>
<p>An Azure Resource Manager template is a JSON file that defines the infrastructure and configuration for your deployment. ARM templates allow you to declare what you intend to deploy without having to write the sequence of programming commands to create it.</p>
<p>A script held in Azure Resource Manager to manage your Azure storage account.</p>
<p><em>2. Which one of these choices is not an element of an Azure Resource Manager template?</em></p>
<p><mark>idempotent</mark></p>
<p>The elements of an Azure Resource Manager template are: <em>schema</em>, <em>contentVersion</em>, <em>apiProfile</em>, <em>parameters</em>, <em>variables</em>, <em>functions</em>, <em>resources</em>, and <em>output</em>.</p>
<p>schema</p>
<p>parameters</p>
<p><em>3. Azure Resource Manager templates are idempotent. This means that if you run a template with no changes a second time:</em></p>
<p>Azure Resource Manager deploys new resources as copies of the previously deployed resources.</p>
<p><mark>Azure Resource Manager doesn't make any changes to the deployed resources.</mark></p>
<p>If the resource already exists and no change is detected in the properties, no action is taken. If the resource already exists and a property changes, the resource is updated. If the resource doesn't exist, the resource is created.</p>
<p>Azure Resource Manager deletes the previously deployed resources and redeploys them.</p>
<p>If the resource already exists and no change is detected in the properties, no action is taken. If the resource already exists and a property changes, the resource is updated. If the resource doesn't exist, the resource is created.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

# Chapter 2: Manage identities and governance in Azure

|     |
|:----|
|     |

## Understand Microsoft Entra ID

| <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/understand-azure-active-directory/> |
|:---|
|  |

### Introduction 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>Welcome to the Microsoft Entra ID learning module! Microsoft Entra ID is a cloud-based identity and access management service provided by Microsoft. Microsoft Entra ID is a comprehensive solution for managing identities, enforcing access policies, and securing your applications and data in the cloud and on-premises.</p>
<p>This module aims to equip you with a comprehensive understanding of the following:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Describe Microsoft Entra ID.</p></li>
<li><p>Compare Microsoft Entra ID to Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS).</p></li>
<li><p>Describe how Microsoft Entra ID is used as a directory for cloud apps.</p></li>
<li><p>Describe Microsoft Entra ID P1 and P2.</p></li>
<li><p>Describe Microsoft Entra Domain Services.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Whether you're a beginner or an experienced IT professional, this module provides you with the knowledge and skills necessary to understand Microsoft Entra ID effectively. So, let's explore the exciting world of Microsoft Entra ID!</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Examine Microsoft Entra ID

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>Students should be familiar with Active Directory Domain Services (AD DS or traditionally called just "Active Directory"). AD DS is a directory service that provides the methods for storing directory data, such as user <strong>accounts and passwords</strong>, and makes this data available to network users, administrators, and other devices and services. It runs as a service on Windows Server, referred to as a domain controller.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Microsoft Entra ID is part of the platform as a service (PaaS) offering and operates as a Microsoft-managed directory service in the cloud.</p></li>
<li><p><strong><u>It’s not a part of the core infrastructure</u></strong> that customers own and manage, nor is it an Infrastructure as a service offering.</p></li>
<li><p>While this implies that you have less control over its implementation, it also means that you don’t have to dedicate resources to its deployment or maintenance.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>With Microsoft Entra ID, you also have access to a set of features that aren’t natively available in AD DS, such as support for multi-factor authentication, identity protection, and self-service password reset.</p>
<p>You can use Microsoft Entra ID to provide more secure access to cloud-based resources for organizations and individuals by:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Configuring access to applications</p></li>
<li><p>Configuring single sign-on (SSO) to cloud-based SaaS applications</p></li>
<li><p>Managing users and groups</p></li>
<li><p>Provisioning users</p></li>
<li><p>Enabling federation between organizations</p></li>
<li><p>Providing an identity management solution</p></li>
