### Exercise – Create custom security attributes 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image57.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:2.95347in" alt="Screenshot of the Custom Security Attributes dialog. Create new security attributes of type String, Integer, or Boolean." /></p>
<p><strong>What is a custom security attribute?</strong></p>
<p>Custom security attributes in Microsoft Entra ID are business-specific attributes (key-value pairs) that you can define and assign to Microsoft Entra objects. These attributes can be used to store information, categorize objects, or enforce fine-grained access control over specific Azure resources.</p>
<p><strong>Why use custom security attributes?</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Extend user profiles, such as add Employee Hire Date and Hourly Salary to all my employees.</p></li>
<li><p>Ensure only administrators can see the Hourly Salary attribute in my employees' profiles.</p></li>
<li><p>Categorize hundreds or thousands of applications to easily create a filterable inventory for auditing.</p></li>
<li><p>Grant users access to the Azure Storage blobs belonging to a project.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>What can I do with custom security attributes?</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Define business-specific information (attributes) for your tenant.</p></li>
<li><p>Add a set of custom security attributes on users, applications, Microsoft Entra resources, or Azure resources.</p></li>
<li><p>Manage Microsoft Entra objects using custom security attributes with queries and filters.</p></li>
<li><p>Provide attribute governance so attributes determine who can get access.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Features of custom security attributes</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Available tenant-wide</p></li>
<li><p>Include a description</p></li>
<li><p>Support different data types: Boolean, integer, string</p></li>
<li><p>Support single value or multiple values</p></li>
<li><p>Support user-defined free-form values or predefined values</p></li>
<li><p>Assign custom security attributes to directory synced users from an on-premises Active Directory</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Explorer automatic user creation 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image58.webp" style="width:5.20637in;height:2.92629in" alt="Diagram of the process flow for auto user provisioning in Microsoft Entra ID. Auto provision users and groups." /></p>
<p><strong>Components of SCIM (System for Cross-Domain Identity Management)</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>HCM system</strong> - Applications and technologies that enable Human Capital Management process and practices that support and automate HR processes throughout the employee lifecycle.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Microsoft Entra Provisioning Service</strong> - Uses the SCIM 2.0 protocol for automatic provisioning. The service connects to the SCIM endpoint for the application, and uses the SCIM user object schema and REST APIs to automate provisioning and de-provisioning of users and groups.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Microsoft Entra ID</strong> - User repository used to manage the lifecycle of identities and their entitlements.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Target system</strong> - Application or system that has SCIM endpoint and works with the Microsoft Entra provisioning to enable automatic provisioning of users and groups.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Why use SCIM?</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>System for Cross-Domain Identity Management (SCIM) is an open standard protocol for automating the exchange of user identity information between identity domains and IT systems.</p></li>
<li><p>SCIM ensures that employees added to the Human Capital Management (HCM) system automatically have accounts created in Microsoft Entra ID or Windows Server Active Directory.</p></li>
<li><p>User attributes and profiles are synchronized between the two systems, updating removing users based on the user status or role change.</p></li>
<li><p>The key is keeping your identity systems up to date. If a user can be automatically deprovisioned from Microsoft Entra ID, as soon as the're removed from your HR-systems; you have less worry on a possible breach.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Check your knowledge 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>1. Typically, Microsoft Entra ID defines users in three ways. Cloud identities and guest users are two of the ways. What is the third way Microsoft Entra ID defines users?</strong></p>
<p>As non-connected users.</p>
<p>As transitional users.</p>
<p><mark>As directory-synchronized identities.</mark></p>
<p><strong> </strong></p>
<p><strong>2. Microsoft Entra group-based licensing makes large-scale management easier. Typically, how soon are license modifications effective after group membership changes are made?</strong></p>
<p>Within the timeframe of local domain controllers being refreshed.</p>
<p><mark>Within minutes of a membership change.</mark></p>
<p><strong> </strong>Within 24 hours of a membership change.</p>
<p><strong>3. Microsoft Entra ID allows for the definition of two different types of groups; one type is Security groups, which are used to manage member and computer access to shared resources. What is the other type of group?</strong></p>
<p>Distribution groups, which are used for communications purposes via applications such as Teams and Exchange.</p>
<p>Licensing groups, which are used to make it easier to administer software licenses.</p>
<p><mark>Microsoft 365 groups, which provide access to shared mailboxes, calendars, SharePoint sites, and so on.</mark></p></th>

## Describe the core architectural components of Azure

| <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/describe-core-architectural-components-of-azure/> |
|:---|
|  |

### Introduction 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>In this module, you’ll be introduced to the core architectural components of Azure. You’ll learn about the physical organization of Azure: datacenters, availability zones, and regions; and you’ll learn about the organizational structure of Azure: resources and resource groups, subscriptions, and management groups.</p>
<p><strong>Learning objectives</strong></p>
<p>After completing this module, you’ll be able to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Describe Azure regions, region pairs, and sovereign regions.</p></li>
<li><p>Describe Availability Zones.</p></li>
<li><p>Describe Azure datacenters.</p></li>
<li><p>Describe Azure resources and Resource Groups.</p></li>
<li><p>Describe subscriptions.</p></li>
<li><p>Describe management groups.</p></li>
<li><p>Describe the hierarchy of resource groups, subscriptions, and management groups.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### What does Azure Offer

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Limitless innovation.</strong> Build intelligent apps and solutions with advanced technology, tools, and services to take your business to the next level. Seamlessly unify your technology to simplify platform management and to deliver innovations efficiently and securely on a trusted cloud.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Bring ideas to life:</strong> Build on a trusted platform to advance your organization with industry-leading AI and cloud services.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Seamlessly unify:</strong> Efficiently manage all your infrastructure, data, analytics, and AI solutions across an integrated platform.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Innovate on trust:</strong> Rely on trusted technology from a partner who's dedicated to security and responsibility.</p></li>
</ul>
<ol start="8" type="1">
<li><p><strong>What can I do with Azure?</strong></p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure provides more than 100 services that enable you to do everything from running your existing applications on virtual machines to exploring new software paradigms, such as intelligent bots and mixed reality.</p>
<p>Many teams start exploring the cloud by moving their existing applications to virtual machines (VMs) that run in Azure. Migrating your existing apps to VMs is a good start, but the cloud is much more than a different place to run your VMs.</p>
<p>For example, Azure provides artificial intelligence (AI) and machine-learning (ML) services that can naturally communicate with your users through vision, hearing, and speech. It also provides storage solutions that dynamically grow to accommodate massive amounts of data. Azure services enable solutions that aren't feasible without the power of the cloud.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Get Started Azure Accounts 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p>To create and use Azure services, you need an Azure subscription.</p></li>
<li><p>When you're completing Learn modules, most of the time a temporary subscription is created for you, which runs in an environment called the Learn sandbox.</p></li>
<li><p>When you're working with your own applications and business needs, you need to create an Azure account, and a subscription will be created for you.</p></li>
<li><p>After you've created an Azure account, you're free to create additional subscriptions.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, your company might use a single Azure account for your business and separate subscriptions for development, marketing, and sales departments.</p></li>
<li><p>After you've created an Azure subscription, you can start creating Azure resources within each subscription.</p></li>
<li></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image59.webp" style="width:2.85696in;height:2.2657in" alt="Diagram showing the different levels of account scope." /></p>
<ul>
<li><p>If you're new to Azure, you can sign up for a free account on the Azure website to start exploring at no cost to you.</p></li>
<li><p>When you're ready, you can choose to upgrade your free account. You can also create a new subscription that enables you to start paying for Azure services you need beyond the limits of a free account.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Create an Azure account</strong></p>
<p>You can purchase Azure access directly from Microsoft by signing up on the Azure website or through a Microsoft representative. You can also purchase Azure access through a Microsoft partner. Cloud Solution Provider partners offer a range of complete managed-cloud solutions for Azure.</p>
<p><strong>What is the Azure free account?</strong></p>
<p>The Azure free account includes:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Free access to popular Azure products for 12 months.</p></li>
<li><p>A credit to use for the first 30 days.</p></li>
<li><p>Access to more than 25 products that are always free.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/free">Azure free account</a> is an excellent way for new users to get started and explore. To sign up, you need a phone number, a credit card, and a Microsoft or GitHub account. The credit card information is used for identity verification only. You won't be charged for any services until you upgrade to a paid subscription.</p>
<p><strong>What is the Azure free student account?</strong></p>
<p>The Azure free student account offer includes:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Free access to certain Azure services for 12 months.</p></li>
<li><p>A credit to use in the first 12 months.</p></li>
<li><p>Free access to certain software developer tools.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/free/students/">Azure free student account</a> is an offer for students that gives $100 credit and free developer tools. Also, you can sign up without a credit card.</p>
<p><strong>What is the Microsoft Learn sandbox?</strong></p>
<p>Many of the Learn exercises use a technology called the sandbox, which creates a temporary subscription that's added to your Azure account. This temporary subscription allows you to create Azure resources during a Learn module. Learn automatically cleans up the temporary resources for you after you've completed the module.</p>
<p>When you're completing a Learn module, you're welcome to use your personal subscription to complete the exercises in a module. However, the sandbox is the preferred method to use because it allows you to create and test Azure resources at no cost to you.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Explore – Sandbox 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Task 1: Use the PowerShell CLI</strong></p>
<p>Once the sandbox launches, half the screen will be in PowerShell command line interface (CLI) mode. If you’re familiar with PowerShell, you can manage your Azure environment using PowerShell commands.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image60.webp" style="width:7.50138in;height:2.31343in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure PowerShell CLI at initial launch." /></p>
<p><strong> Tip:</strong> You can tell you're in PowerShell mode by the PS before your directory on the command line.</p>
<p>Use the PowerShell Get-date command to get the current date and time.</p>
<p>Get-date</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image61.webp" style="width:5.15697in;height:3.28171in" /></p>
<p>Most Azure specific commands will start with the letters az. The Get-date command you just ran is a PowerShell specific command. Let's try an Azure command to check what version of the CLI you're using right now.</p>
<p>az version</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image62.webp" style="width:3.87554in;height:2.44826in" /></p>
<p><strong>Task 2: Use the BASH CLI</strong></p>
<p>If you’re more familiar with BASH, you can use BASH command instead by shifting to the BASH CLI.</p>
<p>Enter bash to switch to the BASH CLI.</p>
<p>bash</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image63.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:0.77083in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure BASH CLI at initial launch." /></p>
<p><strong> Tip</strong></p>
<p>You can tell you're in BASH mode by the username displayed on the command line. It will be your username@azure.</p>
<p>Again, use the Get-date command to get the current date and time.</p>
<p>Get-date</p>
<p>You received an error because Get-date is a PowerShell specific command.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image64.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:1.01597in" alt="Screenshot of BASH error message get-date command not found." /></p>
<p>Use the date command to get the current date and time.</p>
<p>date</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image65.webp" style="width:3.81303in;height:1.86484in" /></p>
<p>Just like in the PowerShell mode of the CLI, you can use the letters az to start an Azure command in the BASH mode. Try to run an update to the CLI with az upgrade.</p>
<p>az upgrade</p>
<p>You can change back to PowerShell mode by entering pwsh on the BASH command line.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image66.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:2.27222in" /></p>
<p><strong>Task 3: Use Azure CLI interactive mode</strong></p>
<p>Another way to interact is using the Azure CLI interactive mode. This changes CLI behavior to more closely resemble an integrated development environment (IDE). Interactive mode provides autocompletion, command descriptions, and even examples. If you’re unfamiliar with BASH and PowerShell, but want to use the command line, interactive mode may help you.</p>
<p>Enter az interactive to enter interactive mode.</p>
<p>az interactive</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image67.webp" style="width:6.34464in;height:3.92763in" /></p>
<p>Decide whether you wish to send telemetry data and enter YES or NO.</p>
<p>You may have to wait a minute or two to allow the interactive mode to fully initialize. Then, enter the letter “a” and auto-completion should start to work. If auto-completion isn’t working, erase what you’ve entered, wait a bit longer, and try again.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image68.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:4.63889in" alt="Screenshot of interactive mode with autocompletion providing commands that start with A." /></p>
<p>Once initialized, you can use the arrow keys or tab to help complete your commands. Interactive mode is set up specifically for Azure, so you don't need to enter az to start a command (but you can if you want to or are used to it). Try the upgrade or version commands again, but this time without az in front.</p>
<p>version</p>
<p>upgrade</p>
<p>The commands should have worked the same as before, and given you the same results. Use the exit command to leave interactive mode.</p>
<p>exit</p>
<p><strong>Task 4: Use the Azure portal</strong></p>
<p>You’ll also have the option of using the Azure portal during sandbox exercises. You need to use the link provided in the exercise to access the Azure portal. Using the provided link, instead of opening the portal yourself, ensures the correct subscription is used and the exercise remains free for you to complete.</p>
<p>Sign in to the <a href="https://portal.azure.com/learn.docs.microsoft.com">Azure portal</a> to check out the Azure web interface. Once in the portal, you can see all the services Azure has to offer as well as look around at resource groups and so on.</p>
<p><strong>Continue</strong></p>
<p>You're all set for now. We'll come back to this sandbox later in this module and actually create an Azure resource!</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Describe Azure Physical Infrastructure 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p>Throughout your journey with Microsoft Azure, you’ll hear and use terms like Regions, Availability Zones, Resources, Subscriptions, and more.</p></li>
<li><p>This module focuses on the core architectural components of Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>The core architectural components of Azure may be broken down into two main groupings:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>the <strong>physical</strong> infrastructure, (this section)</p></li>
<li><p>the <strong>management</strong> infrastructure. (next section)</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Physical infrastructure</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>The physical infrastructure for Azure starts with <strong>datacenters</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Conceptually, the datacenters are the same as large corporate datacenters.</p></li>
<li><p>They’re facilities with resources arranged in racks, with dedicated power, cooling, and networking infrastructure.</p></li>
<li><p>As a global cloud provider, Azure has datacenters around the world.</p></li>
<li><p>However, these individual datacenters aren’t directly accessible.</p></li>
<li><p>Datacenters are grouped into Azure Regions or Azure Availability Zones that are designed to help you achieve resiliency and reliability for your business-critical workloads.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The <a href="https://infrastructuremap.microsoft.com/">Global infrastructure</a> site gives you a chance to interactively explore the underlying Azure infrastructure.</p>
<p><strong>Regions</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>A region is a geographical area on the planet that contains <strong>at least one</strong>, but potentially multiple datacenters that are nearby and networked together with a low-latency network.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure intelligently assigns and controls the resources within each region to ensure workloads are appropriately balanced.</p></li>
<li><p>When you deploy a resource in Azure, you'll often need to choose the region where you want your resource deployed.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Note:</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Some services or virtual machine (VM) features are only available in certain regions, such as specific VM sizes or storage types.</p></li>
<li><p>There are also some <strong>global Azure services</strong> that don't require you to select a particular region, such as Microsoft <strong><u>Entra ID, Azure Traffic Manager, and Azure DNS</u></strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Availability Zones</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Availability zones are physically separate datacenters within an Azure region.</p></li>
<li><p>Each availability zone is made up of one or more datacenters equipped with independent power, cooling, and networking.</p></li>
<li><p>An availability zone is set up to be an isolation boundary.</p></li>
<li><p>If one zone goes down, the other continues working.</p></li>
<li><p>Availability zones are connected through high-speed, private fiber-optic networks.</p></li>
</ul>
<table style="width:97%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 48%" />
<col style="width: 48%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image69.webp" style="width:3.29851in;height:3.29851in" alt="Diagram showing three datacenters connected in a single Azure region representing an availability zone." /></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image70.webp" style="width:3.22513in;height:3.73119in" /></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong> Important</strong></p>
<p>To ensure resiliency, a minimum of three separate availability zones are present in all availability zone-enabled regions. However, not all Azure Regions currently support availability zones.</p>
<p><strong>Use availability zones in your apps</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>You want to ensure your services and data are redundant so you can protect your information in case of failure.</p></li>
<li><p>When you host your infrastructure, setting up your own redundancy requires that you create duplicate hardware environments.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure can help make your app highly available through availability zones.</p></li>
<li><p>You can use availability zones to run mission-critical applications and build high-availability into your application architecture by co-locating your compute, storage, networking, and data resources within an availability zone and replicating in other availability zones.</p></li>
<li><p>Keep in mind that there could be a cost to duplicating your services and transferring data between availability zones.</p></li>
<li><p>Availability zones are primarily for <strong>VMs, managed disks, load balancers, and SQL databases</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure services that support availability zones fall into three categories:</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p>Zonal services: You pin the resource to a specific zone (for example, VMs, managed disks, IP addresses).</p></li>
<li><p>Zone-redundant services: The platform replicates automatically across zones (for example, zone-redundant storage, SQL Database).</p></li>
<li><p>Non-regional services: Services are always available from Azure geographies and are resilient to zone-wide outages as well as region-wide outages.</p></li>
<li><p>Even with the additional resiliency that availability zones provide, it’s possible that an event could be so large that it impacts multiple availability zones in a single region.</p></li>
<li><p>To provide even further resilience, Azure has <strong>Region Pairs</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Region pairs</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Most Azure regions are paired with another region within the same geography (such as US, Europe, or Asia) at least 300 miles away.</p></li>
<li><p>This approach allows for the replication of resources across a geography that helps reduce the likelihood of interruptions because of events such as natural disasters, civil unrest, power outages, or physical network outages that affect an entire region.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, if a region in a pair was affected by a natural disaster, services would automatically fail over to the other region in its region pair.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong> Important</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Not all Azure services automatically replicate data or automatically fall back from a failed region to cross-replicate to another enabled region.</p></li>
<li><p>In these scenarios, recovery and replication must be configured by the customer.</p></li>
<li><p>Examples of region pairs in Azure are West US paired with East US and South-East Asia paired with East Asia.</p></li>
<li><p>Because the pair of regions are directly connected and far enough apart to be isolated from regional disasters, you can use them to provide reliable services and data redundancy.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image71.webp" style="width:6.46362in;height:3.57689in" alt="Diagram showing the relationship between geography, region pair, region, and availability zone." /></p>
<p><strong>Additional advantages of region pairs:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>If an extensive Azure outage occurs, one region out of every pair is prioritized to make sure at least one is restored as quickly as possible for applications hosted in that region pair.</p></li>
<li><p>Planned Azure updates are rolled out to paired regions one region at a time to minimize downtime and risk of application outage.</p></li>
<li><p>Data continues to reside within the same geography as its pair (except for Brazil South) for tax- and law-enforcement jurisdiction purposes.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong> Important</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Most regions are paired in two directions, meaning they are the backup for the region that provides a backup for them (West US and East US back each other up).</p></li>
<li><p>However, some regions, such as West India and Brazil South, are paired in <strong>only one direction</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>In a <strong>one-direction pairing</strong>, the Primary region does not provide backup for its secondary region.</p></li>
<li><p>So, even though West India’s secondary region is South India, South India does not rely on West India.</p></li>
<li><p>West India's secondary region is South India, but South India's secondary region is Central India.</p></li>
<li><p>Brazil South is unique because it's paired with a region outside of its geography.</p></li>
<li><p>Brazil South's secondary region is South Central US.</p></li>
<li><p>The secondary region of South Central US isn't Brazil South.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Sovereign Regions</strong></p>
<p>In addition to regular regions, Azure also has sovereign regions. Sovereign regions are instances of Azure that are isolated from the main instance of Azure. You may need to use a sovereign region for compliance or legal purposes.</p>
<p>Azure sovereign regions include:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>US DoD Central, US Gov Virginia, US Gov Iowa and more: These regions are physical and logical network-isolated instances of Azure for U.S. government agencies and partners. These datacenters are operated by screened U.S. personnel and include additional compliance certifications.</p></li>
<li><p>China East, China North, and more: These regions are available through a unique partnership between Microsoft and 21Vianet, whereby Microsoft doesn't directly maintain the datacenters.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Describe Azure Management Infrastructure

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Azure resources and resource groups</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>A resource is the basic building block of Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>Anything you create, provision, deploy, etc. is a resource.</p></li>
<li><p>Virtual Machines (VMs), virtual networks, databases, cognitive services, etc. are all considered resources within Azure.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image72.webp" style="width:3.81406in;height:1.71182in" alt="Diagram showing a resource group box with a function, VM, database, and app included." /></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Resource groups are simply groupings of resources.</p></li>
<li><p>When you create a resource, you’re required to place it into a resource group.</p></li>
<li><p>While a resource group can contain many resources, a single resource can only be in one resource group at a time.</p></li>
<li><p>Some resources may be moved between resource groups, but when you move a resource to a new group, it will no longer be associated with the former group.</p></li>
<li><p>Additionally, resource groups can't be nested, meaning you can’t put resource group B inside of resource group A.</p></li>
<li><p>Resource groups provide a convenient way to group resources together.</p></li>
<li><p>When you apply an action to a resource group, that action will apply to all the resources within the resource group.</p></li>
<li><p>If you delete a resource group, all the resources will be deleted.</p></li>
<li><p>If you grant or deny access to a resource group, you’ve granted or denied access to all the resources within the resource group.</p></li>
<li><p>When you’re provisioning resources, it’s good to think about the resource group structure that best suits your needs.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, if you’re setting up a temporary dev environment, grouping all the resources together means you can deprovision all of the associated resources at once by deleting the resource group.</p></li>
<li><p>If you’re provisioning compute resources that will need three different access schemas, it may be best to group resources based on the access schema, and then assign access at the resource group level.</p></li>
<li><p>There aren’t hard rules about how you use resource groups, so consider how to set up your resource groups to maximize their usefulness for you.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Azure subscriptions</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>In Azure, subscriptions are a unit of management, billing, and scale.</p></li>
<li><p>Similar to how resource groups are a way to logically organize resources, subscriptions allow you to logically organize your resource groups and facilitate billing.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image73.webp" style="width:6.8125in;height:2.80208in" alt="Diagram showing Azure subscriptions using authentication and authorization to access Azure accounts." /></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Using Azure requires an Azure subscription.</p></li>
<li><p>A subscription provides you with authenticated and authorized access to Azure products and services.</p></li>
<li><p>It also allows you to provision resources.</p></li>
<li><p>An Azure subscription links to an Azure account, which is an identity in Microsoft Entra ID or in a directory that Microsoft Entra ID trusts.</p></li>
<li><p>An account can have multiple subscriptions, but it’s only required to have one.</p></li>
<li><p>In a multi-subscription account, you can use the subscriptions to configure different billing models and apply different access-management policies.</p></li>
<li><p>You can use Azure subscriptions to define boundaries around Azure products, services, and resources. There are <strong>two types</strong> of subscription boundaries that you can use:</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Billing boundary</strong>: This subscription type determines how an Azure account is billed for using Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>You can create multiple subscriptions for different types of billing requirements.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure generates separate billing reports and invoices for each subscription so that you can organize and manage costs.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Access control boundary</strong>: Azure applies access-management policies at the subscription level, and you can create separate subscriptions to reflect different organizational structures.</p></li>
<li><p>An example is that within a business, you have different departments to which you apply distinct Azure subscription policies.</p></li>
<li><p>This billing model allows you to manage and control access to the resources that users provision with specific subscriptions.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Create additional Azure subscriptions</strong></p>
<p>Similar to using resource groups to separate resources by function or access, you might want to create additional subscriptions for resource or billing management purposes. For example, you might choose to create additional subscriptions to separate:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Environments</strong>: You can choose to create subscriptions to set up separate environments for development and testing, security, or to isolate data for compliance reasons. This design is particularly useful because resource access control occurs at the subscription level.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Organizational structures</strong>: You can create subscriptions to reflect different organizational structures. For example, you could limit one team to lower-cost resources, while allowing the IT department a full range. This design allows you to manage and control access to the resources that users provision within each subscription.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Billing</strong>: You can create additional subscriptions for billing purposes. Because costs are first aggregated at the subscription level, you might want to create subscriptions to manage and track costs based on your needs. For instance, you might want to create one subscription for your production workloads and another subscription for your development and testing workloads.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Azure management groups</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>The final piece is the management group.</p></li>
<li><p>Resources are gathered into resource groups, and resource groups are gathered into subscriptions.</p></li>
<li><p>If you’re just starting in Azure that might seem like enough hierarchy to keep things organized.</p></li>
<li><p>But imagine if you’re dealing with multiple applications, multiple development teams, in multiple geographies.</p></li>
<li><p>If you have many subscriptions, you might need a way to efficiently manage access, policies, and compliance for those subscriptions.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure management groups provide a level of scope above subscriptions.</p></li>
<li><p>You organize subscriptions into containers called management groups and apply governance conditions to the management groups.</p></li>
<li><p>All subscriptions within a management group automatically inherit the conditions applied to the management group, the same way that resource groups inherit settings from subscriptions and resources inherit from resource groups.</p></li>
<li><p>Management groups give you enterprise-grade management at a large scale, no matter what type of subscriptions you might have.</p></li>
<li><p>Management groups can be nested.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Management group, subscriptions, and resource group hierarchy</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>You can build a flexible structure of management groups and subscriptions to organize your resources into a hierarchy for unified policy and access management.</p></li>
<li><p>The following diagram shows an example of creating a hierarchy for governance by using management groups.</p></li>
</ul>
<table style="width:97%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 69%" />
<col style="width: 28%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image74.webp" style="width:5.17688in;height:3.20149in" alt="Diagram showing an example of a management group hierarchy tree." /></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image75.webp" style="width:1.75777in;height:1.54445in" /></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Some examples of how you could use management groups might be:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Create a hierarchy that applies a policy</strong>. You could limit VM locations to the US West Region in a group called Production. This policy will inherit onto all the subscriptions that are descendants of that management group and will apply to all VMs under those subscriptions. This security policy can't be altered by the resource or subscription owner, which allows for improved governance.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Provide user access to multiple subscriptions</strong>. By moving multiple subscriptions under a management group, you can create one Azure role-based access control (Azure RBAC) assignment on the management group. Assigning Azure RBAC at the management group level means that all sub-management groups, subscriptions, resource groups, and resources underneath that management group would also inherit those permissions. One assignment on the management group can enable users to have access to everything they need instead of scripting Azure RBAC over different subscriptions.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Important facts about management groups:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>10,000</strong> management groups can be supported in a single directory.</p></li>
<li><p>A management group tree can support up to <strong>six levels of depth</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>This limit doesn't include the root level or the subscription level.</p></li>
<li><p>Each management group and subscription can support only one parent.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

## Azure Policy initiatives

| <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/sovereignty-policy-initiatives/> |
|:---|
|  |

### Introduction 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p>Azure Policy is a service that allows you to create, assign, and manage governance policies that enforce rules and effects over Azure resources to ensure that they stay compliant with your IT governance standards.</p></li>
<li><p>These policies enforce various rules and effects on resources, ensuring that they adhere to corporate standards and service-level agreements.</p></li>
<li><p>These policies are described in JSON format and are known as policy definitions. Azure Policy is crucial for enforcing organizational standards and assessing compliance on a large scale.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy initiatives are a collection of Azure Policy definitions that are grouped together toward a specific goal or purpose.</p></li>
<li><p>By consolidating multiple Azure policies into a single item, Azure Policy initiatives allow centralized control and enforcement of configurations across Azure resources.</p></li>
<li><p>Industry-specific organizations in sectors like government, public sector, finance, and others accelerate digital transformation and achieve better business outcomes.</p></li>
<li><p>They can achieve this objective by adopting specific, sovereignty-focused Azure Policy initiatives to address the complexity of compliance with national and regional regulatory requirements.</p></li>
<li><p>Customers can create Azure Policy initiatives that aid in customizing deployments to reduce the time needed to audit environments and help meet established regulatory compliance frameworks and government requirements.</p></li>
<li><p>The initiatives help public sector partners and customers create cloud guardrails and enforce specific regulations effectively.</p></li>
<li><p>They can layer policy initiatives to help form a complete solution for their specific needs, and they can use deployment automation to ensure consistency, best practices, and save time.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>In this module, you learn how Azure Policy can help do the common tasks related to creating, assigning, and managing policies across your organization, such as:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Assign a policy to enforce a condition for resources you create in the future</p></li>
<li><p>Create and assign an initiative definition to track compliance for multiple resources</p></li>
<li><p>Resolve a noncompliant or denied resource</p></li>
<li><p>Implement a new policy across an organization</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong> Important</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Organizations are wholly responsible for ensuring their own compliance with all applicable laws and regulations.</p></li>
<li><p>The information provided in this document does not constitute legal advice, and organizations should consult their legal advisors for any questions regarding regulatory compliance.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Cloud Adoption Frame for Azure 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p>The Microsoft Cloud Adoption Framework for Azure offers comprehensive technical guidance for Microsoft Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>This end-to-end framework helps cloud architects, IT experts, and business leaders reach their cloud adoption objectives.</p></li>
<li><p>Cloud Adoption Framework includes best practices, documentation, and tools that Microsoft employees, partners, and customers contribute to formulate and implement effective business and technology strategies for the cloud.</p></li>
<li><p>For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/cloud-adoption-framework/overview">Microsoft Cloud Adoption Framework for Azure documentation - Cloud Adoption Framework | Microsoft Learn</a>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The following diagram provides high-level information about different methodologies that are included in Microsoft Cloud Adoption Framework for Azure for each phase of your cloud adoption life cycle. Within the framework, Microsoft Azure Policy plays a significant role in the governance framework to help govern your cloud environment and workloads.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image78.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:3.60069in" alt="Screenshot of the high-level information about different methodologies that are included in Microsoft Cloud Adoption Framework for Azure for each phase of your cloud adoption life cycle." /></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Cloud governance refers to the management of cloud usage in your organization.</p></li>
<li><p>The Cloud Adoption Framework - Govern methodology offers a systematic framework for setting up and improving cloud governance in Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>This guidance applies to organizations across various industries and it addresses crucial areas, such as regulatory compliance, security, operations, cost management, data, resource management, and AI.</p></li>
<li><p>It's essential for defining and maintaining efficient cloud use.</p></li>
<li><p>Comprehensive cloud governance oversees all aspects of cloud use, minimizes various risks (such as compliance, security, resource management, and data-related concerns), and optimizes cloud operations throughout the organization.</p></li>
<li><p>It ensures that cloud activities are consistent with the overall cloud strategy, facilitating the achievement of business objectives with reduced obstacles.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Steps for cloud governance</strong></p>
<p>Cloud governance is a continuous process. It requires ongoing monitoring, evaluation, and adjustments to adapt to evolving technologies, risks, and compliance requirements. The Cloud Adoption Framework - Govern methodology divides cloud governance into five steps.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><strong>Build a governance team</strong> - Establish a dedicated cloud governance team that's responsible for defining, maintaining, and reporting on the progress of cloud governance policies.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><em>Example: Include a cloud architect, cybersecurity officer, and financial analyst.</em></p></li>
</ol></li>
<li><p><strong>Assess cloud risks</strong> - Conduct a thorough risk assessment that's unique to your organization, addressing all risk categories, including regulatory compliance, security, operations, costs, data management, resource management, and AI-related risks.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><em>Example: Risk of misconfigured storage accounts leading to unauthorized access.</em></p></li>
<li><p><em>Who: Risk manager, cybersecurity officer, cloud architect.</em></p></li>
</ol></li>
<li><p><strong>Document cloud governance policies</strong> - Clearly document cloud governance policies that dictate acceptable cloud usage and outline the rules and guidelines that mitigate identified risks.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><em>Example: Policy requiring encryption for all data stored in Azure Blob Storage.</em></p></li>
<li><p><em>Who: Compliance manager, cloud architect, legal advisor.</em></p></li>
</ol></li>
<li><p><strong>Enforce cloud governance policies</strong> - Implement a systematic approach to ensure compliance with cloud governance policies. Use automated tools alongside manual oversight to enforce compliance. These tools help set guardrails, monitor configurations, and ensure adherence to policies.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><em>Example: Deploy Azure Policy to restrict VM sizes to approved types only.</em></p></li>
<li><p><em>Who: Cloud engineer, DevOps team, cybersecurity officer.</em></p></li>
</ol></li>
<li><p><strong>Monitor cloud governance</strong> - Regularly monitor cloud usage and the governance teams to ensure ongoing compliance with the established cloud governance policies.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><em>Example: Use Azure Monitor to alert on non-compliant resource configurations.</em></p></li>
<li><p><em>Who: Cloud operations team, compliance auditor, cloud architect.</em></p></li>
</ol></li>
</ol>
<p>You should complete all five steps to establish cloud governance and regularly iterate on steps 2-5 to maintain cloud governance over time.</p>
<p><strong>Considerations for defining a cloud governance policy</strong></p>
<p>The key considerations when defining a corporate cloud governance policy are as follows:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Business risk</strong> – You must document the evolving business risks and the business's tolerance for risk based on data classification and application criticality.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Policy and compliance</strong> – You must convert risk decisions into policy statements to establish cloud adoption boundaries efficiently.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Process</strong> – You must establish processes to monitor violations and adherence to corporate policies.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image79.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:3.53889in" alt="Screenshot of the key considerations when defining a corporate cloud governance policy." /></p>
<p>The five core disciplines of cloud governance are as follows:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Cost management</strong> – Evaluates and monitors costs, including controlling IT expenditures to establish well-defined cost management. It also includes adjusting resources according to demand. It's crucial to exercise control over cloud expenditure to derive greater value from your investments.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Security baseline</strong> – Ensures compliance with IT security requirements by applying a security baseline to all adoption efforts.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Resource consistency</strong> – Ensures consistency in resource configuration and enforcing practices for onboarding, recovery, and discoverability.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Identity baseline</strong> – Ensures that the baseline for identity and access is enforced by consistently applying role definitions and assignments.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Deployment acceleration</strong> – Accelerates the deployment of policies through centralization, consistency, and standardization across deployment templates.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Adopting these measures simplifies the compliance process and also enhances the security and efficiency of your cloud environment.</p>
<p><strong>Cloud governance with Azure Policy</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure's primary governance tool is <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/overview">Azure Policy</a>.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy facilitates the governance of all resources, including current and forthcoming resources.</p></li>
<li><p>It helps to enforce organizational standards and to assess compliance at scale by establishing guardrails across various resources.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy allows for centralized management, allowing you to track compliance status and investigate changes leading to noncompliance.</p></li>
<li><p>You can consolidate all compliance data into a singular repository, which simplifies auditing processes for effective cloud compliance and resource governance. The compliance dashboard offered by Azure Policy presents an aggregated view of the environment's overall state, with the ability to examine details at each resource and each policy level.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy ensures consistent adherence to compliance standards and prevents misconfigurations. It also helps bring your resources to compliance through bulk remediation for existing resources and automatic remediation for new resources. Additionally, embedding Azure Policy directly into the Azure platform can significantly reduce the need for external approval processes, thus boosting developer productivity.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Some useful governance actions that you can enforce with Azure Policy include:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Ensure that your team deploys Azure resources only to allowed regions.</p></li>
<li><p>Enforce geo-replication rules to comply with your data residency requirements.</p></li>
<li><p>Allow only certain virtual machine sizes for your cloud environment.</p></li>
<li><p>Enforce the consistent application of taxonomic tags across resources.</p></li>
<li><p>Recommend system updates on your servers.</p></li>
<li><p>Allow multifactor authentication for all subscription accounts.</p></li>
<li><p>Require resources to send diagnostic logs to an Azure Monitor Logs workspace.</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure Policy evaluates your resources and highlights resources that aren't compliant with the policies that you create.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy can also prevent noncompliant resources from being created.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy comes with built-in policy and initiative definitions for Storage, Networking, Compute, Security Center, and Monitoring.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, if you define a policy that only allows a certain size for the virtual machines (VMs) to be used in your environment, that policy is invoked when you create a new VM and whenever you resize existing VMs.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy also evaluates and monitors all current VMs in your environment, including VMs that were created before the policy was created.</p></li>
<li><p>In some cases, Azure Policy can automatically remediate noncompliant resources and configurations to ensure the integrity of the state of the resources.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, if all resources in a specific resource group need to have the <em>AppName</em> tag with a value of <em>SpecialOrders</em>, Azure Policy can automatically apply that tag if it's missing.</p></li>
<li><p>However, you maintain full control over your environment.</p></li>
<li><p>If there's a particular resource that you don't want Azure Policy to automatically update, you can mark that resource as an exception, and the policy won't automatically update it.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy also integrates with Azure DevOps by applying any continuous integration and delivery pipeline policies that pertain to the predeployment and post-deployment phases of your applications.</p></li>
<li><p>The objective when designing an Azure Policy should be to strike a balance between control and stability in one area with speed and results in the other.</p></li>
<li><p>The main reason to maintain balance is to ensure that the environment remains highly manageable so that you can implement necessary levels of control to ensure governance without hindering operational efficiency or productivity.</p></li>
<li><p>In establishing and enforcing these controls, you must take care that your speed to achieve efficiency isn't affected.</p></li>
<li><p>Hence, achieving this balance between control and stability with speed and results, often requires thoughtful compromise, and it's necessary to carefully evaluate the potential impact before introducing new policies.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/cloud-adoption-framework/">Microsoft Cloud Adoption Framework for Azure - Cloud Adoption Framework | Microsoft Learn</a>.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Azure Policy Design Principles

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p>Governance provides mechanisms and processes to maintain control over your applications and resources in Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>It involves planning your policy in Azure Policy and setting strategic priorities.</p></li>
<li><p>While designing your policy, you must organize your cloud-based resources to secure, manage, and track costs that are related to your workloads.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Hierarchy for governance</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure provides <strong><u>four levels</u></strong> of management to establish proper governance:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Management groups,</p></li>
<li><p>Subscriptions,</p></li>
<li><p>Resource groups,</p></li>
<li><p>Resources.</p></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p>You can build a flexible structure of management groups and subscriptions to organize your resources into a hierarchy for unified policy and access management.</p></li>
<li><p>The following diagram shows an example of creating a hierarchy for governance by using management groups.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image80.webp" style="width:4.54837in;height:2.9624in" alt="Screenshot showing an example of creating a hierarchy for governance by using management groups." /></p>
<ul>
<li><p>The Azure structure starts with the tenant root group at the top, followed by a hierarchy of management groups, which can extend to six levels beneath the root. The definition of each level in the management hierarchy and relationship between these levels is as follows:</p></li>
</ul>
<table style="width:84%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 21%" />
<col style="width: 63%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Concept</strong></p></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Description</strong></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Resource</strong></p></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p>A resource is the basic building block of Azure, and it includes instances of services that you create, provision, deploy, and so on.</p></li>
<li><p>Virtual machines (VMs), virtual networks, databases, AI services, and so on, are considered resources in Azure.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Resource groups</strong></p></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Resource groups are groupings of resources.</p></li>
<li><p>When you create a resource, you must place it into a resource group.</p></li>
<li><p>While a resource group can contain many resources, a single resource can only be in one resource group at a time.</p></li>
<li><p>When you apply an action to a resource group, that action applies to all resources in the resource group.</p></li>
<li><p>If you delete a resource group, all resources are deleted.</p></li>
<li><p>If you grant or deny access to a resource group, all resources in the resource group are also granted or denied access.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Subscriptions</strong></p></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p>In Azure, subscriptions are a unit of management, billing, and scale.</p></li>
<li><p>Similar to how resource groups are a way of logically organizing resources, subscriptions allow you to logically organize your resource groups and facilitate billing.</p></li>
<li><p>Each subscription has limits or quotas on the number of resources that you can create and use.</p></li>
<li><p>Organizations can use subscriptions to manage costs and the resources that users, teams, and projects create.</p></li>
<li><p>Using Azure requires an Azure subscription.</p></li>
<li><p>An Azure subscription provides you with authenticated and authorized access to Azure products and services.</p></li>
<li><p>It also allows you to provision resources.</p></li>
<li><p>An Azure subscription links to an Azure account, which is an identity in Microsoft Entra ID or in a directory that Microsoft Entra ID trusts.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Management groups</strong></p></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Azure management groups provide a level of scope above subscriptions.</p></li>
<li><p>If you have many subscriptions, you might need a way to efficiently manage access, policies, and compliance for those subscriptions.</p></li>
<li><p>You organize subscriptions into containers called management groups and then apply governance conditions to the management groups.</p></li>
<li><p>Management groups give you enterprise-grade management at a large scale, no matter what type of subscriptions you might have.</p></li>
<li><p>Management groups can be nested.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<ul>
<li><p>You can apply management settings at any of these levels of scope.</p></li>
<li><p>The level that you select determines how widely the setting is applied.</p></li>
<li><p>Lower levels inherit settings from higher levels.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, when you apply a policy to the subscription, the policy is applied to all resource groups and resources in that subscription.</p></li>
<li><p>When you apply a policy on the resource group, that policy is applied to that resource group and all its resources.</p></li>
<li><p>However, another resource group won't have that policy assignment.</p></li>
<li><p>All subscriptions within a management group automatically inherit the conditions that are applied to the management group.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Introduction to Azure Resource Manager (ARM)</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure Resource Manager is the deployment and management service for Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>It provides a management layer that allows you to create, update, and delete resources in your Azure account.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure operations are classified into two main types:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>control plane</p></li>
<li><p>data plane.</p></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p>The control plane helps you manage resources in your subscription, while the data plane allows you to access the capabilities provided by instances of specific resource types.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong><u>Control plane</u></strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure Policy <strong>operates in the control plane</strong> to enforce rules and compliance on your resources.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Resource Manager manages all control plane operations in Azure and includes the different components that are centralized between the different services.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy is integrated with Azure Resource Manager.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image81.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:3.9875in" alt="Screenshot of the control plane operations, components, and essential functions that Azure Resource Manager manages." /></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure Resource Manager manages essential functions, such as template-based deployments, role-based access control (RBAC), auditing, monitoring, and tagging, which provides a unified management experience for Azure resources after deployment.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, consider a scenario where you have a storage account.</p></li>
<li><p>With Azure Resource Manager, you can create the storage account and enforce a policy that mandates encryption for all storage accounts.</p></li>
<li><p>When you send a control plane request through any of the Azure APIs, tools, or SDKs, Azure Resource Manager receives the request.</p></li>
<li><p>All capabilities that are available in the portal are also available through PowerShell, Azure CLI, REST APIs, and client SDKs.</p></li>
<li><p>These tools use the same API to process requests, ensuring uniform results and capabilities.</p></li>
<li><p>The Resource Manager authenticates and authorizes the request before forwarding it to the appropriate Azure resource provider that completes the operation.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Data plane</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>The data plane is where the actual data operations occur, and Azure Policy ensures that the resources you interact with in the data plane are compliant with your policies.</p></li>
<li><p>Data plane operations involve direct interaction with the data stored in a resource.</p></li>
<li><p>Continuing with the previous example, you engage with the storage account to upload or download files.</p></li>
<li><p>This interaction is handled directly by the data plane of the storage account rather than being managed by Azure Resource Manager.</p></li>
<li><p>Data plane operations <strong>aren't limited</strong> to REST API.</p></li>
<li><p>Requests are made directly to the data endpoints of services (for example, accessing data in Microsoft Azure Storage, querying an SQL database, or reading secrets from Microsoft Azure Key Vault).</p></li>
<li><p>Each Azure service handles these requests internally, bypassing Azure Resource Manager, and directly managing the data through its resource provider.</p></li>
<li><p>Service-specific access controls, such as RBAC or access control lists (ACLs), often manage data plane permissions.</p></li>
<li><p>The service responds with the data or result of the data operation, ensuring that the requester has the correct permissions.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure Policy allows individual Azure services to implement an Azure Policy extension, enhancing policy behavior and integration with specific resource providers.</p>
<p>Azure Policy currently supports data plane operations through the following resource provider modes:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Microsoft.Kubernetes.Data</strong> - Used for managing Kubernetes clusters and components such as pods, containers, and ingresses.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Microsoft.KeyVault.Data</strong> - Used for managing vaults and certificates in Azure Key Vault.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Microsoft.Network.Data</strong> - Used for managing Microsoft Azure Virtual Network Manager custom membership policies by using Azure Policy.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Microsoft.ManagedHSM.Data</strong> - Used for managing Azure Key Vault Managed HSM keys by using Azure Policy.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Microsoft.DataFactory.Data</strong> - Used for using Azure Policy to deny Microsoft Azure Data Factory outbound traffic domain names.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Microsoft.MachineLearningServices.v2.Data</strong> - Used for managing Microsoft Azure Machine Learning model deployments. This Resource Provider mode reports compliance for newly created and updated components.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/definition-structure-basics#resource-provider-modes">Resource Provider Modes</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Operation flows of Azure Resource Manager</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure Resource Manager includes two scenarios for handling Azure requests: <strong>Greenfield</strong> and <strong>Brownfield</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>As you deploy resources, Azure Resource Manager understands when to create new resources and when to update existing resources.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image82.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:3.76667in" alt="Screenshot of two scenarios for handling Azure requests, Greenfield and Brownfield." /></p>
<p><strong>Greenfield</strong> refers to a scenario where an <strong><u>Azure Policy (policy-first) exists</u></strong>, and when you're creating or updating an Azure resource.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>For example, you're creating a new resource by calling an HTTPS REST API into Azure Resource Manager, that is, targeting a specific resource provider.</p></li>
<li><p>In Azure Resource Manager, the request goes through different layers, <u>including</u> role-based access control (<strong>RBAC</strong>) and <strong>Azure Policy</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>These layers are only two of the many other layers, but it's important to understand that Azure Policy comes after RBAC.</p></li>
<li><p>If you don't have permissions to run a given operation, then Azure Policy isn't considered or called because it would fail on the RBAC stage.</p></li>
<li><p>If you have permissions, the request goes through Azure Policy and is evaluated against any policy.</p></li>
<li><p>For resource updates, you send only the changes (delta) in the request payload in the existing resource.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy requires full visibility of the current state of the resource.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy merges the delta that you're sending by reading the current state.</p></li>
<li><p>Then, the target state is obtained as a result of the merger, and that's what gets evaluated against your policies.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Brownfield</strong> is the scenario where the <strong><u>resources exist already</u></strong> (resource-first), and you're assigning a new Azure Policy to those resources.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>In this case, policy evaluation happens through a compliance scan, which runs automatically every 24 hours, or it can be manually triggered.</p></li>
<li><p>The duration of the scan is unpredictable, but after it's completed, the compliance state of existing resources is updated. To perform this evaluation, Azure Policy reads all existing resources in the scope. You can create an Azure policy that prohibits resources from being created outside of a certain region, such as West Europe. Existing resources outside this region aren't deleted but are flagged as noncompliant after the policy is applied, and future attempts to create resources outside West Europe fail.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><table style="width:86%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 13%" />
<col style="width: 31%" />
<col style="width: 41%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;">Scenario</th>
<th style="text-align: left;">Policy Exists (Eg. Region Lock)</th>
<th style="text-align: left;">Resource Exists (Eg. VM)</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">Greenfield</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Yes</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">No (being created now with policy in place)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">Brownfield</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Yes (being applied now on resource)</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Yes</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Azure Policy resources 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p>Azure Policy enforces organizational standards and assesses compliance at scale.</p></li>
<li><p>It evaluates Azure resources and actions by comparing their properties to business rules, providing an aggregated view of the environment's overall state.</p></li>
<li><p>This policy allows for detailed analysis down to each resource and policy level with granularity.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Six policy</strong> resources are available in Azure, and multiple different concepts apply to these Azure policy resources.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Deinas Exatre</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<table style="width:97%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 52%" />
<col style="width: 45%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image83.webp" style="width:3.94063in;height:1.87313in" alt="Screenshot of six policy resources that are available in Azure and multiple concepts apply to these Azure policy resources." /></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image84.webp" style="width:3.38908in;height:1.47687in" /></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>
<table style="width:97%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 68%" />
<col style="width: 29%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image85.webp" style="width:4.92382in;height:2.95293in" /></p></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>Some of the options can be seen directly on the left side blade.</p>
<p>Eg. Remediation, Definitions, Assignments, Exemptions.</p>
<p>Initiatives can be found under Definition.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Definitions</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure Policy definitions describe resource compliance conditions and the effect to take if a condition is met.</p></li>
<li><p>Several settings determine which resources are evaluated by any Azure Policy.</p></li>
<li><p>You explore these settings in the next unit, <strong>Azure Policy definitions</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>The primary concept to which these settings can be applied is scope.</p></li>
<li><p>Scope in Azure Policy is the same as the levels of hierarchy for governance in Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>Four levels of management scope are under the</p>
<ul>
<li><p>root tenant:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>the management groups,</p></li>
<li><p>subscriptions,</p></li>
<li><p>resource groups,</p></li>
<li><p>resources.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p>The definition might be saved in a management group or a subscription.</p></li>
<li><p>The definition location determines the scope to which the initiative or policy can be assigned.</p></li>
<li><p>An assignment also has several properties that set a scope.</p></li>
<li><p>The use of these properties determines which resource for Azure Policy to evaluate and which resources count toward compliance</p></li>
<li><p>You can apply management settings at any of these levels of scope. The level that you select determines how widely the setting is applied. Lower levels inherit settings from higher levels. For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/scope">Scope in Azure Policy</a>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Initiatives</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure Policy initiatives, also known as a policy set, allow you to group several policy definitions to simplify assignments and management because you work with the initiatives as a single item.</p></li>
<li><p>Initiatives offer a streamlined and automated approach to governance, allowing organizations to manage and monitor compliance at scale.</p></li>
<li><p>The initiative definition contains all policy definitions to help track your compliance state for a larger goal, such as organizational compliance goals or compliance with regulatory frameworks.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, multiple tagging policy definitions can be grouped into a single initiative, and rather than assigning each policy individually, you can apply the initiative to Azure resources.</p></li>
<li><p>A JSON can be used to create a policy initiative definition. For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/initiative-definition-structure">Azure Policy initiative definition structure</a>.</p></li>
<li><p>For more information about Azure Policy built-ins and patterns, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/samples/">Azure Policy samples</a>.</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Built-in policy</strong> is a type of policy definition that's generated by Azure Resource Providers and is available by default.</p></li>
<li><p>A group of such policy definitions is known as a <strong>built-in initiative</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure built-in policy initiatives are a powerful tool set that enables centralized control across Azure resources and enforcement of specific configurations.</p></li>
<li><p>These initiatives comprise a collection of policy definitions and support compliance with various regulatory frameworks, industry standards, and security best practices.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Custom policy</strong> is a type of policy definition that's written by a policy user when no built-in policy maps to your requirements.</p></li>
<li><p>A group of such policy definitions is known as a <strong>custom initiative</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Policy custom initiatives help you to tailor a set of policies specifically to your organization's unique requirements, giving you control to enforce the standards and rules that best fit your environment.</p></li>
<li><p>Microsoft Cloud for Sovereignty makes several custom policy initiatives and compliance mappings accessible through the <a href="https://github.com/Azure/industry-policy-portfolio">industry-policy-portfolio</a> repository on GitHub.</p></li>
<li><p>Microsoft Cloud for Sovereignty initiatives and compliance mappings, which expand on the Azure built-in initiatives, help you automate policy enforcement and foster a robust governance framework that reduces the risk of noncompliance.</p></li>
<li><p>Further, the initiatives also strengthen data protection measures.</p></li>
<li><p>Organizations can use the large suite of available regulatory compliance built-in initiatives while we continue to expand on other frameworks. These initiatives are available as Azure built-in and custom policy initiatives.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Assignments</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Policy assignments define which resources are evaluated by a policy definition or initiative. Policy assignments can be done in the portal, an API call, or through the command line interface.</p></li>
<li><p>Policy and initiative definitions are deployed to a definition location (management group or subscription).</p></li>
<li><p>This location determines the scope to which the initiative or policy can be assigned.</p></li>
<li><p>The location should be the resource container shared by all resources that you want to use the policy definition on.</p></li>
<li><p>Policies and initiatives are assigned to a specific scope (management group, subscription, or resource group).</p></li>
<li><p>While doing so, you can define several optional aspects, including the resource scope and policy definition.</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p>Optional <em>resource selectors</em> to allow gradual rollout based on resource location or type.</p></li>
<li><p>Optional <em>overrides</em> to change the effect of a policy definition without modifying the underlying definition.</p></li>
<li><p><em>enforcementMode</em> can be disabled to support "what-if" scenarios without changing the definition, which is equivalent to changing the definition to an audit effect mode, but a way to do it at assignment level.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, if the policy has <em>Deny</em> effects, that denial isn't effective, but you can still view the result of the compliance evaluation of that policy.</p></li>
<li><p>Optional <em>excluded scopes</em> to exclude inner containers or resources from the assignment scope.</p></li>
<li><p><em>Noncompliance messages</em> can be defined.</p></li>
<li><p><em>Parameters</em> can be assigned values.</p></li>
<li><p>If you have a policy with the <em>deployIfNotExists</em> effect type, a <em>managed identity</em> can be assigned (system-assigned or user-assigned) to turn on remediation actions.</p></li>
<li><p>An assignment has several properties that set a scope. The use of these properties determines which resource for Azure Policy to evaluate and which resources count toward compliance. These properties map to the following concepts:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Inclusion</strong> - For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/assignment-structure">Azure Policy assignment structure</a>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Exclusion</strong> - For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/assignment-structure#excluded-scopes">Azure Policy assignment structure excluded scopes</a>.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Exemptions</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Use the Policy exemptions feature to <em>exempt</em> a resource hierarchy or an individual resource from evaluation of initiatives or definitions.</p></li>
<li><p>Resources that are <em>exempt</em> count toward overall compliance but can't be evaluated or have a temporary waiver.</p></li>
<li><p>They're created as a child object on the resource hierarchy, or the individual resource granted the exemption.</p></li>
<li><p>Policy exemptions aren't created during assignment time, but after, and the effect is still the same as an excluded scope.</p></li>
<li><p>Two exemption categories exist and are used to group exemptions:</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Mitigated</strong> - The exemption is granted because the policy intent is met through another method.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Waiver</strong> - The exemption is granted because the noncompliance state of the resource is temporarily accepted.</p></li>
</ul>
<blockquote>
<p>For more information about policy exemption, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/exemption-structure">Azure Policy exemption structure</a>.</p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong>Attestations</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Policy attestations are used by Azure Policy to set <strong>compliance states of resources</strong> or scopes targeted by <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/effect-manual">manual policies</a>.</p></li>
<li><p>Each applicable resource requires one attestation for each manual policy assignment.</p></li>
<li><p>For ease of management, manual policies should be designed to target the scope that defines the boundary of resources whose compliance state needs to be attested.</p></li>
<li><p>For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/attestation-structure">Azure Policy attestation structure</a>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Remediations</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>The policy remediation task feature is used to bring resources into compliance based on a definition and assignment.</p></li>
<li><p>Resources that are noncompliant to a <em>modify</em> or <em>deployIfNotExists</em> definition assignment can be brought into compliance by using a remediation task.</p></li>
<li><p>Resources that are newly created or updated that are applicable to a <em>deployIfNotExists</em> or <em>modify</em> definition assignment are automatically remediated.</p></li>
<li><p>For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/remediation-structure">Azure Policy remediation task structure</a>.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
