### Azure Policy Definitions

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Azure Policy definition</strong> describes resource compliance conditions and the action or effects that take place if those conditions are met. The policy consists of two parts:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>A <strong>condition</strong> that compares a resource property field or a value, accessed by using aliases, to a required value.</p></li>
<li><p>The <strong>effect</strong> determines what happens when the policy rule is evaluated to match the condition.</p></li>
<li><p>For each new resource, an updated resource, or an existing resource, the effects behave differently.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Anatomy of a policy definition</strong></p>
<p>You use JSON to create a policy definition that contains the elements shown in the following table.</p>
<p>Expand table</p>
<table style="width:95%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 15%" />
<col style="width: 37%" />
<col style="width: 42%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Element</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
<th><strong>Properties or values</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><em>displayName (string, max 128 characters)</em></td>
<td>Used to identify the policy definition.</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><em>description (string, max 512 characters)</em></td>
<td>Provides context for when the definition is used.</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><em>policyType (read-only string)</em></td>
<td>Indicates the origin of the policy definition. This property can't be set, but SDK returns three values which are visible in the portal.</td>
<td>● Built in: Provided and maintained by Microsoft.<br />
● Custom: Custom definitions created by the customer.<br />
● Static: Regulatory Compliance policy with Microsoft ownership.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><em>mode (string)</em></td>
<td>Configured depending on the target of the policy: an Azure Resource Manager property or a Resource Provider property.</td>
<td>● Resource Manager Modes:<br />
    o On All: Evaluates resource groups, subscriptions, and all resource types.<br />
    o Indexed: Evaluates resource groups, subscriptions, and all resource types.<br />
● Resource Provider Modes (limited to Built in policies and fully supported):<br />
    o Microsoft.Kubernetes.Data<br />
    o Microsoft.KeyVault.Data<br />
    o Microsoft.Network.Data<br />
● Resource Provider Modes (limited to Built in policies and in preview mode):<br />
    o Microsoft.ManagedHSM.Data<br />
    o Microsoft.DataFactory.Data</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><em>version (string, optional)</em></td>
<td>Built-in policy definitions can host multiple versions with the same definitionID. If no version number is specified, all experiences show the latest version of the definition.</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><em>metadata (object, optional, max 1,024 characters)</em></td>
<td>Stores information about the policy definition.</td>
<td>Common properties (for Built-in policies):<br />
● <em>version (string)</em>: Tracks details about the version of the contents of a policy definition.<br />
● <em>category (string)</em>: Determines under which category in the Azure portal the policy definition is displayed.<br />
● <em>preview (Boolean)</em>: True or false flag that indicates if the policy definition is in preview.<br />
● <em>deprecated (Boolean)</em>: True or false flag that indicates if the policy definition is deprecated.<br />
● <em>portalReview (string)</em>: Determines if parameters require review in the portal.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><em>parameters (object, optional)</em></td>
<td>Help simplify your policy management by reducing the number of policy definitions. By including parameters in a policy definition, you can reuse that policy for different scenarios by using different values.</td>
<td>Properties:<br />
● name<br />
● type (String, Array, Object, Boolean, Integer, Float, DateTime)<br />
● metadata (description, displayName, strongType, assignPermissions)<br />
● defaultValue<br />
● allowedValues<br />
● schema</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><em>policyRule (object)</em></td>
<td>The effect of a policy is defined in the <em>policyRule</em>. The policy rule consists of the <em>if and then</em> blocks.<br />
● In the <em>if</em> block, you define one or more conditions that specify when the policy applies.<br />
● In the <em>then</em> block, you define the effect that happens when the <em>if</em> conditions result true.</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>{</p>
<p>"displayName": "Allowed locations",</p>
<p>"description": "This policy enables you to restrict the locations your organization can specify when deploying resources. Use to enforce your geo-compliance requirements. Excludes resource groups, Microsoft.AzureActiveDirectory/b2cDirectories, and resources that use the 'global' region.",</p>
<p>"policyType": "BuiltIn",</p>
<p>"mode": "Indexed",</p>
<p>"metadata": {</p>
<p>"version": "1.0.0",</p>
<p>"category": "General"</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"parameters": {</p>
<p>"listOfAllowedLocations": {</p>
<p>"type": "Array",</p>
<p>"metadata": {</p>
<p>"description": "The list of locations that can be specified when deploying resources.",</p>
<p>"strongType": "location",</p>
<p>"displayName": "Allowed locations"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"policyRule": {</p>
<p>"if": {</p>
<p>"allOf": [</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"field": "location",</p>
<p>"notIn": "[parameters('listOfAllowedLocations')]"</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"field": "location",</p>
<p>"notEquals": "global"</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"field": "type",</p>
<p>"notEquals": "Microsoft.AzureActiveDirectory/b2cDirectories"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"then": {</p>
<p>"effect": "deny"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>In the given example, <em>b2cDirectories</em> is excluded from the policy logic because its location field isn't a region (it can be "United States," "Europe," "Asia Pacific," or "Australia"). This logic can be enforced with a separate policy.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Logical operators and conditions (<em>if</em> blocks)</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>The first part of the <em>policyRule</em> in an Azure Policy definition is the <em>if</em> block. This block defines the conditions for which the policy evaluates the resources. A policy definition can contain several conditional statements. Depending on your evaluation requirements, you might or might not need each statement to be true, and you might only need some of them to be true.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Logical operators supported in the <em>if</em> block</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>In the <em>if</em> condition, you can put different logical operators.</p>
<p>Expand table</p>
<table style="width:92%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 7%" />
<col style="width: 29%" />
<col style="width: 55%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Operator</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Type</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>not</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">{condition or operator}</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>not</em> syntax inverts the result of the condition.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>allOf</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">[{condition or operator}, {condition or operator}]</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>allOf</em> syntax (like the logical <em>and</em> operation) requires all conditions to be true.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>anyOf</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">[{condition or operator}, {condition or operator}]</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>anyOf</em> syntax (like the logical <em>or</em> operation) requires one or more conditions to be true.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The following example shows use of the <em>allOf</em> logical operator:</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"if": {</p>
<p>"allOf": [</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"field": "location",</p>
<p>"notIn": "[parameters('listOfAllowedLocations')]"</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"field": "location",</p>
<p>"notEquals": "global"</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"field": "type",</p>
<p>"notEquals": "Microsoft.AzureActiveDirectory/b2cDirectories"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"then": {</p>
<p>"effect": "deny"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Nested logical operations</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>Logical operations are optional and can be nested to create complex scenarios.</p>
<p>The following example shows a <em>not</em> operation nested in an <em>allOf</em> operation:</p>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>"if": {</p>
<p>"allOf": [</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"not": {</p>
<p>"field": "tags",</p>
<p>"containsKey": "application"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"field": "type",</p>
<p>"equals": "Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>},</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Conditions</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>Properties like fields, values, or counts can be evaluated within a condition.</p>
<table style="width:95%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 5%" />
<col style="width: 53%" />
<col style="width: 37%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Fields</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;">Conditions that evaluate whether the values of properties in the resource request payload meet certain criteria can be formed by using a field expression.</th>
<th style="text-align: left;">Name, fullName, kind, type, location, ID, identity.type, tags, tags['tagName'], property aliases</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Value</strong></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Conditions that evaluate whether a value meets certain criteria can be formed by using a value expression.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Count</strong></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Conditions that count how many members of an array meet certain criteria can be formed by using a count expression.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">● Field count, value count<br />
● The current () function returns the value of the array member that's being evaluated</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The condition in an Azure Policy assesses whether the evaluated values for the properties, such as Fields, Value, or Count, meets certain criteria. If the result of a function is an error, the policy results in a deny effect. This result can be avoided while testing by disabling <em>enforcementMode</em> in the assignment. For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/assignment-structure#enforcement-mode">Enforcement Mode</a>.</p>
<table style="width:91%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 37%" />
<col style="width: 54%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Evaluation criteria</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Value type</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>equals</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>notEquals</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>like</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>notLike</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>match</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>notMatch</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>matchInsensitively</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>notMatchInsensitively</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>contains</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>notContains</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>In</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">["stringValue1", "stringValue2"]</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>notIn</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">["stringValue1", "stringValue2"]</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>containsKey</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">keyName</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>notContainsKey</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">keyName</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>less</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">dateValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>less</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>less</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">intValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>lessOrEquals</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">dateValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>lessOrEquals</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>lessOrEquals</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">intValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>greater</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">dateValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>greater</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>greater</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">intValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>greaterOrEquals</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">dateValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>greaterOrEquals</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">stringValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>greaterOrEquals</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">intValue</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>exists</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">bool</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The following JSON is an example of using conditions:</p>
<p>JSONCopy</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"if": {</p>
<p>"allOf": [</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"value": "[resourceGroup().name]",</p>
<p>"like": "*netrq"</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"field": "type",</p>
<p>"notLike": "Network/*"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>"then": {</p>
<p>"effect": "deny",</p>
<p>"details": {</p>
<p>"count": {</p>
<p>"field": "Microsoft.Network/virtualNetworks/addressSpace.addressPrefixes[*]",</p>
<p>"where": {</p>
<p>"value": "[ipRangeContains('10.0.0.0/24', current('Microsoft.Network/virtualNetworks/addressSpace.addressPrefixes[*]'))]",</p>
<p>"equals": "greater"</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Policy functions</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>Functions can be used to introduce extra logic into a policy rule. They're resolved in the policy rule of a policy definition and in the parameter values that are assigned to the policy definitions in an initiative.</p>
<p>The Resource Manager template functions are available to use in a policy rule except a <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/definition-structure-policy-rule#policy-functions">few policy functions and user-defined functions</a>.</p>
<p>The <em>utcNow()</em> function is available to use in a policy rule but differs from use in an Azure Resource Manager template (ARM template). Unlike an ARM template, this function can be used outside <em>defaultValue</em>. It returns a string set to the current date and time in Universal ISO 8601 DateTime format yyyy-MM-ddTHH:mm:ss.fffffffZ.</p>
<p>The following table describes the functions that are only available in policy rules.</p>
<table style="width:95%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 25%" />
<col style="width: 70%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Function</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">addDays(dateTime, numberOfDaysToAdd)</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">● dateTime: [Required] string - String in the Universal ISO 8601 DateTime format 'yyyy-MM-ddTHH:mm:ss.FFFFFFFZ'.<br />
● numberOfDaysToAdd: [Required] integer - Number of days to add.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">Field(fieldName)</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">● fieldName: [Required] string - Name of the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/definition-structure-policy-rule#fields">field</a> to retrieve.<br />
● Returns the value of that field from the resource evaluated by the <em>If</em> condition.<br />
● field is primarily used with auditIfNotExists and deployIfNotExists to reference fields on the resource that are being evaluated.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">requestContext().apiVersion</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Returns the API version of the request that triggered policy evaluation. This value is the API version that was used in the PUT/PATCH request for evaluations on resource creation/update. The latest API version is always used during compliance evaluation on existing resources.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">policy()</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Returns the following information about the policy that's being evaluated. Properties can be accessed from the returned object.<br />
"assignmentId": "",<br />
"definitionId": "",<br />
"setDefinitionId": "",<br />
"definitionReferenceId": ""</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">ipRangeContains(range, targetRange)</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">● range: [Required] string - String specifying a range of IP addresses to check if the <em>targetRange</em> is within range.<br />
● targetRange: [Required] string - String specifying a range of IP addresses to validate as included within the <em>range</em>.<br />
Returns a <em>boolean</em> for whether the <em>range</em> IP address range contains the <em>targetRange</em> IP address range. Empty ranges or mixing between IP families isn't allowed and results in evaluation failure.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">current(indexName)</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Special function that can only be used inside count expressions.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Effect types (<em>then</em> blocks)</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>The second part of the <em>policyRule</em> in an Azure Policy definition is the <em>then</em> block. This block defines the effect that takes place when the policy rule is evaluated to match the condition resources. More than one effect can be valid for a given policy definition. Parameters are often used to specify allowed effect values (<em>allowedValues</em>) in such cases so that a single definition can be more versatile during assignment. Resource properties and logic in the policy rule can determine whether a certain effect is considered valid to the policy definition.</p>
<p>Expand table</p>
<table style="width:94%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 14%" />
<col style="width: 66%" />
<col style="width: 13%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Effect</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Description</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Type</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>disabled</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>disabled</em> effect is a way to deactivate the policy. If a policy definition has <em>Disabled</em> as its effect, any assignments of that policy aren't active. This effect is checked first to determine if the policy rule should be evaluated. This flexibility makes it possible to deactivate a single assignment instead of deactivating all of that policy's assignments.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Synchronous evaluation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>append</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>append</em> effect is used to add more fields to the requested resource during creation or update. It's mostly obsolete because <em>Modify</em> can also be used to add fields to the request.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Synchronous evaluation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>modify</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>modify</em> effect is used to add, update, or remove properties or tags on a subscription or resource during creation or update. It allows Azure Policy to modify requests to Azure Resource Manager by altering fields to ensure compliance.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Synchronous evaluation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>deny</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>deny</em> effect is used to prevent a resource request that doesn't match defined standards through a policy definition and fails the request.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Synchronous evaluation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>denyAction</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>denyAction</em> effect is used to block requests based on intended action to resources at scale. Currently, the only supported action is DELETE.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Synchronous evaluation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>audit</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>audit</em> effect is used to create a warning event in the activity log when you're evaluating a noncompliant resource, but it doesn't stop the request.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Asynchronous evaluation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>auditIfNotExists</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>auditIfNotExists</em> effect allows the auditing of resources that are related to the resource that matches the <em>if</em> condition but doesn't have the properties specified in the details of the <em>then</em> condition.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Asynchronous evaluation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>deployIfNotExists</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>deployIfNotExists</em> policy definition runs a template deployment when the condition is met. It can trigger deployment of a related resource based on the compliance state of the currently evaluated resource.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Asynchronous evaluation</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>manual</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The <em>manual</em> effect allows you to self-attest the compliance of resources or scopes. When a policy definition with <em>Manual</em> effect is assigned, you can set the compliance states of targeted resources or scopes through custom attestations.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Manual attestation</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The following list provides general guidance around interchangeable effects:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><em>audit</em>, <em>deny</em>, and either <em>modify</em> or <em>append</em> are often interchangeable.</p></li>
<li><p><em>auditIfNotExists</em> and <em>deployIfNotExists</em> are often interchangeable.</p></li>
<li><p><em>manual</em> isn't interchangeable.</p></li>
<li><p><em>disabled</em> is interchangeable with any effect.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Multiple policies can be assigned to a single resource at the same scope or at different scopes. Each policy mostly has a different effect defined. The condition and effect for each policy is independently evaluated. The net result of layering policy definitions is considered <strong>cumulative most restrictive</strong>.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Evaluation of resources through Azure Policy 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p>A significant benefit of Azure Policy is the insight and controls that it provides over resources in a subscription or management group of subscriptions.</p></li>
<li><p>This control can be used to prevent resources from being created in the wrong location, enforce common and consistent tag usage, or audit existing resources for appropriate configurations and settings.</p></li>
<li><p>Before you review compliance data and react to it, you need to understand the evaluation triggers, timings, and the resource compliance states.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Evaluation triggers</strong></p>
<p>Evaluations of assigned policies and initiatives happen as the result of various events:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>A policy or initiative is newly assigned to a scope</p></li>
<li><p>A policy or initiative already assigned to a scope is updated</p></li>
<li><p>A resource is deployed to or updated in a scope with an assignment through Azure Resource Manager, REST API, or a supported SDK</p></li>
<li><p>A subscription (resource type Microsoft.Resources/subscriptions) is created or moved in a management group hierarchy with an assigned policy definition that targets the subscription resource type</p></li>
<li><p>A policy exemption is created, updated, or deleted</p></li>
<li><p>Standard compliance evaluation cycle</p></li>
<li><p>The machine configuration resource provider is updated with compliance details by a managed resource</p></li>
<li><p>On-demand scan</p></li>
</ul>
<p>For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/how-to/get-compliance-data#evaluation-triggers">Evaluation triggers</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Evaluation timing</strong></p>
<p>When you're working with policy assignments in Azure, you need to understand the behavior and timing of compliance scans, especially in Brownfield scenarios, where new policies are applied to existing resources. Compliance scans through Azure policies are triggered by various methods:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Automatic full scan</strong> - A full compliance scan is triggered automatically every 24 hours.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Manual scan for Brownfield scenarios</strong> - In cases where a new policy is applied to existing resources (Brownfield scenarios), you can manually trigger a compliance scan by running <em>az policy state trigger-scan</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>When you assign a new policy, a delay can occur in the policy taking effect, which can be up to 30 minutes. The Azure Resource Manager cache holds session data, and it can take time for the policy to propagate in the same session. To bypass the caching delay, you can sign out and sign back in to refresh the Azure Resource Manager cache, which ensures that the new policy is applied immediately to the defined scope.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>After the scan starts, several factors influence how long it takes for a compliance scan to complete:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Policy definitions</strong> - The size and complexity of the policy definitions can increase scan time.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Number of policies</strong> - The more policies applied, the longer the scan might take.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Scope size</strong> - The size of the resource scope assigned to the policy also plays a role.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>System load</strong> - Compliance scans are a low-priority operation, meaning that if the system is busy with more critical tasks, the scan might take longer. The system prioritizes interactive and high-importance operations, so scans might take several minutes, or tens of minutes, even in smaller environments.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Synchronous scan (Low-Priority Execution)</strong> - Because compliance scans are synchronous and assigned a low priority in Azure's system, they're delayed if the system is busy. This scan can significantly extend the time it takes for the scan to complete, even for smaller scopes or policies.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This understanding of the compliance scan process and potential delays allows you to better manage the application and avoid unnecessary waiting, especially in environments with complex or extensive policy definitions.</p>
<p><strong>Resource compliance states</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>When initiative or policy definitions are assigned, Azure Policy determines which resources are applicable.</p></li>
<li><p>Then, it evaluates those resources that aren't excluded or exempted. Evaluation provides one of the compliance states to each resource based on conditions in the policy rule and each resource's adherence to those requirements.</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Non-compliant</strong></p></li>
<li><p><strong>Compliant</strong></p></li>
<li><p><strong>Error</strong> (for template or evaluation error)</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Conflicting</strong> (two or more policy assignments in the same scope with contradicting rules, such as two policies appending the same tag with different values)</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Protected</strong> (resource covered under an assignment with a <em>denyAction</em> effect)</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Exempted Unknown</strong> (default state for definitions with a <em>manual</em> effect)</p></li>
<li><p>When multiple resources or policies have varying compliance states, the overall compliance state is assessed individually for each resource and for each policy assignment. Azure Policy ranks each compliance state so that one wins over another in this situation. The rank order of the states is as given in the previous list of compliance states.</p></li>
<li><p>The compliance percentage is determined by dividing <strong>Compliant</strong>, <strong>Exempt</strong>, and <strong>Unknown</strong> resources by total resources. Total resources include resources with <strong>Compliant</strong>, <strong>Non-compliant</strong>, <strong>Unknown</strong>, <strong>Exempt</strong>, <strong>Conflicting</strong>, and <strong>Error</strong> states.</p></li>
<li><p>For more information on when the policies return these states for any particular resource, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/compliance-states">Azure Policy compliance states</a>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Enforcement Mode</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p><em>enforcementMode</em> is a property of a policy assignment that lets you deactivate the enforcement of certain policy effects.</p></li>
<li><p>This mode allows you to test the policy's outcome on existing resources without initiating the policy effect or triggering entries in the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-monitor/essentials/platform-logs-overview">Azure Activity log</a>.</p></li>
<li><p>The <em>enforcementMode</em> can be changed to Enabled after the policy is thoroughly tested.</p></li>
<li><p>This scenario is commonly referred to as <em>What If</em> and aligns to safe deployment practices.</p></li>
<li><p>The <em>enforcementMode</em> is different from the <em>disabled</em> effect.</p></li>
<li><p>The <em>disabled</em> effect prevents resource evaluation from happening at all while <em>enforcementMode</em> lets the evaluation happen without the effect taking place.</p></li>
<li><p>The following table describes this property's values.</p></li>
</ul>
<table style="width:93%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 7%" />
<col style="width: 11%" />
<col style="width: 4%" />
<col style="width: 15%" />
<col style="width: 12%" />
<col style="width: 41%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Mode</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>JSON value</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Type</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Remediate manually</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Activity log entry</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">Enabled</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Default</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">string</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Yes</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Yes</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The policy effect is enforced during resource creation or update.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">Disabled</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">DoNotEnforce</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">string</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Yes</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">No</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">The policy effect isn't enforced during resource creation or update.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>If <em>enforcementMode</em> isn't specified in a policy or initiative definition, the value <em>Default</em> is used. Remediation tasks can be started for <em>deployIfNotExists</em> policies, even when <em>enforcementMode</em> is set to <em>DoNotEnforce</em>.</p>
<p><strong>Policy enforcement and safe deployment best practices</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Without the appropriate knowledge of best practices and proper testing, applying a set of policy to an existing environment that's running production workloads can result in unintended behaviors of policy resources.</p></li>
<li><p>Treating policy as code (keeping your policy definitions in source control, and whenever a change is made, testing and validating that change) allows you to automate testing and make sure that no manual error factor happens.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The best practices framework focuses on minimizing the impact of policy changes while ensuring compliance, and it includes two aspects:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>First aspect</strong> - Start from Assignments of new policies with <em>enforcementMode</em> Disabled.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>When assigning policies that include deny or modify actions, beginning with <em>enforcementMode</em> Disabled allows you to view the compliance state and evaluate policy outcomes without triggering actions or denying operations.</p></li>
<li><p>This "what-if" scenario minimizes impact and helps identify issues in the new policies or changes without disrupting the environment.</p></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p><strong>Second aspect</strong> - Deploy policies in deployment rings.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>To control potential negative impacts, policies should be deployed gradually in smaller subsets and then in bigger sets.</p></li>
<li><p>You can start with test and development environments and then move to production by applying the policy to a small subset first.</p></li>
<li><p>This strategy helps in testing the policy thoroughly.</p></li>
<li><p>Gradually expanding the scope (through deployment rings) can cover the full production environment.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ul>
<p>The following diagram shows how to apply the safe deployment best practices framework for Azure Policy assignments.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image86.webp" style="width:7.416in;height:3.10373in" alt="Screenshot of how to apply the safe deployment best practices framework for Azure Policy assignments." /></p>
<p>The following steps correspond with the outlined steps in the preceding screenshot:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><strong>Create definition</strong> - Begin by defining the policy definition with the scope as root (tenant).</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Create assignment</strong> - Define deployment rings (1 to 5) by using resource selectors. Assign the policy to a specific scope (such as a resource group, subscription, or management group) in Ring 5. Assign with <em>enforcementMode</em> Disabled to evaluate compliance without enforcing changes.</p></li>
<li><p>a) <strong>Compliance check</strong> - Verify that the policy is being applied correctly and that the desired compliance state is achieved for the resources in Ring 5.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>b) <strong>Application health check</strong> - Assess the impact of the policy for the resources in Ring 5. Ensure that no unexpected side effects exist.</p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p><strong>Repeat for each ring (Non-production)</strong> - Repeat step 3 for all nonproduction environment rings.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Update assignment (Optional)</strong> - If necessary, adjust the policy definition or assignment based on the evaluation of resources of the nonproduction environment and then reassign it to the resources in Ring 5 with the <em>enforcementMode</em> Enabled.</p></li>
<li><p>a) <strong>Compliance check</strong> - Reevaluate compliance after making changes (same as step 3a).</p></li>
</ol>
<p>b) <strong>Application health check</strong> - Again, verify that the policy isn't causing issues (same as step 3b).</p>
<ol start="7" type="1">
<li><p><strong>Repeat for each ring (Non-production)</strong> - Repeat step 6 for all nonproduction environment rings.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Repeat for production rings</strong> - After the policy is validated in a nonproduction environment, gradually deploy it to production environments, starting with a smaller subset (ring) and expanding the scope over time.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>For more detailed steps on the safe deployment of Azure Policy assignments with different effects, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/how-to/policy-safe-deployment-practices#steps-for-safe-deployment-of-azure-policy-assignments-with-deny-or-append-effects">Safe deployment of Azure Policy assignments</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Reacting to policy state changes</strong></p>
<p>Azure Policy events allow applications to react to state changes.</p>
<p>This integration is done without the need for complicated code or expensive and inefficient polling services.</p>
<p>Events from Azure Policy (Event Source) are pushed through Microsoft Azure Event Grid to Event Handlers.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image87.webp" style="width:6.43641in;height:5.416in" alt="Screenshot of how Azure Policy events allow applications to react to state changes." /></p>
<p>Azure Event Grid provides reliable delivery services to your applications through rich retry policies and dead-letter delivery. Event Grid takes care of the proper routing, filtering, and multicasting of the events to destinations through Event Grid subscriptions. For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/event-grid/">Azure Event Grid</a>.</p>
<p>An Event Handler is the place where the event is sent. Several services, such as Microsoft Azure Functions, Microsoft Azure Logic Apps, or your own custom HTTP listener, can be configured to handle events. You can also use any webhook for handling events.</p>
<p>For more information, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/event-grid/event-handlers">Event handlers in Azure Event Grid</a>, <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/concepts/event-overview?tabs=event-grid-event-schema">Reacting to Azure Policy state change events</a>, and the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/governance/policy/tutorials/route-state-change-events">Route policy state change events to Event Grid with Azure CLI</a> tutorial.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p>Typical Policy definition and its end state – Steps.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image88.webp" style="width:3.60885in;height:3.4in" /></p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Knowledge Check 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>1. What is the purpose of Azure Policy and how does it help in managing resources?</strong></p>
<p>Azure Policy is used to manually configure resources on a case-by-case basis.</p>
<p><mark>Azure Policy assesses compliance at scale and enforces organizational and regulatory standards across Azure environments.</mark></p>
<p><strong>Correct. Azure Policy assesses compliance at scale and enforces organizational and regulatory standards across Azure environments. Many built-in policies are included, and you can create custom policies. Examples of what you can achieve with Azure Policy include allowing only certain virtual machine sizes for your project, recommending system updates on servers, and allowing multifactor authentication for subscription accounts.</strong></p>
<p>Azure Policy is a networking tool that manages traffic between virtual networks.</p>
<p>Azure Policy is solely for encrypting data at rest in Azure.</p>
<p><strong>2. What are the two recommended steps to deploy policies safely in an existing environment?</strong></p>
<p><mark>Start from assignments with Enforcement Mode Disabled and then deploy policies in deployment rings.</mark></p>
<p><strong>Correct. Starting with Enforcement Mode Disabled and deploying policies in deployment rings ensures a controlled and safe approach to managing policy resources in your existing environment.</strong></p>
<p>Start from assignments with Enforcement Mode Enabled and then deploy policies in deployment rings.</p>
<p>Start from assignments with Enforcement Mode Disabled and then deploy policies in parallel.</p>
<p>Start from assignments with Enforcement Mode Enabled and then deploy policies in parallel.</p>
<p><strong>3. What is the purpose of the Enforcement Mode in Azure Policy?</strong></p>
<p>It prevents resource evaluation from happening at all.</p>
<p>It triggers entries in the Azure Activity log.</p>
<p><mark>It allows testing the policy's outcome on existing resources without initiating the policy effect.</mark></p>
<p><strong>Correct. Enforcement Mode lets you deactivate the enforcement of certain policy effects for testing purposes.</strong></p>
<p><strong>4. What is the role of Azure Resource Manager in the governance of Azure applications and resources?</strong></p>
<p><mark>It's the deployment and management service for Azure, providing a management layer that allows creation, update, and deletion of resources.</mark></p>
<p><strong>Correct. Azure Resource Manager is the deployment and management service for Azure, allowing control over resources.</strong></p>
<p>It's a unit of management, billing, and scale in Azure.</p>
<p>It provides a level of scope above subscriptions.</p>
<p><strong>5. At what levels can Azure Policies be assigned?</strong></p>
<p>Management Group</p>
<p>Management Group and Subscription</p>
<p><mark>Management Group, Subscription, and Resource Group</mark></p>
<p><strong>Correct. Azure Policies can be assigned at the Management Group, Subscription, and Resource Group levels.</strong></p>
<p>Subscription and Resource Group</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

## Secure your Azure resources with Azure role-based access control (Azure RBAC)

| <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/secure-azure-resources-with-rbac/> |
|:---|
|  |

### Introduction

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p>For any organization using the cloud, securing your Azure resources such as virtual machines, websites, networks, and storage is a critical function.</p></li>
<li><p>You want to ensure that your data and assets are protected, but still grant your employees and partners the access they need to perform their jobs.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure RBAC is an authorization system in Azure that helps you manage who has access to Azure resources, what they can do with those resources, and where they have access.</p></li>
<li><p>Suppose you work for First Up Consultants, which is an engineering firm that specializes in circuit and electrical design.</p></li>
<li><p>They've moved their workloads and assets to Azure to make collaboration easier across several offices and other companies.</p></li>
<li><p>You work in the IT department at First Up Consultants.</p></li>
<li><p>You're responsible for keeping the company's assets secure, but still allowing users to access the resources they need.</p></li>
<li><p>You've heard that Azure RBAC can help you manage resources in Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>In this module, you'll learn how to use Azure RBAC to manage access to resources in Azure.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### What is Azure RBAC

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>When it comes to identity and access, most organizations that are considering using the public cloud are concerned about two things:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Ensuring that when people leave the organization, they lose access to resources in the cloud.</p></li>
<li><p>Striking the right balance between autonomy and central governance.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, giving project teams the ability to create and manage virtual machines in the cloud, while centrally controlling the networks those VMs use to communicate with other resources.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Microsoft Entra ID and Azure RBAC work together to make it simple to carry out these goals.</p>
<p><strong>Azure subscriptions</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>First, remember that each Azure subscription is associated with a single Microsoft Entra directory.</p></li>
<li><p>Users, groups, and applications in that directory can manage resources in the Azure subscription.</p></li>
<li><p>The subscriptions use Microsoft Entra ID for single sign-on (SSO) and access management.</p></li>
<li><p>You can extend your on-premises Active Directory to the cloud by using <strong>Microsoft Entra Connect</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>This feature allows your employees to manage their Azure subscriptions by using their existing work identities.</p></li>
<li><p>When you disable an on-premises Active Directory account, it automatically loses access to all Azure subscriptions connected with Microsoft Entra ID.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>What's Azure RBAC?</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure RBAC is an authorization system built on Azure Resource Manager that provides fine-grained access management for resources in Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>With Azure RBAC, you can grant the exact access that users need to do their jobs.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, you can use Azure RBAC to let one employee manage virtual machines in a subscription, while another manages SQL databases within the same subscription.</p></li>
<li><p>The following video describes Azure RBAC in detail:</p></li>
<li><p>You can grant access by assigning the appropriate Azure role to users, groups, and applications at a certain scope.</p></li>
<li><p><u>The scope of a role assignment can be a management group, subscription, a resource group, or a single resource.</u></p></li>
<li><p>A role assigned at a parent scope also grants access to the child scopes contained within it.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, a user with access to a resource group can manage all the resources it contains like websites, virtual machines, and subnets.</p></li>
<li><p>The Azure role that you assign dictates what resources the user, group, or application can manage within that scope.</p></li>
<li><p>The following diagram depicts how the classic subscription administrator roles, Azure roles, and Microsoft Entra roles are related at a high level.</p></li>
<li><p>Child scopes, such as service instances, inherit roles assigned at a higher scope, like an entire subscription.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image89.webp" style="width:7.5in;height:5.03125in" alt="Diagram that depicts how the classic subscription administrator roles, Azure roles, and Microsoft Entra roles are related at a high level." /></p>
<ul>
<li><p>In the preceding diagram, a subscription is associated with only one Microsoft Entra tenant.</p></li>
<li><p>Also note that a resource group can have multiple resources, but it's associated with only one subscription.</p></li>
<li><p>Although it's not obvious from the diagram, a resource can be bound to only one resource group.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>What can I do with Azure RBAC?</strong></p>
<p>Azure RBAC allows you to grant access to Azure resources that you control. Suppose you need to manage access to resources in Azure for the development, engineering, and marketing teams. You’ve started to receive access requests, and you need to quickly learn how access management works for Azure resources.</p>
<p>Here are some scenarios you can implement with Azure RBAC:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Allow one user to manage virtual machines in a subscription and another user to manage virtual networks.</p></li>
<li><p>Allow a database administrator group to manage SQL databases in a subscription.</p></li>
<li><p>Allow a user to manage all resources in a resource group, such as virtual machines, websites, and subnets.</p></li>
<li><p>Allow an application to access all resources in a resource group.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Azure RBAC in the Azure portal</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>In several areas in the Azure portal, you'll see a pane named <strong>Access control (IAM)</strong>, also known as <em>identity and access management</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>On this pane, you can see who has access to that area and their role.</p></li>
<li><p>Using this same pane, you can grant or remove access.</p></li>
<li><p>The following shows an example of the Access control (IAM) pane for a resource group.</p></li>
<li><p>In this example, Alain has been assigned the Backup Operator role for this resource group.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image90.webp" style="width:6.7429in;height:4.424in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure portal showing the Access control Role assignment pane with the Backup operator section highlighted." /></p>
<p>Below is another sample of a VM with role-assignments, where the role can be seen as ‘owner’</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image91.webp" style="width:6.9197in;height:3.648in" /></p>
<p>Various Roles and their permissions can be tabulated as below.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image92.webp" style="width:3.9675in;height:4.22412in" /></p>
<p><strong>How does Azure RBAC work?</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>You can control access to resources using Azure RBAC by creating role assignments, which control how permissions are enforced.</p></li>
<li><p>To create a role assignment, you need three elements: a security principal, a role definition, and a scope.</p></li>
<li><p>You can think of these elements as <em>who</em>, <em>what</em>, and <em>where</em>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>1. Security principal (who)</strong></p>
<p>A <em>security principal</em> is just a fancy name for a user, group, or application to which you want to grant access.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image93.webp" style="width:2.92708in;height:1.10417in" alt="An illustration showing security principal including user, group, and service principal." /></p>
<p><strong>2. Role definition (what)</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>A <em>role definition</em> is a collection of permissions.</p></li>
<li><p>It's sometimes just called a role.</p></li>
<li><p>A role definition lists the permissions the role can perform such as read, write, and delete.</p></li>
<li><p>Roles can be high-level, like Owner, or specific, like Virtual Machine Contributor.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image94.webp" style="width:4.5in;height:2.98958in" alt="An illustration listing different built-in and custom roles with zoom-in on the definition for the contributor role." /></p>
<p>Azure includes several built-in roles that you can use. The following lists <strong>four</strong> fundamental built-in roles:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Owner</strong>: Has full access to all resources, including the right to delegate access to others.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Contributor</strong>: Can create and manage all types of Azure resources, but can’t grant access to others.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Reader</strong>: Can view existing Azure resources.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>User Access Administrator</strong>: Lets you manage user access to Azure resources.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>If the built-in roles don't meet the specific needs of your organization, you can create your own custom roles.</p>
<p><strong>3. Scope (where)</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p><em>Scope</em> is the level where the access applies.</p></li>
<li><p>This is helpful if you want to make someone a Website Contributor but only for one resource group.</p></li>
<li><p>In Azure, you can specify a scope at multiple levels: management group, subscription, resource group, or resource.</p></li>
<li><p>Scopes are structured in a parent-child relationship.</p></li>
<li><p>When you grant access at a parent scope, the child scopes automatically inherit those permissions.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, if a group is assigned the Contributor role at the subscription scope, it will inherit the role for all resource groups and resources within the subscription.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image95.webp" style="width:2.08333in;height:2.25in" alt="An illustration showing a hierarchical representation of different Azure levels to apply scope. The hierarchy, starting with the highest level, is in this order: Management group, subscription, resource group, and resource." /></p>
<p><strong>Role assignment</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Once you have determined the who, what, and where, you can combine those elements to grant access.</p></li>
<li><p>A <em>role assignment</em> is the process of binding a role to a security principal at a particular scope for the purpose of granting access.</p></li>
<li><p>To grant access, you'll create a role assignment.</p></li>
<li><p>To revoke access, you'll remove a role assignment.</p></li>
<li><p>The following example shows how the Marketing group has been assigned the Contributor role at the sales resource group scope.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image96.webp" style="width:6.98387in;height:3.432in" alt="An illustration showing a sample role assignment process for Marketing group, which is a combination of security principal, role definition, and scope. The Marketing group falls under the Group security principal and has a Contributor role assigned for the Resource group scope." /></p>
<p><strong>Azure RBAC is an allow model</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure RBAC is an <em>allow</em> model.</p></li>
<li><p>This means that when you're assigned a role, Azure RBAC allows you to perform certain actions such as read, write, or delete.</p></li>
<li><p>If one role assignment grants you read permissions to a resource group, and a different role assignment grants you write permissions to the same resource group, then you'll have read and write permissions on that resource group.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure RBAC has something called NotActions permissions.</p></li>
<li><p>You can use NotActions to create a set of not allowed permissions.</p></li>
<li><p>The access a role grants—the <em>effective permissions</em>—is computed by subtracting the NotActions operations from the Actions operations.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/role-based-access-control/built-in-roles#contributor">Contributor</a> role has both Actions and NotActions.</p></li>
<li><p>The wildcard (*) in Actions indicates that it can perform all operations on the control plane.</p></li>
<li><p>You'd then subtract the following operations in NotActions to compute the effective permissions:</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p>Delete roles and role assignments</p></li>
<li><p>Create roles and role assignments</p></li>
<li><p>Grant the caller User Access Administrator access at the tenant scope</p></li>
<li><p>Create or update any blueprint artifacts</p></li>
<li><p>Delete any blueprint artifacts</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Knowledge Check 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>1. What is a role definition in Azure?</strong></p>
<p><mark>A collection of permissions with a name that is assignable to a user, group, or application.</mark></p>
<p><strong>A role definition in Azure is a collection of permissions with a name that you can assign to a user, group, or application.</strong></p>
<p>The collection of users, groups, or applications that have permissions to a role.</p>
<p>The binding of a role to a security principal at a specific scope to grant access.</p>
<p><strong>2. Suppose an administrator wants to assign a role to allow a user to create and manage Azure resources but not be able to grant access to others. Which of the following built-in roles would support this?</strong></p>
<p>Owner</p>
<p><mark>Contributor</mark></p>
<p><strong>A contributor can create and manage all types of Azure resources, but they can't grant access to other users.</strong></p>
<p>Reader</p>
<p>User Access Administrator</p>
<p><strong>3. What is the inheritance order for scope in Azure?</strong></p>
<p>Management group, Resource group, Subscription, Resource</p>
<p><mark>Management group, Subscription, Resource group, Resource</mark></p>
<p><strong>The inheritance order for scope is Management group, Subscription, Resource group, Resource. For example, if you assigned a Contributor role to a group at the Subscription scope level, it'll be inherited by all Resource groups and Resources.</strong></p>
<p>Subscription, Management group, Resource group, Resource</p>
<p>Subscription, Resource group, Management group, Resource</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Exercise: List access using Azure RBAC

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>List role assignments for yourself</strong></p>
<p>Follow these steps to see what roles are currently assigned to you.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Sign in to the <a href="https://portal.azure.com/">Azure portal</a>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Profile</strong> menu, select the ellipsis (<strong>...</strong>) to see more links.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image97.webp" style="width:3.35417in;height:2.9375in" alt="Screenshot of user menu with My permissions highlighted." /></p>
<p><strong><u>My Free Account using Gmail</u></strong></p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image98.webp" style="width:4.9275in;height:3.58412in" /></p>
<p><strong><u>My Corporate Account using Bring Mail</u></strong></p>
<table style="width:97%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 52%" />
<col style="width: 45%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image99.webp" style="width:3.88596in;height:2.94833in" /></th>
<th style="text-align: left;">This is from BringGlobal Account</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" style="text-align: left;"><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image100.webp" style="width:5.7915in;height:2.27998in" /></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Select <strong>My permissions</strong> to open the <strong>My permissions</strong> pane.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image101.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:3.60278in" alt="Screenshot of the My permissions pane." /></p>
<p>You'll find the roles that you've been assigned and the scope. Your list will look different.</p>
<p><strong>List role assignments for a resource group</strong></p>
<p>Follow these steps to see what roles are assigned at the resource group scope.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the Search box at the top, search for and select <strong>Resource groups</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image102.webp" style="width:5.91667in;height:3.08875in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure portal that shows how to search for resource groups." /></p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>In the list of resource groups, select a resource group.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>These steps use a resource group named <strong>example-group</strong>, but your resource group's name will be different.</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>On the left menu pane, select <strong>Access control (IAM)</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image103.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:4.97083in" alt="Screenshot showing Access control (IAM) option on the resource group pane." /></p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>Select the <strong>Role assignments</strong> tab.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>This tab shows who has access to the resource group. Notice that some roles are scoped to <strong>This resource</strong>, while others are <strong>(Inherited)</strong> from a parent scope.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image104.webp" style="width:7.2513in;height:4.05208in" alt="Screenshot showing Role assignments tab for the selected resource group." /></p>
<p>Below is the Bring <strong>Resource Group</strong> example.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image105.webp" style="width:5.61458in;height:3.11363in" /></p>
<p><strong>List roles</strong></p>
<p>As you learned in the previous unit, a role is a collection of permissions. Azure has more than 70 built-in roles that you can use in your role assignments. To list the roles:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>In the menu bar at the top of the pane, select the <strong>Roles</strong> tab to list of all the built-in and custom roles.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Select a role's <strong>View</strong> link in the <strong>Details</strong> column, then select the <strong>Assignments</strong> tab to display the number of users and groups assigned to that role.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image106.webp" style="width:6.04167in;height:3.95137in" alt="Screenshot showing a list of Roles and users and groups assigned to each role." /></p>
<p>In this unit, you learned how to list the role assignments for yourself in the Azure portal. You also learned how to list the role assignments for a resource group.</p></th>

### Exercise: Granting a user Access

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Use case: A coworker named Alain at First Up Consultants needs permission to create and manage virtual machines for a project on which they're working. Your manager has asked that you handle this request. Using the best practice to grant users the least privileges to get their work done, you decide to assign Alain the Virtual Machine Contributor role for a resource group.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Grant access</strong></p>
<p>Follow this procedure to assign the Virtual Machine Contributor role to a user at the resource group scope.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Sign in to the <a href="https://portal.azure.com/">Azure portal</a> as an administrator that has permissions to assign roles, such as <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/role-based-access-control/built-in-roles#user-access-administrator">User Access Administrator</a> or <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/role-based-access-control/built-in-roles#owner">Owner</a>.</p></li>
<li><p>In the Search box at the top, search for <strong>Resource groups</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image102.webp" style="width:7.25in;height:3.7848in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure portal that shows how to search for resource groups." /></p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>In the list of resource groups, select a resource group. These steps use a resource group named <strong>example-group</strong>, but your resource group's name will be different.</p></li>
<li><p>On the left menu pane, select <strong>Access control (IAM)</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the <strong>Role assignments</strong> tab to display the current list of role assignments at this scope.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image107.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:4.29583in" alt="Screenshot showing Role assignments tab for the selected resource group." /></p>
<ol start="6" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Add</strong> &gt; <strong>Add role assignment</strong>. If you don't have permissions to assign roles, the <strong>Add role assignment</strong> option will be disabled.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image108.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:2.40625in" alt="Screenshot that shows Add role assignment menu." /></p>
<p>The <strong>Add role assignment</strong> page opens.</p>
<ol start="7" type="1">
<li><p>On the <strong>Role</strong> tab, search for and select <strong>Virtual Machine Contributor</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image109.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:4.64722in" alt="Screenshot that shows Add role assignment and list of roles." /></p>
<ol start="8" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Next</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Members</strong> tab, select <strong>Select members</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Search for and select a user.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image110.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:4.64722in" alt="Screenshot of the add role assignment page that shows the select members option." /></p>
<ol start="11" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Select</strong> to add the user to the Members list.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Next</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Review + assign</strong> tab, review the role assignment settings.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Review + assign</strong> to assign the role.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>After a few moments, the user is assigned the Virtual Machine Contributor role at the resource group scope. The user can now create and manage virtual machines just within this resource group.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image111.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:4.29722in" alt="Screenshot that shows the Virtual Machine Contributor role assigned to a user." /></p>
<p><strong>Remove access</strong></p>
<p>In Azure RBAC, you can remove a role assignment to remove access.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the list of role assignments, select <strong>View Assignments</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Search for and check the box for the user with the Virtual Machine Contributor role.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Delete</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image112.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:4.66458in" alt="Screenshot that shows the Remove role assignment message." /></p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>In the <strong>Remove role assignments</strong> message that appears, select <strong>Yes</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>In this unit, you learned how to grant a user access to create and manage virtual machines in a resource group using the Azure portal.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>

<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<col style="width: 99%" />

You know that changes get logged in the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-monitor/essentials/activity-log">Azure Activity Log</a>. Your manager has asked if you can generate a report of the role assignment and custom role changes for the last month.</p>

</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image113.webp" style="width:6.99506in;height:2.40625in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure portal showing the location of Activity logs option." /></p>

<ol start="3" type="1">

<p>After a moment, you'll get a list of all the role assignment and role definition operations for the last month. There's also a button at the top of the screen to download the activity log as a CSV file.</p>
<ol start="6" type="1">

</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image116.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:2.78542in" alt="Screenshot showing the details for an activity log." /></p>
<p>In this unit, you learned how to use Azure Activity Log to list Azure RBAC changes in the portal and generate a simple report.</p></th>

<p><mark>At the resource scope, assign the role with the appropriate access.</mark></p>

<p>Resource</p>

<p>At the appropriate scope, go to <strong>Access control (IAM)</strong> &gt; <strong>Download role assignments</strong>.</p>
