## Allow users to reset their password with Microsoft Entra self-service password reset

| <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/allow-users-reset-their-password/> |
|:---|
|  |

### Introduction

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p>Suppose you're an IT administrator for a large retail organization.</p></li>
<li><p>Your organization starts using Microsoft Entra ID to allow employees to securely sign in and use software as a service (SaaS) apps.</p></li>
<li><p>It also allows access to the organization's resources in Microsoft 365.</p></li>
<li><p>You're overwhelmed with password-reset requests because you currently reset employees' passwords manually.</p></li>
<li><p>To get these employees back to being productive quickly and reduce your workload, you decide to evaluate and set up self-service password reset in Microsoft Entra ID.</p></li>
<li><p>In this module, you learn how Azure supports this feature and how to set it up.</p></li>
<li><p>Only paid subscriptions can leverage this, while free and pay-as-you-go can't.</p></li>
<li><p>By the end of this module, you'll be able to configure self-service password reset in Microsoft Entra ID.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### What is SSPR

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p>You've been asked to assess ways to reduce help-desk costs in your retail organization.</p></li>
<li><p>You've noticed that the support staff spends a lot of their time resetting passwords for users.</p></li>
<li><p>Users often complain about delays with this process, and these delays impact their productivity.</p></li>
<li><p>You want to understand how you can configure Azure to allow users to manage their own passwords.</p></li>
<li><p>In this unit, you'll learn how self-service password reset (SSPR) works in Microsoft Entra ID.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Why use SSPR?</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>In Microsoft Entra ID, any user can change their password if they're already signed in.</p></li>
<li><p>But if they're not signed in, forgot their password, or it's expired, they'll need to reset their password.</p></li>
<li><p>With SSPR, users can reset their passwords in a web browser or from a Windows sign-in screen to regain access to Azure, Microsoft 365, and any other application that uses Microsoft Entra ID for authentication.</p></li>
<li><p>SSPR reduces the load on administrators because users can fix password problems themselves without having to call the help desk.</p></li>
<li><p>Also, it minimizes the productivity impact of a forgotten or expired password.</p></li>
<li><p>Users don't have to wait until an administrator is available to reset their password.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>How SSPR works</strong></p>
<p>The user initiates a password reset either by going directly to the password-reset portal, or by selecting the <strong>Can't access your account</strong> link on a sign-in page. The reset portal takes these steps:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><strong>Localization</strong>: The portal checks the browser's locale setting and renders the SSPR page in the appropriate language.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Verification</strong>: The user enters their username and passes a CAPTCHA to ensure that it's a user and not a bot.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Authentication</strong>: The user enters the required data to authenticate their identity. They might enter a code or answer security questions.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Password reset</strong>: If the user passes the authentication tests, they can enter a new password and confirm it.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Notification</strong>: A message is sent to the user to confirm the reset.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>There are several ways you can customize the SSPR user experience. For example, you can add your company logo to the sign-in page so users know they're in the right place to reset their password.</p>
<p><strong>Authenticate a password reset</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>It's critical to verify a user's identity before you allow a password reset.</p></li>
<li><p>Malicious users might exploit any weakness in the system to impersonate that user.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure supports six different ways to authenticate reset requests.</p></li>
<li><p>As an administrator, you can choose the methods to use when you configure SSPR.</p></li>
<li><p>Enable two or more of these methods so that users can choose the ones they can easily use.</p></li>
<li><p>The methods are:</p></li>
</ul>
<table style="width:95%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 14%" />
<col style="width: 39%" />
<col style="width: 41%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Authentication method</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>How to register</strong></th>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>How to authenticate for a password reset</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">Mobile app notification</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Install the Microsoft Authenticator app on your mobile device, then register it on the multifactor authentication setup page.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Azure sends a notification to the app, which you can either verify or deny.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">Mobile app code</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">This method also uses the Authenticator app, and you install and register it in the same way.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Enter the code from the app.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">Email</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Provide an email address that's external to Azure and Microsoft 365.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Azure sends a code to the address, which you enter in the reset wizard.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">Mobile phone</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Provide a mobile phone number.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Azure sends a code to the phone in an SMS message, which you enter in the reset wizard. You can also choose to get an automated call.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">Office phone</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Provide a nonmobile phone number.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">You receive an automated call to this number and press #.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">Security questions</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Select questions such as "In what city was your mother born?" and save their responses.</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Answer the questions.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>In trial Microsoft Entra organizations, phone call options aren't supported.</p>
<p><strong>Require the minimum number of authentication methods</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>You can specify the minimum number of methods that the user must set up, either one or two.</p></li>
<li><p>For example, you might enable the mobile app code, email, office phone, and security questions methods and specify a minimum of two methods.</p></li>
<li><p>Users can then choose the two methods they prefer, like mobile app code and email.</p></li>
<li><p>For the security-question method, you can specify a minimum number of questions the user must set up to register for this method.</p></li>
<li><p>You also can specify a minimum number of questions they must answer correctly to reset their password.</p></li>
<li><p>After your users register the required information for the minimum number of methods you've specified, they're considered registered for SSPR.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Recommendations</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Enable <strong>two or more</strong> of the authentication reset request methods.</p></li>
<li><p>Use the mobile app notification or code as the primary method.</p></li>
<li><p>But also enable the email or office phone methods to support users without mobile devices.</p></li>
<li><p>The mobile phone method isn't a recommended method, because it's possible to send fraudulent SMS messages.</p></li>
<li><p>The security-question option is the least recommended method, because the answers to the security questions might be known to other people.</p></li>
<li><p>Only use the security-question method in combination with at least one other method.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Accounts associated with administrator roles</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>A strong, two-method authentication policy is always applied to accounts with an administrator role, regardless of your configuration for other users.</p></li>
<li><p>The security-question method isn't available to accounts associated with an administrator role.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Configure notifications</strong></p>
<p>Administrators can choose how users are notified of password changes. There are two options you can enable:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Notify users on password resets</strong>: The user who resets their own password is notified to their primary and secondary email addresses. If the reset was done by a malicious user, this notification alerts the user, who can take mitigation steps.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Notify all admins when other admins reset their password</strong>: All administrators are notified when another administrator resets their password.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>License requirements</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>There are two editions of Microsoft Entra ID, Premium P1 and Premium P2.</p></li>
<li><p>The password-reset functionality you can use depends on your edition.</p></li>
<li><p>Any user who is signed in can change their password, regardless of the edition of Microsoft Entra ID.</p></li>
<li><p>What if you're not signed in, and you've forgotten your password or your password has expired?</p></li>
<li><p>In this case, you can use SSPR in Microsoft Entra ID P1 or P2.</p></li>
<li><p>It's also available with Microsoft 365 Apps for business or Microsoft 365.</p></li>
<li><p>In a hybrid situation, where you have Active Directory on-premises and Microsoft Entra ID in the cloud, any password change in the cloud must be written back to the on-premises directory.</p></li>
<li><p>This writeback support is available in Microsoft Entra ID P1 or P2.</p></li>
<li><p>It's also available with Microsoft 365 Apps for business.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>SSPR deployment options</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>You can deploy SSPR with password writeback by using <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/identity/authentication/tutorial-enable-sspr-writeback/">Microsoft Entra Connect</a> or <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/identity/authentication/tutorial-enable-cloud-sync-sspr-writeback/">cloud sync</a>, depending on user needs.</p></li>
<li><p>You can deploy each option side-by-side in different domains to target different sets of users.</p></li>
<li><p>This helps existing users on-premises to write back password changes, while adding an option for users in disconnected domains because of a company merger or split.</p></li>
<li><p>Users from an existing on-premises domain can use Microsoft Entra Connect, while new users from a merger can use cloud sync in another domain.</p></li>
<li><p>Cloud sync can also provide higher availability, because it doesn't rely on a single instance of Microsoft Entra Connect. For a feature comparison between the two deployment options, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/identity/hybrid/cloud-sync/what-is-cloud-sync#how-is-azure-ad-connect-cloud-sync-different-from-azure-ad-connect-sync/">Comparison between Microsoft Entra Connect and cloud sync</a>.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Knowledge Check 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>1. When is a user considered registered for SSPR?</strong></p>
<p>They registered at least one of the permitted authentication methods.</p>
<p><mark>They registered at least the number of methods that you've required to reset a password.</mark></p>
<p><strong>A user is considered registered for SSPR when they've registered at least the number of methods that you've required to reset a password. You can set this number in the Azure portal.</strong></p>
<p>They set up the minimum number of security questions.</p>
<p><strong>2. When you enable SSPR for your Microsoft Entra organization...</strong></p>
<p>Users can only change their password when they're signed in.</p>
<p>Admins can reset their password by using one authentication method.</p>
<p><mark>Users can reset their passwords when they can't sign in.</mark></p>
<p><strong>If the user passes the authentication tests, then they can reset their password.</strong></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

### Implement SSPR

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><ul>
<li><p>You've decided to implement <strong>self-service password reset (SSPR)</strong> in Microsoft Entra ID for your organization.</p></li>
<li><p>You want to start using SSPR for a group of 20 users in the marketing department as a trial deployment.</p></li>
<li><p>If everything works well, you'll enable SSPR for your whole organization.</p></li>
<li><p>In this unit, you'll learn how to enable SSPR in Microsoft Entra ID.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Prerequisites</strong></p>
<p>Before you start to configure SSPR, you need a:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Microsoft Entra organization</strong>: This organization must have at least a P1 or P2 trial license enabled.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Microsoft Entra account with Authentication Policy Administrator role</strong>: You'll use this account to set up SSPR.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Non-administrative user account</strong>: You'll use this account to test SSPR. It's important that this account isn't an administrator, because Microsoft Entra imposes extra requirements on administrative accounts for SSPR. This user, and all user accounts, must have a valid license to use SSPR.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Security group with which to test the configuration</strong>: The non-administrative user account must be a member of this group. You'll use this security group to limit who you roll SSPR out to.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Scope of SSPR rollout</strong></p>
<p>There are three settings for the <strong>Self-service password reset enabled</strong> property:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>None</strong>: No users in the Microsoft Entra organization can use SSPR. This value is the default.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Selected</strong>: Only the members of the specified security group can use SSPR. You can use this option to enable SSPR for a targeted group of users who can test it and verify that it works as expected. When you're ready to roll it out broadly, set the property to <strong>Enabled</strong> so that all users have access to SSPR.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>All</strong>: All users in the Microsoft Entra organization can use SSPR.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Configure SSPR</strong></p>
<p><strong>On Personal Azure Portal. On Bring portal it was Access Denied option.</strong></p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image118.webp" style="width:5.61458in;height:3.02335in" /></p>
<p>Here are the high-level steps to configure SSPR:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Go to the <a href="https://portal.azure.com/">Azure portal</a>, then to <strong>Microsoft Entra ID</strong> &gt; <strong>Manage</strong> &gt; <strong>Password reset</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Properties</strong>:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Enable SSPR.</p></li>
<li><p>You can enable it for all users in the Microsoft Entra organization or for selected users.</p></li>
<li><p>To enable for selected users, you must specify the security group. Members of this group can use SSPR.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image119.webp" style="width:7.08333in;height:2.40206in" alt="Screenshot of the Password Reset configuration panel. Properties option is selected allowing user to enable self service password resets." /></p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p><strong>Authentication methods</strong>:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Choose whether to require one or two authentication methods.</p></li>
<li><p>Choose the authentication methods that the users can use.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image120.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:4.97083in" alt="Screenshot of the Password Reset panel&#39;s Authentication methods option selected displaying panel with authentication options." /></p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p><strong>Registration</strong>:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Specify whether users are required to register for SSPR when they next sign in.</p></li>
<li><p>Specify how often users are asked to reconfirm their authentication information.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image121.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:2.58333in" alt="Screenshot of the Password Reset panel&#39;s Registration option selected displaying panel with registration options." /></p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p><strong>Notifications</strong>: Choose whether to notify users and administrators of password resets.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image122.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:3.77778in" alt="Screenshot of the Password Reset panel&#39;s Notification option selected displaying panel with notification options." /></p>
<ol start="6" type="1">
<li><p><strong>Customization</strong>: Provide an email address or web page URL where your users can get help.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image123.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:2.58403in" alt="Screenshot of the Password Reset panel&#39;s Customization option selected displaying panel with helpdesk options." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<th style="text-align: left;"><p>In this unit, you'll configure and test self-service password reset (SSPR) by using your email. You'll need to use your email to complete the password-reset process in this exercise.</p>
<p><strong>Create a group</strong></p>
<p>You want to roll out SSPR to a limited set of users first to make sure your SSPR configuration works as expected. Let's begin by creating a security group for the limited rollout.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the Microsoft Entra organization you created, under <strong>Manage</strong>, select <strong>Groups</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>New Group</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Enter the following values:</p></li>

<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image124.webp" style="width:4.3195in;height:3.8298in" alt="Screenshot that shows new group form filled out and the create button highlighted." /></p>
<p><strong>Create a user account</strong></p>
<p>To test your configuration, create an account that's not associated with an administrator role. You'll also assign the account to the group you created.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In your Microsoft Entra organization, under <strong>Manage</strong>, select <strong>Users</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>+ New user</strong>, select <strong>Create new user</strong> in the drop-down, and use the following values:</p></li>
</ol>

</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p>Password</p></td>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p>Select the <strong>Copy</strong> icon next to the autogenerated password, then paste the password to a text editor like Notepad.</p>

<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>Select the <strong>Assignments</strong> tab.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Add group</strong>, check the box for the <strong>SSPRTesters</strong> group, and then the <strong>Select</strong> button.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Review + create</strong> and then select <strong>Create</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Enable SSPR</strong></p>
<p>Now, you're ready to enable SSPR for the group.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In your Microsoft Entra organization, under <strong>Manage</strong>, select <strong>Password reset</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Properties</strong> page, select <strong>Selected</strong>. Select the link under <strong>Select Group</strong>, select the box next to the <strong>SSPRTesters</strong> group, and then the <strong>Select</strong> button.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Save</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image125.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:2.67569in" alt="Screenshot of the Password Reset properties panel wwith SSPR enabled and selected group set to SSPRTesters." /></p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>Under <strong>Manage</strong>, select the <strong>Authentication methods</strong>, <strong>Registration</strong>, and <strong>Notifications</strong> pages to review the default values. Ensure <strong>Authentication methods</strong> has <strong>Email</strong> selected.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Customization</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Yes</strong>, and then in the <strong>Custom helpdesk email or URL</strong> text box, enter <strong>admin@organization-domain-name.onmicrosoft.com</strong>. Replace "organization-domain-name" with the domain name of the Microsoft Entra organization you created. If you've forgotten the domain name, hover over your profile in the Azure portal.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Save</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Register for SSPR</strong></p>
<p>Now that the SSPR configuration is complete, register an email for the user you created.</p>
<p><strong> Note</strong></p>
<p>If you get a message that says "The administrator has not enabled this feature," use private/incognito mode in your web browser.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In a new browser window, go to <a href="https://aka.ms/ssprsetup">https://aka.ms/ssprsetup</a>.</p></li>
<li><p>Sign in with the user name <strong>balas@organization-domain-name.onmicrosoft.com</strong> and the password that you noted earlier. Remember to replace "organization-domain-name" with the domain name of the Microsoft Entra organization you created.</p></li>
<li><p>If you're asked to update your password, enter a new password of your choice. Make sure you note the new password.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the <strong>Security info</strong> tab, and then select <strong>+ Add sign-in method</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Add a method</strong> box, select <strong>Email</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Enter your email details.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image126.webp" style="width:7.6875in;height:2.39792in" alt="Screenshot that shows mobile phone registration form for SSPR." /></p>
<ol start="7" type="1">
<li><p>When you receive the code in your email, enter the code in the text box and select <strong>Next</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Test SSPR</strong></p>
<p>Now, let's test whether the user can reset their password.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In a new browser window, go to <a href="https://aka.ms/sspr">https://aka.ms/sspr</a>.</p></li>
<li><p>For <strong>User ID</strong>, type <strong>balas@organization-domain-name.onmicrosoft.com</strong>. Replace "organization-domain-name" with the domain you used for your Microsoft Entra organization.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image127.webp" style="width:4.1435in;height:3.80775in" alt="Screenshot that shows the password reset dialog." /></p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>Complete the CAPTCHA and select <strong>Next</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>The <strong>Email my alternate email</strong> radio button is selected. Select <strong>Email</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>When the email arrives, in the <strong>Enter your verification code</strong> text box, enter the code you were sent. Select <strong>Next</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Enter a new password, and then select <strong>Finish</strong>. Make sure you note the new password.</p></li>
<li><p>Close the browser window.</p></li>
</ol></th>
</tr>

### Exercise: Customize directory branding

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>Suppose you've been asked to display your retail organization's branding on the Azure sign-in page to reassure users that they're passing credentials to a legitimate system. Here, you'll learn how to configure this custom branding.</p>
<p>To complete this exercise, you must have two image files:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>A page background image. This must be a PNG or JPG file, 1920 x 1080 pixels, and smaller than 300 KB.</p></li>
<li><p>A company logo image. This must be a PNG or JPG file, 32 x 32 pixels, and smaller than 5 KB.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Customize Microsoft Entra organization branding</strong></p>
<p>Let's use Microsoft Entra ID to set up the custom branding.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Sign in to the <a href="https://portal.azure.com/">Azure portal</a>.</p></li>
<li><p>Go to your Microsoft Entra organization by selecting <strong>Microsoft Entra ID</strong>. If you're not in the right Microsoft Entra organization, go to your Azure profile, and select <strong>Switch directory</strong> to find your organization.</p></li>
<li><p>Under <strong>Manage</strong>, select <strong>Company branding</strong>. Then select the <strong>Customize</strong> button in the center of the screen.</p></li>
<li><p>Next to <strong>Favicon</strong>, select <strong>Browse</strong>. Select your logo image.</p></li>
<li><p>Next to <strong>Background image</strong>, select <strong>Browse</strong>. Select your page background image.</p></li>
<li><p>Select a <strong>Page background color</strong> or accept the default.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image128.webp" style="width:4.72458in;height:5.30672in" alt="Screenshot that shows the configure company branding form." /></p>
<ol start="7" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Review + Create</strong>, and then select <strong>Create</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Test the organization's branding</strong></p>
<p>Now, let's use the account that we created in the last exercise to test the branding.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In a new browser window, go to <a href="https://login.microsoft.com/">https://login.microsoft.com</a>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the account for <strong>Bala Sandhu</strong>. Your custom branding is displayed.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image129.webp" style="width:3.3835in;height:2.42805in" alt="Screenshot that shows the customized sign-in page." /></p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Forgot my password</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image130.webp" style="width:4.22641in;height:3.85875in" alt="Screenshot that shows organization logo on password reset page." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>

# Chapter 3: Configure and manage VNETs

## Configure Virtual Networks

| <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/configure-virtual-networks/> |
|:---|
|  |

### Introduction 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p><mark>Azure virtual networks are an essential component for creating private networks in Azure. They allow different Azure resources to securely communicate with each other, the internet, and on-premises networks.</mark></p>
<p><mark>Suppose you work for a company in the healthcare industry. Your company is looking to migrate their on-premises infrastructure to Azure. They want to ensure secure communication between their resources in Azure and their on-premises network. The company is also concerned about scalability and availability. By using Azure virtual networks, they can create a private network in Azure and securely connect their resources.</mark></p>
<p><mark>The topics covered in this module include subnetting, creating virtual networks, and using private and public IP addresses. You also learn about the different scenarios where virtual networks can be used, such as creating a dedicated private cloud-only network or extending a data center securely. The module provides a detailed explanation of subnets and their benefits, and how to plan IP addressing for Azure resources.</mark></p>
<p><mark>By the end of this module, you have a clear understanding of how to create and configure virtual networks in Azure. You're able to effectively use subnets, assign IP addresses, and ensure secure communication between your Azure resources and on-premises network.</mark></p>
<p><strong><mark>Learning objectives</mark></strong></p>
<p><mark>In this module, you learn how to:</mark></p>
<ul>
<li><p><mark>Describe Azure virtual network features and components.</mark></p></li>
<li><p><mark>Identify features and usage cases for subnets and subnetting.</mark></p></li>
<li><p><mark>Identify usage cases for private and public IP addresses.</mark></p></li>
<li><p><mark>Create a virtual network and assign an IP address.</mark></p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p><strong><mark>Skills measured</mark></strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p><mark>The content in the module helps you prepare for <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/credentials/certifications/resources/study-guides/az-104">Exam AZ-104: Microsoft Azure Administrator</a>.</mark></p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong><mark>Prerequisites</mark></strong></p></li>
<li><p><mark>Basic knowledge of virtual networking in cloud environments.</mark></p></li>
<li><p><mark>Familiarity with IP address formats and subnetting.</mark></p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

## Azure CLI

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>What is the Azure CLI?</strong></p>
<p>The Azure CLI is Microsoft's cross-platform command-line tool for managing Azure resources. It's available for macOS, Linux, and Windows, or in the browser using <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/cloud-shell/overview">Azure Cloud Shell</a>.</p>
<p>The Azure CLI can help you manage Azure resources such as virtual machines and disks from the command line or in scripts. Let's get started and see what it can do with Azure Virtual Machines.</p>
<p><strong>Learning objectives</strong></p>
<p>In this module, you will:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create a virtual machine with the Azure CLI.</p></li>
<li><p>Resize virtual machines with the Azure CLI.</p></li>
<li><p>Perform basic management tasks using the Azure CLI.</p></li>
<li><p>Connect to a running VM with SSH and the Azure CLI.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Create VM

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>While the Azure portal is easy to use for one-off tasks, navigating through the various panes adds time when you have to create, change, or delete multiple things. This is where the command line shines; you can issue commands quickly and efficiently, or even use scripts to run repetitive tasks. With Azure, you have two different command-line tools you can work with: Azure PowerShell and the Azure CLI.</p>
<p>With either of these tools, you can write scripts to check the cloud-server status, deploy new configurations, open ports in the firewall, or connect to a virtual machine to change a setting. Windows admins tend to prefer Azure PowerShell, while developers and Linux admins often use the Azure CLI.</p>
<p>This module focuses on using the Azure CLI to create and manage virtual machines hosted in Azure. If you'd like to get an overview of the Azure CLI, how to install it, and how to work with your Azure subscriptions, make sure to check out the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/control-azure-services-with-cli/">Control Azure services with the CLI</a> training module.</p>
<p><strong>Exercise - Create a virtual machine</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>10 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Sandbox activated! Time remaining:</p>
<p>You have used 4 of 10 sandboxes for today. More sandboxes will be available tomorrow.</p>
<p>Let's start with the most obvious task: creating an Azure Virtual Machine.</p>
<p><strong>Logins, subscriptions, and resource groups</strong></p>
<p>You'll be working in the Azure Cloud Shell on the right. Once you activate the sandbox, you'll be logged into Azure with a free subscription that Microsoft Learn manages. You don't have to log in to Azure on your own or select a subscription; this is done for you. You'd also normally create a <em>resource group</em> to hold new resources. In this module, the Azure sandbox creates a resource group for you, which you'll use to execute all the commands.</p>
<p><strong>Create a Linux VM with the Azure CLI</strong></p>
<p>The Azure CLI includes the vm command to work with virtual machines in Azure. We can supply several subcommands to do specific tasks. The most common include:</p>
<table style="width:56%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 12%" />
<col style="width: 43%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Sub-command</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>create</td>
<td>Create a new virtual machine</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>deallocate</td>
<td>Deallocate a virtual machine</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>delete</td>
<td>Delete a virtual machine</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>list</td>
<td>List the created virtual machines in your subscription</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>open-port</td>
<td>Open a specific network port for inbound traffic</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>restart</td>
<td>Restart a virtual machine</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>show</td>
<td>Get the details for a virtual machine</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>start</td>
<td>Start a stopped virtual machine</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>stop</td>
<td>Stop a running virtual machine</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>update</td>
<td>Update a property of a virtual machine</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Note</p>
<p>For a complete list of commands, you can check the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cli/azure/reference-index">Azure CLI reference documentation</a>.</p>
<p>Let's start with the first one: az vm create. You can use this command to create a virtual machine in a resource group. There are several parameters you can pass to configure all the aspects of the new VM. The four parameters that you must supply are:</p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 14%" />
<col style="width: 83%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Parameter</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>--resource-group</td>
<td>The resource group that will own the virtual machine; use <strong>learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5</strong>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>--name</td>
<td>The name of the virtual machine; must be unique within the resource group.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>--image</td>
<td>The operating system image to use to create the VM.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>--location</td>
<td>The region in which to place the VM. Typically, this would be close to the VM's consumer.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>In addition, it's helpful to add the --verbose flag to see progress while the VM is being created.</p>
<p><strong>Create a Linux virtual machine</strong></p>
<p>Let's create a new Linux virtual machine. Execute the following command in Azure Cloud Shell to create an Ubuntu VM in the <em>West US</em> location.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm create \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5" \</p>
<p>--location westus \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM \</p>
<p>--image Ubuntu2204 \</p>
<p>--admin-username azureuser \</p>
<p>--generate-ssh-keys \</p>
<p>--verbose</p>
<p>Tip</p>
<p>You can use the <strong>Copy</strong> button to copy commands to the clipboard. To paste, right-click on a new line in the Cloud Shell terminal and select <strong>Paste</strong>, or use the Shift+Insert keyboard shortcut (⌘+V on macOS).</p>
<p>This command creates a new <strong>Ubuntu</strong> Linux virtual machine with the name SampleVM. Notice that the Azure CLI tool waits while the VM is being created. You can add the --no-wait option to tell the Azure CLI tool to return immediately and have Azure continue creating the VM in the background. This is useful if you're executing the command in a script.</p>
<p>We're specifying the administrator account name through the --admin-username flag to be azureuser. If you omit this, the az vm create command will use your <em>current user name</em>. Because the rules for account names are different for each OS, it's safer to specify a specific name.</p>
<p>Note</p>
<p>Common names such as "root" and "admin" aren't allowed for most images.</p>
<p>We're also using the generate-ssh-keys flag. Linux distributions use this parameter, and it creates a pair of security keys so we can use the ssh tool to access the virtual machine remotely. The two files are placed into the .ssh folder on your machine and in the VM. If you already have an SSH key named id_rsa in the target folder, then that SSH key will be used rather than generating a new key.</p>
<p>Once Azure CLI finishes creating the VM, you'll get a JSON response which includes the current state of the virtual machine and its public and private IP addresses assigned by Azure:</p>
<p>JSON</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"fqdns": "",</p>
<p>"id": "/subscriptions/aaaa0a0a-bb1b-cc2c-dd3d-eeeeee4e4e4e/resourceGroups/Learn-bbbb1b1b-cc2c-dd3d-ee4e-ffffff5f5f5f/providers/Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachines/SampleVM",</p>
<p>"location": "westus",</p>
<p>"macAddress": "00-0D-3A-58-F8-45",</p>
<p>"powerState": "VM running",</p>
<p>"privateIpAddress": "10.0.0.4",</p>
<p>"publicIpAddress": "40.83.165.85",</p>
<p>"resourceGroup": "bbbb1b1b-cc2c-dd3d-ee4e-ffffff5f5f5f",</p>
<p>"zones": ""</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>Simple VM Creation using the ARM Template</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image131.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.0625in" alt="A screenshot of a computer AI-generated content may be incorrect." /></p>
<p>Connect using the ssh</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image132.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.0625in" alt="A screenshot of a computer AI-generated content may be incorrect." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Explorer VMs – Commands 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Exercise - Explore other VM images</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Sandbox activated! Time remaining:</p>
<p>You have used 4 of 10 sandboxes for today. More sandboxes will be available tomorrow.</p>
<p>We used UbuntuLTS for the image to create the virtual machine. Azure has several standard VM images you can use to create a virtual machine.</p>
<p><strong>Listing images</strong></p>
<p>You can get a list of the available VM images using the following command:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm image list --output table</p>
<p>Note</p>
<p>If you get the error <em>az: command not found</em>, type exit into the shell and try again.</p>
<p>This outputs the most popular images that are part of an offline list built into the Azure CLI. However, there are <em>hundreds</em> of image options available in the Azure Marketplace.</p>
<p><strong>Getting all images</strong></p>
<p>You can get a full list by adding the --all flag to the command. Because the list of images in the Marketplace is very large, it's helpful to filter the list with the --publisher, --sku or –-offer options.</p>
<p>For example, try the following command to see <em>all</em> Wordpress images available:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm image list --sku Wordpress --output table --all</p>
<p>Or this command to see all images provided by Microsoft:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm image list --publisher Microsoft --output table --all</p>
<p>These commands can take a few moments to complete.</p>
<p><strong>Location-specific images</strong></p>
<p>Some images are only available in certain locations. Try adding the --location [location] flag to the command to scope the results to ones available in the region where you want to create the virtual machine. For example, type the following into Azure Cloud Shell to get a list of images available in the eastus region.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm image list --location eastus --output table</p>
<p>Try checking some of the images in the other Azure sandbox available locations:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>westus2</p></li>
<li><p>southcentralus</p></li>
<li><p>centralus</p></li>
<li><p>eastus</p></li>
<li><p>westeurope</p></li>
<li><p>southeastasia</p></li>
<li><p>japaneast</p></li>
<li><p>brazilsouth</p></li>
<li><p>australiasoutheast</p></li>
<li><p>centralindia</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Tip</p>
<p>These are the standard images that are provided by Azure. Keep in mind that you can also <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/linux/tutorial-custom-images">create and upload your own custom images</a> to create VMs based on unique configurations or less common versions or distributions of an operating system.</p>
<p>Your command window is probably full now. If you like, you can type clear to clear the screen.</p>
<p>List images</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image133.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.0625in" alt="A screenshot of a computer screen AI-generated content may be incorrect." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>

## VM sizing 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Predefined VM sizes</strong></p>
<p>When you create a virtual machine, you can supply a <em>VM size</em> value that determines the amount of compute resources devoted to the VM, including CPU, GPU, and memory made available to the virtual machine from Azure.</p>
<p>Azure defines a set of predefined VM sizes for Linux and Windows from which to choose based on the expected usage.</p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 14%" />
<col style="width: 22%" />
<col style="width: 60%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Type</strong></th>
<th><strong>Sizes</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>General purpose</td>
<td>Dsv3, Dv3, DSv2, Dv2, DS, D, Av2, A0-7</td>
<td>Balanced CPU-to-memory. Ideal for dev/test and small to medium applications and data solutions.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Compute optimized</td>
<td>Fs, F</td>
<td>High CPU-to-memory. Good for medium-traffic applications, network appliances, and batch processes.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Memory optimized</td>
<td>Esv3, Ev3, M, GS, G, DSv2, DS, Dv2, D</td>
<td>High memory-to-core. Great for relational databases, medium to large caches, and in-memory analytics.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Storage optimized</td>
<td>Ls</td>
<td>High disk throughput and IO. Ideal for big data, SQL, and NoSQL databases.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>GPU optimized</td>
<td>NV, NC</td>
<td>Specialized VMs targeted for heavy graphic rendering and video editing.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>High performance</td>
<td>H, A8-11</td>
<td>Our most powerful CPU VMs with optional high-throughput network interfaces (RDMA).</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The available sizes change based on the region in which you're creating the VM. You can get a list of the available sizes using the vm list-sizes command. Try typing the following command into Azure Cloud Shell:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm list-sizes --location eastus --output table</p>
<p>Here's an abbreviated response for eastus:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p>MaxDataDiskCount MemoryInMb Name NumberOfCores OsDiskSizeInMb ResourceDiskSizeInMb</p>
<p>------------------ ------------ ---------------------- --------------- ---------------- ----------------------</p>
<p>2 2048 Standard_B1ms 1 1047552 4096</p>
<p>2 1024 Standard_B1s 1 1047552 2048</p>
<p>4 8192 Standard_B2ms 2 1047552 16384</p>
<p>4 4096 Standard_B2s 2 1047552 8192</p>
<p>8 16384 Standard_B4ms 4 1047552 32768</p>
<p>16 32768 Standard_B8ms 8 1047552 65536</p>
<p>4 3584 Standard_DS1_v2 1 1047552 7168</p>
<p>8 7168 Standard_DS2_v2 2 1047552 14336</p>
<p>16 14336 Standard_DS3_v2 4 1047552 28672</p>
<p>32 28672 Standard_DS4_v2 8 1047552 57344</p>
<p>64 57344 Standard_DS5_v2 16 1047552 114688</p>
<p>....</p>
<p>64 3891200 Standard_M128-32ms 128 1047552 4096000</p>
<p>64 3891200 Standard_M128-64ms 128 1047552 4096000</p>
<p>64 3891200 Standard_M128ms 128 1047552 4096000</p>
<p>64 2048000 Standard_M128s 128 1047552 4096000</p>
<p>64 1024000 Standard_M64 64 1047552 8192000</p>
<p>64 1792000 Standard_M64m 64 1047552 8192000</p>
<p>64 2048000 Standard_M128 128 1047552 16384000</p>
<p>64 3891200 Standard_M128m 128 1047552 16384000</p>
<p><strong>Specify a size during VM creation</strong></p>
<p>We didn't specify a size when we created our VM, so Azure selected a default general-purpose size for us. However, we can specify the size as part of the vm create command using the --size parameter. For example, you could use the following command to create a two-core virtual machine:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm create \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5" \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM2 \</p>
<p>--image Ubuntu2204 \</p>
<p>--admin-username azureuser \</p>
<p>--generate-ssh-keys \</p>
<p>--verbose \</p>
<p>--size "Standard_DS2_v2"</p>
<p>Warning</p>
<p>Your subscription tier <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-subscription-service-limits">enforces limits</a> on how many resources you can create, as well as the total size of those resources. Quota limits depend upon your subscription type and region. The Azure CLI lets you know when you exceed this limit with a <strong>Quota Exceeded</strong> error. If you hit this error in your own paid subscription, you can request to raise the limits associated with your paid subscription (up to 10,000 vCPUs) through a <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-resource-manager/resource-manager-quota-errors">free online request</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Resize an existing VM</strong></p>
<p>We can also resize an existing VM if the workload changes or if it was incorrectly sized at creation. Let's use the first VM we created, SampleVM. Before requesting a resize, we must check to see if the desired size is available in the cluster of which our VM is a part. We can use the vm list-vm-resize-options command:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm list-vm-resize-options \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5" \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM \</p>
<p>--output table</p>
<p>This command returns a list of all the possible size configurations available in the resource group. If the size we want isn't available in our cluster but <em>is</em> available in the region, we can <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cli/azure/vm#az-vm-deallocate">deallocate the VM</a>. This command stops the running VM and removes it from the current cluster without losing any resources. We can then resize it, which re-creates the VM in a new cluster where the size configuration is available.</p>
<p>Note</p>
<p>The Microsoft Learn sandbox is limited to a few VM sizes.</p>
<p>To resize a VM, we'll use the vm resize command. For example, perhaps we find our VM is underpowered for the task we want it to perform. We could bump it up to a D2s_v3, where it has 2 vCores and 8 GB of memory. Type this command in Cloud Shell:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm resize \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5" \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM \</p>
<p>--size Standard_D2s_v3</p>
<p>This command takes a few minutes to reduce the resources of the VM, and once it's done, it returns a new JSON configuration.</p>
<p><strong>Exercise - Query system and runtime information about the VM</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>10 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Sandbox activated! Time remaining:</p>
<p>You have used 4 of 10 sandboxes for today. More sandboxes will be available tomorrow.</p>
<p>Now that we've created a virtual machine, we can get information about it through other commands.</p>
<p>Let's start by running vm list.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm list</p>
<p>This command will return <em>all</em> virtual machines defined in this subscription. You can filter the output to a specific resource group through the --resource-group parameter.</p>
<p><strong>Output types</strong></p>
<p>Notice that the default response type for all the commands we've done so far is JSON. This is great for scripting, but most people find it harder to read. You can change the output style for any response through the --output flag. For example, run the following command in Azure Cloud Shell to see the different output style.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm list --output table</p>
<p>Along with table, you can specify json (the default), jsonc (colorized JSON), or tsv (Tab-Separated Values). Try a few variations with the preceding command to see the difference.</p>
<p><strong>Get the IP address</strong></p>
<p>Another useful command is vm list-ip-addresses, which lists the public and private IP addresses for a VM. If they change, or you didn't capture them during creation, you can retrieve them at any time.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm list-ip-addresses -n SampleVM -o table</p>
<p>This returns output like:</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p>VirtualMachine PublicIPAddresses PrivateIPAddresses</p>
<p>---------------- ------------------- --------------------</p>
<p>SampleVM 168.61.54.62 10.0.0.4</p>
<p>Tip</p>
<p>Notice that we're using a shorthand syntax for the --output flag as -o. You can shorten most parameters to Azure CLI commands to a single dash and letter. For example, you can shorten --name to -n and --resource-group to -g. This is handy for entering keyboard characters, but we recommend using the full option name in scripts for clarity. Check the documentation for details about each command.</p>
<p><strong>Get VM details</strong></p>
<p>We can get more detailed information about a specific virtual machine by name or ID running the vm show command.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm show --resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5" --name SampleVM</p>
<p>This returns a fairly large JSON block with all sorts of information about the VM, including attached storage devices, network interfaces, and all of the object IDs for resources that the VM is connected to. Again, we could change to a table format, but that omits almost all of the interesting data. Instead, we can turn to a built-in query language for JSON called <a href="http://jmespath.org/">JMESPath</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Add filters to queries with JMESPath</strong></p>
<p>JMESPath is an industry-standard query language built around JSON objects. The simplest query is to specify an <em>identifier</em> that selects a key in the JSON object.</p>
<p>For example, given the object:</p>
<p>JSON</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"people": [</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"name": "Fred",</p>
<p>"age": 28</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"name": "Barney",</p>
<p>"age": 25</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"name": "Wilma",</p>
<p>"age": 27</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>We can use the query people to return the array of values for the people array. If we just want <em>one</em> of the people, we can use an indexer. For example, people[1] would return:</p>
<p>JSON</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"name": "Barney",</p>
<p>"age": 25</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>We can also add specific qualifiers that would return a subset of the objects based on some criteria. For example, adding the qualifier people[?age &gt; '25'] would return:</p>
<p>JSON</p>
<p>[</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"name": "Fred",</p>
<p>"age": 28</p>
<p>},</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"name": "Wilma",</p>
<p>"age": 27</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>Finally, we can constrain the results by adding a select: people[?age &gt; '25'].[name] that returns just the names:</p>
<p>JSON</p>
<p>[</p>
<p>[</p>
<p>"Fred"</p>
<p>],</p>
<p>[</p>
<p>"Wilma"</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>JMESQuery has several other interesting query features. When you have time, check out the <a href="http://jmespath.org/tutorial.html">online tutorial</a> available on the <a href="http://jmespath.org/">JMESPath.org</a> site.</p>
<p><strong>Filter your Azure CLI queries</strong></p>
<p>With a basic understanding of JMES queries, we can add filters to the data returned by queries like the vm show command. For example, we can retrieve the admin username:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm show \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5" \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM \</p>
<p>--query "osProfile.adminUsername"</p>
<p>We can get the size assigned to our VM:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm show \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5" \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM \</p>
<p>--query hardwareProfile.vmSize</p>
<p>Or, to retrieve all the IDs for your network interfaces, we can run the query:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm show \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5" \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM \</p>
<p>--query "networkProfile.networkInterfaces[].id"</p>
<p>This query technique works with any Azure CLI command, and you can use it to pull specific bits of data out on the command line. It's useful for scripting, as well. For example, you can pull a value out of your Azure account and store it in an environment or script variable. If you decide to use it this way, it's useful to add the --output tsv parameter (which you can shorten to -o tsv). This will return results that only include the actual data values with tab separators.</p>
<p>For example:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm show \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5" \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM \</p>
<p>--query "networkProfile.networkInterfaces[].id" -o tsv</p>
<p>returns the text: /subscriptions/aaaa0a0a-bb1b-cc2c-dd3d-eeeeee4e4e4e/resourceGroups/bbbb1b1b-cc2c-dd3d-ee4e-ffffff5f5f5f/providers/Microsoft.Network/networkInterfaces/SampleVMVMNic</p>
<p>List sizes of vms</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image134.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.0625in" alt="A screenshot of a computer AI-generated content may be incorrect." /></p>
<p>VM listing</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image135.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.0625in" alt="A screenshot of a computer AI-generated content may be incorrect." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Stop / Start VMs

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Stop a VM</strong></p>
<p>We can stop a running VM with the vm stop command. You must pass the name and resource group or the unique ID for the VM:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm stop \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5"</p>
<p>You can verify the VM has stopped by attempting to ping the public IP address, using ssh, or through the vm get-instance-view command. This final approach returns the same basic data as vm show, but includes details about the instance itself. Try entering the following command into Azure Cloud Shell to see the current running state of your VM:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm get-instance-view \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5" \</p>
<p>--query "instanceView.statuses[?starts_with(code, 'PowerState/')].displayStatus" -o tsv</p>
<p>This command should return VM stopped as the result.</p>
<p><strong>Start a VM</strong></p>
<p>We can do the reverse through the vm start command.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm start \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5"</p>
<p>This command starts a stopped VM. You can verify it through the vm get-instance-view query you used in the last section, which should now return VM running.</p>
<p><strong>Restart a VM</strong></p>
<p>Finally, we can restart a VM if we've made changes that require a reboot by running the vm restart command. You can add the --no-wait flag if you want the Azure CLI to return immediately without waiting for the VM to reboot.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Install Software

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Install NGINX web server</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Locate the public IP address of your <em>SampleVM</em> Linux virtual machine.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> az vm list-ip-addresses --name SampleVM --output table</p>
<p> Next, open an ssh connection to <em>SampleVM</em> using the Public IP address from the preceding step.</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> ssh azureuser@&lt;PublicIPAddress&gt;</p>
<p> After you're logged in to the virtual machine, run the following command to install the nginx web server. The command takes a few moments to complete.</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> sudo apt-get -y update &amp;&amp; sudo apt-get -y install nginx</p>
<p> Exit the Secure Shell:</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>exit</p></li>
<li></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Retrieve your default page</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In Azure Cloud Shell, use curl to read the default page from your Linux web server by running the following command, replacing &lt;PublicIPAddress&gt; with the public IP you found previously. You can also open a new browser tab and try to browse to the public IP address.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Bash</p>
<p> curl -m 80 &lt;PublicIPAddress&gt;</p>
<p>This command will fail, because the Linux virtual machine doesn't expose port 80 (http) through the network security group that secures the network connectivity to the virtual machine. We can fix the failure by running the Azure CLI command vm open-port.</p>
<p> Enter the following command into Cloud Shell to open port 80:</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> az vm open-port \</p>
<p>--port 80 \</p>
<p>--resource-group "learn-8533116e-0c23-4daf-a8bc-74bbd37747b5" \</p>
<p>--name SampleVM</p>
<p>It takes a moment to add the network rule and open the port through the firewall.</p>
<p> Run the curl command again.</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>curl -m 80 &lt;PublicIPAddress&gt;</p>
<p>This time, it should return data like the following. You can see the page in a browser as well.</p>
<p>HTML</p>
<p>&lt;!DOCTYPE html&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;html&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;head&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;title&gt;Welcome to nginx!&lt;/title&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;style&gt;</p>
<p>body {</p>
<p>width: 35em;</p>
<p>margin: 0 auto;</p>
<p>font-family: Tahoma, Verdana, Arial, sans-serif;</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>&lt;/style&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;/head&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;body&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;h1&gt;Welcome to nginx!&lt;/h1&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;p&gt;If you see this page, the nginx web server is successfully installed and</p>
<p>working. Further configuration is required.&lt;/p&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;p&gt;For online documentation and support, refer to</p>
<p>&lt;a href="http://nginx.org/"&gt;nginx.org&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;br/&gt;</p>
<p>Commercial support is available at</p>
<p>&lt;a href="http://nginx.com/"&gt;nginx.com&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Thank you for using nginx.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;/body&gt;</p>
<p>&lt;/html&gt;</p>
<p>Ssh to vm</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image136.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.0625in" alt="A screenshot of a computer AI-generated content may be incorrect." /></p>
<p>Install some software</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image137.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.0625in" alt="A screen shot of a computer AI-generated content may be incorrect." /></p>
<p>Open port</p>
