## Check your knowledge

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Check your knowledge</strong></p>
<p>Top of Form</p>
<p>1.</p>
<p>Suppose you're an administrator of several Azure virtual machines. You get a text message indicating some problems with your VMs. You are at a friend's house and only have your tablet with you. True or false: you'll still be able to access the Azure CLI using the tablet, even though you can't install the CLI on it.</p>
<p>True</p>
<p>The Azure Cloud Shell is available in the browser and runs with the full Azure CLI. If you prefer PowerShell, the Azure Cloud Shell has that as well.</p>
<p>False</p>
<p>2.</p>
<p>Suppose you have a script that creates several VMs with different images. When the script issues the command to create the first VM, you don't want to block the script while the VM is created. Instead, you want the script to immediately move on to the next command. What is the best way to do this?</p>
<p>Add the `--async`` argument to your create command.</p>
<p>Use the ampersand (&amp;) to run the process in the background.</p>
<p>Add the `--no-wait`` argument to your create command.</p>
<p>Adding --no-wait`` will cause azure VM create` to return immediately without waiting for the VM to actually be created.</p>
<p>3.</p>
<p>Most Azure commands return JSON by default. Sometimes this dataset can be large, which makes it difficult to read and tricky to use the result of one command as input to another command. What can you use with Azure CLI to filter the results to get only the data that you need?</p>
<p>You can use the --query argument.</p>
<p>All Azure commands support the --query argument, which lets you select the useful data in any Azure command response.</p>
<p>You can use the --filter argument.</p>
<p>Azure CLI doesn't have a --filter argument.</p>
<p>You can pipe the results to a JSON parsing utility and use filtering capability there.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Adding Size to VM 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Learning objectives</strong></p>
<p>In this module, you'll:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create a virtual machine (VM).</p></li>
<li><p>Configure and attach virtual hard drives (VHDs) to an existing VM.</p></li>
<li><p>Determine whether you need premium disks.</p></li>
<li><p>Resize disks for a VM.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Understand storage options for virtual machines (VMs)</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Just like any other computer, virtual machines in Azure use disks as storage for operating systems, applications, and data. These disks are called <em>managed disks</em>.</p>
<p>Suppose you create a virtual machine (VM) in Azure that hosts the database of case histories on which your law firm relies. A well-designed disk configuration is fundamental to good performance and resilience for SQL Server.</p>
<p>In this unit, you learn how to choose the right configuration values for your disks and how to attach those disks to a VM.</p>
<p><strong>How VMs use disks</strong></p>
<p>There are three main disk roles in Azure: the data disk, the OS disk, and the temporary disk. These roles map to disks that are attached to your virtual machine.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>OS Disk</strong>: Every VM includes one disk that stores the operating system. This drive is registered as a SATA drive and labeled as the C: drive in Windows or mounted at "/" in Unix-like operating systems. This disk has a maximum capacity of 4,095 GiB; however, many operating systems are partitioned with <a href="https://wikipedia.org/wiki/Master_boot_record">master boot record (MBR)</a> by default. MBR limits the usable size to 2 TiB. If you need more than 2 TiB, create and attach data disks and use them for data storage. If you need to store data on the OS disk and require the extra space, <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/storage/disk-management/change-an-mbr-disk-into-a-gpt-disk">convert it to GUID Partition Table</a> (GPT). To learn about the differences between MBR and GPT on Windows deployments, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-hardware/manufacture/desktop/windows-and-gpt-faq">Windows and GPT FAQ</a>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Data storage</strong>: A data disk is a managed disk attached to a virtual machine to store application data, or other data you need to persist across reboots. Some VM images include data disks by default. You can also add more data disks up to the maximum number specified by the VM's size. Each data disk is registered as a SCSI drive and has a max capacity of 32,767 GiB. You can choose drive letters or mount points for your data drives.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Temporary storage</strong>: Most VMs contain a temporary disk, which isn't a managed disk. The temporary disk provides short-term storage for applications and processes, and is intended to only store data such as page files, swap files, or SQL Server tempdb. Data on this drive can be lost during a maintenance event or redeployment. The drive is labeled as D: on a Windows VM by default. Don't use this drive to store important data that you don't want to lose.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Attach data disks to VMs</strong></p>
<p>You can add data disks to a virtual machine at any time by attaching them to the VM. Attaching a disk associates the disk with the VM.</p>
<p>A disk can't be deleted while attached to a VM. You must detach the disk from the VM before deleting it.</p>
<p><strong>Attach an existing data disk to an Azure VM</strong></p>
<p>You might already have a VHD that stores the data you want to use in your Azure VM. In our law firm scenario, for example, perhaps you already converted your physical disks to VHDs locally. In this case, you can upload your VHD directly to a managed disk. Generally, you should upload a disk by using the PowerShell Add-AzVhd cmdlet. This cmdlet is optimized for transferring VHD files, and can complete the upload faster than other methods. The transfer is completed by using multiple threads for best performance. Once the VHD is uploaded, attach it to an existing VM as a data disk. This approach is an excellent way to deploy data of all types to Azure VMs. The data is automatically present in the VM, and there's no need to partition or format the new disk.</p>
<p><strong>Attach a new data disk to an Azure VM</strong></p>
<p>You can use the Azure portal to add a new, empty data disk to a VM.</p>
<p>This process creates a <strong>.vhd</strong> file as a page blob in the storage account that you specify, and attaches that .vhd file as a data disk to the VM.</p>
<p>Before you can use the new VHD to store data, you have to initialize, partition, and format the new disk. We'll practice these steps in the next exercise.</p>
<p>In physical on-premises servers, you store data on physical hard disks. You store data in an Azure virtual machine (VM) on virtual hard disks (VHDs). These VHDs are stored as page blobs in Azure storage accounts. For example, when you migrate your law firm's database of case histories to Azure, you must create VHDs where the database files can be saved.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Adding Disk 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>Tip</p>
<p>This exercise uses Linux as the example, but the basic process of creating VMs and adding disks is the same for Windows. The primary difference is in partitioning and formatting the disk. On Windows, you can connect to your VM over Remote Desktop and use the built-in Disk Management tools or deploy a PowerShell script that's similar to the Bash script you'll use here.</p>
<p>Your goal is to create a Linux VM and attach a new virtual hard disk (VHD) named <strong>uploadDataDisk1</strong> to store the /uploads directory.</p>
<p><strong>Set Azure CLI default values</strong></p>
<p>The Azure CLI lets you set default values so you don't have to repeat them each time you run a command.</p>
<p>You can specify the default Azure location, or region. This location is where your Azure VM is placed.</p>
<p>Ideally, this location is close to your clients. In this case, select the closest region to you from the locations available to the Azure sandbox.</p>
<p>The free sandbox allows you to create resources in a subset of the Azure global regions. Select a region from this list when you create resources:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>westus2</p></li>
<li><p>southcentralus</p></li>
<li><p>centralus</p></li>
<li><p>eastus</p></li>
<li><p>westeurope</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p>southeastasia</p></li>
<li><p>japaneast</p></li>
<li><p>brazilsouth</p></li>
<li><p>australiasoutheast</p></li>
<li><p>centralindia</p></li>
</ul>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Run az configure to set the default location you want to use. Replace <strong>eastus</strong> with the location you chose in the previous step.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> az configure --defaults location=eastus</p>
<p>Tip</p>
<p>You can use the <strong>Copy</strong> button to copy commands to the clipboard. To paste, right-click on a new line in the Cloud Shell terminal and select <strong>Paste</strong>, or use the Shift+Insert keyboard shortcut (⌘+V on macOS).</p>
<p> Set the default resource group name to the preconfigured resource group created for you through the Azure sandbox: <strong>learn-a5dff5aa-02db-4429-8719-7c3619f37bd5</strong></p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>az configure --defaults group="learn-a5dff5aa-02db-4429-8719-7c3619f37bd5"</p></li>
<li></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Create a Linux VM</strong></p>
<p>Here, you create a Linux VM to host your web server.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Run this az vm create command to create an Ubuntu Linux VM.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm create \</p>
<p>--name support-web-vm01 \</p>
<p>--image Canonical:UbuntuServer:16.04-LTS:latest \</p>
<p>--size Standard_DS1_v2 \</p>
<p>--admin-username azureuser \</p>
<p>--generate-ssh-keys</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The VM's name is <strong>support-web-vm01</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Its size is <strong>Standard_DS1_v2</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>The admin username is <strong>azureuser</strong>. In practice, this name can be whatever you like.</p></li>
<li><p>The --generate-ssh-keys argument generates an SSH keypair for you, allowing you to connect to your VM over SSH.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The VM takes a few minutes to deploy. When the VM is ready, you get information about it in JSON format. Here's an example:</p>
<p>JSON</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>{</p></li>
<li><p>"fqdns": "",</p></li>
<li><p>"id": "/subscriptions/aaaa0a0a-bb1b-cc2c-dd3d-eeeeee4e4e4e/resourceGroups/aaaa0a0a-bb1b-cc2c-dd3d-eeeeee4e4e4e/providers/Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachines/support-web-vm01",</p></li>
<li><p>"location": "eastus",</p></li>
<li><p>"macAddress": "00-0D-3A-10-63-0A",</p></li>
<li><p>"powerState": "VM running",</p></li>
<li><p>"privateIpAddress": "10.0.0.4",</p></li>
<li><p>"publicIpAddress": "104.211.38.211",</p></li>
<li><p>"resourceGroup": "aaaa0a0a-bb1b-cc2c-dd3d-eeeeee4e4e4e",</p></li>
<li><p>"zones": ""</p></li>
<li><p>}</p></li>
<li><p>Note</p></li>
</ol>
<p>In this lesson, you're using this VM to learn how to manage disks. In practice, you might also install web server and other software and then run az vm open-port to make the ports you need available to the outside world.</p>
<p><strong>Add an empty data disk to your VM</strong></p>
<p>Here, you create an empty data disk and attach it to your VM. Initially, Your data disk is 64 GB in size. Later, you'll mount this disk to the /uploads directory on your VM.</p>
<p>Tip</p>
<p>For learning purposes, you're creating the VM and data disk as separate steps. In practice, you can specify the --data-disk-sizes-gb argument to the az vm create command to add data disks when the VM is created.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Run the following az vm disk attach command to add a new empty disk to the VM.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>az vm disk attach \</p></li>
<li><p>--vm-name support-web-vm01 \</p></li>
<li><p>--name uploadDataDisk1 \</p></li>
<li><p>--size-gb 64 \</p></li>
<li><p>--sku Premium_LRS \</p></li>
<li><p>--new</p></li>
<li><p>This command:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Names the disk <strong>uploadDataDisk1</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Sets its size to 64 GB.</p></li>
<li><p>Specifies the use of premium storage with local redundancy.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ol>
<p>To use the disk, you need to partition and format it. Let's do that next.</p>
<p><strong>Initialize and format your data disk</strong></p>
<p>Your empty data drive needs to be initialized and formatted. The process to do that is the same as for a physical disk.</p>
<p>For one-time tasks, you might manually connect to your VM over SSH and run the commands you need. However, to make the process more repeatable and less error-prone, you can specify your commands in a Bash script or a PowerShell script (if it's available).</p>
<p>Using a script to automate the process has an added benefit: your script serves as documentation for how the process is performed. Others can read your script to understand how the system is configured. If you need to change the process, you can just modify your script and test it on a temporary scratch VM before you deploy your change to production.</p>
<p>To automate the process in this lesson, you can use the <em>Custom Script Extension</em>. The Custom Script Extension is an easy way to download and run scripts on your Azure VMs. It's just one of the many ways you can configure the system after your VM is up and running.</p>
<p>You can store your scripts in Azure storage, or in a public location such as GitHub. You can run scripts manually or as part of a more automated deployment. Here, you run an Azure CLI command to download a premade Bash script from GitHub and execute it on your VM.</p>
<p>For learning purposes, let's also run a few commands on your VM to verify that the VM is configured as you expect.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Run az vm show to get your VM's public IP address and save the IP address as a Bash variable.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> ipaddress=$(az vm show \</p>
<p>--name support-web-vm01 \</p>
<p>--show-details \</p>
<p>--query [publicIps] \</p>
<p>--output tsv)</p>
<p> Run the following ssh command to run the lsblk command on your VM over an SSH connection using the ipaddress variable data you created in the previous step. Recall that azureuser was the admin username we used when we created the VM. If you chose a different name, use that instead. Enter <em>yes</em> when prompted.</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ssh azureuser@$ipaddress lsblk</p>
<p>The output of this command should look like the following.</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> NAME MAJ:MIN RM SIZE RO TYPE MOUNTPOINT</p>
<p>sdb 8:16 0 7G 0 disk</p>
<p>└─sdb1 8:17 0 7G 0 part /mnt</p>
<p>sr0 11:0 1 628K 0 rom</p>
<p>sdc 8:32 0 64G 0 disk</p>
<p>sda 8:0 0 30G 0 disk</p>
<p>├─sda14 8:14 0 4M 0 part</p>
<p>├─sda15 8:15 0 106M 0 part /boot/efi</p>
<p>└─sda1 8:1 0 29.9G 0 part /</p>
<p>Notice that the 64-GB drive (sdc) you created isn't mounted. The drive is listed this way because it isn't initialized yet.</p>
<p> Run the following az vm extension set command to run the premade Bash script on your VM.</p>
<p>Warning</p>
<p>The script modifies /etc/fstab. Improperly modifying the /etc/fstab file could result in an unbootable system. Always test configuration changes on a temporary scratch system before you deploy to production. Refer to your distribution's documentation to learn how to properly modify this file. In production, we also recommend that you create a backup of this file so you can restore the configuration if needed.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> az vm extension set \</p>
<p>--vm-name support-web-vm01 \</p>
<p>--name customScript \</p>
<p>--publisher Microsoft.Azure.Extensions \</p>
<p>--settings '{"fileUris":["https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/mslearn-add-and-size-disks-in-azure-virtual-machines/master/add-data-disk.sh"]}' \</p>
<p>--protected-settings '{"commandToExecute": "./add-data-disk.sh"}'</p>
<p>While the command runs, you can <a href="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/mslearn-add-and-size-disks-in-azure-virtual-machines/master/add-data-disk.sh">examine the Bash script</a> from a separate browser tab, if you'd like.</p>
<p>To summarize, the script:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Partitions the drive /dev/sdc.</p></li>
<li><p>Creates an ext4 filesystem on the drive.</p></li>
<li><p>Creates the /uploads directory, which we use as our mount point.</p></li>
<li><p>Attaches the disk to the mount point.</p></li>
<li><p>Updates /etc/fstab so that the drive is mounted automatically after the system reboots.</p></li>
</ul>
<p> To verify the configuration, run the same ssh command as you did previously to run the lsblk command on your VM over an SSH connection.</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ssh azureuser@$ipaddress lsblk</p>
<p>You should find that sdc/sdc1 is partitioned and mounted to the /uploads directory as you expect.</p>
<p>Output</p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>NAME MAJ:MIN RM SIZE RO TYPE MOUNTPOINT</p></li>
<li><p>sdb 8:16 0 7G 0 disk</p></li>
<li><p>└─sdb1 8:17 0 7G 0 part /mnt</p></li>
<li><p>sr0 11:0 1 628K 0 rom</p></li>
<li><p>sdc 8:32 0 64G 0 disk</p></li>
<li><p>└─sdc1 8:33 0 64G 0 part /uploads</p></li>
<li><p>sda 8:0 0 30G 0 disk</p></li>
<li><p>├─sda14 8:14 0 4M 0 part</p></li>
<li><p>├─sda15 8:15 0 106M 0 part /boot/efi</p></li>
<li><p>└─sda1 8:1 0 29.9G 0 part /</p></li>
<li></li>
</ol>
<p>Tip</p>
<p>Some Linux kernels support TRIM to discard unused blocks on disks. This feature is available on Azure disks and can save you money if you create large files and then delete them. Learn how to <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/linux/attach-disk-portal#trimunmap-support-for-linux-in-azure">turn this feature on</a> in the Azure documentation.</p>
<p><strong>Summary</strong></p>
<p>Here, you created a data disk and attached it to your VM. You used the Custom Script Extension to run a premade Bash script on your VM to make the process more repeatable. The Bash script partitions, formats, and mounts your disk so that your web server can write to it.</p>
<p>Now that the data disk on your VM is prepared, let's explore a bit more about the various types of disks you can create. Your primary decision is whether to choose Standard or Premium storage.</p>
<p>Set defaults</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image139.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.0625in" alt="A screenshot of a computer AI-generated content may be incorrect." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Determine Premium storage

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Determine whether to use premium storage</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>7 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Some applications place greater demands on storage than others. Apps such as Dynamics CRM, Exchange Server, SAP Business Suite, SQL Server, Oracle, and SharePoint require constant high performance and low latency to run at their best.</p>
<p>When creating your VMs or adding new disks, you have a few choices that make a dramatic impact on disk performance, starting with the <em>type</em> of storage you choose.</p>
<p><strong>Types of disks</strong></p>
<p>Azure Disks are designed for 99.999% availability.</p>
<p>There are five types of disks you can choose when creating a managed disk: Ultra disks, Premium SSD v2, Premium SSD, Standard SSD, and Standard HDD storage. Depending on the virtual machine (VM) size, you can mix and match these disk types.</p>
<p><strong>Ultra Disk</strong></p>
<p>Azure Ultra Disks deliver high throughput, high input/output per second (IOPS), and consistent low-latency disk storage for Azure IaaS VMs. Ultra Disks include the ability to dynamically change the performance of the disk without the need to restart your VMs. Ultra disks are suited for data-intensive workloads such as SAP HANA, top tier databases, and transaction-heavy workloads. You can only use Ultra disks as data disks. We recommend using Premium SSDs as OS disks.</p>
<p><strong>Premium SSD v2</strong></p>
<p>Premium SSD v2 offers higher performance than Premium SSDs while also generally being less costly. You can individually tweak the performance (capacity, throughput, and IOPS) of Premium SSD v2 disks at any time, allowing workloads to be cost efficient while meeting shifting performance needs. For example, a transaction-intensive database might need a large amount of IOPS at a small size, or a gaming application might need a large amount of IOPS but only during peak hours. Because of these requirements, for most general-purpose workloads, Premium SSD v2 can provide the best price performance. Premium SSD v2 is suited for a broad range of workloads, including SQL server, Oracle, MariaDB, SAP, Cassandra, Mongo DB, big data analytics, and gaming, on virtual machines or stateful containers.</p>
<p>You can only use Premium SSD v2 disks as data disks, so we recommend using Premium SSDs as OS disks.</p>
<p><strong>Premium SSD</strong></p>
<p>Premium SSD disks have solid-state drives (SSDs) backing them. They deliver high-performance, low-latency disk support for VMs running I/O-intensive workloads. These drives tend to be more reliable because they have no moving parts. A read or write head doesn't have to move to the correct location on a disk to find the requested data.</p>
<p>You can use Premium SSD disks with VM sizes that include an "s" in the series name. For example, there's the <strong>Dv3-Series</strong> and the <strong>Dsv3-series</strong>; you can use the <strong>Dsv3-series</strong> with Premium SSD disks.</p>
<p><strong>Standard SSD</strong></p>
<p>Premium storage is limited to specific VM sizes, so the VM type you create affects the storage capabilities: size, max capacity, and storage type. What if you have a low-end VM, but you need SSD storage for I/O performance? That's what Standard SSDs are for. Standard SSDs are between Standard HDDs and Premium SSDs from a performance and cost perspective.</p>
<p>You can use standard SSDs with any VM size, including VM sizes that don't support premium storage. Using standard SSDs is the only way to use SSDs with those VMs. This disk type is only available in specific regions and only with <em>managed disks</em>.</p>
<p><strong>Standard HDD</strong></p>
<p>Standard HDD disks have traditional hard disk drives (HDDs) backing them. Standard HDD disks are billed at a lower rate than the Premium disks. You can use Standard HDD disks with any VM size.</p>
<p><strong>Disk comparison</strong></p>
<p>The following table provides a comparison of Ultra disk, Premium SSD, Standard SSD, and Standard HDD to help you decide what to use.</p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 10%" />
<col style="width: 22%" />
<col style="width: 22%" />
<col style="width: 14%" />
<col style="width: 15%" />
<col style="width: 12%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th></th>
<th><strong>Ultra disk</strong></th>
<th><strong>Premium SSD v2</strong></th>
<th><strong>Premium SSD</strong></th>
<th><strong>Standard SSD</strong></th>
<th><strong>Standard HDD</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>Disk type</strong></td>
<td>SSD</td>
<td>SSD</td>
<td>SSD</td>
<td>SSD</td>
<td>HDD</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Scenario</strong></td>
<td>IO-intensive workloads such as <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/sap/workloads/hana-vm-operations-storage">SAP HANA</a>, top tier databases (for example, SQL, Oracle), and other transaction-heavy workloads.</td>
<td>Production and performance-sensitive workloads that consistently require low latency and high IOPS and throughput.</td>
<td>Production and performance sensitive workloads.</td>
<td>Web servers, lightly used enterprise applications and dev/test.</td>
<td>Backup data, noncritical, infrequent access.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Max disk size</strong></td>
<td>65,536 GiB</td>
<td>65,536 GiB</td>
<td>32,767 GiB</td>
<td>32,767 GiB</td>
<td>32,767 GiB</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Max throughput</strong></td>
<td>4,000 MB/s</td>
<td>1,200 MB/s</td>
<td>900 MB/s</td>
<td>750 MB/s</td>
<td>500 MB/s</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Max IOPS</strong></td>
<td>160,000</td>
<td>80,000</td>
<td>20,000</td>
<td>6,000</td>
<td>2,000, 3,000*</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Usable as OS Disk?</strong></td>
<td>No</td>
<td>No</td>
<td>Yes</td>
<td>Yes</td>
<td>Yes</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The following sections provide more detail on disk performance.</p>
<p><strong>Data replication</strong></p>
<p>The data in your managed disk is automatically replicated to ensure durability and high availability. Azure Storage replication copies your data to protect it from planned and unplanned events like transient hardware failures, network or power outages, natural disasters, and so on. You can choose to replicate your data within the same data center, across zonal data centers within the same region, and even across regions.</p>
<p>There are two types of replication:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Locally redundant storage (LRS)</strong>: Azure replicates the data within the same Azure data center. The data remains available if a node fails. However, if an entire data center fails, data might be unavailable.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Zone-redundant storage (ZRS)</strong>: Azure replicates your data synchronously across three storage clusters in a single region. Each storage cluster is physically separated from the others and resides in its own availability zone (AZ). With this type of replication, you can still access and manage your data if a zone becomes unavailable.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Disk performance</strong></p>
<p>In most cases, the capacity of a disk determines its performance tier, and the performance tier determines the performance of the disk. However, Ultra Disks and Premium SSD v2 offer the ability to set individual performance characteristics independently of the size of the disk.</p>
<p>There are a few other ways to alter the performance of your disk. You can change the performance tier of a Premium SSD, independent of the size of the disk. Both Premium SSD and Standard SSD offer disk bursting, as well.</p>
<p><strong>Resize virtual machine disks</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>5 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>When you create the VM, it chooses a size for the OS disk. The specific size is based on the image you select. On Linux, it's often around 30 GB, and on Windows it's about 127 GB.</p>
<p>For more storage space, you can add data disks, but you might also want to expand an existing disk. Perhaps a legacy application can't split its data across drives, or you're migrating a physical PC's drive to Azure and need a larger OS drive.</p>
<p>Note</p>
<p>Shrinking managed disks isn't supported. You can only resize a disk to a larger size.</p>
<p>Depending on how large the increase is, increasing the disk's size could also change the disk's performance tier (for example, from P10 to P20). Keep this fact in mind: increasing the disk's size can be beneficial for performance upgrades, but also costs more as you move up the premium tiers.</p>
<p><strong>VM size versus disk size</strong></p>
<p>The VM size you choose when you create your VM determines how many resources it can allocate. For storage, the size controls the number of disks you can add to the VM and the maximum size of each disk.</p>
<p>As mentioned previously, some VM sizes support only Standard storage drives, limiting the I/O performance.</p>
<p>If you find that you need more storage than what your VM size allows for, you can change the VM size. We cover that subject in the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/intro-to-azure-virtual-machines">Introduction to Azure Virtual Machines</a> module.</p>
<p><strong>Expand a disk using the Azure CLI</strong></p>
<p>Warning</p>
<p>Always make sure that you back up your data before performing disk resize operations!</p>
<p>You can't perform operations on VHDs with the VM running. The first step is to stop and deallocate the VM with az vm deallocate, supplying the VM name and resource group name.</p>
<p>When you deallocate a VM, instead of just <em>stopping</em> a VM, it releases the associated computing resources and allows Azure to make configuration changes to the virtualized hardware.</p>
<p>Note</p>
<p>Don't run these commands just yet. You practice this process in the next unit.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm deallocate \</p>
<p>--resource-group &lt;resource-group-name&gt; \</p>
<p>--name &lt;vm-name&gt;</p>
<p>Next, to resize a disk, use az disk update, passing the disk name, resource group name, and newly requested size. When you expand a managed disk, the specified size is mapped to the nearest managed disk size.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az disk update \</p>
<p>--resource-group &lt;resource-group-name&gt; \</p>
<p>--name &lt;disk-name&gt; \</p>
<p>--size-gb 200</p>
<p>Finally, you run az vm start to restart the VM.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm start \</p>
<p>--resource-group &lt;resource-group-name&gt; \</p>
<p>--name &lt;vm-name&gt;</p>
<p><strong>Expanding a disk using the Azure portal</strong></p>
<p>You can also expand a disk through the Azure portal:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>To stop the VM, on the <strong>Overview</strong> page for the VM, select <strong>Stop</strong> in the toolbar.</p></li>
<li><p>In the left menu pane, under <strong>Settings</strong>, select <strong>Disks</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the data disk you want to resize.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image140.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.69792in" alt="Screenshot showing the disks section of a VM with the VHD we want to edit highlighted." /></p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Size + performance</strong> under <strong>Settings</strong>. In the list, select a size <em>larger</em> than the current size. You can also change from Premium to Standard (or vice-versa) here. These settings adjust your performance as shown in the predicted IOPS section.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image141.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.30208in" alt="Screenshot showing the VHD edit screen with the new size field highlighted." /></p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Save</strong> to save the changes.</p></li>
<li><p>Restart the VM.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Expand the partition</strong></p>
<p>Just like adding a new data disk, an expanded disk doesn't add any usable space until you expand the partition and filesystem. You must do this using the OS tools available to the VM.</p>
<p>On Windows, you might use the Disk Manager tool or the diskpart command line tool.</p>
<p>On Linux, you might use parted and resize2fs. You'll do that in the next unit.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Resize the data disk 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Resize the data disk</strong></p>
<p>To resize a disk, you need the disk's ID or name. In this case, you already know the name—*<em>uploadDataDisk1</em>—but in case you didn't, or someone else created the disk, you can run az disk list to find the name.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Run the az disk list command to print the list of the managed disks in the resource group. This list might include other disks if you have multiple VMs in the same resource group.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az disk list \</p>
<p>--query '[*].{Name:name,Gb:diskSizeGb,Tier:sku.tier}' \</p>
<p>--output table</p>
<p>You see the disk named <strong>uploadDataDisk1</strong>.</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> Name Tier</p>
<p>---------------------------------------------------------- -------</p>
<p>support-web-vm01_OsDisk_1_a7c59897dfda42dfab2edf4933e713a6 Premium</p>
<p>uploadDataDisk1 Premium</p>
<p> Run the following az vm deallocate command to stop and deallocate your VM. This command doesn't delete your VM, but puts it in a state where you can modify the virtual disks.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> az vm deallocate --name support-web-vm01</p>
<p> Run the az disk update command to resize the disk to 128 GB.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> az disk update --name uploadDataDisk1 --size-gb 128</p>
<p> Run the az vm start command to restart the VM.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>az vm start --name support-web-vm01</p></li>
<li><p>We aren't finished yet. The operating system on the VM can't use the extra space yet. We handle this situation in the next section.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Expand the disk partition</strong></p>
<p>The final step is to tell the OS about the available space. Just like the partitioning and format steps you did earlier, this process is identical to the one you follow to expand a physical on-premises disk.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Although you can reserve a fixed public IP address for your VM, by default your VM receives a new public IP address when the VM is deallocated and restarted. Run the following az vm show command to update your Bash variable with your VM's new public IP address.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> ipaddress=$(az vm show --name support-web-vm01 -d --query [publicIps] -o tsv)</p>
<p> As you did earlier, run lsblk on your VM over SSH to understand its current state.</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ssh azureuser@$ipaddress lsblk</p>
<p>You can see that disk sdc/sdc1 still has a size of 64 GB.</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> NAME MAJ:MIN RM SIZE RO TYPE MOUNTPOINT</p>
<p>sdb 8:16 0 7G 0 disk</p>
<p>└─sdb1 8:17 0 7G 0 part /mnt</p>
<p>sdc 8:32 0 128G 0 disk</p>
<p>└─sdc1 8:33 0 64G 0 part /uploads</p>
<p>sda 8:0 0 30G 0 disk</p>
<p>├─sda14 8:14 0 4M 0 part</p>
<p>├─sda15 8:15 0 106M 0 part /boot/efi</p>
<p>└─sda1 8:1 0 29.9G 0 part /</p>
<p> Similar to what you did previously to initialize your disk, run the following az vm extension set command to tell the OS on the VM about the newly available space by executing a premade Bash script we created to help you along.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> az vm extension set \</p>
<p>--vm-name support-web-vm01 \</p>
<p>--name customScript \</p>
<p>--publisher Microsoft.Azure.Extensions \</p>
<p>--settings '{"fileUris":["https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/mslearn-add-and-size-disks-in-azure-virtual-machines/master/resize-data-disk.sh"]}' \</p>
<p>--protected-settings '{"commandToExecute": "./resize-data-disk.sh"}'</p>
<p>While the command runs, you can <a href="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/mslearn-add-and-size-disks-in-azure-virtual-machines/master/resize-data-disk.sh">examine the Bash script</a> from a separate browser tab if you'd like.</p>
<p>To summarize, the script:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Unmounts the disk /dev/sdc1.</p></li>
<li><p>Resizes partition 1 to be 128 GB.</p></li>
<li><p>Verifies partition consistency.</p></li>
<li><p>Resizes the filesystem.</p></li>
<li><p>Remounts the drive /dev/sdc1 back to the mount point /uploads.</p></li>
</ul>
<p> To verify the configuration, run lsblk on your VM over SSH a second time.</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ssh azureuser@$ipaddress lsblk</p>
<p>This time, you see that disk sdc/sdc1 is expanded to accommodate the increased size of your disk.</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> NAME MAJ:MIN RM SIZE RO TYPE MOUNTPOINT</p>
<p>sdb 8:16 0 7G 0 disk</p>
<p>└─sdb1 8:17 0 7G 0 part /mnt</p>
<p>sdc 8:32 0 128G 0 disk</p>
<p>└─sdc1 8:33 0 119.2G 0 part /uploads</p>
<p>sda 8:0 0 30G 0 disk</p>
<p>├─sda14 8:14 0 4M 0 part</p>
<p>├─sda15 8:15 0 106M 0 part /boot/efi</p>
<p>└─sda1 8:1 0 29.9G 0 part /</p>
<p> As a final verification step, run the operating system's df utility on your VM over SSH to prove that the OS can see it correctly.</p>
<p>Bash</p>
<p>ssh azureuser@$ipaddress df -h</p>
<p>You see that the drive's size is 128 GB.</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p>Filesystem Size Used Avail Use% Mounted on</p>
<p>udev 1.7G 0 1.7G 0% /dev</p>
<p>tmpfs 345M 9.3M 335M 3% /run</p>
<p>/dev/sda1 29G 1.9G 28G 7% /</p>
<p>tmpfs 1.7G 0 1.7G 0% /dev/shm</p>
<p>tmpfs 5.0M 0 5.0M 0% /run/lock</p>
<p>tmpfs 1.7G 0 1.7G 0% /sys/fs/cgroup</p>
<p>/dev/sda15 105M 3.2M 102M 3% /boot/efi</p>
<p>/dev/sdb1 6.8G 16M 6.4G 1% /mnt</p>
<p>/dev/sdc1 118G 60M 112G 1% /uploads</p>
<p>tmpfs 345M 0 345M 0% /run/user/1000</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

# Monitor AZ VMs 

|     |
|-----|

## Monitoring Layers

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>In this unit, you explore Azure monitoring capabilities for VMs, and the types of monitoring data you can collect and analyze with Azure Monitor. Azure Monitor is a comprehensive monitoring solution for collecting, analyzing, and responding to monitoring data from Azure and non-Azure resources, including VMs. Azure Monitor has two main monitoring features: Azure Monitor Metrics and Azure Monitor Logs.</p>
<p>Metrics are numerical values collected at predetermined intervals to describe some aspect of a system. Metrics can measure VM performance, resource utilization, error counts, user responses, or any other aspect of the system that you can quantify. Azure Monitor Metrics automatically monitors a predefined set of metrics for every Azure VM, and retains the data for 93 days with some exceptions.</p>
<p>Logs are recorded system events that contain a timestamp and different types of structured or free-form data. Azure automatically records activity logs for all Azure resources. This data is available at the resource level. Azure Monitor doesn't collect logs by default, but you can configure Azure Monitor Logs to collect from any Azure resource. Azure Monitor Logs stores log data in a Log Analytics workspace for querying and analysis.</p>
<p><strong>VM monitoring layers</strong></p>
<p>Azure VMs have several layers that require monitoring. Each of the following layers has a distinct set of telemetry and monitoring requirements.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Host VM</p></li>
<li><p>Guest operating system (OS)</p></li>
<li><p>Client workloads</p></li>
<li><p>Applications that run on the VM</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image142.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.35208in" alt="Diagram that shows fundamental VM architecture." /></p>
<p><strong>Host VM monitoring</strong></p>
<p>The VM host represents the compute, storage, and network resources that Azure allocates to the VM.</p>
<p><strong>VM host metrics</strong></p>
<p>VM host metrics measure technical aspects of the VM such as processor utilization and whether the machine is running. You can use VM host metrics to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Trigger an alert when your VM is reaching its disk or CPU limits.</p></li>
<li><p>Identify trends or patterns.</p></li>
<li><p>Control your operational costs by sizing VMs according to usage and demand.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure automatically collects basic metrics for VM hosts. On the VM's <strong>Overview</strong> page in the Azure portal, you can see built-in graphs for the following important VM host metrics.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>VM availability</p></li>
<li><p>CPU usage percentage (average)</p></li>
<li><p>OS disk usage (total)</p></li>
<li><p>Network operations (total)</p></li>
<li><p>Disk operations per second (average)</p></li>
</ul>
<p>You can use Azure Monitor Metrics Explorer to plot more metrics graphs, investigate changes, and visually correlate metrics trends for your VMs. With Metrics Explorer you can:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Plot multiple metrics on a graph to see how much traffic hits your VM and how the VM performs.</p></li>
<li><p>Track the same metric over multiple VMs in a resource group or other scope, and use splitting to show each VM on the graph.</p></li>
<li><p>Select flexible time ranges and granularity.</p></li>
<li><p>Specify many other settings such as chart type and value ranges.</p></li>
<li><p>Send graphs to workbooks or pin them to dashboards for quickly viewing health and performance.</p></li>
<li><p>Group metrics by time intervals, geographic regions, server clusters, or application components.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image143.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.92292in" alt="Screenshot showing CPU percentage usage and inbound flow chart." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Alert Rules

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Recommended alert rules</strong></p>
<p>Alerts proactively notify you of specified occurrences and patterns in your VM host metrics. <em>Recommended alert rules</em> are a predefined set of alert rules based on commonly monitored host metrics. These rules define recommended CPU, memory, disk, and network usage levels to alert on. The rules also include VM availability, which alerts you when the VM stops running.</p>
<p>You can quickly enable and configure recommended alert rules when you create an Azure VM, or afterwards from the VM's portal page. You can also view, configure, and create custom alerts by using Azure Monitor Alerts.</p>
<p><strong>Activity logs</strong></p>
<p>Azure Monitor automatically records and displays activity logs for Azure VMs. Activity logs include information like VM startup or modifications. You can create diagnostic settings to send activity logs to the following destinations:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Azure Monitor Logs:</strong> For more complex querying and alerting, and for longer retention up to two years.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Azure Storage:</strong> For cheaper, long-term archiving.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Azure Event Hubs:</strong> To forward outside of Azure.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Boot diagnostics</strong></p>
<p>Boot diagnostics are host logs you can use to help troubleshoot boot issues with your VMs. You can enable boot diagnostics by default when you create a VM, or afterwards for existing VMs.</p>
<p>Once you enable boot diagnostics, you can see screenshots from the VM's hypervisor for both Windows and Linux machines, and view the serial console log output of the VM boot sequence for Linux machines. Boot diagnostics stores data in a managed storage account.</p>
<p><strong>Guest OS, client workload, and application monitoring</strong></p>
<p>VM client monitoring can include monitoring the operating system (OS), workloads, and applications that run on the VM. To collect metrics and logs from guest OS and client workloads and applications, you need to install Azure Monitor Agent and set up a DCR.</p>
<p>DCRs define what data to collect and where to send that data. You can use a DCR to send Azure Monitor metrics data, or <em>performance counters</em>, to Azure Monitor Logs or Azure Monitor Metrics. You can also send event log data to Azure Monitor Logs. In other words, Azure Monitor Metrics can store only metrics data, but Azure Monitor Logs can store both metrics and event logs.</p>
<p><strong>VM insights</strong></p>
<p>VM insights is an Azure Monitor feature that helps get you started monitoring your VM clients. VM insights is especially useful for exploring overall VM usage and performance when you don't yet know the metric of primary interest. VM insights provides:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Simplified Azure Monitor Agent onboarding to enable monitoring a VM's guest OS and workloads.</p></li>
<li><p>A preconfigured DCR that monitors and collects the most common performance counters for Windows and Linux.</p></li>
<li><p>Predefined trending performance metrics charts and workbooks from the VM's guest OS.</p></li>
<li><p>A set of predefined workbooks that show collected VM client metrics over time.</p></li>
<li><p>Optionally, a collection of processes running on the VM, dependencies with other services, and a dependency map that displays interconnected components with other VMs and external sources.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Predefined VM insights workbooks show performance, connections, active ports, traffic, and other collected data from one or several VMs. You can view VM insights data directly from a single VM, or see a combined view of multiple VMs to view and assess trends and patterns across VMs. You can edit the prebuilt workbook configurations, or create your own custom workbooks.</p>
<p><strong>Client event log data</strong></p>
<p>VM insights creates a DCR that collects a specific set of performance counters. To collect other data, such as event logs, you can create a separate DCR that specifies the data you want to collect from the VM and where to send it. Azure Monitor stores collected log data in a Log Analytics workspace. From there, you can access and analyze the data by using log queries written in Kusto Query Language (KQL).</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Check knowledge

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Check your knowledge</strong></p>
<p>Top of Form</p>
<p>1.</p>
<p>What are the two main types of monitoring data that Azure Monitor collects for Azure VMs?</p>
<p>Metrics and logs.</p>
<p>Azure Monitor can collect two main types of data for monitoring VMs, metrics and logs.</p>
<p>VM insights and Alerts.</p>
<p>VM insights and Alerts are based on Azure Monitor Metrics data, but Azure Monitor can also collect log data.</p>
<p>Workbooks and Workspaces.</p>
<p>2.</p>
<p>What are the layers of a VM that need to be monitored?</p>
<p>VM host OS, SKU, and disks.</p>
<p>VM host, guest OS, client workloads, and applications.</p>
<p>VM monitoring layers consist of the VM host and the client VM, which includes the guest OS and the workloads and applications that run on the VM.</p>
<p>Subscription, resource group, and VM.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Monitor VM Host Data

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Create a VM and enable recommended alerts</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Sign in to the <a href="https://portal.azure.com/">Azure portal</a>, and in the Search field, enter <em>virtual machines</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Virtual machines</strong> page, select <strong>Create</strong>, and then select <strong>Azure virtual machine</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Basics</strong> tab of the <strong>Create a virtual machine</strong> page:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>In the <strong>Subscription</strong> field, select the correct subscription if not already selected.</p></li>
<li><p>Under <strong>Resource group</strong>:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Create new</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Under <strong>Name</strong>, enter <em>learn-monitor-vm-rg</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>OK</strong>.</p></li>
</ol></li>
<li><p>For <strong>Virtual machine name</strong>, enter <em>monitored-linux-vm</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>For <strong>Image</strong>, select <strong>Ubuntu Server 20.04 LTS - x64 Gen2</strong>.</p></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p>Leave the other settings at their current values, and select the <strong>Monitoring</strong> tab.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image144.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:5.52917in" alt="Screenshot that shows the Basics tab of the Create a virtual machine page." /></p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>On the <strong>Monitoring</strong> tab, select the checkbox next to <strong>Enable recommended alert rules</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Set up recommended alert rules</strong> screen:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Select all the listed alert rules if not already selected, and adjust the values if desired.</p></li>
<li><p>Under <strong>Notify me by</strong>, select the checkbox next to <strong>Email</strong>, and enter an email address to receive alert notifications.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Save</strong>.</p></li>
</ol></li>
<li><p>Under <strong>Diagnostics</strong>, for <strong>Boot diagnostics</strong>, ensure that <strong>Enable with managed storage account (recommended)</strong> is selected.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Note</p>
<p>Don't select <strong>Enable OS guest diagnostics</strong>. The Linux Diagnostics Agent (LAD) is deprecated, and you can enable OS guest and client monitoring later.</p>
<ol start="8" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Review + create</strong> at the bottom of the page, and when validation passes, select <strong>Create</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image145.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.10139in" alt="Screenshot that shows the Monitoring tab and alert rule configuration screen of the Create a virtual machine page." /></p>
<ol start="9" type="1">
<li><p>On the <strong>Generate new key pair</strong> popup dialog box, select <strong>Download private key and create resource</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>It can take a few minutes to create the VM. When you get the notification that the VM is created, select <strong>Go to resource</strong> to see basic metrics data.</p>
<p><strong>View built-in metrics graphs</strong></p>
<p>Once your VM is created, Azure starts collecting basic metrics data automatically. Built-in metrics graphs, along with the recommended alerts you enabled, can help you monitor whether and when your VM encounters health or performance issues. You can then use more advanced monitoring and analytics capabilities to investigate issue causes and remediation.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>To view basic metrics graphs, on the VMs <strong>Overview</strong> page, select the <strong>Monitoring</strong> tab.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image146.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.53819in" alt="Screenshot that shows Monitoring tab on a VMs Overview screen." /></p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>Under <strong>Performance and utilization</strong> &gt; <strong>Platform metrics</strong>, review the following metrics graphs related to the VMs performance and utilization. Select <strong>Show more metrics</strong> if all the graphs don't appear immediately.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>VM Availability</strong></p></li>
<li><p><strong>CPU (average)</strong></p></li>
<li><p><strong>Disk bytes (total)</strong></p></li>
<li><p><strong>Network (total)</strong></p></li>
<li><p><strong>Disk operations/sec (average)</strong></p></li>
</ul></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image147.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.13403in" alt="Screenshot that shows the platform metrics graphics on the VM Overview page." /></p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>Under <strong>Guest OS metrics</strong>, notice that guest OS metrics aren't being collected yet. In the next units, you configure VM insights and data collection rules to collect guest OS metrics.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>View the activity log</strong></p>
<p>You can view the VMs activity log by selecting <strong>Activity log</strong> from the VMs left navigation menu. You can also retrieve entries by using PowerShell or the Azure CLI.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image148.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.14583in" alt="Screenshot of the activity log for a VM." /></p>
<p><strong>View boot diagnostics</strong></p>
<p>You enabled boot diagnostics when you created the VM. You can view boot diagnostics to view boot data and troubleshoot startup issues.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the left navigation menu for the VM, select <strong>Boot diagnostics</strong> under <strong>Help</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Boot diagnostics</strong> page, select <strong>Screenshot</strong> to see a startup screenshot from the VMs hypervisor. Select <strong>Serial log</strong> to view log messages created when the VM started.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image149.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.7125in" alt="Screenshot that shows the boot diagnostic image captured." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

<table style="width:99%;">

<tr>

## Metrics Explorer

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Understand Metrics Explorer</strong></p>
<p>To open Metrics Explorer, you can:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Select <strong>Metrics</strong> from the VM's left navigation menu under <strong>Monitoring</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the <strong>See all Metrics</strong> link next to <strong>Platform metrics</strong> on the <strong>Monitoring</strong> tab of the VM's <strong>Overview</strong> page.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Metrics</strong> from the left navigation menu on the Azure Monitor <strong>Overview</strong> page.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image150.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.49861in" alt="Screenshot that shows Metrics Explorer." /></p>
<p>In Metrics Explorer, you can select the following values from the dropdown fields:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Scope:</strong> If you open Metrics Explorer from a VM, this field is prepopulated with the VM name. You can add more items with the same resource type (VMs) and location.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Metric Namespace</strong>: Most resource types have only one namespace, but for some types, you must pick a namespace. For example, storage accounts have separate namespaces for files, tables, blobs, and queues.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Metric</strong>: Each metrics namespace has many metrics available to choose from.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Aggregation</strong>: For each metric, Metrics Explorer applies a default aggregation. You can use a different aggregation to get different information about the metric.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>You can apply the following aggregation functions to metrics:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Count</strong>: Counts the number of data points.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Average (Avg)</strong>: Calculates the arithmetic mean of values.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Maximum (Max)</strong>: Identifies the highest value.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Minimum (Min)</strong>: Identifies the lowest value.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Sum</strong>: Adds up all the values.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>You can select flexible time ranges for graphs from the past 30 minutes to the last 30 days, or custom ranges. You can specify time interval granularity from one minute to one month.</p>
<p><strong>Create a metrics graph</strong></p>
<p>To create a Metrics Explorer graph that shows host VM maximum percentage CPU and inbound flows together for the past 30 minutes:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Open <strong>Metrics Explorer</strong> by selecting <strong>See all Metrics</strong> on the VM's <strong>Monitoring</strong> tab or selecting <strong>Metrics</strong> from the VM's left navigation menu.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Scope</strong> and <strong>Metric Namespace</strong> are already populated for the host VM. Select <strong>Percentage CPU</strong> from the <strong>Metrics</strong> dropdown list.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Aggregation</strong> is automatically populated with <strong>Avg</strong>, but change it to <strong>Max</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image151.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.36944in" alt="Screenshot of the Percentage CPU metrics graph for a VM." /></p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Add metric</strong> at upper left.</p></li>
<li><p>Under <strong>Metric</strong>, select <strong>Inbound Flows</strong>. Leave <strong>Aggregation</strong> at <strong>Avg</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>At upper right, select <strong>Local Time: Last 24 hours (Automatic - 15 minutes)</strong>, change it to <strong>Last 30 minutes</strong>, and select <strong>Apply</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Your graph should look similar to the following screenshot:</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image152.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.01111in" alt="Screenshot that shows a graph of CPU usage and inbound traffic." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Host Metrics - Collect Performance Counters using VM Insights 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>Besides monitoring your VM host's health, utilization, and performance, you need to monitor the software and processes running on your VM. These are called the VM guest or client. In this unit, you enable the Azure Monitor VM insights feature, which offers a quick way to start monitoring the VM client.</p>
<p>The VM client includes the operating system and other workloads and applications. To monitor the software running on your VM, you install the Azure Monitor Agent, which collects data from inside the VM. VM insights:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Installs Azure Monitor Agent on your VM.</p></li>
<li><p>Creates a DCR that collects and sends a predefined set of client performance data to a Log Analytics workspace.</p></li>
<li><p>Presents the data in curated workbooks.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Although you don't need to use VM insights to install Azure Monitor Agent, create DCRs, or set up workbooks, VM insights makes setting up VM client monitoring easy. VM insights provides you with a basis for monitoring the performance of your VM client and mapping the processes running on your machine.</p>
<p><strong>Enable VM insights</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the Azure portal, on your VM's <strong>Overview</strong> page, select <strong>Insights</strong> from the left navigation menu under <strong>Monitoring</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Insights</strong> page, select <strong>Enable</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Under <strong>Data collection rule</strong>, note the properties of the DCR that VM insights creates. In the DCR description, <strong>Processes and dependencies (Map)</strong> is set to <strong>Disabled</strong>, which you can change to <strong>Enabled</strong> or review <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-monitor/vm/vminsights-maps">this article on how-to</a> if greyed out. Also a default <strong>Log Analytics workspace</strong> is created or assigned.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Configure</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image153.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.50556in" alt="Screenshot that shows enabling and configuring VM insights." /></p>
<p>Configuration of the workspace and the agent installation typically takes 5 to 10 minutes. It can take another 5 to 10 minutes for data to become available to view in the portal.</p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>When the deployment finishes, confirm that the Azure Monitor Agent is installed by looking on the <strong>Properties</strong> tab of the VM's <strong>Overview</strong> page under <strong>Extensions + applications</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>On the <strong>Monitoring</strong> tab of the <strong>Overview</strong> page, under <strong>Performance and utilization</strong>, you can see that <strong>Guest OS metrics</strong> are now being collected.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image154.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.98681in" alt="Screenshot that shows Guest OS metrics on the VM&#39;s Monitoring tab." /></p>
<p><strong>View VM insights</strong></p>
<p>VM insights creates a DCR that sends client VM performance counters to Azure Monitor Logs. Because the DCR sends its metrics to Azure Monitor Logs, you don't use Metrics Explorer to view the metrics data that VM insights collects.</p>
<p>To view the VM insights performance graphs and maps:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Insights</strong> from the VM's left navigation menu under <strong>Monitoring</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Near the top of the <strong>Insights</strong> page, select the <strong>Performance</strong> tab. The prebuilt VM insights Performance workbook shows charts and graphs with performance-related data for the current VM.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image155.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.42431in" alt="Screenshot that shows the prebuilt VM insights Performance workbook." /></p>
<ul>
<li><p>You can customize the view by specifying a different <strong>Time range</strong> at the top of the page and different aggregations at the top of each graph.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>View Workbooks</strong> to select from other available prebuilt VM insights workbooks. Select <strong>Go To Gallery</strong> to select from a gallery of other VM insights workbooks and workbook templates, or to edit and create your own workbooks.</p></li>
</ul>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>If enabled earlier, select the <strong>Map</strong> tab on the <strong>Insights</strong> page to see the workbook for the Map feature. The map visualizes the VM's dependencies by discovering running process groups and processes that have active network connections over a specified time range.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image156.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:5.82569in" alt="Screenshot that shows a dependency map on the Map tab of VM insights." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Check your knowledge

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Check your knowledge</strong></p>
<p>Top of Form</p>
<p>1.</p>
<p>What capabilities does enabling VM insights provide?</p>
<p><strong>Prebuilt client performance workbooks and guest OS metrics.</strong></p>
<blockquote>
<p>Enable VM insights with the Azure Monitor Agent to collect guest OS metrics and use prebuilt workbooks to visualize VM client performance.</p>

</blockquote>
<p>2.</p>
<p>What's a quick way to install the Azure Monitor Agent to collect guest OS metrics?</p>
<p>Install the diagnostics extension under <strong>Diagnostics settings</strong>.</p>
<p>You don't have to install or enable anything to use the Azure Monitor Agent to collect guest OS metrics.</p>
<p><strong>Select the Azure Monitor Agent when you enable VM insights.</strong></p>
<blockquote>
<p>Select the Azure Monitor Agent instead of the legacy Log Analytics Agent when you enable VM insights.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Bottom of Form</p></th>

## VM Client Event Logs

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>Azure Monitor Metrics and VM insights performance counters help you identify performance anomalies, and alert when thresholds are reached. But to analyze the root causes of issues you detect, you need to analyze log data to see which system events caused or contributed to the issues. In this unit, you set up a DCR to collect Linux VM Syslog data, and view the log data in Azure Monitor Log Analytics by using a simple Kusto Query Language (KQL) query.</p>
<p>VM insights installs the Azure Monitor Agent, and creates a DCR that collects predefined performance counters, maps process dependencies, and presents the data in prebuilt workbooks. You can create your own DCRs to collect VM performance counters that the VM insights DCR doesn't collect, or to collect log data.</p>
<p>When you create DCRs in the Azure portal, you can select from a range of performance counters and sampling rates or add custom performance counters. Alternatively, you can select from a predefined set of log types and severity levels or define custom log schemas. You can associate a single DCR to any or all VMs in your subscription, but you might need multiple DCRs to collect different types of data from different VMs.</p>
<p><strong>Create a DCR to collect log data</strong></p>
<p>In the Azure portal, search for and select <em>monitor</em> to go to the Azure Monitor <strong>Overview</strong> page.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image157.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.04792in" alt="Screenshot that shows the Azure Monitor Overview page." /></p>
<p><strong>Create a Data Collection Endpoint</strong></p>
<p>You must have a data collection endpoint to send log data to. To create an endpoint:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the Azure Monitor left navigation menu under <strong>Settings</strong>, select <strong>Data Collection Endpoints</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Data Collection Endpoints</strong> page, select <strong>Create</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Create data collection endpoint</strong> page, for <strong>Name</strong>, enter <em>linux-logs-endpoint</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the same <strong>Subscription</strong>, <strong>Resource group</strong>, and <strong>Region</strong> as your VM uses.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Review + create</strong>, and when validation passes, select <strong>Create</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Create the Data Collection Rule</strong></p>
<p>To create the DCR to collect the event logs:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the Monitor left navigation menu under <strong>Settings</strong>, select <strong>Data Collection Rules</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Data Collection Rules</strong> page, you can see the DCR that VM insights created. Select <strong>Create</strong> to create a new data collection rule.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image158.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.83611in" alt="Screenshot of the Data Collection Rules screen with Create highlighted." /></p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>On the <strong>Basics</strong> tab of the <strong>Create Data Collection Rule</strong> screen, provide the following information:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Rule name</strong>: Enter <em>collect-events-linux</em>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Subscription</strong>, <strong>Resource Group</strong>, and <strong>Region</strong>: Select the same as for your VM.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Platform Type</strong>: Select <strong>Linux</strong>.</p></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Next: Resources</strong> or the <strong>Resources</strong> tab.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image159.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:5.41528in" alt="Screenshot of the Basics tab of the Create Data Collection Rule screen." /></p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>On the <strong>Resources</strong> screen, select <strong>Add resources</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Select a scope</strong> screen, select the <strong>monitored-linux-vm</strong> VM, and then select <strong>Apply</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Resources</strong> screen, select <strong>Enable Data Collection Endpoints</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Under <strong>Data collection endpoint</strong> for the <strong>monitored-linux-vm</strong>, select the <strong>linux-logs-endpoint</strong> you created.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Next: Collect and deliver</strong>, or the <strong>Collect and deliver</strong> tab.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image160.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:5.55417in" alt="Screenshot of the Resources tab of the Create Data Collection Rule screen." /></p>
<ol start="10" type="1">
<li><p>On the <strong>Collect and deliver</strong> tab, select <strong>Add data source</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Add data source</strong> screen, under <strong>Data source type</strong>, select <strong>Linux Syslog</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Add data source</strong> screen, select <strong>Next: Destination</strong> or the <strong>Destination</strong> tab, and make sure the <strong>Account or namespace</strong> matches the Log Analytics workspace that you want to use. You can use the default Log Analytics workspace that VM insights set up, or create or use another Log Analytics workspace.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Add data source</strong> screen, select <strong>Add data source</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Create Data Collection Rule</strong> screen, select <strong>Review + create</strong>, and when validation passes, select <strong>Create</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image161.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.28333in" alt="Screenshot of Review + create highlighted on the Create Data Collection Rule screen." /></p>
<p><strong>View log data</strong></p>
<p>You can view and analyze the log data collected by your DCR by using KQL log queries. A set of sample KQL queries is available for VMs, but you can write a query to look at the events your DCR is collecting.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>On your VM's <strong>Overview</strong> page, select <strong>Logs</strong> from the left navigation menu under <strong>Monitoring</strong>. Log Analytics opens an empty query window with the scope set to your VM.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>You can also access log data by selecting <strong>Logs</strong> from the left navigation of the Azure Monitor <strong>Overview</strong> page. If necessary, select <strong>Select scope</strong> at the top of the query window to scope the query to the desired Log Analytics workspace and VM.</p>
<p>Note</p>
<p>The <strong>Queries</strong> window with sample queries might open when you open Log Analytics. For now, close this window, because you're going to manually create a simple query.</p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>In the empty query window, type <em>Syslog</em>, and then select <strong>Run</strong>. All the system log events the DCR collected within the <strong>Time range</strong> are displayed.</p></li>
<li><p>You can refine your query to identify events of interest. For example, you can display only the events that had a <strong>SeverityLevel</strong> of <strong>warning</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image162.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.93819in" alt="Screenshot that shows the events returned from the Syslog by the DCR." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>
