# Protect VM - Backing up VMs

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>Your company has several critical virtual machine (VM) workloads running on Azure. As the lead solution architect, you're asked to ensure that the company can recover these VMs if there's data loss or corruption. You're asked to use the built-in capabilities of Azure Backup to help protect these VMs.</p>
<p>Azure Backup is a service that allows you to back up Azure virtual machines, on-premises servers, Azure file shares, and SQL Server or SAP HANA (High-performance Analytic Appliance) running on Azure VMs, and other application workloads.</p>
<p>In this module, you learn about Azure Backup and you see how you can use the Azure portal to back up and restore a VM.</p>
<p><strong>Learning objectives</strong></p>
<p>In this module, you:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Identify the scenarios for which Azure Backup provides backup and restore capabilities.</p></li>
<li><p>Back up and restore an Azure virtual machine.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Prerequisites</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Basic knowledge of Azure virtual machines.</p></li>
<li><p>Basic knowledge of disk storage for virtual machines.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Note</p>
<p>For this module, you need use your own subscription to complete the optional exercises. A trial subscription or a subscription that you already have access to works for these exercises.</p>
<p><strong>Azure Backup features and scenarios</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>5 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Your company's business continuity and disaster recovery (BCDR) plan requires a full backup and restore capability for all of your high-risk servers. You're asked to enable and test the backup and restore functionality for these critical Windows and Linux assets.</p>
<p>In this unit, you look at how Azure Backup works, and study some of the supported use cases for Azure Backup.</p>
<p><strong>What is Azure Backup?</strong></p>
<p>Azure Backup is a built-in Azure service that provides secure backup for all Azure-managed data assets. It uses zero-infrastructure solutions to enable self-service backups and restores, with at-scale management at a lower and predictable cost. Azure Backup currently offers specialized backup solutions for Azure and on-premises virtual machines (VMs). Azure Backup also gives workloads like SQL Server or SAP HANA (High-performance Analytic Appliance) running in Azure VMs enterprise-class backup and restore options.</p>
<p>In contrast to traditional backup solutions that can take considerable effort to set up, Azure Backup is easily managed through the Azure portal.</p>
<p><strong>Azure Backup versus Azure Site Recovery</strong></p>
<p>Both Azure Backup and Azure Site Recovery aim to make the system more resilient to faults and failures, but they use two different approaches. The primary goal of Backup is to maintain copies of stateful data that allow you to go back in time. Site Recovery, however, replicates the data in almost real time and allows for a failover.</p>
<p>In that sense, if there are issues like network or power outages, you can use availability zones. For a region-wide disaster (such as natural disasters), Site Recovery is used. Backups are used in cases of accidental data loss, data corruption, or ransomware attacks.</p>
<p>Additionally, the choice of a recovery approach depends on the criticality of the application, recovery point objective (RPO) and recovery time objective (RTO) requirements, and the cost implications.</p>
<p><strong>Why use Azure Backup?</strong></p>
<p>Traditional backup solutions, such as disk and tape, don't offer the highest level of integration with cloud-based solutions. Azure Backup has several benefits over more traditional backup solutions:</p>
<p><strong>Zero-infrastructure backup</strong>: Azure Backup eliminates the need to deploy and manage any backup infrastructure or storage. There's no overhead in maintaining backup servers or scaling the storage up or down as the needs vary.</p>
<p><strong>Long-term retention</strong>: Meet rigorous compliance and audit needs by retaining backups for many years, after which the built-in lifecycle management capability prunes the recovery points automatically.</p>
<p><strong>Security</strong>: Azure Backup provides security to your backup environment, both when your data is in transit and at rest:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Azure role-based access control</strong>: Role-based access control allows you to segregate duties within your team and grant only the amount of access to users necessary to do their jobs.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Encryption of backups</strong>: Backup data is automatically encrypted using Microsoft-managed keys. Alternatively, you can encrypt your backed-up data using customer-managed keys stored in the Azure Key Vault. </p></li>
<li><p><strong>No internet connectivity required</strong>: When you use Azure VMs, all the data transfer happens only on the Azure backbone network without needing to access your virtual network. So no access to any IPs or fully qualified domain names (FQDNs) is required.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Soft delete</strong>: With soft delete, the backup data is retained for 14 more days even after the deletion of the backup item. This retention protects against accidental deletion or malicious deletion scenarios, allowing the recovery of those backups with no data loss. Azure Backup also provides <strong>Enhanced soft delete</strong> that enables you to retain a deleted item in the <em>soft deleted</em> state for a longer duration.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure Backup also offers the ability to back up VMs encrypted with Azure Disk Encryption.</p>
<p><strong>High availability</strong>: Azure Backup offers three types of replication:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Locally redundant storage (LRS)</strong>: The lowest-cost option with basic protection against server rack and drive failures. We recommend it for noncritical scenarios.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Geo-redundant storage (GRS)</strong>: The intermediate option has failover capabilities in a secondary region. We recommend it for backup scenarios.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Zone-redundant storage (ZRS)</strong>: This option protects against datacenter-level failures by replicating your storage account synchronously across three Azure availability zones. We recommend it for high-availability scenarios.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Centralized monitoring and management</strong>: Azure Backup provides built-in monitoring and alerting capabilities in a Recovery Services vault. These capabilities are available without any other management infrastructure.</p>
<p><strong>Azure Backup supported scenarios</strong></p>
<p>Azure Backup supports the following scenarios:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Azure VMs</strong> - Back up Windows or Linux Azure VMs<br />
Azure Backup provides independent and isolated backups to guard against unintended destruction of the data on your VMs. Backups are stored in a Recovery Services vault with built-in management of recovery points. Configuration and scaling are simple, backups are optimized, and you can easily restore as needed.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>On-premises</strong> - Back up files, folders, and system state using the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/backup/backup-support-matrix-mars-agent">Microsoft Azure Recovery Services (MARS) agent</a>. Or use <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/backup/backup-support-matrix-mabs-dpm">Microsoft Azure Backup Server (MABS)</a> or <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/backup/backup-support-matrix-mabs-dpm">Data Protection Manager (DPM) server</a> to protect on-premises VMs (Hyper-V and VMware) and other on-premises workloads.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Azure Files shares</strong> - Azure Files provides snapshot management by Azure Backup.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>SQL Server in Azure VMs</strong> and <strong>SAP HANA databases in Azure VMs</strong> - Azure Backup offers stream-based, specialized solutions to back up SQL Server, or SAP HANA running in Azure VMs. These solutions take workload-aware backups that support different backup types such as full, differential and log, 15-minute RPO, and point-in-time recovery.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Azure Backup

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Back up an Azure virtual machine by using Azure Backup</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>5 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>You want to ensure that the backup and restore jobs you put in place offer a way to recover your company's servers. With this requirement in mind, you want to investigate the best way to implement backup for your virtual machines (VMs).</p>
<p>VMs that are hosted on Azure can take advantage of Azure Backup. You can easily back up and restore machines without installing extra software.</p>
<p>In this unit, you explore all the methods of backing up Azure VMs provided by Azure Backup and make a decision on which to implement.</p>
<p><strong>Azure VMs</strong> are backed up by taking snapshots of the underlying disks at user-defined intervals and transferring those snapshots to the Recovery Services vault as per the customer-defined policy.</p>
<p><strong>Recovery Services vault</strong></p>
<p>Azure Backup uses a Recovery Services vault to manage and store the backup data. A vault is a storage-management entity, which provides a simple experience to carry out and monitor backup and restore operations. With Azure Backup, you don't need to worry about deploying or managing storage accounts. In fact, all you need to specify is the vault that you want to back up the virtual machine (VM) to. The backup data is transferred to the Azure Backup storage accounts (in a separate fault domain) in the background. The vault also acts as a role-based access control boundary to allow secure access to the data.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image163.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.26597in" alt="Screenshot that highlights the Recovery Services vaults that are available in context to the resources they&#39;re protecting." /></p>
<p><strong>Snapshots</strong></p>
<p>A snapshot is a point-in-time backup of all disks on the VM. For Azure VMs, Azure Backup uses different extensions for each supporting operating system:</p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 15%" />
<col style="width: 8%" />
<col style="width: 74%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Extension</strong></th>
<th><strong>OS</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>VM Snapshot</td>
<td>Windows</td>
<td>The extension works with Volume Shadow Copy Service (VSS) to take a copy of the data on disk and in memory.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>VM SnapshotLinux</td>
<td>Linux</td>
<td>The snapshot is a copy of the disk.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Depending on how the snapshot is taken and what it includes, you can achieve different levels of consistency:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Application consistent</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>The snapshot captures the VM as a whole. It uses VSS writers to capture the content of the machine memory and any pending I/O operations.</p></li>
<li><p>For Linux machines, you need to write custom pre or post scripts per app to capture the application state.</p></li>
<li><p>You can get complete consistency for the VM and all running applications.</p></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p><strong>File system consistent</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>If VSS fails on Windows, or the pre and post scripts fail on Linux, Azure Backup still creates a file-system-consistent snapshot.</p></li>
<li><p>During a recovery, no corruption occurs within the machine. But installed applications need to do their own cleanup during startup to become consistent.</p></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p><strong>Crash consistent</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>This level of consistency typically occurs if the VM is shut down at the time of the backup.</p></li>
<li><p>No I/O operations or memory contents are captured during this type of backup. This method doesn't guarantee data consistency for the OS or app.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Backup policy</strong></p>
<p>You can define the backup frequency and retention duration for your backups. Currently, the VM backup can be triggered daily or weekly, and can be stored for multiple years. The backup policy supports two access tiers: <em>snapshot tier</em> and the <em>vault tier</em>. By using the Enhanced policy, you can trigger hourly backups.</p>
<p><strong>Selective disk backup</strong>: Azure Backup provides <strong>Selective Disk backup and restore</strong> capability using <strong>Enhanced policy</strong>. By using this capability, you can selectively back up a subset of the data disks that are attached to your VM. Then, you can restore a subset of the disks that are available in a recovery point, both from instant restore and vault tier. It helps you manage critical data in a subset of the VM disks and use database backup solutions when you want to back up only their OS disk to reduce cost.</p>
<p><strong>Snapshot tier</strong>: All the snapshots are stored locally for a maximum period of five days, in what is called the snapshot tier. For all types of operation recoveries, we recommended that you restore from the snapshots because it's faster to do so. This capability is called <strong>instant restore</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>Vault tier</strong>: All snapshots are additionally transferred to the vault for more security and longer retention. At this point, the recovery point type changes to "snapshot and vault."</p>
<p><strong>Backup process for an Azure virtual machine</strong></p>
<p>Here's how Azure Backup completes a backup for Azure VMs:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>For Azure VMs that are selected for backup, Azure Backup starts a backup job according to the backup frequency you specify in the backup policy.</p></li>
<li><p>During the first backup, a backup extension is installed on the VM, if the VM is running:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>For Windows VMs, the VM Snapshot extension is installed.</p></li>
<li><p>For Linux VMs, the VM SnapshotLinux extension is installed.</p></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p>After the snapshot is taken, the data is stored locally and transferred to the vault.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The backup is optimized by backing up each VM disk in parallel.</p></li>
<li><p>For each disk that's being backed up, Azure Backup reads the blocks on the disk and identifies and transfers only the data blocks that changed (the delta) since the previous backup.</p></li>
<li><p>Snapshot data might not be immediately copied to the vault. It might take several hours at peak times. Total backup time for a VM is less than 24 hours for daily backup policies.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image164.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.92292in" alt="Diagram that shows Azure Backup architecture." /></p>
<p>You can additionally enable <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/backup/encryption-at-rest-with-cmk#configuring-a-vault-to-encrypt-using-customer-managed-keys?azure-portal=true">vault encryption with customer-managed keys (CMK)</a>. By using <strong>Enhanced soft delete</strong> for a Recovery Services vault, you can protect backups from deletion. You can also keep Enhanced soft delete <em>always on</em> to prevent turning it off, thus protecting your backups from accidental deletion or from malware attacks.</p>
<p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/protect-virtual-machines-with-azure-backup/4-exercise-back-up-azure-virtual-machine">https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/protect-virtual-machines-with-azure-backup/4-exercise-back-up-azure-virtual-machine</a></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Excercise

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Exercise - Back up an Azure virtual machine</strong></p>
<p>Your company runs a combination of Windows and Linux workloads. You're asked to prove that Azure Backup is a good fit for both kinds of virtual machines (VMs). By using a combination of the Azure CLI and the Azure portal, you help protect both kinds of virtual machines with Azure Backup.</p>
<p>Azure Backup can be quickly enabled for virtual machines in Azure. You can enable Azure Backup from the portal, from the Azure CLI, or by using PowerShell commands.</p>
<p>In this exercise, you create a virtual machine (VM), set up a backup, and start a backup.</p>
<p>Note</p>
<p>This exercise is optional. If you don't have an Azure account, you can read through the instructions so you understand how to back up virtual machines by using Azure Backup. If you want to complete this exercise, but you don't have an Azure subscription or you prefer not to use your own account, create a <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/free/">free account</a> before you begin.</p>
<p><strong>Create a backup for Azure virtual machines</strong></p>
<p><strong>Set up the environment</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Sign in to the <a href="https://portal.azure.com/">Azure portal</a>, and select the icon to open Azure Cloud Shell.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image165.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.6625in" alt="Screenshot of the Cloud Shell icon in the Azure portal." /></p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>Create a resource group to contain all the resources for this exercise.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> RGROUP=$(az group create --name vmbackups --location westus2 --output tsv --query name)</p>
<p> Use Cloud Shell to create the <strong>NorthwindInternal</strong> virtual network and the <strong>NorthwindInternal1</strong> subnet.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>az network vnet create \</p></li>
<li><p>--resource-group $RGROUP \</p></li>
<li><p>--name NorthwindInternal \</p></li>
<li><p>--address-prefixes 10.0.0.0/16 \</p></li>
<li><p>--subnet-name NorthwindInternal1 \</p></li>
<li><p>--subnet-prefixes 10.0.0.0/24</p></li>
<li></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Create a Windows virtual machine by using the Azure CLI</strong></p>
<p>Create the <em>NW-APP01</em> virtual machine by running the following command. Replace &lt;password&gt; with a password of your choice, enclosed in double quotes. For example, --admin-password "PassWord123!".</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm create \</p>
<p>--resource-group $RGROUP \</p>
<p>--name NW-APP01 \</p>
<p>--size Standard_DS1_v2 \</p>
<p>--public-ip-sku Standard \</p>
<p>--vnet-name NorthwindInternal \</p>
<p>--subnet NorthwindInternal1 \</p>
<p>--image Win2016Datacenter \</p>
<p>--admin-username admin123 \</p>
<p>--no-wait \</p>
<p>--admin-password &lt;password&gt;</p>
<p><strong>Create a Linux virtual machine by using the Azure CLI</strong></p>
<p>Create the <em>NW-RHEL01</em> virtual machine by running the following command.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az vm create \</p>
<p>--resource-group $RGROUP \</p>
<p>--name NW-RHEL01 \</p>
<p>--size Standard_DS1_v2 \</p>
<p>--image RedHat:RHEL:8-gen2:latest \</p>
<p>--authentication-type ssh \</p>
<p>--generate-ssh-keys \</p>
<p>--vnet-name NorthwindInternal \</p>
<p>--subnet NorthwindInternal1</p>
<p>The command can take a few minutes to complete. Wait for it to finish before moving on to the next step.</p>
<p><strong>Enable backup for a virtual machine by using the Azure portal</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the Azure portal, search for and select <strong>Virtual machines</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image166.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:1.54792in" alt="Screenshot that shows searching for virtual machines." /></p>
<p>The <strong>Virtual machines</strong> pane appears.</p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>From the list, select the <strong>NW-RHEL01</strong> virtual machine that you created.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image167.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.17847in" alt="Screenshot that shows selecting a virtual machine." /></p>
<p>The <strong>NW-RHEL01</strong> virtual machine pane appears.</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>In the middle menu pane, select the <strong>Capabilities</strong> tab, then scroll down to and select <strong>Backup</strong>. The <strong>Backup</strong> pane for the <em>NW-RHEL01</em> virtual machine appears.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the radio button for <strong>Standard</strong>. You can accept the defaults for the following options:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Backup vault</strong>: <strong>vaultXXX</strong> for the name.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Backup policy</strong>: <strong>DailyPolicy-xxxxxxxx</strong>, which creates a daily backup at 12:00 PM UTC with a retention range of 180 days.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image168.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.65764in" alt="Screenshot that shows the backup options." /></p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>Select the <strong>Enable backup</strong> button.</p></li>
<li><p>Once deployment completes, go back to the <strong>NW-RHEL01</strong> virtual machine, select the <strong>Capabilities</strong> tab, then scroll down to and select <strong>Backup</strong>. The <strong>Backup</strong> pane for the <em>NW-RHEL01</em> virtual machine appears.</p></li>
<li><p>To perform the first backup for this server, in the top menu bar, select <strong>Backup now</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>The <strong>Backup Now</strong> pane for <em>NW-RHEL01</em> appears.</p>
<ol start="8" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>OK</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Enable a backup by using the Azure CLI</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>First, create the azure-backup vault by using Cloud Shell:</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> az backup vault create \</p>
<p>--resource-group vmbackups \</p>
<p>--location westus2 \</p>
<p>--name azure-backup</p>
<p> Using Cloud Shell, enable a backup for the <em>NW-APP01</em> virtual machine.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p> az backup protection enable-for-vm \</p>
<p>--resource-group vmbackups \</p>
<p>--vault-name azure-backup \</p>
<p>--vm NW-APP01 \</p>
<p>--policy-name EnhancedPolicy</p>
<p> Monitor the progress of the setup using the Azure CLI.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<p>az backup job list \</p>
<p>--resource-group vmbackups \</p>
<p>--vault-name azure-backup \</p>
<p>--output table</p>
<p>Keep running the preceding command until you see that ConfigureBackup is finished.</p>
<p>Output</p>
<p> Name Operation Status Item Name Start Time UTC Duration</p>
<p>------------------------------------ --------------- ---------- ----------- -------------------------------- --------------</p>
<p>a3df79b4-be4f-4cc9-8b2c-a5ead44a6a12 ConfigureBackup Completed NW-APP01 2019-08-01T06:19:12.101048+00:00 0:00:31.305975</p>
<p>5e1531a9-8b3d-4983-a642-86ee982f7036 Backup InProgress NW-RHEL01 2019-08-01T06:18:35.955118+00:00 0:01:22.734182</p>
<p>860d4dca-9603-4a4e-9f3b-93f242a0a64d ConfigureBackup Completed NW-RHEL01 2019-08-01T06:13:33.860598+00:00 0:00:31.256773</p>
<p> Do an initial backup of the virtual machine, instead of waiting for the schedule to run it.</p>
<p>Azure CLI</p>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p>az backup protection backup-now \</p></li>
<li><p>--resource-group vmbackups \</p></li>
<li><p>--vault-name azure-backup \</p></li>
<li><p>--container-name NW-APP01 \</p></li>
<li><p>--item-name NW-APP01 \</p></li>
<li><p>--retain-until 18-10-2030 \</p></li>
<li><p>--backup-management-type AzureIaasVM</p></li>
<li><p>There's no need to wait for the backup to finish, because the next section shows you how to monitor the progress in the portal.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Monitor backups in the portal</strong></p>
<p><strong>View the status of a backup for a single virtual machine</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>On the Azure portal menu or from the <strong>Home</strong> page, select <strong>All resources</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Enter <em>Virtual machines</em> in the search field at the top of the page and select <strong>Virtual machines</strong> from the results.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the <strong>NW-APP01</strong> virtual machine. The <em>NW-APP01</em> virtual machine pane appears.</p></li>
<li><p>In the middle menu pane, select the <strong>Capabilities</strong> tab, then scroll to and select <strong>Backup</strong>. The <strong>Backup</strong> pane for the <em>NW-APP01</em> virtual machine appears.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Under the <strong>Backup status</strong> section, the <strong>Last backup status</strong> field displays the current status of the backup.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image169.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.69931in" alt="Screenshot of the Backup page after it has been set up." /></p>
<p><strong>View the status of backups in the Recovery Services vault</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>On the Azure portal menu or from the <strong>Home</strong> page, select <strong>All resources</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Sort the list by <em>Type</em>, and then select the <strong>azure-backup</strong> Recovery Services vault. The <strong>Azure-backup</strong> recovery services vault pane appears.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Overview</strong> pane, select the interior <strong>Backup</strong> tab to display a summary of all the backup items, the storage being used, and the current status of any backup jobs.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image170.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.28542in" alt="Screenshot of the Backup dashboard." /></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Restore VM 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Restore virtual machine data</strong></p>
<p>200 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>5 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Companies that have a business continuity and disaster recovery (BCDR) plan typically schedule test runs to ensure that the business can successfully recover from disasters. Now that you successfully backed up your virtual machines, you want to explore the options available for restoring them as part of your BCDR testing.</p>
<p>In this unit, you learn about the options for restoring an Azure virtual machine (VM) from a previous backup.</p>
<p><strong>Restore types</strong></p>
<p>Azure Backup provides many ways to restore a VM. As explained earlier, you can either instantly restore from the snapshot tier (optimal for operational recoveries) or from the vault tier.</p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 15%" />
<col style="width: 82%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Restore option</strong></p></th>
<th><p><strong>Details</strong></p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Create a new VM</strong></p></td>
<td><p>Quickly creates and gets a basic VM up and running from a restore point. The new VM must be created in the same region as the source VM.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Restore disk</strong></p></td>
<td><p>Restores a VM disk, which can then be used to create a new VM. The disks are copied to the resource group you specify. Azure Backup provides a template to help you customize and create a VM. Alternatively, you can attach the disk to an existing VM, or create a new VM.<br />
<br />
This option is useful if you want to customize the VM, add configuration settings that weren't there at the time of backup. Or, add settings that must be configured using the template or PowerShell.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Replace existing</strong></p></td>
<td><p>You can restore a disk and use it to replace a disk on the existing VM. Azure Backup takes a snapshot of the existing VM before replacing the disk and stores it in the staging location you specify. Existing disks connected to the VM are replaced with the selected restore point. The current VM must exist. You can't use this option if the VM is deleted.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Cross region (secondary region)</strong></p></td>
<td><p>Cross region restore can be used to restore Azure VMs in the secondary region, which is an Azure paired region.<br />
This feature is available for the following options:</p>
<p> Create a VM</p>
<p> Restore Disks<br />
We don't currently support the Replace existing disks option.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Cross Subscription Restore</strong></p></td>
<td><p>Backup Admins and App admins can perform the restore operation on secondary regions.<br />
Cross Subscription Restore:<br />
<br />
- Allows you to restore Azure Virtual Machines or disks to a different subscription within the same tenant as the source subscription. As per the Azure role-based access control capabilities from restore points.<br />
- Allowed only if the Cross Subscription Restore property is enabled for your Recovery Services vault.<br />
- Works with Cross Region Restore and Cross Zonal Restore.<br />
- You can trigger Cross Subscription Restore for managed virtual machines only.<br />
- Cross Subscription Restore is supported for Restore with Managed System Identities (MSI).<br />
- It's unsupported for snapshots tier recovery points.<br />
- It's unsupported for unmanaged VMs and VMs encrypted with Advanced Digital Encryption (ADE).</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Cross Zonal Restore</strong></p></td>
<td><p>Allows you to restore Azure Virtual Machines or disks pinned to any zone to different available zones (as per the Azure Role-based access control capabilities) from restore points. When you select a zone to restore, it selects the logical zone (and not the physical zone) as per the Azure subscription you use to restore to.<br />
- You can trigger Cross Zonal Restore for managed virtual machines only.<br />
- Cross Zonal Restore is supported for Restore with Managed System Identities (MSI).<br />
- Cross Zonal Restore supports restore of an Azure zone pinned/non-zone pinned VM from a vault with Zonal-redundant storage (ZRS) enabled. Learn how to set Storage Redundancy.<br />
- You can only use Cross Zonal Restore to restore a VM pinned to an Azure zone from a vault with Cross Region Restore (CRR) under these conditions: The secondary region supports zones, or Zone Redundant Storage (ZRS) is enabled.<br />
- Cross Zonal Restore is supported from secondary regions.<br />
- It's unsupported from snapshots restore point.<br />
- It's unsupported for Encrypted Azure VMs.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Selective disk backup</strong></p></td>
<td><p>Allows you to back up and restore selective VM disks through Enhanced policy. Using this capability, you can selectively back up a subset of the data disks that are attached to your VM. Then, you can restore a subset of the disks that are available in a recovery point, both from instant restore and vault tier.<br />
<br />
Selective disk backup is useful when you:<br />
<br />
- Manage critical data in a subset of the VM disks.<br />
- Use database backup solutions and want to back up only their OS disk to reduce cost.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Recover files from a backup</strong></p>
<p>You can also recover individual files from a recovery point by mounting the snapshot on the target machine using the iSCSI initiator in the machine. To learn more, see <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/backup/backup-azure-restore-files-from-vm">Recover files from Azure virtual machine backup</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Restore an encrypted virtual machine</strong></p>
<p>Azure Backup supports the backup and restore of machines encrypted through Azure Disk Encryption. Disk Encryption works with Azure Key Vault to manage the relevant secrets that are associated with the encrypted disk. For an extra layer of security, you can use key vault encryption keys (KEKs) to encrypt the secrets before they're written to the key vault.</p>
<p>Certain limitations apply when you restore encrypted VMs:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure Backup supports only standalone key encryption. Any key that's part of a certificate isn't currently supported.</p></li>
<li><p>File-level or folder-level restores aren't supported with encrypted VMs. To restore to that level of granularity, the entire VM has to be restored. You can then manually copy the file or folders.</p></li>
<li><p>The <strong>Replace existing VM</strong> option isn't available for encrypted VMs.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Restore VM data

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Exercise - Restore Azure virtual machine data</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>6 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>A few days after you backed up your first Azure virtual machine (VM), the server had issues. The VM needs to be restored from a backup. You want to restore the VM's disk and attach it to the problematic live server, and then track the restore to ensure that it finished successfully.</p>
<p>In this exercise, you see how to restore a successful backup to replace a VM that became corrupted, and monitor its progress.</p>
<p><strong>Restore a virtual machine in the Azure portal</strong></p>
<p><strong>Create a storage account to use as a staging location</strong></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>If you closed Azure, sign in to the <a href="https://portal.azure.com/">Azure portal</a> by using the same account that you used in the previous exercise.</p></li>
<li><p>In the Azure portal, enter <strong>Storage accounts</strong> in the top search bar, and then select it.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image171.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:1.42222in" alt="Select storage accounts." /></p>
<p>The <strong>Storage accounts</strong> pane appears.</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>In the menu bar, select <strong>Create</strong>. The <strong>Create a storage account</strong> pane appears.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Basics</strong> tab, enter the following values for each setting to create a storage account.</p></li>
</ol>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li></li>
</ol>
<table style="width:91%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 17%" />
<col style="width: 74%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Setting</strong></th>
<th><strong>Value</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Resource group</td>
<td>From the dropdown list, select <strong>vmbackups</strong>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Instance details</strong></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Storage account name</td>
<td>Enter a unique name like <strong>restorestagingYYYYMMDD</strong>, where YYYYMMDD is replaced with today's date.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Region</strong></td>
<td>From the dropdown list, select <strong>(US) West US 2</strong>.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li><p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image172.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:6.62014in" alt="Specify storage account options." /></p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Review + create</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>After validation passes, select <strong>Create</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Wait for the storage account to be deployed.</p>
<p><strong>Stop the virtual machine</strong></p>
<p>A backup can't be restored if the VM is allocated and running. If you forget to stop the VM and attempt to restore it, you see an error that's similar to the following example.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image173.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.47361in" alt="Screenshot that shows the error details when a VM is running." /></p>
<p>To prevent this error, follow these steps:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>In the top left of the Azure portal, select <strong>Home</strong>, select <strong>Virtual Machines</strong>, and then select <strong>NW-APP01</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image174.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.55347in" alt="Screenshot that shows the VM overview page." /></p>
<p>The <em>NW-APP01</em> virtual machine pane appears.</p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>In the menu bar, select <strong>Stop</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image175.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.5375in" alt="Screenshot that shows how to stop the VM." /></p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>In the <strong>Stop this virtual machine</strong> dialog box, select <strong>OK</strong>.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image176.webp" style="width:5.27083in;height:2.79167in" alt="Screenshot of the prompt for stopping this VM." /></p></th>
</tr>

<p>The Recovery Services vaults are accessible at the subscription level. When you're viewing the VM, Azure provides a quick link to the specific vault under <strong>Operations</strong>.</p>

<td></td>
</tr>

<ol start="5" type="1">

<li><p>Select <strong>Restore</strong>. The <strong>Backup</strong> pane for the <em>NW-APP01</em> virtual machine appears. Notice the notifications in the top right of the toolbar. The latest notification shows <strong>Triggering restore for NW-APP01</strong></p></li>

</ol>

<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image181.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.24097in" alt="Screenshot of the restore progress." /></p>
<p>The <strong>Restore</strong> pane appears for <em>NW-APP01</em>.</p>
<ol start="3" type="1">
<li><p>You can monitor the progress of the VM restore job:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Job details</strong>: Details about the restore job you launched for this VM.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Job status</strong>: Real-time progress of the restore job.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Sub tasks</strong>: Name and status of the tasks within the job.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ol>

<hr />
<p>Implement access management for Azure resources</p>
<p>Explore how to use built-in Azure roles, managed identities, and RBAC-policy to control access to Azure resources. Identity is the key to secure solutions.</p>
<p><strong>Learning objectives</strong></p>
<p>By the end of this module, you will be able to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Configure and use Azure roles within Microsoft Entra ID</p></li>
<li><p>Configure and managed identity and assign it to Azure resources</p></li>
<li><p>Analyze the role permissions granted to or inherited by a user</p></li>
<li><p>Configure access to data in Azure Key Vault using RBAC-policy</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>

<tbody>
</tbody>

# Azure Roles

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Introduction</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This module will cover how to assign and manage access to resources in Azure using Azure roles. When you create a resource, you want to know that only specific access is granted to users and groups. Only allow users that need to access data or a resource, the permissions to do so. How can you control access? By assigning a role with the specific permissions needed. There are built-in Azure roles and you can create custom-roles as needed.</p>
<p>An application might also need to have permission to access data or other Azure resources. Learn how to set up managed identities, which allow the application to gain access to only resources you allow. You can give granular access to secrets, keys, and certificates stored in a key vault to your users and applications. You protect both the items stored in the key vault, and who can use them. And finally, you'll look at the new tool Microsoft Entra Permission Management. Learn to gather, review, and restrict the permission assigned across your cloud solutions.</p>
<p><strong>Learning objectives</strong></p>
<p>By the end of this module will be able to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Assign Azure roles and custom roles to access Azure resources.</p></li>
<li><p>Create and manage application access with managed identities.</p></li>
<li><p>Configure and manage access into Azure Key Vault.</p></li>
<li><p>Retrieve object from a key vault securely.</p></li>
<li><p>Explore the capabilities of Microsoft Entra Permissions Management.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Prerequisites</strong></p>
<p>None</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Assign azure roles

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Assign Azure roles</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure role-based access control (Azure RBAC) is the authorization system you use to manage access to Azure resources. To grant access, you assign roles to users, groups, service principals, or managed identities at a particular scope. Primary steps to follow when assigning an Azure role:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Who needs access?</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>User</strong> - Only a single person is needed for the task. You can assign a role to users in other tenants.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Group</strong> - Use when you need to grant a set of users the same role.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Service Principal</strong> - Assign a role to a service principal when you want to grant an application access to an Azure resource.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Managed Identity</strong> - Use the managed identity when you want an application to manage credentials for authentication.</p></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p>Select the right role. Use the built-in roles or create a custom role with the specific capabilities you need.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Built-in Azure roles</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Owner - full access to all resources.</p></li>
<li><p>Contributor - Can create and manage all types of Azure resources, but can't grant access.</p></li>
<li><p>Reader - Can view the available Azure resources.</p></li>
<li><p>User Access Administrator - Assign access to Azure resources.</p></li>
<li><p>Other task specific roles, like Virtual Machine Contributor, can be assigned.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p>Identify what level to assign the role (the Scope). Scope is the set of resources that the access applies to. In Azure, you can specify a scope at four levels: management group, subscription, resource group, and resource. Scopes are structured in a parent-child relationship. Each level of hierarchy makes the scope more specific. You can assign roles at any of these levels of scope. The level you select determines how widely the role is applied. Lower levels inherit role permissions from higher levels. Example:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>If you assign the <strong>Reader role</strong> to a user at the <strong>management group scope</strong>, that user can read everything in all subscriptions in the management group.</p></li>
<li><p>If you assign the <strong>Billing Reader role</strong> to a group at the <strong>subscription scope</strong>, the members of that group can read billing data for every resource group and resource in the subscription.</p></li>
<li><p>If you assign the <strong>Contributor role</strong> to an application at the <strong>resource group scope</strong>, it can manage resources of all types in that resource group, but not other resource groups in the subscription. It's a best practice to grant security-principals the least privilege they need to perform their job. Avoid assigning broader roles at broader scopes even if it initially seems more convenient. By limiting roles and scopes, you limit what resources are at risk if the security-principal is ever compromised. For more information, see Understand scope.</p></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p>Confirm the currently logged in user has the rights need to assign the Azure role.</p></li>
<li><p>Assign the role. Once you know the security-principal, role, and scope, you can assign the role. You can assign roles using the Azure portal, Azure PowerShell, Azure CLI, Azure SDKs, or REST APIs. You can have up to 2000 role assignments in each subscription. This limit includes role assignments at the subscription, resource group, and resource scopes. You can have up to 500 role assignments in each management group.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Assign an Azure role from the portal</strong></p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image183.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.85486in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure portal. It shows a sample resource group with the Assign Role selected." /></p>
<p>Whether you are in the User, Group, Resource Group, or Subscription you'll generally use the Access content (IAM) page to make the assign. It's also known as identity and access management (IAM) and appears in several locations in the Azure portal.</p>
<p><strong>Assign an Azure role with script</strong></p>
<p>PowerShell using the Microsoft Graph PowerShell cmdlet</p>
<p>New-AzRoleAssignment -ObjectId &lt;objectId&gt; `</p>
<p>-RoleDefinitionName &lt;roleName&gt; `</p>
<p>-Scope /subscriptions/&lt;subscriptionId&gt;/resourcegroups/&lt;resourceGroupName&gt;/providers/&lt;providerName&gt;/&lt;resourceType&gt;/&lt;resourceSubType&gt;/&lt;resourceName&gt;</p>
<p>CLI scripting</p>
<p>az role assignment create --assignee "{assignee}" \</p>
<p>--role "{roleNameOrId}" \</p>
<p>--resource-group "{resourceGroupName}"</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Create custom roles

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Configure custom Azure roles</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>If the Azure built-in roles don't meet the specific needs of your organization, you can create your own Azure custom roles. Just like built-in roles, you can assign custom roles to users, groups, and service principals at management group (in preview only), subscription and resource group scopes. Custom roles are stored in a Microsoft Entra ID and can be shared across subscriptions. Each directory can have up to 5000 custom roles. Custom roles can be created using the Azure portal, Azure PowerShell, Azure CLI, or the REST API.</p>
<p><strong>Create the custom role from the user interface</strong></p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image184.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.62361in" alt="Screenshot of the Microsoft Entra ID screen with a new custom role dialog. Select the attributes you want to role to have." /></p>
<p>You would assign a custom role to a user, group, or other resource the same as you do for built-in. Your admin gets to control exactly with capabilities the custom role has access to. The principle of least privilege let's you pick just the capabilities you need. To create the custom role:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Open Microsoft Entra admin center.</p></li>
<li><p>From the <strong>Identity</strong> menu, Select <strong>Roles and administration</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>+ New custom role</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Then name and assign the capabilities needed.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Create a custom role from a JSON template</strong></p>
<p>You can use a JSON file to create a custom role. Here's a sample:</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"properties": {</p>
<p>"roleName": "Billing Reader Plus",</p>
<p>"description": "Read billing data and download invoices",</p>
<p>"assignableScopes": [</p>
<p>"/subscriptions/your-subscription-number"</p>
<p>],</p>
<p>"permissions": [</p>
<p>{</p>
<p>"actions": [</p>
<p>"Microsoft.Authorization/*/read",</p>
<p>"Microsoft.Billing/*/read",</p>
<p>"Microsoft.Commerce/*/read",</p>
<p>"Microsoft.Consumption/*/read",</p>
<p>"Microsoft.Management/managementGroups/read",</p>
<p>"Microsoft.CostManagement/*/read",</p>
<p>"Microsoft.Support/*"</p>
<p>],</p>
<p>"notActions": [],</p>
<p>"dataActions": [],</p>
<p>"notDataActions": []</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>]</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>The asterisk (*) is used as a wildcard. If you need to assign all of the <strong>read</strong> permissions from the <strong>Billing</strong> resource that use this command <strong>Microsoft/Billing/*/read</strong>. The wildcard can exist at any level.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Create and configure managed identities 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Create and configure managed identities</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>A common challenge when creating a cloud solution is the management of secrets, credentials, certificates, and keys. These secure elements are used to secure communication between services. Managed identities eliminate the need for developers to manage these credentials.</p>
<p>While developers can securely store the secrets in Azure Key Vault, services need a way to access Azure Key Vault. Managed identities provide an automatically managed identity in Microsoft Entra ID for applications to use when connecting to resources. The managed identity supports authentication via Microsoft Entra ID. Applications can use managed identities to obtain Microsoft Entra tokens without having to manage any credentials.</p>
<p><strong>Benefits of using managed identities</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>You don't need to manage credentials. Credentials aren’t even accessible to you.</p></li>
<li><p>You can use managed identities to authenticate to any resource that supports Microsoft Entra authentication, including your own applications. Managed identities can be used without any extra cost.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Types of managed identity</strong></p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 9%" />
<col style="width: 88%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Identity type</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description and usage</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>System-assigned</td>
<td>Some Azure services allow you to enable a managed identity directly on a service instance. When you enable a system-assigned managed identity, an identity is created in Microsoft Entra ID. The identity is tied to the lifecycle of that service instance. When the resource is deleted, Azure automatically deletes the identity for you. By design, only that Azure resource can use this identity to request tokens from Microsoft Entra ID.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>User-assigned</td>
<td>You might also create a managed identity as a standalone Azure resource. You can create a user-assigned managed identity and assign it to one or more instances of an Azure service. For user-assigned managed identities, the identity is managed separately from the resources that use it.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Always remember that managed identities are assigned to an application. So, you need to configure and manage the identity within the services they're being used. If you have an application running in a virtual machine (Linux or Windows), then you add and configure the identity there. If you're using a managed identity with a cloud-app, function, or app service, then you configure and manage it there. Let's look at adding a managed identity to a cloud-built app using the App Service.</p>
<p><strong>Managed identity in Azure portal for an App Service</strong></p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image185.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.47917in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure portal in the App Services screen. Add a user-assigned identity is highlighted." /></p>
<p>The basic steps, to create and add an identity to your app, are:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Build your App.</p></li>
<li><p>Open the App in the Azure portal.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Identity</strong> from the menu then select either <strong>System assigned</strong> or <strong>User assigned</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the <strong>+ Add</strong> item and complete the wizard.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>You can perform a similar action using script within the CLI, PowerShell, or with a template. Sample could look like:</p>
<p><strong>Using the CLI</strong></p>
<p>az webapp identity assign --resource-group &lt;group-name&gt; --name &lt;app-name&gt; --identities &lt;identity-name&gt;</p>
<p>Or using <strong>PowerShell</strong> with the AZ.ManagedServiceIdentity module installed</p>
<p>Update-AzFunctionApp -Name &lt;app-name&gt; -ResourceGroupName &lt;group-name&gt; -IdentityType UserAssigned -IdentityId $userAssignedIdentity.Id</p>
<p>Or within a <strong>template</strong></p>
<p>"identity": {</p>
<p>"type": "UserAssigned",</p>
<p>"userAssignedIdentities": {</p>
<p>"&lt;RESOURCEID&gt;": {}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p>}</p>
<p><strong>Value of managed identity</strong></p>
<p>As stated at the beginning of this page, when you build an app, you need a method to grant it access to resources. To take advantage of the concepts of <strong>zero trust</strong> you can use managed identities. You only assign the minimum privileges that the managed identity needs. Then only assign access the minimum resources needed. Least-privilege will keep your applications and data protected.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Access Azure resources with managed identities 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Access Azure resources with managed identities</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Managed identities for Azure resources are a feature of Microsoft Entra ID. Each Azure service that supports managed-identities are subject to their own timeline. Make sure you review the availability status of managed identities for your resource and known issues before you begin. After you've configured an Azure resource with a managed identity, you can give the managed identity access to another resource.</p>
<p><strong>Add access to other resources</strong></p>
<p>After you've enabled managed identity on an Azure resource, such as an Azure App Service application or Azure virtual machine, you might need to grant access to more resource. Let's say you want add access to a storage account to your managed identity.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Sign in to the Azure portal using an account associated with the Azure subscription under which you've configured the managed identity.</p></li>
<li><p>Navigate to the desired resource on which you want to modify access control. In this example, we're giving an Azure virtual machine access to a storage account, so we navigate to the storage account.</p></li>
<li><p>Select Access control (IAM).</p></li>
<li><p>Select Add &gt; Add role assignment to open the Add role assignment page.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image186.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.22708in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure portal in the storage account page, adding a new role to a managed identity." /></p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>Pick the Owner, Contributor, or Reader based on the least privilege rules for your applications needs.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the managed identity you want assigned.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image187.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.02083in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure portal in the key vault page, choose the managed identity to grant role and access to." /></p>
<ol start="7" type="1">
<li><p>Complete the assignment with the <strong>Review + assign</strong> option.</p></li>
</ol></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Analyze Azure role permissions

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Analyze Azure role permissions</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>What is a permission? The dictionary definition of permission is the <strong>consent or authorization to perform a specific action</strong>. In Microsoft Entra ID, you've permissions for each of the operations you're able to do. Permission can range from viewing your settings, to be able to change your setting. Then move on to granting permission to add or remove users and beyond. There are two primary places where permission can be assigned, at a user or group level. However, they all pass down to the user at the final point. When dealing with users, you've both a member-user and a guest-user. The default permissions for the guest-user are slightly less than the member.</p>
<p><strong>What are a sample of the default permissions for users?</strong></p>
<table style="width:84%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 37%" />
<col style="width: 46%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Member Users</strong></th>
<th><strong>Guest Users</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Enumerate list of users and their contacts</td>
<td>Read own properties</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Invite guest users</td>
<td>Invite guest users</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Can create Security and Microsoft 365 Groups</td>
<td>Can search for nonhidden groups by name</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Register new applications</td>
<td>Read properties of registered and enterprise applications</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Note</p>
<p>This is just a small subset, to show differences. If you want a full list of the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/active-directory/fundamentals/users-default-permissions">Default User Permissions</a></p>
<p><strong>Controlling permissions - add and restrict</strong></p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 45%" />
<col style="width: 52%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>User settings</strong></th>
<th><strong>Roles and administrators</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image188.webp" style="width:5.51458in;height:9in" alt="Screenshot of the Microsoft Entra user settings, where permissions can be restricted." /></td>
<td><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image189.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:7.69653in" alt="Screenshot of the Roles and administrators screen in Microsoft Entra ID. List of roles that can be applied." /></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>You can use the <strong>User Settings</strong> inside of Microsoft Entra ID – Manage menu to restrict or control the default permissions of the default users. Or you can use Roles and administrators to add new permissions onto your users and group. Always use the concept of Least Privilege and make sure the users only have the rights they need. In User settings you can restrict the user's ability to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Register applications</p></li>
<li><p>Access the Azure portal</p></li>
<li><p>Block LinkedIn connections</p></li>
<li><p>Manage settings for external collaboration</p></li>
</ul>
<p>By adding roles to a given user account or group, you can add permissions on to member users, guest users, and service principals. Adding roles gives permissions to perform specific activities. Actions are limited, which allows the rule of least privilege.</p>
<p><strong>Exploring available permissions</strong></p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image190.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.7125in" alt="Screenshot of the Attribute definition reader. You can see which permissions a built-in role grants." /></p>
<p>You only want to grant the permissions a user needs. So be careful to know what all permissions are granted when you assign a role. You can see the list of permissions in the <strong>Attribute definition reader</strong>. To open it, launch Microsoft Entra ID, then open the <strong>Roles and administrators</strong> screen. Next select a role, and open its description page from the ellipsis (...) menu. Depending on the role you chose, you'll see a large number of permissions or possibly a small number. Two sets of permissions:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Role permissions</p></li>
<li><p>Guest and service principal basic read permissions</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Azure Key Vault RBAC policies 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Configure Azure Key Vault RBAC policies</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>You can grant access to Azure Key Vault using either role-based access control (RBAC) or using Key Vault access policies. Either method works to protect your secrets, certificates, and keys. Access policies give you a little more granular control, but can be harder to manage. Choose the best option based on your security posture needs.</p>
<p><strong>Assign a Key Vault access policy</strong></p>
<p>A Key Vault access policy determines whether a user, application, or group, can perform operations on Key Vault secrets, keys, and certificates. You can assign access policies using the Azure portal, the Azure CLI, or Azure PowerShell. Key vault supports up to 1024 access policy entries, with each entry granting a distinct set of permissions to a particular security principal. Because of this limitation, we recommend assigning access policies to groups of users, where possible, rather than individual users. Using groups makes it much easier to manage permissions for multiple people in your organization.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image191.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:8.6125in" alt="Screenshot of Key Vault access policy options that can be enabled and assigned." /></p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Open <strong>Key Vault</strong> in the Azure portal.</p></li>
<li><p>Select your key vault or create a new one.</p></li>
<li><p>From the menu select <strong>Access policies</strong>, then select <strong>+ Add Access Policy</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Use the dialog to assign the specific permission you want service principal to have.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Note</p>
<p>Service principal represents the user, group, or application you're assigning the policy to.</p>
<ol start="5" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Add</strong> to save and apply the access policy.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>You can complete this activity using a saved template, PowerShell, CLI, and the Azure portal.</p>
<p><strong>Assign a Key Vault access using Role-based access control (RBAC)</strong></p>
<p>Azure RBAC allows users to manage Key, Secrets, and Certificates permissions. It provides one place to manage all permissions across all key vaults. The Azure RBAC model allows you to set permissions on different scope levels: management group, subscription, resource group, or individual resources. Azure RBAC for key vault also enables you to have separate permissions on individual keys, secrets, and certificates. Our recommendation is to use a vault per application per environment (Development, Pre-Production, and Production).</p>
<p>There are two actions required to use roles to access data within your Key Vault.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Enable role-based access control in your key vault.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image192.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:5.45139in" alt="Screenshot of the key vault access policies screen, with the allow role-based access control selected." /></p>
<ol start="2" type="1">
<li><p>Open key vault <strong>Identity and Access (IAM)</strong> from the menu. Then assign the role as you've done in other scenarios; like managed identity.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image193.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.19514in" alt="Screenshot of the key vault management screen with the Identity and Access (IAM) screen open. Ready to assign a role." /></p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 22%" />
<col style="width: 75%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Built-in role</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Key Vault Administrator</td>
<td>Perform all data plane operations on a key vault and all objects in it, including certificates, keys, and secrets. Can't manage key vault resources or manage role assignments.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Key Vault Certificates Officer</td>
<td>Perform any action on the certificates of a key vault, except manage permissions.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Key Vault Crypto Officer</td>
<td>Perform any action on the keys of a key vault, except manage permissions.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Key Vault Crypto Service Encryption User</td>
<td>Read metadata of keys and perform wrap/unwrap operations.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Key Vault Crypto User</td>
<td>Perform cryptographic operations using keys.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Key Vault Reader</td>
<td>Read metadata of key vaults and its certificates, keys, and secrets. Can't read sensitive values such as secret contents or key material.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Key Vault Secrets Officer</td>
<td>Perform any action on the secrets of a key vault, except manage permissions.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Key Vault Secrets User</td>
<td>Read secret contents.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Retrieve Objects from AKV 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Retrieve objects from Azure Key Vault</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure Key Vault is a secure tool for storing secrets, keys, and certificate. Once stored, these items can be used by users and applications to perform actions and operations in a secure method. The process to retrieve any of these resources is common. So we'll look at how to review a secret from a key vault.</p>
<p><strong>Add a secret to your key vault</strong></p>
<p>To add a secret to the vault, follow the steps:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Navigate to your new key vault in the Azure portal</p></li>
<li><p>On the Key Vault settings pages, select <strong>Secrets</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select on <strong>Generate/Import</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the Create a secret screen choose the following values:</p></li>
</ol>
<ol start="4" type="1">
<li></li>
</ol>
<table style="width:33%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 13%" />
<col style="width: 20%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Setting</strong></th>
<th><strong>Value to enter</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Upload options</td>
<td>Manual</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Name</td>
<td>mySC300keyvaultSecret</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td>This is my secret</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<ol start="4" type="1">

</ol>
<p><strong>Retrieve a secret using the Azure portal</strong></p>
<p>This process is simple. Open your key vault, then open the secret you created. Select the <strong>Show secret value</strong> button.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image194.webp" style="width:4.89583in;height:6.61458in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure Key Vault, with a secret open and the show secret value selected. You can read or copy the value in plain text." /></p>
<p><strong>Retrieve a secret using CLI or PowerShell</strong></p>
<p>You can quickly and easily grab a secret from your key vault using scripting languages.</p>
<p><strong>CLI</strong></p>
<p>az keyvault secret show --name "mySC300keyvaultSecret" --vault-name "&lt;your-unique-keyvault-name&gt;" --query "value"</p>
<p><em><strong>PowerShell</strong></em></p>
<p>$secret = <removed> -VaultName "&lt;your-unique-keyvault-name&gt;" -Name "mySC300keyvaultSecret" -AsPlainText</p>
<p><strong>Retrieve a secret in an application</strong></p>
<p>If you're building an application that needs access to your key vault secrets, certificates, and keys that can be done. You can access the key vault using .NET, Node.js, Python, and other languages.</p>
<p><strong>Explore Microsoft Entra Permissions Management</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Microsoft Entra Permissions Management is a cloud infrastructure entitlement management (CIEM) solution. Permissions Management provides comprehensive visibility into permissions assigned to all identities. The software detects, automatically right-sizes, and continuously monitors unused and excessive permissions.</p>
<p>Organizations have to consider permissions management as a central piece of their Zero Trust security to implement least privilege access across their entire infrastructure:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Organizations are increasingly adopting multicloud strategy and are struggling with the lack of visibility and complexity of managing access permissions.</p></li>
<li><p>With the proliferation of identities and cloud services, the number of high-risk cloud permissions is increasing. The increase expands the attack surface a bad actor can use to gain access.</p></li>
<li><p>IT security teams are under increased pressure to ensure access to their expanding cloud estate is secure and compliant.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Key use cases for Microsoft Entra Permissions Management</strong></p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 9%" />
<col style="width: 88%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Use case</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Discover</td>
<td>Customers can assess permission risks by evaluating gaps between permissions granted and permissions used.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Remediate</td>
<td>Customers can right-size permissions based on usage, then grant new permissions on-demand, and automate just-in-time access for cloud resources.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Monitor</td>
<td>Customers can detect anomalous activities with machine language-powered (ML-powered) alerts and generate detailed forensic reports.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Process flow</strong></p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image195.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.65833in" alt="Diagram of how Microsoft Entra Permissions Management takes input from many sources to analyze what permissions are being used." /></p>
<p>Microsoft Entra Permissions Management comprehensive visibility into permissions assigned to all identities – users and workloads – actions, and resources across cloud infrastructures and identity providers. It detects, right-sizes, and monitors unused and excessive permissions and enables Zero Trust security through least privilege access in Microsoft Azure, AWS, and GCP.</p></th>
</tr>

# Azure files 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />

<th><p><strong>Configure Azure Files</strong></p>
<p><strong>Learning objectives</strong></p>
<p>In this module, you learn how to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Identify storage for file shares versus blob data.</p></li>
<li><p>Configure Azure file shares and file share snapshots.</p></li>
<li><p>Identify features and use cases of Azure File Sync.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Introduction</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>1 minute</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure Files offers fully managed file shares in the cloud that are accessible via industry standard protocols. Azure File Sync is a service that allows you to cache several Azure Files shares on an on-premises Windows Server or cloud virtual machine.</p>
<p>In this module, your company has a large repository of organizational documents. Offices are located in different geographical regions, and users need the most current versions of the documents. You're researching how to implement Azure Files shares to provide a central location for the documents.</p>
<p><strong>Learning objectives</strong></p>
<p>In this module, you learn how to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Identify storage for file shares.</p></li>
<li><p>Compare file shares to blob storage.</p></li>
<li><p>Configure Azure file shares, file share snapshots, and soft delete.</p></li>
<li><p>Use Azure Storage Explorer to access your file share.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Skills measured</strong></p>
