<p>Implement and manage storage (15–20%).</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Configure Azure Files and Azure Blob Storage</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create and configure a files share in Azure storage.</p></li>
<li><p>Configure snapshots and soft delete for Azure Files.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Prerequisites</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Familiarity with shared file systems.</p></li>
<li><p>Familiarity with navigating the Azure portal.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Compare storage options 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Compare storage for file shares and blob data</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>5 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/storage/files/storage-files-introduction">Azure Files</a> offers fully managed file shares in the cloud. You can access Azure file shares by using the Server Message Block (SMB), Network File System (NFS), and HTTP protocols. Clients can connect to Azure file shares from Windows, Linux, and macOS devices.</p>
<p><strong>Things to know about Azure Files</strong></p>
<p>Here are some characteristics of Azure files:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Serverless deployment</strong>. An Azure file share is a PaaS offering of a fully managed file share that doesn't require any infrastructure. You don't need to take care of any VMs, operating systems, or updates.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Almost unlimited storage</strong>. A single Azure file share can store up to 100 tebibytes (TiB) of files, and a file can be up to 4 TiB in size. The files are organized in a hierarchical folder structure in the same way as on on-premises file servers.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Data encryption</strong>. The data on an Azure file share is encrypted at rest in an Azure datacenter and in transit on a network.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Access from anywhere</strong>. By default, clients can access Azure file shares from anywhere if they have internet connectivity.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Integration into an existing environment</strong>. You can control access to Azure file shares by using Microsoft Entra identities or AD DS identities that are synced to Microsoft Entra ID. This helps ensure that users can have the same experience accessing an Azure file share as when they access an on-premises file server.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Previous versions and backups</strong>. You can create Azure file share snapshots that integrate with the Previous Versions feature in File Explorer. You can also use Azure Backup to back up Azure file shares.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Data redundancy</strong>. Azure file share data replicates to multiple locations in the same Azure datacenter or across many Azure datacenters. The replication setting of the Azure storage account that includes the file share controls the data redundancy.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Things to consider when using Azure Files</strong></p>
<p>There are many common scenarios for using Azure Files. As you review the following suggestions, think about how Azure Files can provide solutions for your organization.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Consider replacement and supplement options</strong>. Replace or supplement traditional on-premises file servers or NAS devices by using Azure Files.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Consider global access</strong>. Directly access Azure file shares by using most operating systems, such as Windows, macOS, and Linux, from anywhere in the world.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Consider lift and shift support</strong>. <em>Lift and shift</em> applications to the cloud with Azure Files for apps that expect a file share to store file application or user data.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Consider using Azure File Sync</strong>. Replicate Azure file shares to Windows Servers by using Azure File Sync. You can replicate on-premises or in the cloud for performance and distributed caching of the data where it's being used. We take a closer look at Azure File Sync in a later unit.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Consider shared applications</strong>. Store shared application settings such as configuration files in Azure Files.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Consider diagnostic data</strong>. Use Azure Files to store diagnostic data such as logs, metrics, and crash dumps in a shared location.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Consider tools and utilities</strong>. Azure Files is a good option for storing tools and utilities that are needed for developing or administering Azure VMs or cloud services.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Compare Azure Files to Azure Blob Storage</strong></p>
<p>It's important to understand when to use Azure Files to store data in file shares rather than using Azure Blob Storage to store data as blobs. The following table compares different features of these services and common implementation scenarios.</p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 51%" />
<col style="width: 46%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Azure Files (file shares)</strong></th>
<th><strong>Azure Blob Storage (blobs)</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Azure Files provides the SMB and NFS protocols, client libraries, and a REST interface that allows access from anywhere to stored files.</td>
<td>Azure Blob Storage provides client libraries and a REST interface that allows unstructured data to be stored and accessed at a massive scale in block blobs.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>- Files in an Azure Files share are true directory objects.<br />
- Data in Azure Files is accessed through file shares across multiple virtual machines.</td>
<td>- Blobs in Azure Blob Storage are a flat namespace.<br />
- Blob data in Azure Blob Storage is accessed through a container.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><em><strong>Azure Files</strong> is ideal to lift and shift an application to the cloud that already uses the native file system APIs. Share data between the app and other applications running in Azure.</em><br />
<br />
<em>Azure Files is a good option when you want to store development and debugging tools that need to be accessed from many virtual machines.</em></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Azure file shares

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Manage Azure file shares</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>Azure Files offers two industry-standard file system protocols for mounting Azure file shares: the Server Message Block (SMB) protocol and the Network File System (NFS) protocol. Azure file shares don't support both the SMB and NFS protocols on the same file share, although you can create SMB and NFS Azure file shares within the same storage account.</p>
<p><strong>Types of Azure file shares</strong></p>
<p>Azure Files supports two storage tiers: premium and standard. Standard file shares are created in general purpose (GPv2) storage accounts, while premium file shares are created in FileStorage storage accounts. The two storage tiers have the attributes described in the following table.</p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 8%" />
<col style="width: 89%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Storage tier</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Premium</td>
<td>Premium file shares store data on solid-state drives (SSDs), and are available only in the FileStorage storage account kind. They provide consistent high performance and low latency, and are available in LRS redundancy, with ZRS available in some regions. Not available in all Azure regions.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Standard</td>
<td>Standard file shares store data on hard disk drives (HDDs) and deploy in the general-purpose version 2 (GPv2) storage account type. Provide performance for workloads such as general-purpose file shares and dev/test environments. Standard file shares are available for LRS, ZRS, GRS, and GZRS, in all Azure regions.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Types of authentication</strong></p>
<p>Three are three main authentications methods that Azure Files supports.</p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 17%" />
<col style="width: 80%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Authentication method</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Identity-based authentication over SMB</td>
<td>Provides the same seamless single sign-on (SSO) experience when accessing Azure file shares as accessing on-premises file shares.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Access key</td>
<td>An access key is an older and less flexible option. An Azure storage account has two access keys that can be used when making a request to the storage account, including to Azure Files. Access keys are static and provide full control access to Azure Files. Access keys should be secured and not shared with users, because they bypass all access control restrictions. A best practice is to avoid sharing storage account keys and use identity-based authentication whenever possible.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>A Shared Access Signature (SAS) token</td>
<td>SAS is a dynamically generated Uniform Resource Identifier (URI) that's based on the storage access key. SAS provides restricted access rights to an Azure storage account. Restrictions include allowed permissions, start and expiry time, allowed IP addresses from where requests can be sent, and allowed protocols. With Azure Files, a SAS token is only used to provide REST API access from code.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Creating SMB Azure file shares</strong></p>
<p>There are two important settings that you need to be aware of when creating and configuring SMB Azure file shares.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Open port 445</strong>. SMB communicates over TCP port 445. Make sure port 445 is open. Also, make sure your firewall isn't blocking TCP port 445 from the client machine. If you can't unblock port 445, then a VPN or ExpressRoute connection from on-premises to your Azure network is required, with Azure Files exposed on your internal network using private endpoints.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Enable secure transfer</strong>. The Secure transfer required setting enhances the security of your storage account by limiting requests to your storage account from secure connections only. Consider the scenario where you use REST APIs to access your storage account. If you attempt to connect, and secure transfer required is enabled, you must connect by using HTTPS. If you try to connect to your account by using HTTP, and secure transfer required is enabled, the connection is rejected.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Mount an SMB Azure file share on Windows</strong></p>
<p>You can use Azure file shares seamlessly in Windows and Windows Server. You can connect to your Azure file share with Windows or Windows Server in the Azure portal. Specify the <strong>Drive</strong> where you want to mount the share, and choose the <strong>Authentication method</strong>. The Azure portal supplies you with PowerShell commands to run when you're ready to work with the file share. This video shows you how to mount the SMB file share on Windows.</p>
<p><strong>Mount SMB Azure file share on Linux</strong></p>
<p>You can also connect to Azure file shares from Linux machines. From your virtual machine page, select <strong>Connect</strong>. SMB Azure file shares can be mounted in Linux distributions by using the CIFS kernel client. File mounting can be done on-demand with the mount command or on-boot (persistent) by creating an entry in /etc/fstab.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Create file share snapshots

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Create file share snapshots</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure Files provides the capability to take share <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/storage/files/storage-snapshots-files">snapshots of file shares</a>. File share snapshots capture a point-in-time, read-only copy of your data.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image196.webp" style="width:5in;height:1.15625in" alt="Screenshot of a file share snapshot that shows the snapshot name and date it was created." /></p>
<p><strong>Things to know about file share snapshots</strong></p>
<p>Let's review some characteristics of file share snapshots.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The Azure Files share snapshot capability is provided at the file share level.</p></li>
<li><p>Share snapshots are incremental in nature. Only data changed since the most recent share snapshot is saved.</p></li>
<li><p>Incremental snapshots minimize the time required to create share snapshots and saves on storage costs.</p></li>
<li><p>Even though share snapshots are saved incrementally, you only need to retain the most recent share snapshot to restore the share.</p></li>
<li><p>You can retrieve a share snapshot for an individual file. This level of support helps with restoring individual files rather than having to restore to the entire file share.</p></li>
<li><p>Share snapshots provide only file-level protection. They don't prevent fat-finger deletions on a file share or storage account. If you delete a file share that has share snapshots, all of its snapshots are deleted along with the share.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Things to consider when using file share snapshots</strong></p>
<p>There are several benefits to using file share snapshots and having access to incremental point-in-time data storage. As you review the following suggestions, think about how you can implement file share snapshots in your Azure Files storage solution.</p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 19%" />
<col style="width: 78%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Benefit</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><em><strong>Protect against application error and data corruption</strong></em></td>
<td>Applications that use file shares perform operations like writing, reading, storage, transmission, and processing. When an application is misconfigured or an unintentional bug is introduced, accidental overwrite or damage can happen to a few data blocks. To help protect against these scenarios, you can take a share snapshot before you deploy new application code. When a bug or application error is introduced with the new deployment, you can go back to a previous version of your data on that file share.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><em><strong>Protect against accidental deletions or unintended changes</strong></em></td>
<td>Imagine you're working on a text file in a file share. After the text file is closed, you lose the ability to undo your changes. In this scenario, you need to recover a previous version of your file. You can use share snapshots to recover previous versions of the deleted or renamed file.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><em><strong>Support backup and recovery</strong></em></td>
<td>After you create a file share, you can periodically create a snapshot of the file share to use it for data backup. A share snapshot, when taken periodically, helps maintain previous versions of data that can be used for future audit requirements or disaster recovery.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Implement soft delete 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Implement soft delete for Azure Files</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>2 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure Files offers <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/storage/files/storage-files-prevent-file-share-deletion?toc=%2Fazure%2Fstorage%2Ffile-sync">soft delete for file shares</a>. Soft delete lets you recover deleted files and file shares.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image197.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.82083in" alt="Illustration that depicts how to enable soft delete on an Azure file share." /></p>
<p><strong>Things to know about soft delete for Azure Files</strong></p>
<p>Let's take a look at the characteristics of soft delete for Azure Files.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Soft delete for file shares is enabled at the file share level.</p></li>
<li><p>Soft delete transitions content to a soft deleted state instead of being permanently erased.</p></li>
<li><p>Soft delete lets you configure the retention period. The retention period is the amount of time that soft deleted file shares are stored and available for recovery.</p></li>
<li><p>Soft delete provides a retention period between 1 and 365 days.</p></li>
<li><p>Soft delete can be enabled on either new or existing file shares.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Things to consider when using soft delete for Azure Files</strong></p>
<p>There are many advantages to using soft delete for Azure Files. Consider the following scenarios, and think about how you can use soft delete.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Recover from accidental data loss</strong>. You can recover deleted or corrupted data with soft delete.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Upgrade scenarios</strong>. Use soft delete to restore to a known good state after a failed upgrade attempt.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Ransomware protection</strong>. Use soft delete to recover data without paying ransom to cybercriminals.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Long-term retention</strong>. Use soft delete to comply with data retention requirements.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Business continuity</strong>. Use soft delete to prepare your infrastructure to be highly available for critical workloads.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Azure storage explorer 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Use Azure Storage Explorer</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>4 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/storage/storage-explorer/vs-azure-tools-storage-manage-with-storage-explorer?tabs=windows">Azure Storage Explorer</a> is a standalone application that makes it easy to work with Azure Storage data on Windows, macOS, and Linux. With Azure Storage Explorer, you can access multiple accounts and subscriptions, and manage all your Storage content.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image198.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.77986in" alt="Screenshot of Azure Storage Explorer that shows the Emulator storage account open, which has a folder and several documents. The access tier information is visible." /></p>
<p><strong>Things to know about Azure Storage Explorer</strong></p>
<p>Azure Storage Explorer has the following characteristics.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure Storage Explorer requires both management (Azure Resource Manager) and data layer permissions to allow full access to your resources. You need Microsoft Entra ID permissions to access your storage account, the containers in your account, and the data in the containers.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Storage Explorer lets you connect to different storage accounts.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Connect to storage accounts associated with your Azure subscriptions.</p></li>
<li><p>Connect to storage accounts and services that are shared from other Azure subscriptions.</p></li>
<li><p>Connect to and manage local storage by using the Azure Storage Emulator.</p></li>
</ul></li>
</ul>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image199.webp" style="width:5.90625in;height:6.03125in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure Explorer Manage Accounts page." /></p>
<p><strong>Things to consider when using Azure Storage Explorer</strong></p>
<p>Azure Storage Explorer supports many scenarios for working with storage accounts in Azure. As you review these options, think about which scenarios apply to your Azure Storage implementation.</p>
<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 19%" />
<col style="width: 78%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Scenario</strong></th>
<th><strong>Description</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>Connect to an Azure subscription</strong></td>
<td>Manage storage resources that belong to your Azure subscription.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Work with local development storage</strong></td>
<td>Manage local storage by using the Azure Storage Emulator.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Attach to external storage</strong></td>
<td>Manage storage resources that belong to another Azure subscription or that are under national Azure clouds by using the storage account name, key, and endpoints. This scenario is described in more detail in the next section.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Attach a storage account with a SAS</strong></td>
<td>Manage storage resources that belong to another Azure subscription by using a shared access signature (SAS).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Attach a service with a SAS</strong></td>
<td>Manage a specific Azure Storage service (blob container, queue, or table) that belongs to another Azure subscription by using a SAS.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Attach to external storage account</strong></p>
<p>Azure Storage Explorer lets you attach to external storage accounts so storage accounts can be easily shared.</p>
<p>To create the connection, you need the external storage <strong>Account name</strong> and <strong>Account key</strong>. In the Azure portal, the account key is called <strong>key1</strong>.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image200.webp" style="width:4.29167in;height:3.6875in" alt="Screenshot of the Azure Storage Explorer wizard to connect to an external storage account." /></p>
<p>To use a storage account name and key from a national Azure cloud, use the <strong>Storage endpoints domain</strong> drop-down menu to select <strong>Other</strong>, and then enter the custom storage account endpoint domain.</p>
<p><strong>Access keys</strong></p>
<p>Access keys provide access to the entire storage account. You're provided two access keys so you can maintain connections by using one key while regenerating the other.</p>
<p>Important</p>
<p>Store your access keys securely. We recommend regenerating your access keys regularly.</p>
<p>When you regenerate your access keys, you must update any Azure resources and applications that access this storage account to use the new keys. This action doesn't interrupt access to disks from your virtual machines.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Azure file sync

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Consider Azure File Sync</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>4 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/storage/file-sync/file-sync-introduction">Azure File Sync</a> enables you to cache several Azure Files shares on an on-premises Windows Server or cloud virtual machine. You can use Azure File Sync to centralize your organization's file shares in Azure Files, while keeping the flexibility, performance, and compatibility of an on-premises file server.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image201.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:3.30139in" alt="Illustration that depicts how Azure File Sync can be used to cache an organization&#39;s file shares in Azure Files." /></p>
<p><strong>Things to know about Azure File Sync</strong></p>
<p>Let's take a look at the characteristics of Azure File Sync.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure File Sync transforms Windows Server into a quick cache of your Azure Files shares.</p></li>
<li><p>You can use any protocol that's available on Windows Server to access your data locally with Azure File Sync, including SMB, NFS, and FTPS.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure File Sync supports as many caches as you need around the world.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Cloud tiering</strong></p>
<p>Cloud tiering is an optional feature of Azure File Sync. Frequently accessed files are cached locally on the server while all other files are tiered to Azure Files based on policy settings.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>When a file is tiered, Azure File Sync replaces the file locally with a pointer. A pointer is commonly referred to as a <em>reparse point</em>. The parse point represents a URL to the file in Azure Files.</p></li>
<li><p>When a user opens a tiered file, Azure File Sync seamlessly recalls the file data from Azure Files without the user needing to know that the file is stored in Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>Cloud tiering files have greyed icons with an offline O file attribute to let the user know when the file is only in Azure.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Things to consider when using Azure File Sync</strong></p>
<p>There are many advantages to using Azure File Sync. Consider the following scenarios, and think about how you can use Azure File Sync with your Azure Files shares.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Consider application lift and shift</strong>. Use Azure File Sync to move applications that require access between Azure and on-premises systems. Provide write access to the same data across Windows Servers and Azure Files.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Consider support for branch offices</strong>. Support your branch offices that need to back up files by using Azure File Sync. Use the service to set up a new server that connects to Azure storage.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Consider backup and disaster recovery</strong>. After you implement Azure File Sync, Azure Backup backs up your on-premises data. Restore file metadata immediately and recall data as needed for rapid disaster recovery.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Consider file archiving with cloud tiering</strong>. Azure File Sync stores only recently accessed data on local servers. Implement cloud tiering so older data moves to Azure Files.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Knowledge check 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Knowledge check</strong></p>
<p>Completed 200 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Module assessment</p></li>
<li><p>3 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Answer 100% of questions correctly in order to pass. <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/configure-azure-files-file-sync/8-knowledge-check">Retake</a></p>
<p>Your company maintains a large document repository. You're implementing Azure Files shares to provide a central location for the documents. Users at offices in different geographical regions need access to the latest versions of the documents. You're configuring Azure File Sync to keep the information up to date across multiple offices.</p>
<p>One scenario you're working to resolve involves the manufacturing division. They're running dedicated software in their warehouse to keep track of product stock. The software needs to run on machines in the warehouse, but the management team wants to access the stock data from the main office. Limited bandwidth in the warehouse is causing issues when accessing cloud based solutions. You proposed using cloud tiering, soft delete, and snapshots.</p>
<p><strong>Answer the following questions</strong></p>
<p>Choose the best response for each question.</p>
<p>Top of Form</p>
<p>1.</p>
<p>Which statement correctly describes cloud tiering?</p>
<p>Cloud tiering prioritizes the sync order of file shares.</p>
<p>Cloud tiering sets the frequency at which the sync job runs.</p>
<p><strong>Cloud tiering archives infrequently accessed files to free up space on the local file share.</strong></p>
<p>Correct. Cloud tiering allows frequently accessed files to be cached on the local server. Infrequently accessed files are tiered or archived to the Azure file share according to the policy created.</p>
<p>2.</p>
<p>Which statement correctly describes soft delete?</p>
<p>Soft delete only retains deleted files for 14 days.</p>
<p><strong>Soft delete can be enabled on either new or existing file shares.</strong></p>
<p>Correct. Soft delete can be enabled on either new or existing file shares.</p>
<p>Soft delete provides protection only for NFS file shares.</p>
<p>3.</p>
<p>Which statement correctly describes file snapshots?</p>
<p><strong>A share snapshot is a point-in-time, read-only copy of your data.</strong></p>
<p>Correct. A share snapshot is a point-in-time, read-only copy of your data.</p>
<p>You can't retrieve a share snapshot for an individual file.</p>
<p>The snapshot capability is provided at the storage account level.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

## Summary 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Summary and resources</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>2 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Azure Administrators are familiar with Azure Files and the Azure File Sync agent. They know how to implement fully managed file shares in the cloud by using industry standard protocols. They understand how to use Azure File Sync to cache Azure Files shares on an on-premises Windows Server or cloud virtual machine.</p>
<p>In this module, you learned when to use Azure Files and how the service compares to Azure Blob Storage. You also reviewed Azure Files features such as snapshots and soft delete. You learned how Azure File Sync can be used with on-premises data stores. You also were introduced to Azure Storage Explorer.</p>
<p><strong>The main takeaways for this module are:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure Files provides the SMB and NFS protocols, client libraries, and a REST interface that allows access from anywhere to stored files.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Files is ideal to lift and shift an application to the cloud that already uses the native file system APIs. Share data between the app and other applications running in Azure.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Files offers two industry-standard file system protocols for mounting Azure file shares: the Server Message Block (SMB) protocol and the Network File System (NFS) protocol.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Files offers two types of file shares: standard and premium. The premium tier stores data on modern solid-state drives (SSDs), while the standard tier uses hard disk drives (HDDs).</p></li>
<li><p>File share snapshots capture a point-in-time, read-only copy of your data.</p></li>
<li><p>Soft delete allows you to recover your deleted file share.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Storage Explorer is a standalone application that makes it easy to work with stored data on Windows, macOS, and Linux.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure File Sync enables you to cache file shares on an on-premises Windows Server or cloud virtual machine.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

# Deploy and Administer VM

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />

<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Learning objectives</strong></p>
<p>In this module, you practice for the <em>Deploy and administer Linux virtual machines on Azure</em> Applied Skill. You also gain skills to help with day-to-day administrator job tasks. The content combines topics on compute, storage, and monitoring tasks.</p>
<p><strong>Introduction</strong></p>
<p>Completed 100 XP</p>
<ul>
<li><p>5 minutes</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This Guided Project focuses on deploying and administering Azure virtual machines.</p>
<p>Azure virtual machines (VMs) are one of several types of on-demand, scalable computing resources that Azure offers. Typically, you choose a virtual machine when you need more control over the computing environment than the other choices offer.</p>
<p><strong>Scenario</strong></p>
<p>Your company is an online retailer providing products and services. The company plans to migrate on-premises operations to Azure virtual machines. A three-tier architecture is proposed.</p>
<p>The Web tier includes web servers running on Linux virtual machines. The App tier processes user requests such as purchases. The Data tier stores the app data such as inventory levels.</p>
<p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image202.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:2.57292in" alt="Diagram of the overarching architecture as described in the exercise." /></p>
<p>The project has several phases. In the first phase, you as the Cloud Administrator must learn how to deploy and administer the virtual machines in the Web tier. You need to prototype a solution that includes configure, monitor, storage, and back up.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Exercise 1 - Configure an Azure Linux virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>Exercise 2 - Monitor a virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>Exercise 3 - Provide storage for virtual machines.</p></li>
<li><p>Exercise 4 - Back up a virtual machine.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>By the end of this module, you have practical experience with Linux virtual machines. You can then apply this knowledge to the on-premises migration.</p>
<p>Note</p>
<p>This is a guided project module where you complete a project by following step-by-step instructions. </p>
<p><strong>Skilling areas</strong></p>
<p>You reviewed the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/">Azure virtual machine documentation</a>. In each area, you identified skills that you need to complete.</p>
<p><strong>Configure a virtual machine</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Create a Linux virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>Connect to a Linux virtual machine using SSH.</p></li>
<li><p>Update Linux virtual machine operating systems.</p></li>
<li><p>Install and run a workload dependency, like Nginx.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Monitor a virtual machine</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Configure VM Insights.</p></li>
<li><p>Create an alert.</p></li>
<li><p>Identify performance issues.</p></li>
<li><p>Resize a virtual machine.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Provide access to data storage for a virtual machine</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Add data disks and configure partitions.</p></li>
<li><p>Assign a managed identity to a virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>Mount a file share on a virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>Assign Azure roles.</p></li>
<li><p>Use AzCopy to transfer data to and from a virtual machine.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Back up a virtual machine</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Configure Azure Backup.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>

<p><strong>Skill 2: Connect to the virtual machine and install OS updates</strong></p>
<p>In this task, you will use SSH to connect to the virtual machine. Connecting will require network traffic to port 22 to be allowed. Once connected, you will check for and update the operating system.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Continue in the portal on the virtual machine page.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Overview</strong> tab, in the top menu, select <strong>Connect</strong> and <strong>Connect</strong> in the drop-down.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>More ways to connect</strong> to display the possible connection methods.</p></li>
<li><p>Review your choices, then select <strong>Native SSH</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Read the steps on connecting with SSH. Notice that port 22 is not configured to allow access with SSH. This must be corrected before continuing. <strong>Close</strong> the Native SSH page.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Networking</strong> section, select <strong>Network settings</strong>. Notice the Network Security Group (NSG) rules.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>A Network Security Group (NSG) acts as a virtual firewall for controlling inbound and outbound traffic to Azure resources. By default, inbound access is allowed from other virtual machines in the virtual network and from load balancers. All other inbound traffic is denied.</p>
<ol start="7" type="1">
<li><p>Select <strong>Create port rule</strong> and then <strong>Inbound port rule</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Service</strong> drop-down, select <strong>SSH</strong>, then <strong>Add</strong> the rule.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>The Nginx web service that you will be installing needs port 80. Repeat the above step to <strong>add</strong> another inbound port rule for service <strong>HTTP</strong>.</p>
<ol start="9" type="1">
<li><p>Check your work and ensure you have two new inbound port rules to <strong>allow</strong> port 22 and port 80.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Connect</strong> menu (left side) select <strong>Connect</strong> and then <strong>select</strong> Native SSH. Confirm port 22 access is now configured (check mark). It may take a minute for the rule to deploy, if necessary, refresh the page.</p></li>
<li><p>Make a note of the <strong>public IP address</strong>. You will need this to connect to the virtual machine. <strong>Close</strong> the Native SSH page.</p></li>
<li><p>Open a <strong>CMD</strong> window so you can run the SSH connection string.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>We are using a key pair, but you could also provide a user and password.</p>
<ol start="13" type="1">
<li><p>At the prompt, connect to the virtual machine using SSH. Be sure to include the correct path to the key and the virtual machine’s public_ip_address. Example of key location: <em>c:\users\admin\downloads\VM1_key.pem</em>. When prompted, type <em>yes</em> to connect.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>code</p>
<p> ssh -i 'c:\users\admin\downloads\VM1_key.pem' adminuser@public_ip_address</p>
<p> Ensure the command is successful and the prompt changes to <em>adminuser@VM1</em>.</p>
<p> Fetch the list of available OS updates and install updates. When prompted, type <strong>yes</strong> to continue. Each command must complete successfully.</p>
<p>shell</p>
<p>sudo apt update</p>
<p>shell</p>
<ol start="15" type="1">
<li><p>sudo apt upgrade</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>Stay connected to the virtual machine, leave the CMD window open, and continue to the next task.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Check your learning.</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Can you configure network security group inbound port rules?</p></li>
<li><p>Can you connect to a Linux virtual machine with native SSH?</p></li>
<li><p>Can you install OS updates on a Linux virtual machine?</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Skill 3: Install the Nginx web service and test to ensure it is working</strong></p>
<p>In this task, you will install the Nginx web service.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>Continue working at the CMD prompt. Run these commands one at a time. Ensure each command completes successfully.</p></li>
<li><p>Install the Nginx service. When prompted indicate <strong>Y</strong> to continue the install.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>shell</p>
<p> sudo apt install nginx</p>
<p> Start the Nginx service.</p>
<p>shell</p>
<p> sudo systemctl start nginx</p>
<p> Configure Nginx to launch on boot. This is optional but good practice.</p>
<p>shell</p>
<p> sudo systemctl enable nginx</p>
<p> Check to ensure the Nginx service is <strong>active (running)</strong>.</p>
<p>shell</p>
<p> service nginx status</p>
<p> Launch the Nginx welcome page. Be sure to substitute your virtual machine public IP address. You can also open the Nginx default page in a browser, http://public_ip_address.</p>
<p>shell</p>
<ol start="6" type="1">
<li><p>curl -m 80 public_ip_address</p></li>
<li><p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image205.webp" style="width:5.52083in;height:2.27083in" alt="Screenshot of the nginx home page." /></p></li>
</ol>
<p>If the home page times out, check to ensure there is an inbound security rule to allow port 80.</p>
<p><strong>Check your learning.</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Can you install software, like Nginx, on a Linux virtual machine?</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Learn more with self-paced training and documentation</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/training/modules/provision-linux-virtual-machine-in-azure/">Provisioning a Linux virtual machine in Microsoft Azure</a>. Azure allows you to use several common provisioning tools to deploy Linux virtual machines (VMs), to include Terraform, Bicep, the Azure portal, and the Azure CLI. In this module, you’ll learn how to deploy a Linux virtual machine using each of these methods.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/virtual-machines/linux/quick-create-portal?tabs=ubuntu">Quickstart: Create a Linux virtual machine in the Azure portal</a> Azure virtual machines (VMs) can be created through the Azure portal. The Azure portal is a browser-based user interface to create Azure resources. This Quickstart shows you how to use the Azure portal to deploy a Linux virtual machine (VM) running Ubuntu Server 22.04 LTS. To see your VM in action, you also SSH to the VM and install the NGINX web server.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Key takeaways</strong></p>
<p>Congratulations on completing the exercise. Here are the main takeaways:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure virtual machines are on-demand, scalable computing resources.</p></li>
<li><p>Configuring Azure virtual machines includes choosing an operating system, size, storage and networking settings. You can create a basic virtual machine by accepting the defaults.</p></li>
<li><p>There are several ways to connect to a Linux virtual machine including SSH and Password.</p></li>
<li><p>To use SSH the virtual machine must have a public IP address and port 22 must be open.</p></li>
<li><p>Network Security Group rules let you allow or deny inbound and outbound port connections. For example, port 22 for SSH and port 80 for Nginx.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Exercise - Monitor virtual machines</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Completed 100 XP</p></li>
<li><p>45 minutes</p></li>
<li><p>You need to monitor and be alerted if there are issues with the virtual machine. You need to create and configure alerts and Log Analytics.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Architecture diagram</strong></p></li>
<li><p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image206.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.10278in" alt="Diagram of the overarching architecture as described in exercise 2." /></p></li>
<li><p><strong>Job skills</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Configure VM Insights.</p></li>
<li><p>Configure action groups and notifications.</p></li>
<li><p>Create alerts.</p></li>
<li><p>Trigger an alert by resizing the virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>Configure an alert processing rule.</p></li>
<li><p>Launch the exercise and follow the instructions. When finished, be sure to return to this page so you can continue learning.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>

</table>

<li><p><strong>Lab requirements</strong></p></li>
<li><p>This lab requires an Azure virtual machine. If you don’t have a virtual machine, there are optional instructions to create one.</p></li>
<li><p>This lab requires an Azure subscription. Your subscription type may affect the availability of features in this lab. You may change the region, but the steps were tested using the <strong>(US) East</strong> region.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Estimated timing: 45 minutes</strong></p></li>
<li><p><strong>Lab scenario</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Your organization is migrating their virtual machine workloads to Azure. It is important that you are notified of any significant infrastructure changes. You plan to explore the capabilities of Azure Monitor, including alerts and Log Analytics.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Interactive lab simulation</strong></p></li>
<li><p>There is an interactive lab simulation that you might find useful for this topic. The simulation lets you click through a similar scenario at your own pace. There are differences between the interactive simulation and this lab, but many of the core concepts are the same. An Azure subscription is not required.</p></li>

Create a Log Analytics workspace and Azure-automation solutions. Review monitoring and diagnostic settings for virtual machines. Review Azure Monitor and Log Analytics functionality.</p></li>

<li><p>Locate and select the <strong>\Allfiles\Lab02\azuredeploy.json</strong> file and select <strong>Open</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Note:</strong> Take a moment to review the template. We are deploying a virtual network with a Linux virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Save</strong> your changes.</p></li>

<li><p><strong>Skill 1: Configure VM insights</strong></p></li>
<li><p>In this task, you will enable Insights for a virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>Continue in the Azure portal.</p></li>
<li><p>Search for and select your virtual machine. Before proceeding, ensure the VM status is <strong>Running</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Monitoring</strong> section, select <strong>Insights</strong>, and then <strong>Enable</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-monitor/vm/vminsights-enable-overview">VM insights</a> is a method for monitoring the client workloads on your virtual machines and virtual machine scale sets. VM Insights collects performance data, displays an inventory of your existing VMs, and provides a guided experience to enable base monitoring for them.</p></li>
<li><p>For <strong>Data collection rule</strong>, select <strong>Create New</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-monitor/vm/monitor-virtual-machine-data-collection">Data collection rules</a> provide a consistent and centralized way to define and customize different data collection scenarios.</p></li>
<li><p>Set the <strong>Data collection rule name</strong> to VM2Rule1.</p></li>
<li><p>Notice the drop-down for <strong>Log Analytics workspace</strong>. Take the default, but notice you could make another selection.</p></li>
<li><p>Review the defaults and then select <strong>Create</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Ensure your new rule is selected in the <strong>Data collection rule</strong> drop-down, then select <strong>Configure</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>It may take a couple of minutes for VM Insights to deploy.</p></li>
<li><p>You do not need to wait for the deployment to finish, continue on.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Check your learning</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Can you enable VM Insights on a virtual machine?</p></li>

<li><p>In this task, you will create an action group and configure a notification method.</p></li>

<li><p>Select <strong>+ Create</strong> and then <strong>Action group</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-monitor/alerts/action-groups">Action groups</a> are a collection of notification preferences and actions. You can add up to five action groups to an alert rule. Action groups are executed concurrently, in no specific order. Multiple alert rules can use the same action group.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Basics</strong> tab, enter the following values for each setting.</p></li>
</ul>

<li><p><strong>Skill 3: Create alerts</strong></p></li>
<li><p>In this task, you will create two alert rules.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Monitor</strong> section, select <strong>Alerts</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-monitor/vm/monitor-virtual-machine-alerts">Virtual machine alerts</a> are automated notifications in Azure Monitor that provide insights into the status of Azure resource. There are no preconfigured alert rules for virtual machines, but there is a set of recommended alert rules you can enable. Or you can just create your own.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Create +</strong> and select <strong>Alert rule</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Create an alert rule based on CPU percentage usage</strong></p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Signal name</strong> drop-down select <strong>Percentage CPU</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>You want to be alerted when the CPU threshold is over 90%. So, change the <strong>Threshold value</strong> to 90.</p></li>
<li><p>Alerts can be based on metrics. Metrics are numerical values that are automatically collected at regular intervals and describe some aspect of a resource. For example, this metric would tell you the processor utilization of a virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>You also need to adjust the <strong>When to evaluate</strong> settings. It is important to be reasonable about how often the alert is checked.</p></li>
</ul>

<li><p><strong>Skill 4: Trigger an alert by resizing the virtual machine</strong></p></li>
<li><p>In this task, you will review performance data and resize a virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>Continue working with your virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Monitoring</strong> section select the <strong>Metrics</strong> blade. Take a few minutes to explore.</p></li>
<li><p>You can create a variety of different charts, like a <strong>Line chart</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>You can <strong>Scope</strong> the chart to include one or more virtual machines.</p></li>
<li><p>You can select the <strong>Metrics</strong> to include in the chart.</p></li>
<li><p>You can create a <strong>New alert rule</strong> from your data.</p></li>
<li><p>You can <strong>Save to the dashboard</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Monitoring</strong> section select the <strong>Logs</strong> blade. If necessary, <strong>close</strong> the <strong>Welcome to Log Analytics</strong> page. Take a few minutes to explore.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Topic</strong> drop-down select <strong>Resource type</strong> and then <strong>Virtual machines</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Notice there are a large number of built-in queries that might be of interest. For example, the <strong>Count heartbeats</strong> or <strong>Virtual Machine available memory</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Take time to explore and <strong>Run</strong> any queries of interest.</p></li>
<li><p>Depending on the virtual machine activity, there may not be a lot of events.</p></li>
<li><p>Based on your monitoring review you learn the CPU percentage is close to being maxed out. You decide to increase the number of CPUs.</p></li>
<li><p>You can scale the VM up or down by <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/virtual-machines/sizes/resize-vm?tabs=portal">changing the VM size</a>. In some cases, you must deallocate the VM first.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Availability + scale</strong> blade, select <strong>Size</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Set the virtual machine size to <strong>DS2_v2</strong> and click <strong>Resize</strong>. If prompted, confirm the change.</p></li>
<li><p>Choose another size if <strong>Standard DS2_v2</strong> is not available. Resizing is also known as vertical scaling, up or down.</p></li>
<li><p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image207.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.24097in" alt="Screenshot of the resize virtual machine page in the portal. " /></p></li>
<li><p>Navigate to the <strong>Alerts</strong> blade. Resizing the virtual machine should have triggered your VM2Changed alert rule. If needed, <strong>Refresh</strong> the page.</p></li>
<li><p>Select an alert and review the details. <strong>Edit</strong> the <strong>User response</strong> and ensure the status is <strong>Acknowledged</strong>. <strong>Save</strong> your changes.</p></li>
<li><p>You should also receive an email notification that reads, <strong>Important notice: Azure Monitor alert VM was updated…</strong> If not, open your email program and look for an email from azure-noreply@microsoft.com.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Check your learning.</strong></p></li>
<li><p>In the portal, can you access and review virtual machine metrics and logs?</p></li>
<li><p>Can you resize a virtual machine?</p></li>
<li><p>Can you manage alerts? For example, acknowledge that an alert has been reviewed.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Skill 5: Configure an alert processing rule (optional)</strong></p></li>
<li><p>In this task, you create an alert processing rule.</p></li>
<li><p>Continue in the <strong>Alerts</strong> blade, select <strong>Alert processing rules</strong> and then <strong>+ Create</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-monitor/alerts/alerts-processing-rules?tabs=portal">Alert processing rules</a> decide what happens when an alert is triggered. Alert processing rules can be used to suppress alert notification during maintenance periods.</p></li>
<li><p>Notice the alert scope is <strong>VM2</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Next: Rule settings</strong>, then select <strong>Suppress notifications</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Next: Scheduling</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Define a rule to suppress notifications during overnight maintenance.</p></li>
</ul>

<ul>
<li><p>Select <strong>Review + create</strong> to validate your input, then select <strong>Create</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Check your learning.</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Can you configure an alert processing rule? For example, to suppress alerts during a maintenance period.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Learn more with self-paced training</strong></p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/incident-response-with-alerting-on-azure/">Improve incident response with alerting on Azure</a>. Respond to incidents and activities in your infrastructure through alerting capabilities in Azure Monitor.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/monitor-azure-vm-using-diagnostic-data/">Monitor your Azure virtual machines with Azure Monitor</a>. Monitor your Azure VMs by using Azure Monitor to collect and analyze VM host and client metrics and logs.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Key takeaways</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Congratulations on completing the exercise. Here are the main takeaways:</p></li>
<li><p>Alerts help you detect and address issues before users notice there might be a problem with your infrastructure or application.</p></li>
<li><p>You can alert on any metric or log data in the Azure Monitor data platform.</p></li>
<li><p>An alert rule monitors your data and captures a signal that indicates something is happening on the specified resource.</p></li>
<li><p>An alert is triggered if the conditions of the alert rule are met. Several actions (email, SMS, push, voice) can be triggered.</p></li>
<li><p>Action groups include individuals that should be notified of an alert.</p></li>
<li><p>Alert processing rules decide what happens when an alert is triggered. Alert processing rules can be used to suppress alert notification during maintenance periods.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>

## Access Storage of Linux VM 

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><ul>
<li><p><strong>Lab requirements</strong></p></li>
<li><p>This lab requires an Azure subscription. Your subscription type may affect the availability of features in this lab. You may change the region, but the steps were tested using the <strong>(US) East</strong> region.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Estimated timing: 45 minutes</strong></p></li>
<li><p><strong>Lab scenario</strong></p></li>
<li><p>You have been asked to explore how virtual machines can use Azure storage. You want to explore using Azure file shares and Blobs. You want to add and mount a data disk on the virtual machine. Lastly, you want to explore how to transfer data from Azure to the virtual machine with azcopy.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Interactive lab simulation</strong></p></li>
<li><p>There is an interactive lab simulation that you might find useful for this topic. The simulation lets you click through a similar scenario at your own pace. There are differences between the interactive simulation and this lab, but many of the core concepts are the same. An Azure subscription is not required.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://mslearn.cloudguides.com/en-us/guides/AZ-900%20Exam%20Guide%20-%20Azure%20Fundamentals%20Exercise%2011">Create a virtual machine with the CLI</a>. Use the CLI to deploy a virtual machine. Review Azure Advisor recommendations.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Job skills</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Skill 1: Create a virtual machine and add a data disk (CLI).</p></li>
<li><p>Skill 2: Access an Azure file share from the virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>Skill 2: Copy a file from Azure blob storage to the virtual machine data disk.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Azure Virtual Machines Architecture Diagram</strong></p></li>
<li><p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image208.webp" style="width:6.5in;height:4.03819in" alt="Diagram for the storage architecture" /></p></li>
<li><p><strong>Skill 1: Create a virtual machine and add a data disk.</strong></p></li>
<li><p>In this task, you will create and configure a virtual machine using the Cloud Shell and the Azure CLI. You will then use the add a data disk and partition and mount the data disk.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Configure the Cloud Shell</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Sign in to the Azure portal - https://portal.azure.com.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Cancel</strong> the <strong>Welcome to Microsoft Azure</strong> splash screen.</p></li>
<li><p>Select the <strong>Cloud Shell</strong> icon in the top right of the Azure Portal.</p></li>
<li><p><img src="subjects/tech/azure/az-104-elearn-portal-labs/img/media/image209.webp" style="width:4.82292in;height:0.83333in" alt="Screenshot of the Cloud Shell icon." /></p></li>
<li><p>The <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/cloud-shell/overview">Cloud Shell</a> is an interactive, authenticated, browser-accessible terminal for managing Azure resources. It provides the flexibility of choosing the shell experience that best suits the way you work, either Bash or PowerShell.</p></li>
<li><p>When prompted to select either <strong>Bash</strong> or <strong>PowerShell</strong>, select <strong>Bash</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Did you know?</strong> If you mostly work with Linux systems, Bash (CLI) feels more familiar. If you mostly work with Windows systems, Azure PowerShell feels more familiar.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>No storage account required</strong> and select your <strong>Subscription</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Click <strong>Apply</strong> and wait while a terminal is created.</p></li>
<li><p>This lab will use the Azure CLI so check out the documentation for virtual machines.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p>az vm --help</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p><strong>Create a virtual machine and add a data disk using the CLI</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Use the CLI to create a new virtual machine named <strong>vm3</strong> in the <strong>(US) East</strong> region. Notice SSH key file are stored in the /home directory.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> az vm create --name vm3 --resource-group rg1 --image Ubuntu2204 --admin-username adminuser --generate-ssh-keys --location eastus</p></li>
<li><p>This is a very simple implemention of the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/cli/azure/vm?view=azure-cli-latest#az-vm-create">az vm create</a> command.</p></li>
<li><p> After the machine deploys, ensure the <strong>powerState</strong> is <strong>VM running</strong>. Make a note of the <strong>publicIpAddress</strong>. In a later step, you will need the public IP address and SSH key file to connect to the virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p> Use <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cli/azure/vm/disk?view=azure-cli-latest">az vm disk</a> to add a 4 GB data disk to the virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> az vm disk attach --resource-group rg1 --vm-name vm3 --name Disk1 --new --size-gb 4</p></li>
<li><p> Verify the data disk was created. This command will also show the operating system disk information.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p>az disk list --output table</p></li>
<li><p>Your next steps will need to be performed on the virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Connect to the virtual machine and configure the data disk</strong></p></li>
<li><p>To format and mount the data disk you will need to connect to the virtual machine. Substitute your public IP address. When prompted, confirm you want to connect. When successfully connected, the prompt will change to <em>adminuser@vm3</em>.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> ssh -i ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pem adminuser@public_ip_address</p></li>
<li><p>The following steps are based on the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/virtual-machines/linux/add-disk?tabs=ubuntu#format-and-mount-the-disk">format and mount the disk</a> documentation.</p></li>
<li><p> Retrieve information about the virtual machine storage. Make a note of the name of your new 4 GB disk. For example, <strong>sdc</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> lsblk -o NAME,SIZE,MOUNTPOINT</p></li>
<li><p> Use <em>parted</em> to partition the disk. If necessary, change <em>sdc</em> to your disk name.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> sudo parted /dev/sdc --script mklabel gpt mkpart xfspart xfs 0% 100%;</p></li>
<li><p> Use <em>partprobe</em> to inform the operating system of partition table changes. If necessary, change <em>sdc</em> to your disk name.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> sudo partprobe /dev/sdc</p></li>
<li><p> Use <em>mkfs</em> to build the Linux file system. If necessary, change <em>sdc</em> to your disk name.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> sudo mkfs.xfs /dev/sdc1</p></li>
<li><p> Make a directory</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> sudo mkdir /datadrive</p></li>
<li><p> Mount the data disk to the directory.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> sudo mount /dev/sdc1 /datadrive</p></li>
<li><p> Verify the disk now has a mount point.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> df</p></li>
<li><p> Verify there are no files on the data disk.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p>ls /datadrive</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>Minimize the Cloud Shell, but don’t close the connection. In the next task, you will continue working in the virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Check your learning.</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Can you access and configure the Cloud Shell?</p></li>
<li><p>Can you create a virtual machine using the CLI?</p></li>
<li><p>Can you add a data disk to a virtual machine (portal or CLI)?</p></li>
<li><p>Can you partition and mount a data disk, so it is usable?</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Skill 2: Access an Azure file share from the virtual machine</strong></p></li>
<li><p>In this task, you will create a storage account and file share. You will then give the virtual machine access to the file share and connect to the file share.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Create and configure a storage account</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Switch to the portal.</p></li>
<li><p>Search for and select Storage accounts.</p></li>
<li><p>An <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/storage/common/storage-account-overview">Azure storage account</a> contains all of your Azure Storage data objects: blobs, files, queues, and tables. The storage account provides a unique namespace for your data that’s accessible from anywhere in the world over HTTP or HTTPS.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>+ Create</strong> and on the <strong>Basics</strong> tab specify the following settings.</p></li>
</ul>
<table style="width:91%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 38%" />
<col style="width: 53%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><ul>
<li><p><strong>Setting</strong></p></li>
</ul></th>
<th><ul>
<li><p><strong>Value</strong></p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Subscription</p></li>
</ul></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p>your Azure subscription</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Resource group</p></li>
</ul></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p><strong>RG1</strong> (create new if necessary)</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Storage account name</p></li>
</ul></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p>any globally unique name between 3 and 24 characters in length consisting of letters and digits</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Region</p></li>
</ul></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p><strong>(US) East</strong></p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Performance</p></li>
</ul></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p><strong>Standard</strong> (notice the Premium option)</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Redundancy</p></li>
</ul></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p><strong>Geo-redundant storage</strong> (notice the other options)</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Make read access to data in the event of regional availability</p></li>
</ul></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Check the box</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<ul>
<li><p>Did you notice a Network Watcher resource group? This is because we took the defaults when we created the virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Review + Create</strong> and then <strong>Create</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Wait for the deployment to finish, then select <strong>Go to resource</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Create and configure a file share</strong></p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Data storage</strong> section, click <strong>File shares</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/storage/files/">Azure Files</a> offers fully managed file shares in the cloud that are accessible via the industry standard Server Message Block (SMB) protocol, Network File System (NFS) protocol, and Azure Files REST API.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>+ File share</strong> and on the <strong>Basics</strong> tab give the file share a name, share1.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>Backup</strong> tab, ensure <strong>Enable backup</strong> is <strong>not checked</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>You will configure backups in another lab.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Review + create</strong>, and then <strong>Create</strong>. Wait for the file share to deploy.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Upload</strong> and <strong>Browse for files</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select a file and then click <strong>Upload</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>If you have downloaded the lab files, there is a filedoc.txt file for you to use.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Browse</strong> left menu, to ensure your file was uploaded.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Connect</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>The portal provides scripts to help connect from Windows, Linux, and macOS.</p></li>
<li><p>Switch to the <strong>Linux</strong> tab and select <strong>Show script</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Copy to the clipboard</strong> (icon bottom right in the script).</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Give the virtual machine authentication to the storage account</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Your virtual machine must be able to authenticate to the storage account.</p></li>
<li><p>In the portal, locate your virtual machine (vm3).</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Security</strong> settings, select <strong>Identity</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>On the <strong>System assigned</strong> tab, toggle the <strong>Status</strong> to <strong>On</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>A system-assigned <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/entra/identity/managed-identities-azure-resources/overview">managed identity</a> is an identity created and managed by Azure for a specific service instance. The managed identity allows the resource to access other Azure resources without storing credentials.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Save</strong> your changes and select <strong>Yes</strong> to <strong>Enable system assigned managed identity</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Run the connect script on the virtual machine and test the file share.</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Switch to the Cloud Shell and your virtual machine connection.</p></li>
<li><p>Create a connection script using the vi editor. If you prefer you may use another editor.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> vi script.sh</p></li>
<li><p> Right click and <strong>Paste as plain text</strong>. Your connection script should display.</p></li>
<li><p> Type <strong>:wq</strong> to write and quit. <strong>Enter</strong> to exit the editor.</p></li>
<li><p> Run the script.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> sh script.sh</p></li>
<li><p> Verify you are able to access the file share. The file you uploaded should be listed.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> ls /mnt/share1</p></li>
<li><p> Copy the file share file to the virtual machine data disk.</p></li>
<li><p>code</p></li>
<li><p> sudo cp /mnt/share1/* /datadrive</p></li>
<li><p> Verify the file share was copied to the data disk.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p>ls /datadrive</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>Minimize the Cloud Shell, but don’t close the connection. In the next task, you will continue working on the virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Check your learning</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Can you create a storage account in the portal (not part of the credential)?</p></li>
<li><p>Can you create a file share and upload a file in the portal (not part of the credential)?</p></li>
<li><p>Can you use the portal to get a script to connect a virtual machine to the file share?</p></li>
<li><p>Can you assign a managed identity to the virtual machine so it can access a resource, like a storage account?</p></li>
<li><p>Can you run a script to mount the file share from the virtual machine?</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Skill 3: Copy a file from Azure blob storage to the virtual machine data disk</strong></p></li>
<li><p>In this task, you will create a blob container. You will assign an Azure role to storage account. Lastly, you will use azcopy to transfer files to the virtual machine.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Create a blob storage container with test file</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Switch to the portal and your storage account.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Data storage</strong> section, click <strong>Containers</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/storage/blobs/storage-blobs-introduction">Azure blob storage</a> is an object storage solution optimized for storing massive amounts of unstructured data. Blob storage is organized into containers.</p></li>
<li><p>Click <strong>+ Container</strong> and <strong>Create</strong> a container with the following settings:</p></li>
</ul>
<table style="width:60%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 21%" />
<col style="width: 38%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><ul>
<li><p><strong>Setting</strong></p></li>
</ul></th>
<th><ul>
<li><p><strong>Value</strong></p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Name</p></li>
</ul></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p>data</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Public access level</p></li>
</ul></td>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Notice the access level is set to private</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<ul>
<li><p>Select your <strong>data</strong> container and then click <strong>Upload</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Browse for files</strong>. Select a file then click <strong>Upload</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>If you have downloaded the lab files, there is a blobimage.png file for you to use.</p></li>
<li><p>Select your uploaded file and copy the <strong>URL</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Assign the virtual machine the Storage Blob Data Contributor role to the storage account</strong></p></li>
<li><p>In the portal, search for and select <strong>vm3</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>In the <strong>Security</strong> settings, select <strong>Identity</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Azure role assignments</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/role-based-access-control/overview">RBAC</a> helps you manage who has access to Azure resources. There are many Azure roles that can be assigned, or you can create a custom set of permissions.</p></li>
<li><p>Select <strong>Add role assignment (Preview)</strong> and provide the managed identity access to the storage account file share. Both read and write capability is required. <strong>Save</strong> your changes.</p></li>
</ul>

<td><ul>

<td><ul>

<td><ul>

<ul>
<li><p>There are many <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/role-based-access-control/built-in-roles/storage#storage-blob-data-contributor">storage roles</a> , including roles specifically for the storage account and for Blob storage. The <strong>Storage Blob Data Contributor</strong> role provides read, write, and delete on Azure storage containers and blobs.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Use AzCopy to transfer files</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Return to the Cloud Shell and ensure you are still connected to vm3.</p></li>
<li><p>Download AzCopy and extract the file.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/storage/common/storage-ref-azcopy">AzCopy</a> is a command-line tool that moves data into and out of Azure Storage.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p>wget https://aka.ms/downloadazcopy-v10-linux</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> sudo tar xzf downloadazcopy-v10-linux</p></li>
<li><p> Make folder for azcopy and copy the executable to that directory.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p>sudo mkdir /opt/azcopy</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> sudo cp ./azcopy_linux_amd64_*/azcopy /opt/azcopy/</p></li>
<li><p> Run this command to login azcopy as the virtual machine managed identity. Ensure there is a <strong>succeeded</strong> response.</p></li>
<li><p>shell</p></li>
<li><p> sudo /opt/azcopy/azcopy login --identity</p></li>
<li><p> Copy from Azure blob storage to the new virtual machine data drive. Substitute the blob file URL you copied from the portal.</p></li>
<li><p>code</p></li>
<li><p> sudo /opt/azcopy/azcopy copy "urlblob" /datadrive</p></li>
<li><p> Verify the file you uploaded to blob storage has been copied to the /datadrive.</p></li>
<li><p>code</p></li>
<li><p>ls /datadrive</p></li>
<li></li>

<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/training/modules/configure-azure-files-file-sync/">Configure Azure file shares and file sync</a>. Learn how to configure Azure file shares.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/training/modules/add-and-size-disks-in-azure-virtual-machines/">Add and size disks in Azure virtual machines</a>. Understand and create the different types of disk storage available to Azure virtual machines (VMs). This includes adding a data disk and resizing a data disk.</p></li>
<li><p><a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/training/modules/implement-access-management-for-azure-resources/">Implement access management for Azure resources</a>. Explore how to use built-in Azure roles, managed identities, and RBAC-policy to control access to Azure resources. Identity is the key to secure solutions.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Key takeaways</strong></p></li>
<li><p>Congratulations on completing the exercise. Here are the main takeaways:</p></li>
<li><p>An Azure storage account contains non-relational data including blobs and files.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure file shares are accessible with the SMB protocol, NFS protocol, and Azure Files REST API.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure virtual machine data disks are block-level storage volumes.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure blob storage is optimized for storing massive amounts of unstructured data, like images and videos.</p></li>
<li><p>AzCopy is a utility to transfer data from virtual machines to Azure storage accounts.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>

## Backup VMs

<th><p><strong>Lab requirements</strong></p>
<p>This lab requires an Azure virtual machine. If you don’t have a virtual machine, there are instructions to create one.</p>
<p>This lab requires an Azure subscription. Your subscription type may affect the availability of features in this lab. You may change the regions, but the steps were tested using the <strong>(US) East</strong> region.</p>
<p><strong>Estimated timing: 30 minutes</strong></p>
<p><strong>Lab scenario</strong></p>
<p>Your organization is evaluating how to backup Azure virtual machines. Backup will protect the virtual machines from accidental or malicious data loss. You plan to explore using the Azure Backup Center.</p>
<p><strong>Interactive lab simulation</strong></p>
<p>There is an interactive lab simulation that you might find useful for this topic. The simulation lets you click through a similar scenario at your own pace. There are differences between the interactive simulation and this lab, but many of the core concepts are the same. An Azure subscription is not required.</p>

Create a recovery services vault and implement an Azure virtual machine backup. Implement on-premises file and folder backup using the Microsoft Azure Recovery Services agent. On-premises backups are outside the scope of this lab, but it might be helpful to view those steps.</p></li>
</ul>

<p>In this module, you learned about Linux virtual machines in Azure.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>In the first exercise, you created a virtual machine in the portal, connected using SSH, and installed the Nginx server.</p></li>
<li><p>In the second exercise, you enabled VM Insights, created action groups and alerts, and monitored the virtual machine metrics and logs.</p></li>
<li><p>In the third exercise, you added a data disk, and accessed blob and file storage. Additionally you assigned an Azure role, configured a managed identity, and used AzCopy.</p></li>
<li><p>In the fourth exercise, you used Azure Backup to create a virtual machine backup policy.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Key takeaways</strong></p>
<p>Here are the main takeaways for the module.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Azure virtual machines are on-demand, scalable computing resources. Both Windows and Linux virtual machines are available.</p></li>
<li><p>Configuring virtual machines includes choosing an operating system, image size, storage, and networking settings.</p></li>
<li><p>There are several ways to securely connect to a Linux virtual machine. One of the most common connections is SSH with a credential file.</p></li>
<li><p>Network Security Group rules let you allow or deny inbound and outbound port connections. For example, port 22 for SSH.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Monitor provides alerts to help you detect and address issues before users notice.</p></li>
<li><p>You can configure alerts on any virtual machine metric or log data.</p></li>
<li><p>Data disks can be added to virtual machines. In Linux, the disk must be formatted and mounted.</p></li>
<li><p>Virtual machines can access Azure file shares and blob storage. Managed identities and Azure roles provide secure access.</p></li>
<li><p>AzCopy is a utility to transfer data from virtual machines to Azure storage accounts.</p></li>
<li><p>Azure Backup provides retention and backup policies for virtual machines.</p>
<hr /></li>
</ul></th>
